### PAGE 7
## HOW IT ALL BEGAN

When the idea of recording the story of the Telfer family in South Australia was first suggested, Hope Simmons had already spent several years researching the early lives of Francis Telfer and his family.

Barbara Lodge was particularly interested in the story of Adam Telfer and his brothers and sister who had followed their Uncle Francis to South Australia.

It was decided to co-operate in the production of a book. Family members were invited to participate by contributing information, and many of them did so.

The project was never intended to be a definitive history, but rather the continuing story of the Telfers from the time they left Scotland, through their pioneering years, their achievements, and the legacy they left to their descendants. We appreciate the co-operation given by those many interested family members who did respond and provide anecdotes, photographs and family trees. Unfortunately some gaps remain where information was not provided.

Thanks go to Neville Simmons who provided invaluable assistance in typing the first section of the manuscript, and to Ted Lane who handled the financial matters associated with the project. Perhaps in years to come, future generations of Telfers will feel inspired to continue the "never-ending story"...

Hope Simmons, Adelaide
Barbara Lodge, Ardrossan
.... 1989

Telfer is an old French name meaning "cut iron" or "iron cleaver". It was originally the nickname for a man who could cleave clean through the iron armour of his foe.

The Telfers probably originated in the south of France. The earliest known reference to the name was that of the jongleur who crossed from Normandy with the armies of William the Conqueror, and who sang "The Song of Roland" at the Battle of Hastings in 1066. The family apparently followed the Norman invasion and gradually moved northwards through England until they finally settled in the Border areas of Scotland.

Throughout the following centuries, there were many references to "Telfers" who have held positions of some importance, or gained notoriety in other ways.

Willelms Taillefer witnessed a Charter by Margaret, Countess of Buchan, in favour of the Abbey of Arbroath c. 1210.

William Tailfere had confirmation of a charter of the land of Harecleuche in the Shire of Lanark in 1369. The Tailfairs of Haircleugh are on record for 300 years.

Andrea Taillefere was a charter witness at Inverkeithing in 1413.

John Talzefir was scrutinifer to Sir William Bothwell in 1450.

Laurence Talliefere, Scottish merchant, had a safe conduct into England in 1464.

Thomas Tailyefere was Burgess of Stirling in 1476.

### PAGE 8
John Tailfeyr and Laurence Taillefere were Burgesses of Edinburgh in 1527.

Sir Alexander Talifar was prebendary of Holy Trinity in Edinburgh in 1527.

Arthur Talliefere had a pension from the great customs of Edinburgh in 1529.

Laurence Talliefeire was Treasurer of Dunkeld in 1539.

Arthur Taillefere de Crechmond, a witness in 1547, may be the Master Arthur Talzefere, Canon of Aberdeen, in 1549.

Cuthbert Tailliefere was servant to Tuedy of Dreva in 1592.

William Tailyeffer was banished from Stirling Castle in 1611.

Alexander Taillephier was heir of Bethia Oliphant in 1643.

Charles Teilfeire was master of a family in the parish of Borgue in 1684.

On 1st November, 1696, twenty-eight people were drowned at Canoubie after attending church. Reference is made to one of them on an inscription in the churchyard.

Here lyes George Tealfer who died in the water, Nov. the 1, 1696, being the Lord's Day, as they were going home from the kirk."

There were many other variations of the spelling until it evolved into its present form of "Telfer" in about the 18th century. It is widely recognised that the modern name of "Telford" evolved from the same source. Thomas Telford, the famous Scottish engineer, used to say, "When I was ignorant of Latin, I did not suspect that Telfer, my true name, might be translated "I bear arms" (tela fera) and, thinking it unmeaning, adopted "Telford".

Our Telfer ancestors settled in Scotland, in the district now known as the County of Roxburghshire. The area became important in history in the 12th century when King David of Scotland established the great abbeys of Jedburgh, Kelso and Melrose, but there was religious activity for at least 300 years

Newcastle and the Liddesdale Hills.

before that. The abbeys became the centres of education, and the very early wool industry was established by the monks. Roxburghshire was very poor and backward, even into the late 18th century, but it grew in importance with the coming of the railway in the 19th century. It was, and is, a most important sheep farming and wool producing county, and the centre of an important wool and knitwear industry.

Much of the county is rolling hills land, with rounded summits and grassy slopes, a few clumps of fir trees and some small streams. The county town is Jedburgh, and the other towns of any size and importance are Hawick and Kelso. The County motto is "Ne cede Contra Audentior Ito" (Do not give way to misfortune; instead advance more boldly).

The winters are very cold with average January temperatures of 2 degrees Celsius. In July the average summer temperature is 15 degrees Celsius. The average annual rainfall is 28 inches.

The area was the scene of much civil strife and turbulence, not only because of the wars with England, but also because of the constant family feuding. In the 12th and 13th centuries, the Kings of Scotland, who were attempting to unify the country, found it impossible to keep order. The area was on the general route

### PAGE 9
The Liddesdale Hills — an ancient sheepfold.

into England, and was worked over many times by the English (with fire and sword). Following the destruction of the great abbeys in the 16th century, the area was controlled by robber families who determined who should pass through it. The Telfers must have been affected by the constant wars and skirmishes which took place all about them.

The Ballad of Jamie Telfer of the "Fair Dodhead", written about the middle of the 16th century, describes one such incident. In this case the English, under the command of the Captain of Bewcastle, were the aggressors. The marauding English went to the "fair Dodhead", stole Jamie Telfer's cows, and ransacked his house. Jamie, left with a weeping wife and three children ran ten miles for help. His first appeal, to Gibbie Elliot, fell on deaf ears, but he continued on to other neighbours who were all prepared to come to his aid and avenge the theft. A raiding party was formed to pursue the English back across the border. Appeals for the return of the cattle failed, so the Scots set upon the English and a bloody battle ensued. The English leader was badly wounded, but the Scots continued to his home where they demanded the release of all the cattle. The poem ends in triumph, for when they returned to the "fair Dodhead", instead of his own ten stolen cattle, Jamie Telfer now had 33!

## THE BALLAD OF JAMIE TELFER

I T fell about the Martinmas tide,
When our Border steeds get corn and hay,
The captain of Bewcastle hath boun' him to ride,
And he's ower to Tividale to drive a prey.

The first ae guide that they met wi',
It was high up Hardhaughswire;
The second guide that they met wi'
It was laigh down in Borthwick water.

"What tidings, what tidings, my trusty guide?"
"Nae tidings, nae tidings, I hae to thee;
But gin ye'll gae to the fair Dodhead,
Mony a cow's calf I'll let thee see."

And whan they cam to the fair Dodhead,
Right hastily they climbed the peel;
They loosed the kye out, ane and a',
And ranshacked the house right weel.

Now Jamie Telfer's heart was sair,
The tear aye rowing in his e'e;
He pled wi' the captain to hae his gear,
Or else revenged he wad be.

The captain turned him round and leugh;
Said — "Man, there's naething in thy house,
But ae auld sword without a sheath,
That hardly now wad fell a mouse!"

The sun was na up, but the moon was down,
It was the griming of a new fa'n snaw,
Jamie Telfer has run ten miles a-foot,
Between the Dodhead and the Stobs's Ha'.

And whan he cam to the fair tower yett,
He shouted loud, and weel cried he,
Till out bespak auld Gibby Elliot —
"Whae's this that brings the fray to me?"

"It's I, Jamie Telfer o' the fair Dodhead.
And a harried man I think I be!
There's naething left at the fair Dodhead,
But a waefu' wife and bairnies three."

"Gae seek your succour at Branksome Ha',
For succour ye'se get nane frae me!
Gae seek your succour where ye paid black-mail,
For, man! ye ne'er paid money to me."

Jamie Telfer has turned him round about,
I wut the tear blinded his e'e—
"I'll ne'er pay mail to Elliot again,
And the fair Dodhead I'll never see!"

He had turned him to the Tiviot side,
E'en as fast as he could dri'e,
Till he cam to the Coulart cleugh,
And there he shouted baith loud and hie.

### PAGE 10
Then up bespoke him auld Jock Grieve—
"Whae's this that brings the fray to me?"
"It's I, Jamie Telfer o' the fair Dodhead,
A harried man I trow I be.

"There's noething left in the fair Dodhead,
But a greeting wife and bairnie's three!
And sax poor calves stand in the sta',
A' routing loud for their minnie."

"Alack and wae!" quo' auld Jock Grieve,
"Alack! my heart is sair for thee!
For I was married on the elder sister,
And you on the youngest of a' the three."

Then he has ta'en out a bonny black,
Was right weel fed wi' corn and hay,
And he's set Jamie Telfer on his back,
To the Catslockhill to tuk' the fray.

And whan he cam to the Catslockhill,
He shouted loud and weel cried he,
Till out and spak him William's Wat —
"O whae's this brings the fray to me?"

"It's I, Jamie Telfer o' the fair Dodhead,
A harried man I think I be!
The captain of Bewcastle has driven my gear;
For God's sake rise, and succour me!"

"Alas for wae!" quo' William's Wat,
"Alack, for thee my heart is sair!
I never cam by the fair Dodhead,
That ever I fand thy basket bare."

He's set his twa sons on coal-black steeds,
Himsel' upon a freckled gray,
And they are on wi' Jamie Telfer,
To Branksome Ha' to take the fray.

And whan they cam to Branksome Ha',
They shouted a' baith loud and hie,
Till up and spak him buuld Buccleuch,
Said — "Whae's this brings the fray to me?"

"It's I, Jamie Telfer o' the fair Dodhead,
And a harried man I think I be!
There's nought left in the fair Dodhead,
But a greeting wife and bairnie's three."

"Alack for wae!" quoth the gude nuld lord,
"And ever my heart is wae for thee!
But fye, gar cry on Willie, my son,
And see that he come to me speedilie!

"Gar warn the water, braid and wide,
Gar warn it soon and hastily!
They that winna ride for Telfer's kye,
Let them never look in the face o' me!

"Warn Wat o' Harden, and his sons,
Wi' them will Borthwick water ride;
Warn Gaudilands, and Allanhaugh,
And Gilmanscleugh, and Commonside.

"Ride by the gate at Priesthaughawire,
And warn the Currors o' the Lee;
As ye come down the Hermitage Slack,
Warn doughty Willie o' Gorrinberry."

The Scots they rade, the Scots they ran.
Sae sturkly and sae steadilie!
And aye the ower-word o' the thrang.
Was — "Rise for Branksome readilie!"

The gear was driven the Frostylee up,
Frae the Frostylee unto the plain,
Whan Willie has looked his men before,
And saw the kye right fast driving.

"Whae drives thir kye?" 'gan Willie say,
"To mak an outspeckle o' me?"
"It's I, the captain o' Bewcastle, Willie;
I winna layne my name for thee."

"O will ye let Telfer's kye gae back,
Or will ye do ought for regard o' me?
Or, by the faith at my body," quo' Willie Scott,
"I'se ware my dame's calf-skin on thee!"

"I winna let the kye gae back,
Neither for thy love, nor yet thy fear,
But I will drive Jamie Telfer's kye,
In spite of every Scott that's here."

"Set on them, lads!" quo' Willie than;
"Fye, lads, set on them cruelie!
For ere they win to the Ritterford,
Many a toom saddle there shall be!"

Then till't they gaed, wi' heart and hand;
The blows fell thick as bickering hail;
And mony a horse ran masterless,
And mony a comely cheek was pale!

But Willie was stricken over the head,
And thro' the knapscap the sword has gone;
And Harden grat for very rage,
Whan Willie on the ground lay slain.

But he's ta'en aff his gude steel-cap,
And thrice he's waved it in the air —
The Dinlay snaw was ne'er mair white,
Nor the lyart locks o' Harden's hair.

"Revenge! revenge!" nuld Wat 'gan cry;
"Fye, lads, lay on them cruelie!
We'll ne'er see Tiviotside again,
Or Willie's denth revenged shall be."

### PAGE 11
O mony a horse ran masterless,
The splintered lances flew on hie;
But or they wan to the Kershope ford,
The Scots had gotten the victory.

John o' Brigham there was slain,
And John o' Barlow, as I hear say,
And thirty mae o' the captain's men,
Lay bleeding on the ground that day.

The captain was run thro' the thick of the thigh —
O weel may his lady for him make maen!
If he had lived this hundred year,
He had never been loved by woman again.

"Hae back thy kye!" the captain said;
"Dear kye, I trow, to some they be!
For gin I should live a hundred years,
There will ne'er fair lady smile on me."

Then word is gane to the captain's bride,
Even in the bower where that she lay,
That her lord was prisoner in enemy's land,
Since to Tividale he had led the way.

"I'd sooner hae sewed a winding-sheet,
And helped to put it over his head,
Than hae seen him maimed, as nae man should be,
Whan he ower Liddel his men did lead."

There was a wild gallant among us a',
His name was Watty wi' the Wudspurs,* Cried — "On for his house in Stanegirthside, If ony man will ride with us!"

When they cam to the Stanegirthside,
They dang wi' trees, and burst the door;
They loosed out a' the captain's kye,
And set them forth our lads before.

There was an auld wife ayont the fire,
A wee bit o' the captain's kin—
"Whae daur loose out the captain's kye,
Or answer to him and his men?"

"It's I, Watty Wudspurs: loose the kye!
I winna layne my name frae thee!
And I will loose out the captain's kye,
In scorn of a' his men and he."

When they cam to the fair Dodhead,
They were a welcome sight to see!
For instead of his ain ten milk-kye,
Jamie Telfer has gotten thirty and three.

And he has paid the rescue shot,
Baith wi' gowd and white monie;
And at the burial o' Willie Scott,
I wot was mony a weeping e'e.

*Hotspur, or Madspur.

The main street of Newcastleton as it passes through Douglas Square.

The parish records of Roxburghshire date back to about 1700. However, these are not always reliable, as the quality and accuracy of the information depended entirely on the conscience, handwriting and efficiency of the Session Clerk! The most frequent entries are baptisms, followed by marriages. Deaths are hardly ever recorded. Many entries would not have been made because of the need to pay a Government tax of one shilling for each birth recorded. This was a great deal of money! Few very old gravestones remain, as the poorer people could not afford a stone and would make do with a wooden cross which would not last.

Religion played an important part in the lives of the people for many centuries, and a sound Biblical training was considered essential. The Telfers were staunch adherents of the Presbyterian Church, and those who came to South Australia were members of the Edgerston parish.

As well as being the place for the worship of God, the Church also undertook some social welfare functions! An article in the "Border Magazine" describes a typical church in the Border areas —

"The old church was in use before 1260 but its date of construction is unknown. One Sunday afternoon the roof collapsed — after the service

### PAGE 12
fortunately! A new church was built in 1690 and that was replaced yet again in 1876. The church built in 1690 was at first protected by a simple roof of rude beams, covered and thatched with heather, and on the bare rafters which supported this primitive covering, lay a gruesome object, the presence of which in such a place forms an instructive comment both on the callous rudeness of the times and the prudent thrift of the Scottish Borderers. This was a coffin used at the burial of those who were "on the parish". It had a hinged bottom and was able to do service at the pauper funerals of quite a few generations."

Scotland had an excellent school system and all the Telfers who came to South Australia could read and write.

It seems probable that the Telfers were shepherds for several generations, hard working, but not well-monied. Their land holdings were small and their families were large so there would have been little to spare.

The Industrial Revolution of the early 19th century brought hardships to most people, including the sheep farmers of Roxburghshire. Emigration was suggested as the best means of relieving the misery of the unemployed, and many Telfers left their homeland to migrate to Canada, New Zealand or Australia.

Many prominent men were attracted by the idea of a new colony in Australia, to be settled by free men. News of the explorations of the River Murray by Captain Charles Sturt and Captain Barker focused attention on the south coast of Australia. The colony was to be established in accordance with the principles of Edward Gibbon Wakefield, who advocated the sale of land to free men, the proceeds of the sales being used to promote emigration, especially of poor people. There were to be no convicts. Land in the new colony was to be open only to British subjects, and benefits were available to emigrants who were "natives of Great Britain and Ireland". All land was to be surveyed and sold by public

auction, in lots of 80 acres. Family emigration was to be actively encouraged.

Public meetings were held all over the United Kingdom, to promote the aims of the new colony. The price of the land was to be £1 per acre, but as there was very little interest, the suggested price was reduced to 12 shillings per acre, and the South Australia Company was formed.

The chief objects of the Company were to establish a town, build houses, construct wharves, survey land for the purposes of agriculture and the production of wool for the European market and the mills of Great Britain, and the development of whale, seal and other fisheries.

Strict criteria applied to all persons who wished to emigrate and every effort was made to ensure that all the new settlers had skills which would be useful in building the new colony. Those who were encouraged to apply were —

1. Married agricultural labourers, shepherds, herdsmen, copper miners and general labourers, not exceeding 45 years of age;

2. Single men or widowers without children under 16; any of the above classes not exceeding 40 years of age;

3. Single family domestic servants or widows without children under 16, not exceeding 35 years of age;

4. Married mechanics such as masons, brick layers, blacksmiths and farmers, wheelwrights, sawyers, carpenters, gardeners, not exceeding 45 years of age;

5. Single men of the above classes, not exceeding 45 years of age;

6. Wives and children of married emigrants.

The cost of the passage was £4.0.0 for each male and £3.0.0 for each female. Children under 14 years of age travelled for half the above rates.

Candidates had to be in the habit of working for wages. They must be sober, industrious, of good moral character, in good health, free from all mental and

### PAGE 13
bodily defects, within the ages specified, appear physically capable of labour, and have been vaccinated or had smallpox.

When the prospective emigrants had passed all of these tests, they then had to find their own outfits, and these too had to be inspected by the Emigration Agent. Each male over the age of 12 years had to have a minimum of 6 shirts, 6 pairs of stockings, 2 warm flannel shirts, 2 pairs of new shoes or boots, 2 complete suits of strong exterior clothing, 4 towels and 2 lbs of marine soap. Each female had to have 6 shifts, 2 flannel petticoats, 6 pairs of stockings, 2 pairs of strong boots or shoes, 2 strong gowns (one of which must be of warm material), 4 towels and 2 lbs of marine soap.

Only half a ton of baggage, closely packed in strong boxes, was allowed for each person over 12 years of age. Mattresses, feather beds, firearms and offensive weapons, wines, spirits, beer, gunpowder, percussion caps, lucifer matches, and any other dangerous or noxious articles were forbidden.

This was a meagre store of worldly goods with which to start a new life in a strange land, but for the poor it must have represented quite a struggle to find the means to purchase even the basic outfit.

The first three ships set sail in February, 1836. The first settlement at Kangaroo Island was abandoned when Captain Hindmarsh arrived on the "Buffalo" and declared the site unsuitable. Colonel Light explored the coast of the mainland and anchored at Holdfast Bay. On 28th December, 1836, the "Buffalo", bringing Captain Hindmarsh, arrived and the proclamation of the new colony of South Australia took place.

The voyage to South Australia was more than enough to test the endurance of the most hardy settlers. Passengers were huddled together in very confined areas equipped with bunks and a long table, and the upper decks were packed with sheep and chickens. The diet consisted mainly of salt pork and weevily biscuits, and the water soon became foul. The

stock had first call on the very limited water supplies, and the passengers were left with very little to drink, and none at all for baths. Cramped quarters, overcrowding, poor diet, inadequate sanitation, extremes of weather and the constant tossing of the tiny vessels must have made the long voyage anything but pleasant.

Francis Telfer, his wife, Jean, and their four children would have endured such conditions and it is no wonder that their tiny baby did not survive the journey in 1838.

There was no proper landing place at Holdfast Bay, but until the end of 1839, nearly all vessels anchored there. Boatloads of passengers were taken as near the shore as possible, and the women and children, and sometimes the men, were carried ashore "pick-a-back" by the sailors. The baggage was dumped on the sand. Accidents were many and frequent. Boats capsized and packages went overboard. Even when the landing was safely negotiated, there remained the daunting task of getting themselves, their luggage and other goods over the plain. All kinds of devices were pressed into service — handcarts, wheelbarrows, sledges and skids — the haulage being by manpower.

In 1837 the "Harbour" was proclaimed the legal port as distinct from the "Bay". A canal was cut and a wharf constructed, but the land was low and swampy and the head of the creek was very shallow. This uninviting place bore the expressive name of "Port Misery".

The new land was an extreme culture shock to the settlers. The heat, flies, ants, mosquitoes, snakes and centipedes, the bright and glaring light, the sandhills, the strange plants and animals, the howling dingoes, the native inhabitants and the bushfires, all combined to make their first days and impressions very bewildering. Added to this was the lack of any shelter or comforts of any kind, so another very real problem would have been homesickness and doubts about

### PAGE 14
their decision to try their luck in a new land.

When Colonel Light finally chose the site for the city, further difficulties were encountered, as there was a journey of seven miles through bush country with no means of transport other than walking. A temporary camp was set up on the banks of the River Torrens, but the only building materials at first available were mud, grass, saplings and reeds and these were quite inadequate in the extreme heat or drenching rains.

Huts were quickly erected. Furniture consisted of a big box in the middle to serve as a table and cupboard. Beds were made of forked sticks driven into the floor, with more sticks laid in the forks to keep the bed off the ground. The bed also served as a seat on one side of the table. Packing cases, boxes and trunks were all brought into service and nothing was wasted. A tea kettle, bucket, camp oven, spoons, knives and forks, tin plates, pannicans, a frying pan and an axe were standard equipment. Any valuables, such as tobacco, were buried under the floor. There were many discomforts, but no snobbery!

Ship's biscuit and salt pork were the staple foods, supplemented by wallaby, black swan, wild duck, parrots and even baked snake. Bags of flour, salt and pepper, and very occasionally mutton, provided some variety. What foodstuffs were available were very expensive. Water was brought from the River Torrens in barrels rolled along the ground.

When Francis Telfer arrived in South Australia in 1838, Adelaide was a collection of shacks, tents, log huts, wurleys, packing case villas, and a few wooden huts. Wallabies hopped over the streets until they were shot for food. New arrivals went to Emigration Square, a government depot and primitive reception centre for labourers and their families whose passages had been paid for by the Land Fund. It was located in the West Parklands near the present site of the Adelaide High School. The only

shelter available was inadequate, and the settlers used umbrellas, sails, thatches of reeds, or anything at all to protect themselves from the weather. They lived in miserable shanties and cooking was done in the open air. The main water supply was the River Torrens.

The women and children were especially vulnerable to the harsh conditions in the new land. It is no wonder that Francis Telfer's wife, Jean, succumbed to illness and disease. That so many of our ancestors survived and prospered is testimony to their strength of character and powers of endurance.

A poem, written by James P. Young, an early settler, describes the scene in early South Australia. Parts of the poem are reproduced here.

From England, Ireland, Scotland, they emigrated here,

For each received a grant of land, still rations they were dear,

The Government stores were open, each settler there did go

Down to the iron shed for food, close where the Torrens flows.

The settlement was soon laid out, a survey of the plan,

And for those city acres, the men of wealth soon ran; Colonel Light laid out the streets just as you see them now,

From east to west, and north to south upon the open prow.

In Rundle Street, upon each side, a picture there was seen

Of iron and wooden fabrics, with tents that lay between;

A shanty and a grog shop and a store or two were there,

And a church of mud and straw was built, a place for common prayer.

Upon a quiet Sabbath day the settlers would repair Down to the early mansion for Pastor Stow was there,

And at the porch the deacon a hand-bell there would ring,

And settlers in those early days beneath the thatch would sing.

Sometime in Rundle Street that time it would be no surprise

A bullock-dray with driver stuck in mud up to his eyes;

Bill Hawkins he came into town, six bullocks in a team,

### PAGE 15
And stuck right opposite the Globe right down into the beam.

The only roads that did abound were of the rudest style,

And cut through tangled bush and scrub
outstretched for many a mile;

And sometimes through the Torrens and every other creek,

And settlers could not reach the town for many a day or week.

But still the early settlers began with heart and hand

To grub the bush that lay around, and cultivate the land;

And things went on so rapidly that day by day they spread

Till o'er the plain as fur's the eye the homesteads thick were spread.

From Adelaide to Port Adelaide the roads with teams did flow,

The only place to cross the flood was Hindmarsh down below;

It sometimes took a day or two to reach from place to place,

With scarce a breeching to their horse, and not a single trace.

Port Adelaide was nothing then but just a swampy bound,

With just a few inhabitants, their huts lay all around;

An iron shed lay near the stream just where the docks lie now,

With here and there a little tug that steamed out to and fro.

From Adelaide to North Adelaide a rough stile there was seen,

That settlers from the northern parts might cross the Torrens stream;

Just nearly where the bridge now stands the object of today,

But that time for to cross the banks were cut away.

North Adelaide was all but scrub upon its noble brow,

Upon Montefiore the gum trees with spacious branches flow;

On either side the Torrens stream, near where the lake now stands,

The tall reeds grew with tangled bush for miles on either hand.

But in the early fifties the native tribes would meet, Five hundred strong they mustered near the Botanic seat,

All painted, with their boomerangs and spears upon their side,

And held their great corroborees down by the Torrens side.

Soon as the sun set in the west their warlike dance began,

With native song and music, with their ding-dong as a drum;

But ere the night had passed away their yells were heard around

And ere the morning watch had come a few lay on the ground.

Their camping place was always known by fire ablaze at night

The settlers knew their whereabouts by watching for the light;

Their wurlies stood by every creek and sometimes o'er the plain,

And several of the settlers' sheep and cows by them were slain.

From place to place they used to beg, and pilfer what they could,

And sometimes to the settlers' wives their speech was rather rude;

And had to hunt them from the place, for sure they could not tell,

Soon as they knew no men were there, they round the hut would yell.

But as the population grew and prospered o'er the place,

The native tribes into the bush their steps they did retrace;

Till only just a few were seen around their wurlies spread,

Squatting beneath an old gum tree, its branches for a shade.

From east to west, from north to south, no roads could there be seen,

But where the teamster's bullock dray their tracks had often been;

But here and there across the bush the early settlers rode

Midst tangled bush and over stumps, a weather beaten road.

But soon like the fair settlement the sections were surveyed,

And roads to several districts around them then were made;

The Greenhill Road was then cut out, that lay towards the Tiers,

A beaten track without a stone it lay for many years.

A Council then was formed to keep the settlement in repair;

The streets were somewhat dangerous, a creek ran here and there;

A bullock-dray with wood sometimes went nearly out of sight

When coming from the southern hills into the town by night.

### PAGE 16
A settler filled a nasty trench that through the city ran,

That used to empty all its filth into the South Parkland,

And took them loads and loads of stuff where'er it could be found

Before that chasm was filled up just like the other ground.

The poem goes on to describe the difficulties encountered in the early days by the attacks from bushrangers, escaped convicts and other criminals. When Governor Hindmarsh left the colony in July, 1838, the outlook was gloomy and law and order was a problem.

The cost of town allotments had risen to £100 or more and “country” blocks of 80 acres in the neighbourhood of Adelaide had risen from 12 shillings to £12 per acre. The high prices precluded most settlers from purchasing their own land, and most settlers were forced to remain in Adelaide and live on their savings. These were soon exhausted and many had to sell what farming implements they owned merely to survive.

Governor Gawler arrived in October, 1838, and found South Australia on the point of collapse. There was no money left in the Treasury. Proceeds from land sales were sent to England to help bring out more settlers. There was little revenue forthcoming as the only items to be taxed in those days were wine, spirits, tobacco and publicans' licences.

Adelaide was a shanty town of over 5,000 inhabitants, with hundreds more settlers arriving. The surveys of lands were well behind schedule, so Governor Gawler persuaded Captain Charles Sturt to accept the position of Surveyor-General. Sturt surveyed the areas along the banks of the River Torrens, and some of this land was purchased by Francis Telfer at Chain of Ponds where he established his property "Macoota".

Using money which he did not have, Governor Gawler used the plentiful supply of immigrant labour to construct public buildings and build roads. By 1840, two years after Francis Telfer arrived in South Australia, cash and credit were

scarce, and the Colony's problems were compounded by crop failures in the eastern colonies which still provided the bulk of food for South Australia. The cost of living rose alarmingly, and the colony became bankrupt. The British Government had to step in and pay all the bills, and Governor Gawler was recalled.

In the meantime the colonists had to cope with the difficult and trying conditions. Heavy falls of rain resulted in ankle deep mud and it was almost impossible to walk from one side of the road to the other. One lady was constrained to break into verse —

Ah me! my dress! my pretty dress!

My dress I bought so cheap;

The mud has caused me sad distress,

I'm plunged in ankle deep.

Could nothing spare me such a shock —

On every gazer scandal —

To see a lady lift her frock

So far above her sandal.

The mud so deep in every part,

I know not what to do,

It grieves me to the very heart

To spoil my polished shoe.

My feet I vainly strive to free,

They sink, I can't prevent them,

They jerk my stocking from my knee,

Close at the door of Bentham.

A friend of mine set fast and stuck

Was forced to leave his slipper,

And ever vanquished in the muck,

Though helped across by Skipper.

Religious services commenced immediately on the arrival of the first settlers. The Rev. C.B. Howard arrived on the "Buffalo" and a site for a church was sought. The first services were held under a sail borrowed by Mr. Howard and dragged on a handcart through seven miles of scrub to Adelaide. Trinity Church was built on the corner of Morphett Street and North Terrace, but the depression of 1843 took its toll on the church finances and it was almost disposed of for a granary store. It was rescued through the generosity of the Colonial Treasurer and the zeal of the members.

### PAGE 17
The Rev. Ralph Drummond of the United Presbyterian Church arrived in 1839 with his wife and 8 children. (Two more were later born in South Australia.) He held services in a small chapel in Angas Street. He was a part time teacher, but he also looked after a huge parish, visiting his flock as far away as Finniss, Mount Barker and Strathalbyn, all his journeys being made on foot. In 1840 he laid the foundation of the first Presbyterian church in Gouger Street. Appeals for help were sent to Scotland from both Adelaide and Melbourne, and these were apparently considered at the same time by the Presbytery in Edinburgh, for it was gravely proposed to send out a minister who could officiate at one of the towns in the morning and the other in the evening on the same day!

There were serious divisions within the Presbyterian Church and the devout but obstinate Scots were not prepared to compromise.

The Rev. Robert Haining reached Adelaide towards the end of 1841. He was

born in Roxburghshire and belonged to the Established Church of Scotland. He had the use of Anglican, Congregational and Wesleyan churches before his own church was built in Grenfell Street.

Francis Telfer's marriage to Dinah Flavel took place at Trinity Church on 4th May, 1839. His later marriage to Margaret Wright was conducted by the Rev. Robert Haining on 27th March, 1845 at North Adelaide "according to the rites and ceremonies of the Church of Scotland after Banns".

In telling the story of the Telfer family, tribute is paid to their courage and fortitude in making the long and hazardous journey from the Borders of Scotland to the unknown prospects of a new life in a new land. They came with little but their courage, but each one prospered and contributed in many ways to the development of South Australia. They truly helped to make "S.A. Great".

### PAGE 18
## PART 1

The Stories of Francis Telfer and his children.

By Hope Simmons

(With help from many family members)

### PAGE 19
## THE SCOTTISH CONNECTION

To bring our family into context with Scottish history, we must go back to a little before Bonnie Prince Charlie lead the Scots into the Battle of Culloden Moor, against the English armies (The Sassenachs) in 1745.

Into the atmosphere of anger, hostility, fear and apprehension which prevailed prior to that battle, probably in one of the Southern Border areas of Scotland a "Bonnie wee laddie" was born. He was named James Telfer.

He grew up in the area and later married Margaret Armstrong. They settled at Sorbietrees (or Sorbytrees) about 2 miles from Newcastle in the county of Roxburghshire, Scotland and not far from the Scottish/English border which followed the line of the Cheviot Hills.

On 24th September, 1762, their son James, was baptised at Sorbietrees. Not quite 2 years later on 24th June, 1764 his sister Jean was baptised at the same place, only this time it was spelt Sorbytrees, in the Baptismal Register in the Session Book of Castleton.

There were 3 other Telfer children born in the following 7 years who we believe could belong to the same family. The only information we have is an inscription on a headstone in Castleton Cemetery which reads:-

"Erected in Memory of Francis Telfer, who died at Newcastle on 4th June, 1836, aged 68 years (b1768). Also Jenet Telfer, his sister, who died at Newcastle on 9th February 1842, aged 72 years (b1770). Also Adam Telfer, their

brother, who died at Newcastle on 6th February, 1846, aged 75 years" (b1771).

The names "Francis" and "Adam" appear fairly regularly in succeeding generations of our family, and their birth dates follow on fairly closely after those of James and Jean. Consequently we believe that they could be younger children of James and Margaret Telfer.

There is a missing link at this stage which we are convinced would link the above James Telfer, who was baptised at Sorbietrees on 9th September, 1762, to the following branches of our Family Tree, which we know to be correct.

One of the problems in tracing family members back into the 1700's is that records are scarce, but even if found they are not as accurate in dates or spelling as we would like, due to the poor literacy and phonetic spelling, etc. of the average working class person. Therefore, (our) James Telfer, who was born sometime in the early 1760's, grew up and married Elizabeth (Betty) Hutton (b 1774).

In due course they had 6 children while living at Greenholm, near Newcastle, and then apparently moved to Blackburnfoot, which lay at the northern end of Newcastle, where 2 more children were born. Old Parish Register, Castleton (Volume 784/1) lists their family as follows:-

1796 August 16

James to James Telfer and Betty Hutton at Greenholm.

1798 February 10

Isobel to James Telfer and Betty Hutton at Greenholm.

### PAGE 20
1799 October 18

Adam to James Telfer and Betty Hutton at Greenholm.

1801 July 17

Margaret to James Telfer and Betty Hutton at Greenholm.

1803 April 23

Robert to James Telfer and Betty Hutton at Greenholm.

1805 March 20

John to James Telfer and Betty Hutton at Greenholm.

These six baptisms were entered together.

The two other children born after the move were:-

1809 February 2

Francis to James Telfer and Betty Hutton at Blackburnfoot

1811 April 6

William to James Telfer and Betty Hutton at Blackburnfoot

From Miss Cathie Telfer, present resident of Newcastle (Scotland) we learnt that Blackburnfoot, situated at the northern end of Newcastle, does not exist today, but in the early 1800's it had its own weaving industry. This is of particular interest as James Telfer, father of the above family was a hand loom weaver.

Nearly everyone at that time had their own cottage and owned their own cow. During the day the cows were all turned out to graze on the hills surrounding the town. In the evenings they could be seen all coming together down the hills and then dividing as they reached the town and going down different streets to their own home yards.

We have no further information about four members of this family; namely Isobel, Adam, Margaret and William.

John we know died on 3rd March, 1839. His name appears on the headstone in Castleton Churchyard Cemetery, with the names of his parents. It reads:-

"In Memory of James Telfer who died at Newcastle on 19th December, 1845, aged 84 years. Also

Elizabeth Hutton his spouse, who died at Newcastleton on 14th April 1853 aged 79 years. Also John Telfer their son who died at Blackburnfoot on 3rd March, 1839, aged 33 years."

He presumably was not married.

This suggests that Blackburnfoot was still there in 1839, but was gone or at least the family had moved by 1845. Miss Cathie Telfer has said that Blackburnfoot was demolished when the Railway line was built there. As the Railway system was just beginning in England (London to Birmingham completed in September 1838) it probably arrived in Scotland in the early 1840's.

In 1841 there was a Census which covered the area "of so much of the Parish of Castleton as form that part of the village of Newcastle from the Town Head Toll to the centre of Douglas Square." Thanks to this census we know a little more about Robert, who was born in 1805 and later married Elizabeth Oliver. Under the Telfer name appears the entry:-

"Robert Telfer 36, and Betty 34 with children, Elizabeth 7, James 5, Jean 3, and John 2, also James aged 75, and Betty Hutton 65 of Newcastle- ton."

Blackburnfoot, the site of the cottage in which Francis Telfer was born. Taken in the early 1980's.

### PAGE 21
* Denotes family members who emigrated to Australia.

James, the oldest of the family (b. 1796), married Elizabeth Beattie and had a number of children. Later five of these children came to South Australia at different times. This family will be covered more fully later in the book.

The eighth member of the family, Francis was born on 2nd February, 1809. He later married Jean Murray and went to live at Langshawburn and worked as a shepherd, possibly for the Murray Family who had been land holders there for many years.

## THE MURRAY FAMILY

Jean's grandfather, Adam Murray lived and died at Castleton, Dumphriesshire, Scotland between the mid 1700's and early 1800's. He married Elizabeth Beatty (b. 1741) who was the daughter of the 1st Earl Beatty, Admiral of the British Fleet. She died in 1819 and is buried in Eskdalmuir Churchyard, near Langshawburn.

Their son John (b. 1779 d. 1849 at Langshawburn) married Esther Cowen.

### PAGE 22
William Murray, brother of Jean Telfer (nee Murray).

(b. 1781 d. 1856). They had twelve children, but the eldest, a boy died very young. Jean who was born on 14th June, 1810, was the oldest of the children to survive to adulthood. Of the other ten — two sisters and four brothers came to South Australia, leaving one brother and two sisters behind in Scotland. Adam (1812-1882) because he was the eldest son, was the brother who remained in Scotland and married Jackie Hope. (1815-1909).

Jean was the first of the family to come to South Australia. Her two brothers, Pultney Malcolm (1819-1879) and Alexander Borthwick (1816-1903) arrived in the Colony on 27th September, 1839, aboard the "SS Lady Lilliford". They bought 4000 acres of land in the Hundred of Parra Wirra. They were joined by their brother John and his wife Alison (nee Hope, sister of Jackie, Adam's wife) on July 1841, having travelled out on the "SS City of Adelaide" with William their brother.

William Murray was to later marry Jane Wright who was the younger sister of Francis Telfer's third wife Margaret.

Jane Murray, (nee Wright). William's wife.

He opened a Jam Factory at Burnside, was also involved in Forestry and in 1857 became the first person in S.A. to produce olive oil.

Pultney Malcolm's first marriage was to Mary Ann Scott in 1846 and produced four children. His later marriage to Miss Vigor produced six more children. He owned a sheep property at Truro called "Glen Turret".

Alexander Borthwick managed a farm at Kersbrook for his cousin, Sir James Malcolm for eighteen months. He then took up land at Mt Crawford, where, with his brother John, he established the famous Murray Merino flocks. Later he took up a cattle station at Warrina and then a large station called Bookpurnong on the River Murray.

In the early 1850's he sold his interest in the Mt Crawford business to John and settled at Tungkillo. He was chairman of Tungkillo District Council for many years before becoming a Member of the House of Assembly in 1862 and later elected to the Legislative Council where he served for nearly 20 years. He was twice married, first to Charlotte Scott,

### PAGE 23
(sister to Mary, wife of Pultney Malcolm) and then to Margaret Tinline and had at least two children per marriage. In later life he retired to Murray Park at Magill.

One of his sons, Sir George Murray was to become a highly respected barrister and the first Adelaide man to be made a Knight Commander. As a supporter of the Adelaide University as a council member and later as Vice Chancellor and

then Chancellor he donated thousands of pounds for various university projects. Other Offices that he held were those of Supreme Court Judge and Chief Justice of South Australia and Lieutenant-Governor.

Other members of the Murray family to come to South Australia were Mary, who married a Mr Armstrong and Agnes who married a Mr Potts.

### PAGE 24
## Francis Telfer

Francis Telfer and his wife Jean (nee Murray) as they began their married life in Langshawburn. Their first child was a son James, who was baptised at Eskdalemuir Church on 3rd March, 1832, by Dr. Browne.

Their two daughters, Esther born 27th May, 1834 and Elizabeth born 1836, do not appear to have been baptised as we can find no record of that happening. However on 23rd June, 1838 another son, John, was baptised at Eskdalemuir by Mr. Dobie.

Six weeks later, on 9th August, 1838, Francis made an application to migrate to South Australia, which at that time had only been settled as a British colony for about eighteen months. His application for passage reads:-

"Francis Telfer, 29, shepherd of Langshawburn, Dumphriesshire, wife Jean, 28 years and four

Eshdalemuir Chapel, where Francis' two little boys were baptised.

children, 2 males, aged 6 years and 5 months and 2 females aged 4 years and 2 years."

They were informed that their application had been accepted and they were due to sail on the "Prince George", from the port of London, on 4th September, 1838. That meant just 26 days to wind up their affairs, pack some things to take with them, dispose of the rest, and say farewell to family and friends, some of them quite a distance away, considering the type of transport available to them. What an awesome task for a mother with a young baby and three little children under 6 years of age!

However, it was accomplished, and they arrived in London. I wonder by what form of transport, and had they ever travelled that far before? Did they travel by horse drawn coach? Francis, Jean, three youngsters and a baby packed into a coach with the other passengers and all their worldly belongings packed on the roof. Alternatively maybe they travelled by sea around the coast to Port of London.

"They were allowed to take with them personal effects, clothing, bedding and some cooking utensils. Furniture, tools and other equipment were to be obtainable in the new colony.

They went aboard the "Prince George", a ship of four hundred and eighty two tons, commanded by Captain Young. The eight year old ship was one hundred and eighteen feet (36 metres) long by thirty feet (9 metres) wide, three masted and with two decks. Between decks they were

### PAGE 25
shown the small space each family was to occupy for the next three and a half months. Of privacy there was little and facilities were of a primitive nature.

The “Prince George” slipped her moorings on 12th September, 1838, and left the Downs, heading down the river into the relentless surge and fall of the ocean. The great adventure had begun.”

(From "Far Called" a history of the Flavel Family, by courtesy.)

With other families, the Telfers crowded into their allotted quarters, wondering with some apprehension what lay ahead of them, and thinking of the friends, and family they left behind in "Dear Auld Scotland."

As the journey proceeded they made many new friends. One family, who became life long friends were the Flavel family, comprising Joseph and Mary Flavel and seven young children and, registered separately as adults, Thomas 19 years, Dinah 17 years and William 16 years.

The journey, with its crowded conditions, the heat, the rise and fall and roll of the ship in the turbulent ocean waters, proved too much for little John Telfer, and he died before reaching his new homeland. Sadly they committed his soul to the Lord and his tiny body to the ocean depths and sailed on.

Christmas 1838 was celebrated on board as they sailed along the west coast of the new colony and into Saint Vincent's Gulf. The following day, 26th December 1838, they dropped anchor in Port Misery, as Port Adelaide was called in its early days.

The passengers spent that first night in the cargo shed, though it's doubtful if they were able to sleep. They were half eaten by mosquitoes and had nothing with which to quench their thirst in the December heat. I guess they quickly realized why it was called Port Misery.

Next morning most of the men set out on foot for the township of Adelaide,

which Colonel William Light had so laboriously, but meticulously laid out. We now acknowledge the wonderful foresight with which he planned our wide streets, spacious parklands and general layout of this city and are deeply grateful. But these were not the thoughts of those men on that hot summer's day, as they trudged the 7 miles (11.2 kilometres) to Adelaide. Among the walkers were Joseph Flavel and his son William.

Some years later William wrote an article on some of their early experiences, which was published in a small book entitled "Historical Records." He wrote:-

"I left my boots in the ship thinking I could walk better without them; but on coming over the sandhills, in the middle of the day, the heat of the sand running between my toes, I have not forgotten yet, in fact I could not stand still except in the shade of a bush or shrub, so had to run from one bush to another till I got clear of the sand."

We have no definite information on the movement of Francis and his family during the next few weeks, but it was quite common for migrants to spend a little time at the Emigration Centre in the West Parklands, where the Adelaide High School now stands. It was there that Francis suffered another bereavement. Just 26 days after arrival, his wife Jean died, on 21st January, 1839.

One's heart goes out to that mother who had not had a chance to get over the weakness of childbirth before she had to go through all the physical and mental strain of packing up and leaving and then the cramped conditions on the ship, the death of her baby and the arrival here in mid-summer weather, the like of which she had never before experienced, with no comforts or conveniences.

The records of Holy Trinity Church (Church of England), which was the only place for recording births, deaths and marriages at that time, shows:-

### PAGE 26
Jane Thelford of Emigration Dept. buried 22-1-39 Aged 28 years.

John Thelford of Emigration Dept. buried 22-1-39 Aged 7 months.

Buried in West Terrace Cemetery.

We believe that although baby John died enroute, his father took this opportunity to place his death on record. Also with reference to the spelling of their surname, in 1841 census the family's name was spelt Telford which probably accounts for the wrong spelling of the name, together with the mumblings of a grief stricken Scotsman.

It is known that the Flavels, along with many other new settlers, erected tents along the Reedbeds, an area along the Torrens River and as far inland as present day Hindmarsh.

Francis' situation was not enviable. A father of three children, of ages 6, 4 and 2, in a new and unfamiliar land, seeking to find a means to support himself and his family, while looking for land on which to erect a more permanent dwelling. How he managed we can only guess, but the support of his friends the Flavels must have been a factor. It is possible that Dinah, aged 17, played a part in caring for his children, and through this a bond developed.

Holy Trinity records the marriage:-

"Francis Telfer, widower of Mackgill, and late of the Parish of Castleton, Dumphriesshire, Scotland; and Dinah Flavel, spinster of Adelaide, and late of the Parish of Pychley, Northamptonshire, England, were married in this Church, by banns, this 4th day of November, 1839, by me...(namenot readable).

Signed Francis Telfer In the presence of

Dinah Flavel

Alex Murray

Elizabeth Farrow

X her mark.

The witnesses at the wedding were Alexander Borthwick Murray, brother to Francis former wife Jean, and Elizabeth Farrow who was the wife of William Farrow.

At the time of the marriage Francis was living at Mackgill (later spelt Magill) but soon after we believe they moved to Woodside and lived there for a while.

Towards the end of 1840 Dinah's father, Joseph Flavel began to show signs of illness and it soon became evident that he had contracted consumption. He died at the Medical Centre of the Emigration Centre on West Terrace on 18th December, 1840 and was buried in West Terrace Cemetery.

His wife Mary, was pregnant at the time of his death, and although the two older boys, William and Thomas were working, they were only earning youth's wages and the family found themselves in dire straits financially. Mary applied for, and received assistance from the Migration Authorities, who controlled a fund, set up in order to help new settlers such as these, who fell on hard times.

On 17th August, 1840, Dinah gave birth to a baby girl, who was named Jean (or Jane). At different times during her life, her name appears on official documents sometimes as Jean, and other times as Jane.

The census taken in S.A. in 1841 read as follows:-

Francis & Mrs Telford, James U/14 years, Esther U/7 years, Elizabeth U/7 years, Jane U/7 years.

On 24th September, 1842, Francis Telfer and Mary Flavel, between them, bought a large portion of Section 6146 in the Hundred of Para Wirra, which was part of a Special Survey of the sources, streams and tributaries of the River Torrens. The land was in the Chain of Ponds area which was called Maidstone and later changed to Kersbrook. A small part of the eighty acre section remained in the hands of Charles Campbell, Mary bought about 20 acres and Francis had the rest.

They each built homes on their portions of the land, and although there is no sign of Mary's house left, Francis' home is still being lived in, with a few alterations and additions that have been made over the years.

### PAGE 27
It appears that under these circumstances Francis was able to reciprocate for some of the kindness and caring concern shown to him and his family after the death of his wife Jean. Mary and family stayed there for about six years.

Early documents of various kinds show Francis as "Storekeeper", but he was obviously busily engaged in clearing land and planting crops as a copy of the 1843 Almanac, held in the S.A. Archives records that in that year he grew 3 acres (1.2 hectares) of wheat, half an acre of potatoes, and owned 1 pony, 2 cattle and 2 pigs. The following year (1844 Almanac) he grew 5 acres (2 hectares) of wheat, a quarter acre of maize, a quarter acre of potatoes and a half acre of garden.

The present owner of that piece of land says that when the land was first taken up it was so thickly covered with scrub, (wattle and banksia) that it was impossible to walk through. There is still a large piece of scrub on the hill at the far end of the section.

Settlers would not have had much time to keep checking at the Post Office to see if they had received any mail, and so the newspaper THE REGISTER, helped in a special section, showing unclaimed mail. On 10th January, 1844, 2 letters were being held for Francis Telfer, and they were still there on 20th and 24th January.

At about that time Francis had more serious things on his mind as Dinah became ill, and was found to have consumption. She died on 17th March, 1844, aged 23 years. On 22nd March she was buried in the West Terrace Cemetery and her home address was given as "Macoota" which was the name Francis had given his farm.

One wonders where she died. Did she die at home, or was she in the city under some medical care? Adelaide Cottage Hospital was established in 1840. If it was at home, it seems a long way (22 miles) to transport the coffin in horse drawn vehicles all the way to West Terrace. Cemeteries were being established around the country, wherever people lived and died.

Once again Francis was left with a family of 4 motherless children aged 12, 10, 8 and 4 years. Probably once again he had to turn to Mary Flavel to help care for the children. How fortunate that she lived nearby!

In common with many of the early settlers Francis was a fervent Christian, and it appears that with his family he regularly made the long trip from Kersbrook to Adelaide (22 miles — 35.4 km) to attend worship on the Sabbath. At first he probably attended services of the United Presbyterian Church which were held in the S.A. School Society Rooms on North Terrace and were conducted by the Rev. Ralph Drummond.

In July of 1839 "a highly respectable meeting of gentlemen interested in the establishment of the Presbyterian Church of Scotland in Adelaide took place in the S.A. School Society Rooms, when it was resolved to appeal to the liberality of the Colonists, and a committee was approved to collect subscriptions". By September of that year over 200 pounds ($400) had been collected.

Architect and builder, Samuel Mills, designed the structure to be built on land donated by William Malcolm in Gouger Street. On Saint Andrews day, 30th November, 1840, the Foundation Stone of the first Presbyterian Church in S.A., was laid by Governor Gawler, and its minister was Rev. Ralph Drummond.

The Church was opened on 27th February, 1842. Its full name at that time was Gouger Street United Secession Church, although it was sometimes simply called "Scotch Kirk". After 1846 it was called Gouger Street United Presbyterian Church.

The building was not completed for a number of years as the Scots who built it were determined not to go into debt. The only known photograph of it was taken by Mr Duryea from the top of the new Town Hall in King William Street in 1865 as part of a panoramic view of the whole

### PAGE 28
The first Presbyterian Church in S.A. (Picture from Duryea's Panorama of Adelaide taken from the newly completed Town Hall Tower 1865, and used with permission of Mortlock Library, and Torrens Valley Historical Journal, from which this photo was copied.)

of the City as seen from that vantage point.

A minute of the meeting of the congregation of this church on 1st December, 1845, reads:-

"The congregation met according to previous instructions, and after a sermon by the Moderator elected Alexander Hay and Francis Telfer to the office of Ruling Elder, who having expressed their willingness to accept and after examination by the Session and being found duly qualified, their edict was appointed to be served on Sabbath next, and then induction to take place on Friday 19th December. Concluded." A later minute:-

"Adelaide 19th December, 1845. The Congregation met after the sermon, the Moderator having stated the previous proceedings to the case, and put the question of the formula to Alexander Hay and Francis Telfer and let them speak by prayer to the Office of Ruling Elder and after exhortation to them and to the congregation, concluded."

## James Telfer

At the time of Dinah's death, Francis' eldest child and only (living) son, James, was just 12 years old. Years later, William Flavel who was only a year younger than Dinah, wrote (in "Historical Records"):-

"Sometime about 1844 my brother and I, with a neighbour, F.T. (who we believe to be his brother-in-law Francis Telfer) started sheep farming on our own at the Sixth Creek, between Mt. Lofty and the Montacute mines with about 1500 mixed sheep of all ages. F.T. sent his son, a boy of about 13 yrs and used to shepherding.

We had plenty of water and with Kangaroo grass 2 or 3 feet (up to 1 metre) high in the gullies, we had too much feed, but a bushfire came during the summer and burned it nearly all away ...

After the fire we had a little rain, but only a little green grass, after which the sheep ran greedily and scattered all over the place ... The wild dogs also were very troublesome, and sheep left out at night were generally found dead next day, or so badly bitten they died.

It took two shepherds in that steep country to keep the sheep together.

During summer feed got very scarce and consequently the stock got very poor and weak, but when the wet season fairly started it seemed as if it was not going to stop raining any more, and creeks were all flooded.

One afternoon the boy Jim brought home part of his flock and said he had lost the others, and so we had to mix the two lots together again while my brother and Jim went in different directions to look for the missing ones. My brother soon returned with the missing sheep and afterwards Jim's dog, but alone and without the boy.

We yarded the sheep and made a big fire and kept it burning all night

### PAGE 29
and in the morning one of us walked about twelve miles to inform his father, who mustered all the men in the neighbourhood, searching for him for several days and then got the police from Adelaide, but Jim was never found.

As the creeks were flooded and it was raining most of the time the search parties were out, the general impression was that the boy had got washed down the creek, and carried by the flood into the river, and when the water subsided his remains would be found either in the bed of the river or in the Reedbeds, but no remains were found ...

Nothing more was heard of the boy until, I think, about the year 1877, when I saw in a paragraph in one of Adelaide's newspapers which stated that the recent heavy floods had removed some large logs and other accumulation that had been lodged many years ago against a tree in the river near Adelaide, and amongst other things was the skeleton of a human being. The police took charge of the bones, and a doctor stated they were the remains of a boy about thirteen years of age and had probably been at that place more than twenty years.

A later paragraph stated the remains had not been claimed."

Vague stories have been passed down through the family that he "disappeared in the bush" that he "was killed by aborigines" and it had been suggested he had "step-mother problems and ran away to sea". At that stage he was between step-mothers, as Dinah had died 3 months earlier and Francis had not yet remarried.

The story as told by William Flavel seems to be the answer, but one wonders whether Francis ever saw, or was ever told about the paragraph in the paper telling of the finding of the bones.

We have searched police files of that time, but there is no mention of the incident.

A paragraph appeared in the S.A. REGISTER on 10th July, 1844 (Page 3. Col.A) as follows:-

"BOY MISSING — On Tuesday last, during the heavy rain then falling, a youth named Telfer, thirteen years old, left his fathers sheep station on the River Torrens, near Chain of Ponds, to recover some stray sheep, but up to Saturday last had not been heard of. The surrounding country has been searched, but no trace of the wanderer has been found: and from the late inclemency of the weather, there is too much reason to believe that he has perished."

Tragedy once again struck Francis, with the sad loss of a fourth member of his family in six years, his two wives and his two sons, for whom his dreams of establishing a solid foundation on which they could build their futures in this land of promise, had been shattered.

Still, he carried on, ran his store, farmed his land, and attended the "Scotch Kirk" on the Sabbath.

Somewhere Francis met Margaret Wright, who came to Australia with her parents, George and Margaret Dougal Wright, on the ship ASIA, in 1839. George Wright was described on the Shipping List as a "Gentleman", from Stirling, Scotland. He and his wife were accompanied to Australia by their son, James, and four of their five daughters — Catherine (Robertson), Margaret (Telfer), Elizabeth Ann (Rogers) and Jane (Murray). Their other daughter, Mary (Drysdale) remained in Scotland.

Family legend has it that Margaret was "born in Stirling Castle" — main bedchamber, guest room, or servants' quarters? You decide!

The last King to live in Stirling Castle was James VI (1567-1625). England and Scotland were united in 1707, after which Stirling was one of four Scottish castles to

### PAGE 30
be kept in good repair and garrisoned. The garrison from Stirling helped the English to defeat the Scottish Highlanders at Culloden Moor in 1745.

The castle is built high on a cliff, and buildings to house the garrisons of soldiers and other employees were built on the hillsides. We have not been able to find out just how George Wright was connected with the activities of Stirling Castle, but we do know that he was a gentleman of some means as he paid the fares of his family and also his sister-in-law, Miss Catherine Dougal, who came to Australia with them.

Margaret's application for passage reads, "Wright, Margaret, Domestic servant, Address Stirling, Age 29".

The Wright family took up land on Marion Road, Black Forest, and named their property "Greenfoot". The property was situated in the area now bounded by Marion Road, Thomas Street, Towers Terrace and Wood Street, South Plympton.

By 1840 "Greenfoot" was developed to the extent of having a well 27 feet deep, with 3 feet of water; seven acres were enclosed with a temporary fence; and five acres of wheat, sown in March, were described as a very good crop with a little smut. The barley sown in April was a very good crop, but the oats, also sown in April, failed. There was a dwelling house and stockyards. All this was accomplished in the first year following their arrival. By 1841, George Wright also had 33 cattle.

Francis and Margaret were married on Thursday 27th March, 1845, at (exact place unknown) "North Adelaide, according to the rites and ceremonies of the Church of Scotland, after banns".

The officiating minister was Rev. Robert Haining, and witnesses were Will Bartley and William Murray (brother of Francis' first wife Jean). Eleven months later William Murray was to marry Margaret's youngest sister Jane who lived at Mackgill.

After their marriage, Francis was made a Ruling Elder of the Gouger Street

Church, on 1st December, 1845. The next major event in their lives was the birth of their first child. On 24th May, 1847, John (my grandfather) was born, and so at last Francis once again had a son to carry on the family name.

In 1849 they had a daughter, and called her Margaret Dougal, after her maternal grandmother. The following year on 22nd November, 1850, another son was born, and he was called George Wright, after his mother's father.

At the time of John's birth, Esther was 11 years old, Elizabeth 9 years and Jean 5 years.

## THE CHURCH

In March 1854 Mr. William Vercoe, the local school teacher during the day, and pastor on Sundays, began holding services of the Church of Christ in the home of Francis and Margaret at Kersbrook. He later transferred them to his own home. He was a bachelor, so maybe there were not the same distractions as there were in a house with small children.

In 1855 the services were moved again to the school house which was next door to the Wesleyan Chapel (which is now the Kersbrook Uniting Church). The children of the Church of Christ attended the Wesleyan Sunday School until 1862 when the Church of Christ opened their own Sabbath School.

This information has been gleaned from an article written by the Rev. J. Paynter and published in the "Salem Messenger", the official organ of the Gumeracha Baptist Church in September 1892. It was later reprinted in the Torrens Valley Historical Journal in January 1980 from which it was obtained, and used with their permission.

Reminiscing about those years the writer recalls that a highlight each year was the June Meeting, the Anniversary of the establishment of the Church of Christ congregation and makes mention of a number of former worthies, and "some who remain to this day" include the

### PAGE 31
pastor and Mr Francis Telfer. There is no mention of any female "worthies". He recalls that "they were eminently spiritual gatherings of men in whom the spiritual element dominated over the critical and intellectual".

Mr. William Vercoe became a victim of consumption, and his place as a pastor was taken by Mr. William Kither, who was a man of super Calvinistic views and a considerable contrast to their former loved pastor, who was considered as a man of fine Christian spirit and excellent ability as a preacher.

In 1862 Mr Thomas T Vercoe took over as pastor and the following year they built their own Church of Christ Chapel and the two leading members of the congregation were Mr John McMillan and Mr Francis Telfer. "They were the pastor's right hand" — helping in the conduct of services and each in turn was Superintendent of the Sunday School.

On 15th February, 1862, a land title deed dealing with the transfer of land from Mr Thomas Scott to twelve men who were the Trustees of that church, included the names of Francis Telfer and also John Charles Dillon (who married Francis' niece, Elizabeth). Both are listed as farmers of Chain of Ponds (the Kersbrook area). Also mentioned as trustees were John McMillan, storekeeper and Thomas Vercoe, school teacher. A

foot note to the deed says, "Upon trust to permit to be erected on said land a Chapel, with or without convenient schoolrooms and other outbuildings, for the public worship of Almighty God. Declaration that the doctrines to be taught and the observance of which is required by Holy Scripture." It was bought for 80 pounds.

Whilst we have been tracing the history of the Church, we have overlooked a few important events in the family.

MARY FLAVEL lived at Macoota, Section 6146 until 1848, with the three younger members of her family, Elizabeth Bayes, Eliza, and Amos William Cordley. Her son William, divided his time between farming that piece of land (6146), and sharing with his younger brother John in the sheep farm near the Mount Lofty, Montecute area mentioned earlier. The eldest son, Thomas, went farming at Hope Valley, which is a little nearer to Adelaide than the Kersbrook area.

On 19th June, 1848, Mary married Joseph Farrow, a widower, who owned land also at Hope Valley and she spent the rest of her life there.

Joseph apparently settled at Kersbrook for some time and later moved to Hope Valley.

## Elizabeth Telfer

Elizabeth, daughter of Francis and Jean Murray was now 19 years old. On 28th March, 1856, she married Charles Farrow, in her home. Charles was the only son of Joseph Farrow who, a few years earlier, had married Mary Flavel.

## Nephews and Nieces

In 1853 two nephews and a niece of Francis came to South Australia. They were the children of his oldest brother James, and were James, aged 24, Elizabeth, aged 21, and Robert, aged 17 years.

James and Robert went to work for Mr. A.B. Murray at Tungkillo. All we know about Elizabeth's movements is that about 3 years later she was married in

Church of Christ Chapel, Kersbrook, built 1863 and still in use.

### PAGE 32
Francis' home at Macoota to John Charles Dillon (who, in 1863 was one of the Trustees of the Church of Christ).

## Jean (Jane) Telfer

It was almost three years before the next family wedding — that of Jean, daughter of Francis and Dinah. Bereft of her mother at just over 3 years of age, she was possibly cared for by her grandmother, Mary Flavel, until she gained a stepmother when her father Francis married again about 12 months later.

On 12th September, 1859, aged 19, Jean married Richard Paynter a 21 year old labourer of Chain of Ponds.

## Esther Telfer

About five years later the oldest member of the family, Esther Telfer, daughter of Jean Murray and Francis, was married to John Smith on 16th November, 1864. She was not married at Kersbrook, but in the Wesleyan Parsonage at Kapunda, about 50 miles away. The Rev. William Munsby Bennett officiated.

## Margaret Telfer

Margaret married Adam Telfer, her cousin on 25th March, 1868, in her father's home at Macoota.

## John Telfer

Margaret's older brother John, was 21 years old when Margaret was married. He was helping his father in growing vegetables on a large scale at "Macoota", and later he began driving a green grocery van to Yorke Peninsula. We know that he went as far as Kadina.

## George Wright Telfer

The youngest member of the Telfer family, George, did not marry for another 10 years. At the age of 33 he decided to marry Ruth Penna, the 21 year old "spinster" daughter of William Penna of Kersbrook. The wedding took place in her parents' home on 17th September, 1884, solemnized by Rev. John Hall ...? Presbyterian Minister.

## MACOOTA

Perhaps now we should say something about the house in which the family lived and where some were married.

The house which Francis built at "Macoota" is still standing and still lived in. It is owned by Mr. B. Pitman and family. When enquiring by phone about the house and property, Mrs Pitman offered to show us the house if we were interested.

The invitation was accepted (of course) and a week or so later Mrs Pitman gave us a guided tour of the house, telling us what she knew of the state and history of the house and land.

Francis Telfer's home, "Macoota", at Kersbrook, built 1840's and still lived in. Verandah added since.

Floor plan of the home at "Macoota".

### PAGE 33
The land itself, (section 6146) was originally covered with thick scrub, (mostly wattle) which was too thick to walk through in most places. Most of it has now been cleared, except for a patch growing on the hill behind the house, possibly near where Mary Flavel had her house.

The house is situated about 400 yards back from the main road, and has an access road now lined with pine trees. The house is built of stone from the surrounding area, with walls about 15 inches thick. It was originally roofed with shingles and they are still there under the galvanized iron, which has been added since.

A passage about 4 feet wide runs down the centre of the house from the front door, but the back (west end) is walled off to make a pantry, with the door opening onto the verandah at the back of the house. There are three rooms opening off the passage on the south side, now used as bedrooms, and two larger rooms on the north side forming a kitchen/dining room and a lounge — or more likely a "drawing room" in those days. Both these rooms had large fire places.

Mrs Pitman said that the thick stone walls made the house extremely cold in winter (hence the fire places), and even in summer a cardigan was required if sitting for any length of time.

The ceilings were made of plaster over wooden slats, but all the rooms now have new ceilings, except the passage, which has been papered over.

There is now a verandah surrounding the house which has been added since Francis' day and a kitchen has also been built just off the verandah, opposite the dining room door.

The walls near the floor are fretting in places due to salt damp, but otherwise the building is as solid as ever.

About 50 yards from the back door was a small well about 3 feet in diameter and more or less round with a few rocks poking out in places. It probably supplied water for household use and animals, and maybe even the market garden.

We don't know if they had a windmill, but almost certainly the water would have been drawn on a windlass with a handle to wind up one bucketful of water on a wire rope and at the same time lowering another bucket for its load. Of course they may have just used a bucket on a rope. The well was not timbered to prevent the sides from collapsing and consequently when we were there it was in the process of being filled in.

It was a great thrill to explore the house and surroundings where my great-grandparents lived, worked and reared their family. Where my grandfather was born and grew up, where family weddings took place, and where my great-grandmother Margaret spent the last weeks of misery and discomfort before she died.

We are very grateful to Mrs Pitman for allowing us to examine her home and for the information she was able to give us.

Apart from the family weddings we have very little information about the family from 1845 until 1885 when an entry appears in "The Minutes of

Francis and Margaret Telfer.

### PAGE 34
proceedings of the Church of Jesus Christ meeting in Salem Chapel, Gumeracha from 14th May, 1885 to 7th July, 1904", Salem being the Baptist Church at Gumeracha. On 25th November, 1885 the pastor of Salem Church, reported to his congregation that Mr and Mrs Frank Telfer, of Kersbrook had applied for membership of the Church, and suggested that Mr and Mrs Cornish visit them. Following a favourable report from the Cornishes, they (the Telfers), along with 6 other people, were received into Church Fellowship on Sunday 30th November, 1885.

The Members Roll later showed:-

No. 57 Telfer, Frank, admitted 29th November, 1885

58 Telfer, Mrs, admitted 29th November, 1885

65 Telfer, George W, admitted 13th December, 1885

66 Telfer, Mrs, admitted 13th December, 1885

It is interesting to note that having spent so many years as active workers in the Church of Christ at Kersbrook, they decided, at an advanced age, to drive a horse drawn vehicle about 4 or 5 miles to Gumeracha, come sunshine or storm; but who knows, strong willed personalities do sometimes clash.

At this point an interesting entry caught my eye in the minute book.

"May 14th 1885, Mr. B. Francis and wife are to meet visitors appointed, in the present of the Church, and express sorrow at having gone astray, ignorantly and having been the means of causing grief to the Church. They deny other charges, apart from having attended the Oakbank races. The Church is not satisfied, as our brother does not show in any way a humbled Christian spirit, but for the good of all concerned, they will let the matter rest."

In March 1892 one of the Secretaries appointed by the Salem Baptist Church was George Telfer.

A further extract from the minute book reads:-

"On 1st June 1892 a number of members visited Mr. and Mrs. Telfer, Senior, at Kersbrook, they being too feeble to attend Salem, and held a Communion Service. After an address on How they desire a better country, That is an Heavenly, two of the brethren offered prayer for our old friends, that while health and flesh fail, God might be their portion for ever. Then the memorials of the broken body and shed blood were distributed. Then the service ended by singing an old fashioned hymn of praise.
