Mr Telfer very feelingly thanked the friends and said it had been a time of refreshing from the Lord and in their ministering to the aged and infirm, the Saviour's words may be heard 'In as much as you did it to one of the least of these, you did it unto me'."

On 15th September, 1892, a memo was placed in the book to the effect that "Our aged sister, Mrs Telfer, passed away on September 13th at her home at Kersbrook". Her death certificate records the cause of death as "Exhaustion following fracture of thigh and bed sores". Imagine her suffering lying there without the relief of modern drugs and conveniences, until she was relieved by death. Mrs Ruth Vercoe was the person who reported her death to the Registrar, who happened to be her husband Thomas a former pastor of the Church of Christ.

Margaret was buried in Kersbrook Cemetery, and according to the death certificate was aged 87 years. Her age varies on different documents spanning her life time. She applied for passage in 1839 at age 29, married in 1845 at age 38, and died in 1892 aged 87 years.

Francis lived with his son George and daughter-in-law Ruth, after Margaret's death, first at Kersbrook and later at Willaston, near Gawler, for about three years.

### PAGE 35
A letter written by Francis to his granddaughter, Jane Murray Farrow, about eleven months before Margaret's death.

Dear Wife and my wife though frail are
still able to move about as usual. We
have been together since the 27 March 1945
and a good few Birth Days have passed
over us if she'll tell you when my wishes
first Birth day was. It was on the
19 December 1804. And once on the
4th of 26th 1809 do you see we are old.
I left Taughnash in Scotland 20, August 1839
came to Adelaide 21 December the same year
in place of writing numerous shall send in this
amall. I would let without him, wishes for you? yours
Francis M. Harper
21 October 1891

### PAGE 36
Early in August 1895 he went to visit his daughter Elizabeth Farrow, at Finnis Point. During his 4 week stay he became ill, and died there on 2nd September. He was buried in Tarlee Cemetery, section 2, grave 5, on Wednesday, 4th September, 1895, aged 86 years.

An Obituary printed in a newspaper reads:-

## THE LATE MR. TELFER, SENIOR

There passed away at his daughter's residence, Tarlee, on Monday, 2nd September, 1895, one of the fast thinning band of those who arrived in the Colony in the Thirties, in the person of Mr. Telfer, senior, late of Kersbrook.

The late gentleman, who was born at Blackburnfoot near Newcastle, Roxburgshire, Scotland, eighty-six years ago, arrived in South Australia in the "Prince George" in 1838.

After living at Magill and Woodside for a short period, he settled at Kersbrook and carried on farming there for many years. His wife died about 3 years ago and latterly he resided with his son, Mr. G.W. Telfer, at Kersbrook and Willaston.

About four weeks ago he paid a visit to his daughter (Mrs Farrow) at Tarlee and while there was taken ill.

Around the grave on Wednesday, there stood three who accompanied him in the "Prince George", which brought him to South Australia, vis, his daughter (Mrs Farrow), Mr. Thomas Flavel, of Finnis Point and Mr. W. Flavel, J.P. of Kapunda. Two sons, Mr. G.W. Telfer of Willaston and Mr. John Telfer of Hawker, and three daughters, survive.

The daughters mentioned in the above were Esther, Elizabeth and Margaret.

On the certificate, Francis' profession is shown as "Gentleman" of Willaston, and the cause of death as "Senile Decay — Necrosis". "Eva Farrow — granddaughter of Finnis Point", and daughter of Elizabeth was the informant.

So ended the life of one of South Australia's early pioneers. A man dogged by disaster, sorrow and hardship, who was able to rise above them and in the strength and support of the Lord, his God, carry on to establish strong roots in this country, from which the sturdy branches of his family have spread and flourished in every state of Australia and beyond.

### PAGE 37
## ESTHER JANE TELFER

Eldest daughter of Jean (nee Murray) and Francis Telfer

## ESTHER JANE TELFER (1834-1909) m. JOHN SMITH (1827-1895)

Esther was born on the Isle of Skye, Scotland on 21st May, 1834, and came to South Australia with her parents, on the "Prince George" at the age of four.

Her mother died less than four weeks later, and she had two step-mothers within the next six years.

Nothing more is known of her movements until 16th November, 1863, when she married John Smith in the Wesleyan Parsonage, at Kapunda. Charles and Elizabeth Farrow (her sister), were witnesses at the wedding and Esther's address was given as "Finnis Point". As this was where the Farrows lived, it is possible that Esther was living with them and helping Elizabeth take care of the four children she had at that time, or she may have been working elsewhere in the district.

John Smith was born in Copenhagen, Denmark in 1830. His father John was a Scottish Sea Captain and his mother Danish. She and her son lived on in Denmark for some years and they then moved to Scotland to live before they eventually came out to S.A. and took up farming at the Barossa. He was 34 at the time of their marriage and Esther was 30 years old.

The Smith family lived in South Australia for some years. We don't know where or when child, Jane Sophie was born. Their second child was born at Burra, and named Margaret Dougal after

Esther Jane Smith (nee Telfer).

Esther's second step-mother, Margaret (nee Wright) whose mother's maiden name was Dougal. The third child, Esther Telfer Smith was born at Tungkillo in 1870. The first son, born in 1871, at Tungkillo, was John James, and followed by Francis Telfer Smith in 1874, at Mount Gambier.

### PAGE 38
They called their new home Ebenezer.

Esther was pregnant with the sixth child when the family set out to move to Victoria. By the time they reached Millicent in the South East, Esther was finding the travelling irksome. Remember they didn't even have a T Model Ford to travel in! Horse drawn vehicles with their steel rimmed wheels and primitive springs, and travelling on dirt roads were not that comfortable for long distance trips, even for one who was not pregnant. Esther therefore stayed in Millicent until after the birth of Elizabeth Anne, in 1878. It is thought that John and possibly some of the children travelled on to Natimuk, Victoria, where they were to make their new home, and later returned to collect Esther and the baby.

Their new home was called Ebenezer, and the house was a pug and thatch construction, situated at Sherwood Dam near Mount Arapiles. The children attended the Arapiles School.

Towards the end of the 1880's the two boys, John and Francis rode their

pushbikes into the "outback", to Nyngan (about 600 miles) to look at land which was coming on the market. They came back with such glowing reports, that late in 1889 the family sold their farm at Natimuk to their good friends and neighbours, the Lange family, and packed up and set out on another long trek to Nyngan.

Travelling in a wagon, drawn by four draught horses, they must have taken many weeks to complete the journey. Their main diet would have been damper, cooked in a camp oven, and corned meat, and possibly a few rabbits and birds caught along the way. Hung on the side of the wagon, down in the shade, in order to keep them cool, would have been the water bags.

Believe it or not — people drank water in those days, and much appreciated the "nice cool drink" from a water bag — it wasn't exactly iced water though.

On arriving at Nyngan, John purchased a block of land on the

### PAGE 39
Gunningbah Creek, north of Nyngan. Here John ran sheep until his death on 5th May, 1895, aged 68 years. He was buried in the Church of England section of the Nyngan Cemetery.

About the same time as John took up his farm at Gunningbah Creek, his oldest son, Jack, bought land at Duck Creek, which he named "Brooklands" where he established a Merino sheep stud.

In March 1898 Frank, the youngest son, drew a block of land in the Mullengudgery district (27 miles out of Nyngan), which he named Eudora and set up the Eudora Merino Stud, now known to sheep breeders right across Australia.

Meanwhile, after John's death Esther moved into Nyngan township and lived the rest of her life in a brick home in Wambiana Street until she died in March 1909, aged 75 years. She was buried with her husband in Nyngan Cemetery.

Esther was a tiny person — just 5 feet tall, a devoted mother and grandmother, a loyal supporter of her Church. Her home still stands in good condition in Wambiana Street Nyngan (1984).

John and Esther's children were :- Jane Sophie, Margaret Dougal, Esther Telfer, John James, Francis Telfer and Elizabeth Anne.

## JANE SOPHIE SMITH

first child of John and Esther Smith

Born in South Australia, she married Benjamin Harper and they lived at Miram, Victoria. In April 1913, at the age of 47 years, Jane died, leaving a husband and six children.

The children were Benjamin Ellis (Ellis), Francis Joseph (Frank), Esther Jane (Essie), Amy Victoria, Louise May (May) and Viola Pearl.

The following was contributed by Essie Ray (eldest daughter of Jane Sophie Harper).

We used to walk two and a half miles to school, but I always milked a cow before I left. We lived near a big swamp and in the wet weather had to walk along the railway line, and for fun we used to bathe in the swamp.

One morning going to school we had a ride in a bullock wagon, which was taking a load of wheat into the railway station.

My mother made all our bread in a wood stove and also made all our soap.

When I was about 11 years old we left Miram and went to live at Mullen-

Jane Sophie Harper (nee Smith).

### PAGE 40
gudgery, New South Wales, where Dad bought a sheep farm. We were only there about 3 years when my mother died of pneumonia. My brother had to ride a bike about 27 miles into Nyngan to get the doctor, as there were no telephones then.

After mother died, and I was 14, I had to keep house for the six of us children. We lived in a big house with only board floors and I used to scrub the whole house each week, on hands and knees, to keep the floors nice and white. I also made butter and did all the cooking, etc.

We moved to Orange, N.S.W. so the three younger girls could go to school. Dad got a housekeeper, so I left home and got a job in a bread shop.

I then married a nice chap Alex Stevenson and had two children.

Alex died in his thirtieth year, and I reared the children and did dressmaking to pay for this house, which I have owned ever since. We only had kerosene lamps and candles then.

Years later I married Robert Ray, a widower with two grown up daughters.

We had an orchard four miles out of Orange, and I learned to drive a truck and used to take fruit in to the railway.

Bob was a lot older than me and he died 26 years ago. I then came back into my own home where I still live, and I am very happy here.

I had my 90th birthday on 4th January, 1989. My daughter, Mavis, died of cancer 8 years ago, and I have one son, Douglas, who lives with wife Joyce, at Trundle, New South Wales, and is a sheep farmer. They have two sons, Ian in Dubbo, and David in Sydney.

I am still able to get about, but I sold my car when I was ill a couple of years ago, so have to depend on a bus which comes very close to me — I also have a good friend with a car, who takes me out playing cards. I also go to Presbyterian Church and Guild.

I worked for Orange Show for many years, so they have now given me a life membership badge.

## MARGARET DOUGAL SMITH

second child of John and Esther Smith

Born at Burra, South Australia she married Thomas Parkins. They had 7 children — Esther, Irene, Miriam Maida, Dougal, Nell, Ruby and Daisy.

The Parkins family lived at "Melrose" on the Crooked Creek, Warren. In 1917, Tom Parkins was killed in an accident with a sulky. This left Margaret to keep the farm running, and ensured she was a very busy person. She spun her own wool and knitted it into socks for the family and her brothers Frank and John. She also did beautiful crochet and needlework.

Miriam Maida Parkins, at about the age of 25, married Oswald Retallick Heckendorf, of the same age.

The following has been contributed by Clarice Botrill (daughter of Miriam and Oswald).

In 1923 — my parents were married and lived on a grazing property in the Nyngan district, (which today is run by my brother Tom and family) and where we all grew up.

Two of their wedding presents were a calf called Polly, and a dog named Coon, which were to become part of their lives and later ours.

Dad's property was bought from his father, who owned several adjoining in the district, which his brothers owned and on which they lived. They never had much money, but we all survived and were fed somehow, life was a constant

### PAGE 41
Old schoolhouse at "Galore".

struggle. In 1925 and 1929 there were floods, and mum was driven to Mullengudgery (a railway siding) in a horse and sulkey to go to Dubbo and await my birth. Their first child died in 1929 as a result of Scarlet Fever.

In 1936 and onward we had "drought years", and Dad left Mum and our Governess and ourselves to run the property, while he went out tank cleaning to earn enough money to keep our property.

We were all educated by Correspondence School with the help of a Governess, and "attended school" in a small tin shed.

As we became older, Dad made a tennis court and cricket oval, and all our friends and neighbours would come over for a game of tennis or cricket, then we'd have gatherings of friends, for tea, etc. — music around the piano, or sit around an open fire and cook toast for supper and "yarn" our time away. They were the "good old days".

Then tragedy struck when Dad became ill and passed away in 1947. Mum lived on in the old homestead for many years, until my brother married and she went to live in Nyngan, where she was a much loved "Aunty Mirrie" to everybody, as well as all her nieces and nephews; and a devoted member of the Methodist Church. During these years we all more or less left home, married and started our

Margaret Dougal Smith.

Four generations. Miriam, Clarice, and seated, Margaret Parkins holding Lesley Maida Bottrill and Kathleen Bottrill.

### PAGE 42
own families. Mum passed away in 1972 and both my parents are buried in Nyngan Cemetery.

Mum's sister, Daisy, (married Don Neill) was a wonderful friend to me when I was growing up, and I loved her dearly. All Mum's family are now deceased.

The following was contributed by Jenny Teasdale, daughter of Dougal and Ivy Parkins, and grand-daughter of Margaret Dougal Parkins (nee Smith).

## WALNUTS IN THE CREVICES

Just when would we set out for Mrs Allison's packing afternoon? At four and a half waiting is interminable — staying put, keeping clean, but for how much longer?

The fuel stove was stoked, a pot of vegetable and barley soup simmered slowly at the very edge of its blackened top, and a nutmeg-speckled junket stood setting in a glass dish on the window ledge.

Mother hastily dusted her smooth, lovely face with the powder puff from her cut glass bowl. I silently wondered if she knew how often I crept into her bedroom to do the same. Quickly she straightened the seams of her stockings and put on her russet brown coat and matching felt hat. At the door she tugged on her black galoshes to protect her Sunday shoes. My shoes were in her basket alongside the pale yellow afternoon tea sponge filled with home made blackberry jam and cream from Daisy. In the string bag I carried a plum pudding in its calico cloth. Little did I realize how many months mother had had to skimp and save with ration tickets to make this fruity pudding. As well, I carried two pairs of hand-knitted socks, a balaclava and a tin of biscuits.

At last we set out to walk the mile or so to Mrs Allison's. Even wearing Cousin Annette's hand-me-downs and my sister's last year's rubber boots didn't dampen my spirits. It was just after one

o'clock. Crisp, late autumn clouds hung about the horizon, but above them it was as blue as the love-in-the-mists that grew in Grandma Wood's garden. Past the apple trees, the quinces and the cherries we went, until we reached our boundary fence, guarded by pines. Carefully we climbed through the fence, careful of Sunday clothes and stockings and hats and hair, and careful to avoid the camouflaged rabbit traps and the clinging blackberry bushes with their remnants of summer lushness only evident in frost blackened fronds.

We were almost there, having walked past the packing shed and the shelter which housed the spray and the old Ford truck. Pausing by the vine covered lavatory mother removed her galoshes and together we crossed the verandah to knock on Mrs Allison's front door. But she had seen us coming, and was there to greet us, ushering us into the dining room, exclaiming over mother's yellow afternoon tea sponge, and turning my shy face up to remark yet again, "How like you she is Ivy!"

We weren't the first to arrive, nor the last. The women's voices rose and fell, but I wasn't interested by the talk and chatter about the new young teacher, about Rosemary's twenty-first, and about the war. All talk soon hovered around "the war" — it hung like a great menacing hawk over their lives — but what did it concern me? Dad was away sometimes in the military forces, but mother was there. I stood by her chair for quite a while, watching as the women arrived. First, their presentation of "a little something" for afternoon tea, placed delicately on the traymobile by Mrs Allison, and then an item for the bring-and-buy stall, an institution that puzzled me greatly. I noticed mother's eyes glance first at a second-hand pair of brown school shoes and then lingeringly on a spray of sweet-scented daphne bush.

The real focus was the dining room table, covered in a heavy old blanket. There, baskets were being emptied: our plum pudding, tinned biscuits, socks and

### PAGE 43
the balaclava were soon intermingling with other items of food and clothing. Then I spied some curious things that just didn't seem to fit in with everything else on the table. Round things like large marbles, earth coloured, all puckered and ridged and wrinkled like pictures of an elephant I'd seen in an old encyclopaedia.

By now the women — about ten of them — had converged around the table and with extreme precision and deft hands were placing various items in neat bundles: a pair of socks, a finely toothed comb, biscuits and balaclavas all packed and padded with recent newspapers and magazines, and then stitched in calico and weighed on the kitchen scales. I knew the process so well, but today it was different because our soldiers at the front, if they ever did receive our parcels, would find walnuts in the crevices.

## SCHOOL IN THE
SUPPER ROOM

We got wet feet going to school that morning. The grass was dewy and cold, and I was six. Dad had put on his suit and hat and walked with Neville and Gwenda and me to the two-teacher school. Dad spoke to Mr Taylor the headmaster in the big room and then led me over the road to the back of the galvanized iron hall, to the supper room. I realized that dad had gone, that Gwenda and Neville were in the big school and obviously didn't care, and that for the first time I was alone — apart rather than alone — apart from those familiar family faces. It was all too much. I cried and called but they didn't come. Instead two big girls called Maureen and Lurline guided me into Miss Anderson.

Somehow I was cajoled into a non-crying activity with coloured beads, and later chalky pastels and grey drawing paper took their place. I drew mother and dad and home, and the tears welled up again — but perhaps Miss Anderson noticed and I found myself with some plasticine and a board, and for a little while enjoyed rolling balls. I called them

apples because I knew that dad would be packing apples in the apple shed today. After I'd eaten sandwiches and an apple I drank water from the galvanized tank at the back of the hall, one hand cupped, the other regulating the stiff spanner-like handle, Maureen and Lurline guiding my every movement. And then it was afternoon. We little ones put our heads on the desk and perhaps we slept. Next came the snub nosed scissors — snip snip snip — my thumb hurt, the scissors wouldn't work, the paper tore. When would this impossible day ever end?

At a word from Miss Anderson everyone stopped, slapping their books untidily under their desks, slamming up their seats, and hurrying to a scrap piece of feltex on the bare floor in front of Miss Anderson, who had taken her place on a diminutive chair. In her lap rested a red book, large, slightly tattered, and obviously a much revered volume judging by the expectant looks and eager country faces before her.

I watched mesmerized by this transformation of a class from work, through chaos and clatter, to attentive orderliness. What should I do? Miss Anderson raised her eyes, noticed me still struggling with the scissors, and ever so quietly said, "Jennifer, come and join us" and so saying beckoned me to a place at her feet. Perhaps because I was new everyone shuffled and wriggled back quite willingly without a word, and as I slipped cross-legged into my space Miss Anderson opened the red book and began, "Suddenly Blinky Bill ...".

## NUGGET, TINKER AND
DICK

At the very beginning of my childhood I remember my three, huge, hoofed friends — sturdy, steadfast and sure — Nugget, Tinker and Dick.

Nugget was so called because his glossy chestnut brown coat resembled the dark tan boot polish that dad and Uncle Wes used on their leather work boots. It

### PAGE 44
was his job to pull the spray — that hollow, wooden-bellied vehicle topped with a clattering, chattering engine which all too frequently chose of its own volition to halt its clattering and listen to the silence. Then would begin the wearisome process of looking at this, poking at that, and numerous vain attempts to start it again. Dad and Uncle Wes, dressed in enveloping mackintoshes and hoods and wearing masks, looked like operating doctors battling for life on the orchard slope. And all the while Nugget just waited. I fed him handfuls of his favourite grass as he eyed me through his leather blinkers and reached down to nuzzle the grass from my four year old hand.

Tinker pulled the burner, a winter task for all with a spare moment. It was a great metal box mounted on smooth slides pulled slowly and methodically along every second orchard row. Dad and Uncle Wes, using long-handled pitch forks, hurled the winter prunings into the fiery furnace. Eagerly I joined in the fun of throwing sticks into the burner and watching them blaze up and glow and finally disintegrate into white ash. But blazing enthusiasm soon became ashlike and I wandered away to speak to Tinker who plodded on, up and down, occasionally stopping to the "Wo-ah" and starting again at the sharp-tongued clickety sound which meant "Get moving Tinker". I loved his purple-black coat, his shaggy mane, and his long, sheer-trimmed tail. His colour reminded me of the purple-black shadows which gradually wrapped up our house on summer evenings. Just sometimes dad lifted me up onto Tinker's broad back where I held on to his great leather collar, leant my fair head against his neck, and gently fondled his alert old ears.

Now Dick was different. He was fat and white and fond of rolling in the winter mud and summer dust until his white coat became dappled with borrowed colour. I can't exactly recall his responsibilities; perhaps it was the plough that turned over the wet black earth, maybe it was the

drill which yearly sowed the clover and lupins, or it might have been the harrows which like giant rakes smoothed and tamed the great clods on the ploughed paddock. But I do remember vividly the day we decided to take Dick's photograph with Auntie Effie's Brownie Box camera. We found Dick grazing on the top of the hill, head down, munching great mouthfuls of moist grass. Quickly Auntie lined him up and instructed us to stand in formal line in front of him. Dick paused only momentarily when he heard the camera's click, and then buried his head to continue.

Imagine our amazement when a few weeks later Auntie showed us Dick's photo. Dick just wasn't there! In the black and white picture we three children stood solemnly on the brow of the hill in front of a seemingly empty horizon. Then we looked a bit closer and could just make out Dick's white feet in the blackened green grass. But where was the rest of him? Somehow, in a way that seemed magic to us, Dick's whiteness and the pale summer sky had merged into one.

A contribution by Roy Pearce. (In 1936 I married Ruby Parkins, who was a great-grand-daughter of Francis Telfer.)

In 1891 when the Smith family left Nativuk to live at Nyngan, N.S.W. Margaret stayed behind in Victoria a while longer, as she had a job as housemaid to a Banker's wife in Horsham.

At Nativuk there were quite a few German settlers and by mixing with them Margaret acquired a smattering of the German language. One day she was travelling in the train and two German women were discussing at length, in their own language, the other passengers in the carriage. After some time one of them asked Margaret the time. She answered them in German and much to her inner delight, they went red as beetroot.

When Margaret married Thomas Parkins they went to live on a property which they called "Spring Valley" at Eulomoga near Dubbo, where they lived

### PAGE 45
until 1911. While there, their seven children were born, four at home and three in Dubbo. The property was very small and in 1911 they bought a grazing property at Marra Creek, 56 miles north of Nyngan, which was called "Melrose".

The family shifted up by road. There was a delay in the purchase of Melrose, so they went and stayed with Margaret's brother, John, until the sale was finalised.

It was rather isolated at Melrose. The mail came once a week if the road was passable. In the wet weather the roads became very boggy and it was often a week or two before they were dry enough for the mail to get through.

The only means of schooling was what was called a subsidized school. The Government paid a small amount towards the cost of the teacher and the parents paid the balance. The school was a room in the house, and the neighbour's children used to attend the "school" also. The only qualifications the teacher needed was that he or she was prepared to go out into the "outback".

On 9th June, 1917 Thomas Parkins met with an accident which claimed his life at the age of 56 years. He had been to Dubbo and was returning home. He proceeded from Nyngan to Buckingay by mail coach and left the latter place about 11 pm to drive home in the sulky, some 13 miles. On the way in the dark the sulky hit a stump and overturned, throwing Thomas out and killing him instantly. He was found by a neighbour next morning. It was a big blow to the family, losing a husband and father.

At Melrose there were numbers of wild pigs about. The family used to catch small ones, lock them up for a while and then have some pork. One sow, they thought they would let grow to bacon. However one morning when they went to feed her, she was gone. One morning, some time later, she turned up in the pig sty being heavily in pig. Being in pig, she no doubt got very hungry and came "home" for a feed. She stayed for a while, but after the little pigs had been weaned

and killed for pork, she disappeared again, but came "home" later to have another lot of little pigs. She did that for a number of years, which gave the family a supply of pork.

About 1920 Margaret Parkins rented a house in Orange, so as her two youngest girls could go to a big school and finish their education. The house happened to be close to where Mrs Woods (Roy Pearse's Auntie) and her four children lived. The families became friendly and in 1927 Daisy Parkins invited Ivy Woods to stay with them at Melrose, which Ivy accepted. When she went to Melrose, she met for the first time Thomas Dougal Parkins (known as Dougal), and they soon became lovers, eventually married.

When the time came for Ivy to go home, Daisy had to drive them in to Nyngan to catch the train, because if Dougal drove he wouldn't have been able to pay full attention to his love. When they got to the station, Daisy stayed in the car, while Dougal went to see Ivy off. Daisy heard the train leave, but after waiting quite a while Dougal hadn't appeared. She went to the station and the station master had just received a phone call from Warrigal (the next station, 10 miles from Nyngan), it was Dougal, asking Daisy to come and get him. He had been that engrossed in saying goodbye to Ivy that he hadn't noticed the train moving until it was too late, and he was carried on to Warrigal.

As time went on, and Dougal was thinking of marriage, he bought a property out the other side of Nyngan from Melrose. Unfortunately he had a bad run of very dry seasons and low prices which made it impossible for him to meet the payments on the place. He had to walk out and leave it to the person he had bought it from. His mother borrowed money on Melrose to help him, but to no avail. The bank was going to foreclose on Margaret Parkins for the money owed to them. When her brother Jack Smith heard of it, he hurried into the bank and when he saw the manager, threw his cheque book on the table and said "Take what you want out of that, but don't you

### PAGE 46
dare put Margaret Parkins (a widow) out of Melrose". Hence Melrose was saved. They had a struggle for a number of years, but eventually things came good and they did real well.

Dougal and Ivy were married in June 1932, and Dougal went into partnership with his brother-in-law (Wesley Woods), in an orchard at Orange, on which they did well and became financially independent.

I have spent all my life in Victoria, but I was holidaying in Orange, with Mum and Dad, visiting Mum's sister, Auntie Alice (Mrs Woods), who Mum hadn't seen for 28 years. While there Ruby Parkins came down to Orange and stayed with the Woods. So we met and a real story book romance began. I was roughly 500 miles from home and Ruby would have been nearly 300 miles in almost the opposite direction. That was in January — February 1933.

We were married at Marra Creek Church on April 21st, 1936, at 8 o'clock in the morning. Ruby was the only Parkins to be married in that church. The minister had to travel 90 miles from Brewarrina to perform the ceremony. It had to be an early wedding as we had to travel 60 miles to Nyngan to catch the train at 2pm. We got on the train and travelled continuously till we got to Albury (approx. mid-day the next day), where we stayed the night before going on to Melbourne for several days, and then up to Lockwood South, near Bendigo, where

we took up occupancy of the farm on which we lived for 40 years.

It will be 50 years in April (1987) since Ruby and I were married in the Marra Creek Church.

We retired into Golden Square, Bendigo 9 years ago.

On the train trip from Nyngan, we had to change trains twice during the night, so there was no sleeping on the train. Ruby and I could say what few married couples could say, and that was that we didn't go to bed together the first night we were married.

I never met Ruby's mother till I went up there to be married and only visited her once since. She came over here to Victoria and that was all I ever saw of her.

Margaret Parkins continued to live at Melrose till she died on 30th October, 1961, which was two days before her 94th birthday. She was buried with her husband, Thomas, in the Marra Creek cemetery. Melrose was left to Margaret's daughter Nellie, who with her husband, Norman Gaull, carried on the property. In March 1970 Norman passed away, leaving Nellie on her own as she had no family. She was unable to carry on the property, so in May 1972 she sold Melrose and shifted into Warren where she lived in retirement until she passed away in July 1977. Melrose had been in the Parkins family for 61 years and Nellie lived there all that time.

The Parkins family have all passed on now, Daisy the youngest and last to go, died May 1985.

## ESTHER TELFER SMITH

third child of John and Esther Smith

Born at Tungkillo, S.A. on 27th September, 1870, she moved with the family to Victoria and grew up at Natimuk.

At the age of 24, on 9th April, 1895, she married Thomas Henry Kent. They had no children.

Thomas was born on 6th December 1863, and entered the ministry in 1890 to become a Wesleyan Minister. The following details are from the Supernumerary Fund records in Melbourne.

He was stationed as per the minutes of

### PAGE 47
the Annual Conference:-

1890 Received on trial — stationed at Euroa.

1891 Rupanyup; 1892 Newport; 1893 Natimuk and Clear Lake;

1894 Admitted into full connexion (ordained), still ministering at Natimuk and Clear Lake.

1895 Nhill; he died of consumption on December 17 that year.

Esther went to Nyngan after his death to be near other members of her family and lived there for 10 years, when she too died of consumption on 5th May, 1896. She was buried in the Methodist section of the Nyngan Cemetery, near her oldest sister Jane Sophie.

On her death bed she asked her sister, Margaret, if she ever went to Melbourne, would she please visit Thomas Kent's grave.

It was forty six years later, that Margaret went to stay with her daughter Ruby, and son-in-law Roy Pearse, at Bendigo. While there, Roy drove her to Melbourne, and she was able to fulfil her promise to Esther.

Esther Kent, (nee Smith).

## JOHN JAMES SMITH

fourth child of John and Esther Smith

John was born in Tungkillo S.A. in 1871. He married Idona Caston Murray who was born 22nd September, 1874. They had 5 children from the marriage: -Ida (Doll), Violet, Myrtle, Hazel and John Murray.

Idona, or Aunt Ida, as she was affectionately known by the family, also raised a neighbour's baby (Marie Roberts), whose mother had died. She was brought up as one of their own.

They lived at "Brooklands" which was a big rambling home at Duck Creek.

John James Smith and his cousin, Lillian May Telfer.

### PAGE 48
## FRANCIS TELFER SMITH

fifth child of John and Esther Smith.

Francis Telfer Smith and wife, Ellie
Valentine Smith (nee Forbes).

Frank was born 14th February, 1874, in Mount Gambier, South Australia. At Mullengudgery on 7th October, 1908, he married Ellie Valentine Forbes.

There were seven children from the marriage:- Vera Esther, Ena Francis, Allan Edward, Laura Ellie, Ronald Malcolm, Jessie Stella and Maxwell Frank.

Ellie died in January 1925, and later Francis (Frank) married Catherine Gibson, and they had a daughter Flora Telfer.

Their home at "Eudora" was a large weather board house surrounded by shade trees and garden, a big orchard of stone fruits, grape vines, lemon, orange, mandarin and grape fruit trees and a tennis court.

Frank owned a few race horses, which were raced at the local picnic races. Two horses he owned, "Pearl George" and "Pharalie", were later sold and shipped to India during the 1st World War.

In his younger days Frank was a keen push-bike rider, winning many bike races. On one trip he rode his push-bike from "Eudora" to Mount Gambier, accompanied by two friends. His main interest was his sheep stud, and through hard work was able to obtain good results from the showing of his sheep at local shows and the Royal Sydney Show.

On Monday, 9th July, 1945, Francis died in the Nyngan District Hospital after a stay of six weeks.

A contribution by Ena Simpson (daughter of Francis Telfer Smith).

I will endeavour to write a little about our early life at "Eudora".

We led rather a sheltered life during my early childhood, with only buggies or sulkys to travel in, a trip from home to Nyngan, a distance of twenty miles, was quite an occasion. We had to be up and away at first light, then a hurried shopping spree and off home. We always managed to be home by sundown.

Once a month the neighbours around would take turns to have a few neighbouring families visit them. It was always an enjoyable day, each family took a hamper of food. I can remember my parents picking vegetables and buckets of grapes and fruit to take and share with the neighbours.

We had a busy life at Eudora. When we were old enough to help on the place, there was always mustering, dipping and all kinds of sheep work, and during shearing we helped in the shed. All mustering was done on horse back, and after the day's work, the horses had to be rubbed down and then fed, and in winter, rugged. That had to be done before we had our meal.

### PAGE 49
I remember on one occasion Dad had market sheep to send to Sydney on the stock train. He decided he would make the trip to see them sold. On the way down, when the train stopped for any length of time at a station, he would get out and go along and check that no sheep were down. At Orange a chap boarded the train, Dad naturally thought he had sheep onboard. When the train reached Flemington, Dad said good-bye, and the chap thanked Dad for sharing his tucker with him. Later Dad booked in at a hotel, and went for a shower, to discover all his clothes were gone out of his port, and old rags put in their place. Dad never liked shopping, he liked it even less in his dirty overalls.

Once a month we went to Church held in our local hall, the Methodist minister coming from Nyngan to conduct the service. Earlier the hall had been used as a school.

In the same hall, dances were held, and everyone around went. The hall was decorated with coloured streamers and tree branches, and lit by kerosene lanterns. The younger children were put to bed in a small room at the rear of the hall. There they slept through the evening while their parents danced and enjoyed themselves. Everyone from about the age of eight danced, by then most could waltz and those a couple of years older were already being guided around by their elders through the Barn Dance and The Lancers. The orchestra consisted of anyone who could play the piano, a concertina or mouth organ. After the dance finished it was back in the buggy for the journey home. Not real pleasant on a frosty night. Saturday nights the young folk from adjoining properties would come over, and we would wind up the old gramophone and dance for hours on the verandah.

With no refrigeration to keep perishables, we relied on the charcoal safe (with the door always giving trouble) to keep our food.

A horrid green frog lived in the water tray on top of the safe. There were always a few canvas water bags hanging on the

verandah or under a shady tree, to catch any breeze to keep the water cool. Kerosene lamps were used in the living room and kitchen, and candles for the bedrooms. Round galvanised tubs were used to bath in, and the only way to fit in them was to sit with your knees under your chin or your legs over the side. With laundry tubs under the pepper tree, wash day was quite an ordeal, especially on a windy day — trying to keep the copper alight to boil the clothes. The clothes not to be boiled were scrubbed up and down on a scrubbing board. Many a time a paddy or lamb rubbed up against the clothes line prop that was holding the line up to catch the breeze. The clothes usually ended up on the ground and had to be washed again.

My mother's parents, Elon and George Forbes, lived only a mile away across the paddocks, so we were frequent visitors to their home. They were a great comfort and help when we lost our dear mother on 18th January, 1925. Vera the eldest was only fifteen years old, she took over running the house and caring for the younger ones, with help from myself and Allan. Max was only four weeks old so it was a big job for her. Later our father remarried. His second wife was Catherine Gibson, better known as "Brownie" by the family.

There was great excitement when dad first arrived home with the first car he owned — a T Model Ford. Many a summers night he would take us for a drive, with the hood down, chugging along at about twenty miles an hour.

A governess taught us for many years, then a home was bought in Nyngan, where we finished our schooling.

The first marriage in our family was ours, Lach's and mine, on 28th April, 1931. The ceremony was in the Presbyterian Church in Nyngan and the reception at Eudora. We lived at "Adavale" Mullengudgery until April 1953 when we came to Dalkeith Nyngan.

Our Dalkeith home is only a few hundred yards from the Bogon River, and during the 1955 flood, our toilet being

### PAGE 50
situated outside was flooded. Each time nature called, the victim made off to where there was a patch of higher ground, carrying a shovel and a roll of toilet paper. No one dawdled there, as the mosquitoes attacked any bare skin.

The Dubbo Aero Club sent their planes out with food parcels to be dropped to the families along the river. Most of the bags burst as they hit the water. Our five older kids had great fun fishing for tins of food in about two feet of water. By the time most of the tins were found, they had lost their labels. Many a time a tin would be opened for lunch, to discover it contained cat food.

Having no garden fence at the time, our neighbour's pigs decided they would have a rest on our verandah, out of the flood water. They were making quite a commotion. Ellie and Harry were courting at the time and sitting up later than the rest of us. Harry said to Ellie "Goodness your mother is snoring loud!" and Ellie replied "That's not her, its Heckendorf's pigs."

## The Family of Francis Telfer Smith:-

VERA ESTHER — born in Nyngan on 16th March, 1909 — married Cecil Roland Forbes of "Arno" Mullengudgery in 1931.

Their son Ian Maxwell was born 1932. When Ian was six weeks old Cecil and a party of young friends, including his brothers, arranged to go fox hunting on his Grandma Forbes property. During a chase Cec was leading the party, and whilst endeavouring to turn his horse a collision occurred with another member of the party, causing his horse to fall and roll on him. He was conveyed to the Nyngan Hospital where he passed away three days later, at the age of 20 years. He was a very popular member of the community.

Vera lived in the Nyngan district for some years, and then moved to Forbes to live and educate Ian. During the War she went to work in a munitions factory, firstly in Orange and then in Forbes. She

became matron of the Church of England Hostel in Forbes.

In 1951 she married Gordon Besgrove of Forbes, and they settled in Forbes, but in 1953 went to Glen Innes to live. Life was not easy, as initially they had to live in a tent, before later moving into a small gatekeeper's house, which Bessie, as she was known, turned into a lovely home and garden. Gordon died in 1983.

In 1988 Bessie passed away. An extract from a tribute read at the funeral, reads:-

Those of us who knew Bessie will remember a woman who was generous and loving. She was always ready to help both the young and the old. She looked after children for at least one young widow I know of, when she had nowhere else to turn. She would sit with the elderly and the sick. She was at times blunt and stern, but underneath always caring. She had a ready smile, and practical ideas.

She was a life member of the R.S.L. Auxiliary, a member of the View Club, the Laurel Club and the Anglican Women's Guild.

Ian — born 9th June, 1932 — married Dulcie Jean Reddy of Forbes in 1953. Having worked in Forbes for some years, Ian accepted a position with Wright Heaton in Bourke. They lived there for a few years before moving to Condobolin, and to a position as salesman in Maitlands Store for Ian.

Their five children went to the Condobolin School, and did well in their studies and sport. Ian and Jean were keen golfers and Ian was President of the Bowling Club.

On 26th December, 1980, Ian suffered a heart attack, and at the age of 48 years, passed away.

ENA FRANCES— born 15th November, 1910 — married Lachlan Alexander Simpson in 1931 and lived at "Adavale", Mullengudgery until the property was sold in 1953 and they moved to "Dalkeith" Nyngan. They had a family of nine children, and when at "Adavale" the children went to school in Nyngan by

### PAGE 51
goods train, and later school bus. After moving to "Dalkeith" the school age children boarded in Nyngan until a school bus started running.

Lach was a keen sportsman, involved in tennis, cricket and horse racing.

In 1981 Lach and Ena celebrated their Golden Wedding, at a dinner at the R.S.L. Club with their family, including great-grandchildren and close friends. Lachlan A. Simpson, born in 1901, died on 23rd November, 1988, aged 87 years.

In April 1982 their sons, Lachlan and Donald took over "Dalkeith" and continued to run sheep and cattle, and later share farmed wheat.

Ellie Mary — born 1932 — “Childhood memories are mostly of riding horses, driving sulkies and helping with stock. Also of visiting Eudora my grandfather Francis Smith’s property, where we had lots of fun in the lovely garden.” After 1953 at Dalkeith “I supervised some of my brother’s and sister’s correspondence school until I married (much to their relief!).”

Ellie married a local policeman, Harry Browne. They have lived in a one-man police station (minus electricity) at Girilambone; close by the police station at Morisset; at the station at Balranald; the juvenile remand centre at Tamworth, and lastly, the police station at Armidale. After 34 years of service Harry has now retired from the force.

They had six children. Henry, who at the age of 3 years 6 months, died of a terminal illness. Terrence, teaches at Werris Creek, and Bernard is teaching handicapped children, at West Mead Hospital. Warwick, is a university graduate, and is working in the C.E.S. at Cowra, while continuing his studies (Honours). Jennifer is a registered nurse, at Newcastle, and Kym, also at university played in the 1988, Australian Universities Soccer Championships, and was selected in the Australian side.

Harry is a keen sportsman and likes to coach the younger generation in sport, especially cricket, at which he excelled — he also loves to work on "Dalkeith" when

Taken after Telfer reunion, 1986. L to R:-Ellie Browne, Karina Dunn, Margaret Dunn, Hope Simmons, Jenny Smith, Ena Simpson and Ron Smith.

in Nyngan. Ellie enjoys gardening, painting, reading and family history.

William Lachlan — born 11th April, 1934 — married Christina Lloyd and had three children, Carmen, Max and William. Remarried to Heather McWhirter, and had another child, Margaret. William is currently married to Kaye Carr.

Lachlan Allan — born 6th December, 1935 — single. In partnership with his brother Don they own “Dalkeith”, and since 1987 Lachlan owns Lachlans Menswear store in Nyngan. He is a keen sportsman and overseas traveller.

Robin Gillian — born 1st April 1938 — married Victor Francis Healey of “Mareblose” Warren. Robin is a telephonist, and they have two children, Catheryne who is a school teacher, and Peter who works on the property.

Ronald Arthur — born 17th January, 1940, — married Christine Boland, and have two children, Erin and Mathew. Ron works in the rural industry, has sporting interests and has served time as President of the Nyngan Tennis Club. At an early age he would saddle his old pony Darby and take a few dogs and spend his day hunting.

Margaret Ena — born 4th October, 1943 — married Ross William Dunn of Molong. Their children are Bryan,

### PAGE 52
Ashley and Karina. Karina is a talented entrant in Eisteddfods. Margaret's knitting ability is well known and she is frequently called on to help someone having trouble.

Donald Bernard — born 5th April, 1946 — married Denise Read of Nyngan. Their children are Rebecca and Nicholas. Don is in partnership with his brother in "Dalkeith" and is also a wool classer. In 1967/68 he did a tour of duty in Vietnam, and on his return served as President of the Nyngan R.S.L. for 7 years. Denise is interested in needlework, and helps with craft classes at the primary school.

Phillip John — born 13th September, 1950 — married Michelle Warren, and they have four children, Jacob, Marcus, Bart and Beau. Phillip works in the rural industry and also with the G.H.A. On a number of occasions he has played cricket for the Western Area. Michelle is a maths teacher at the Nyngan High School.

Terrence (Terry) born 1st July, 1955 (stillborn). He was buried on 2nd July in the Presbyterian portion of the Nyngan Cemetery.

ALLAN EDWARD — born 13th August, 1912 — married Jessie May Griffiths of "Longala" Mullengudgery on 20th August 1938. They have three sons, Malcolm who lives at "Longala", Roger in Nyngan, and Bruce living at "Were". Alan and Jessie are keen golfers and lived at "Wera" Nyngan until they retired to live in Nyngan.

LAURA ELLIE — born 25th April 1915 — single and now living in Sydney after having pursued a nursing career.

RONALD MALCOLM — born 24th July 1917 — married Ella Marion Thomas. Ron is a veteran of the 2nd World War, having served in the R.A.A.F. in New Guinea. On his return to Australia he and Marion settled in Nyngan. They went into partnership with Hunter Johnston in a produce store, later Hunter and Ron were owners of the S. & J. Service Station opposite the produce store. After some years Hunter bought their share and Ron and Marion took over the Caltex

Depot. When they sold the Depot Ron became a stock transport carrier. Ron has interests in cricket and tennis and the R.S.L.

Marion was a much loved teacher at the Nyngan Public School before her marriage. She worked for the Red Cross Association up until the time of her death in April 1982.

Their son Francis (Frank) on numerous occasions played cricket for the Western Area, and the Number 2 sports oval in Nyngan has been called the Frank Smith Oval.

Of their daughters, Audrey is a librarian, and Jennifer works for Quantas in Sydney.

JESSIE STELLA — born 22nd September, 1920. Affectionately known as Gibby, but at the age of 15 years she developed T.B. and was taken to "Bodington" Convalescent Hospital. She passed away during surgery on 27th December, 1937. Gibby was buried beside her mother Ellie, in the Methodist section of the Nyngan Cemetery.

MAXWELL FRANK — born 21st December, 1924. When his mother died Max was six weeks old, and so he was reared by the family. "He has lived all his life at Eudora — with schooling at home, apart from a couple of years at Scotts College, Sydney, with a year home in the middle to cut scrub for the stock. A good all round sportsman, he excelled at cricket and tennis.

After the death of his father Francis Telfer Smith, Max took over Eudora Merino Stud. He found working the property with horses far too time consuming so changed to an army jeep.

In 1948 he married Elizabeth Yeo, and they had four children (Barry, Valentine, Helen and David).

"The rabbit plague was on then so they were very busy times. With trapping, shooting and rabbit drives as well as rabbit proofing the boundary fences, the property was cleared of rabbits. Myxomatosis was introduced and the plague was stayed.

### PAGE 53
I can remember Max sitting up in bed in the middle of the night yelling "Get hold of him Jack! Get hold of him Jack!!" and grabbing my foot wondering if it would have its neck wrung. Fortunately he woke up.

Realizing the future of Poll merinos Max started breeding them in 1957 and discontinued the original horned stud in 1967.

Eudora homestead was burned down in 1965 during one of our worst droughts. In 1967 we built a new house on the same spot, as the sheds and fruit trees were still there.

Since 1960 Eudora has exhibited almost continually at Sydney sheep shows, taking out several Grand Championships and group trophies, as well as exhibiting at local shows.

In 1973 we purchased the entire Bundemar poll stud and in 1984 Lachlan poll stud.

Max was appointed President of N.S.W. Stud Merino Breeders Association in 1977 for three years. As well as serving as a state councillor for many years he served for several years on the Federal Council of Stud Merino Breeders.

For some fifteen years we had a Braford cattle stud, but it was dispersed in 1985 so we could concentrate on the sheep stud."

(Supplied by Beth Smith)

An adjoining property has been acquired and another near Nyngan. The two sons, Barry and David live on these and help run the stud.

Barry Maxwell — born 29th November, 1949 — married Colleen Barrow and have three children — Heath, Carly and Chloe. They live at "Arno" joining "Eudora", and the two eldest children go by bus to school in Nyngan.

Valentine Ida — born 10th June, 1951 — is a secretary, and married to Mark Bullen an architect, and they live at Cremonie, Sydney.

Helen Elizabeth — born 4th January, 1955 — took her BA and LLB degrees at Sydney University and works in the legal department of Westpac Bank. She was transferred to London for three years and is now Manager of Legal Corporate Banking Division in Sydney.

David Paul — born 26th June, 1956 — is married to Andrena Correll, and they have two children, Oona and Philippa. The family live on "Box Cowal" property near Nyngan and David helps run the stud.

FLORA TELFER — the only child from the second marriage of Francis Telfer Smith to Catherine Gibson. Flora never married and lives in Brisbane.

## ELIZABETH ANNE SMITH

sixth child of John and Esther Smith

Elizabeth is the youngest child of Esther and John Smith, and married Edward Ellis. They had two daughters:-

IVY — married Mr Hess, but died about 1981.

DAISY — married to Dr C.T. Underwood, they live in Brisbane and have two children, John and Jennifer.

## ELIZABETH TELFER

Second daughter of Jean Murray and Francis Telfer

ELIZABETH TELFER m. CHARLES FARROW
(1836-1916) (1837-1876)

Elizabeth Farrow, (nee Telfer).

Charles Farrow, as a young man.

Elizabeth was born in Dumphriesshire, Scotland on 7th June, 1836, and was two years old when the family emigrated to S.A.

On 28th March, 1856, at the age of 19 years, she married Charles Farrow, only son of Joseph Farrow. Both Charles and Elizabeth were shown as "Juniors" on

the marriage certificate. The age of majority being 21 years at that time.

The marriage certificate says "Married at the residence of the said Elizabeth Telfer, Section 6146 in the Hundred and District of Parra Wirra". Mr William Vercoe, (Pastor) performed the ceremony and the witnesses were A.B. Murray and

### PAGE 61
P.M. Murray, brothers of Elizabeth's mother Jean. They apparently kept in close contact with the Telfer Family.

## A BRIEF HISTORY OF
THE FARROW FAMILY

(Supplied by Arthur Farrow of Stansbury).

John Farrow married Hannah Smith at Higham Ferrers, in Northamptonshire about 1809. At some stage prior to this, he and his forebears had moved from Rosedale in Yorkshire, to the Higham Ferrers area — a distance of 140 miles, which was a major journey in 1800.

We know that John and Hannah had two sons, who migrated to Australia — Joseph, born in 1816, and William, born in 1818.

William and wife Elizabeth Farrow came to S.A. aboard the KATHLEEN STEWART FORBES, arriving on 8th May, 1838.

William was the husband of Elizabeth Farrow, who was witness to the marriage of Francis and Dinah. He purchased 1 acre of land on Mackinnan Parade, North Adelaide, for £63, from "John Hindmarsh Esq., Knight of the Royal Hanoverian Order, Captain of the Royal Navy, Governor, and Commander-in-Chief of the said Province and Susannah Wilson, his wife".

William died in 1876, and Elizabeth his wife, who was born in 1817, died in 1899.

Joseph came to Australia looking for his younger brother William who had come to South Australia some years before.

Joseph and Anne and their son Charles arrived on 11th March, 1841 at Port Jackson, New South Wales on the ship "Glensvilly" which left Plymouth on 26th November, 1840 for Sydney.

Joseph aboard the "Emma", left Sydney on 20th July, but they had to return after encountering a boisterous gale, and left later in the company of the "Dorset". It seems that Anne Farrow never reached South Australia, as a

passenger list of the "Emma" shows only J. Farrow and son (Chas was 9 years old), on board on her arrival in Adelaide, on 30th August, 1845.

Joseph apparently settled near Kersbrook, and as mentioned previously, married Mary Flavel widow of Joseph Flavel, on 19th June 1848 and later they moved to Hope Valley.

It is on record that Joseph Farrow, with two other gentlemen, purchased land at Houghton in 1848, to establish a school (Tea Tree Gully history). Charles would have been 12 years of age in 1848, and must have received a reasonable education, as he took a prominent part in community and Church affairs in later life. My father (Charles junior), told me that his father (Charles senior), was apprenticed to a carpenter in his early teenage years.

In November 1855, when Charles Farrow was 19 years old, he left Hope Valley and walked to Tarlee, where he bought 152 acres of land from John Ridley (the inventor of the stripper reaping machine), for 319 pounds and 4 shillings, at Finnis Point. He named it Hope Farm. In the four month period between purchasing the land and taking his bride there, Charles laboured long and hard. He would leave Kersbrook on Monday morning and carrying a week's supply of food, would walk to Tarlee (a distance of 50 miles by road), by walking across country possibly 45 miles, to "Hope Farm". Arriving there Monday evening, he would then do four days work, then walk back to Kersbrook on Saturday, to enjoy Sunday as his day of rest and worship. He built a small stone cottage and cleared some land with an axe.

There is an interesting story my father related as it was told to him. About two miles south of Tarlee, on the eastern side of the main road, was a shallow well, called Tuppenny Well. (I think the owner of the well charged two pence to water a team of bullocks or horses.) Charles senior, was walking up to "Hope Farm" on a hot Monday, and in the late

### PAGE 62
Elizabeth Farrow later in life.

Charles Farrow at a later date.

afternoon, called into the well for drink and rest. Suddenly an irate bull came in for a drink. There were no trees nearby, so Charles climbed the post (6 or 7 feet high) of the whip, which was used to draw water from the well. There he sat until darkness fell and the bull wandered off. Charles then continued his journey, still having about 6 miles to walk to "Hope Farm".

Charles and Elizabeth worked hard and prospered. Their first three children were girls. At that time they had a long dry summer and their meagre supply of rain water was exhausted, so each day Elizabeth would tie the baby Elizabeth Telfer, to the leg of the kitchen table with a leather strap, and take the two girls, Jane, aged 5 years and Esther, aged 3 years and two buckets and walk $1\frac{1}{4}$ miles to Deep Creek, to carry back enough water to do the daily washing etc.

On 25th March, 1865 (Saturday), there was a fire which burnt out their cottage.

Charles set to work and built a new house (much larger, as their family was growing) with a galvanized iron roof and guttering. This new house still stands, and was inspected by the people who gathered at the Tarlee reunion, on 2nd December, 1984.

1868 was an eventful year — Amy their sixth child was born — Charles was appointed Chairman of the District Council of Riverton — and in June a group of people met at the Farrow home, for the purpose of uniting themselves into a Church. This was the beginning of the Tarlee Baptist Church.

In December 1884, Charles who owned a stallion draught horse, which he travelled around the district, to be mated with mares on various farms. It would take perhaps three days to cover his round every three weeks. On 2nd December, he came home from his round and said to his son Charles Junior (my Father), "Take the horses son, and put

### PAGE 63
Tarlee Baptist Church.

Memorial Plaque in Tarlee Baptist Church.

them away. I am feeling very ill." He was wet through, after being caught in heavy showers the day before. Charles Junior, took the stallion and saddle horse, and put them in the stable. That night Charles became much worse, and about 1 a.m. Elizabeth woke her son and said he would have to ride to Kapunda to get the Doctor. The Doctor arrived about 4 a.m. and attended Charles and promised to come back that afternoon, but Charles passed away just before the Doctor returned. He had contracted pneumonia and of course before penicillin there was little the Doctor could do.

Charles was buried in the Tarlee cemetery. Sometime later a marble plaque was placed on the wall of the Tarlee Baptist Church as a memorial to the life, work and devoted service of Charles Farrow.

Elizabeth carried on working the farm with the help of her sons. I believe that Joseph, the oldest son went to work with someone back in the Hope Valley/Kersbrook area, and it was Charles Junior, aged 19 years and John James, aged 14 years, who did the major part of the work until 1896, when Joseph married Annie Downton and came back and lived with Elizabeth and worked the farm. By that time there were only the two youngest girls and Charles not married. Charles then left and worked on his own and with a lot of hard work and study became a practising veterinary surgeon.

Some eleven years after the death of her husband Charles, Elizabeth's own father,

Headstone in Tarlee Cemetery of grave of Charles and Elizabeth Farrow.

Francis Telfer, came and stayed with her for a few weeks until he died, on 2nd September 1895. His grave in the Tarlee cemetery is in the next row to that of Charles.

Four years later Joseph Farrow, Elizabeth's father-in-law, who had lived at "Hope Farm" for 31 years after the death of his second wife Mary Flavel in

### PAGE 64
1867, died on 6th November, 1899. Joseph was buried in the Tarlee cemetery only a few feet away from Francis Telfer.

Elizabeth left the farm some time in the early 1900's and with her youngest daughter Eva, lived in a cottage in Tarlee. Eva married Walter Hookings in 1911, and on 8th October, 1916, Elizabeth passed away aged 80 years. She was buried in the Tarlee cemetery beside her husband Charles, who had died 32 years earlier. So ended a life of sacrifice and loving service to her family — I was told that she was a very kind and a most gracious lady and always quick to help anyone in need.

Elizabeth and Charles Farrow became foundation members of the Tarlee Baptist Church, and on 24th June, 1868 a group of people met at the Farrow home for the purpose of uniting themselves into a Church. Charles was appointed a Deacon and held this office for many years, until his death in 1884. The following is recorded in the Tarlee Baptist Church Minute Book dated December 1884:-

This Church deeply mourns the death of Brother Farrow Senior and desires to record the Church's appreciation of his devoted labours in the cause of Christ among us, having faithfully discharged the office of deacon and lay preacher for more than 16 years.

Charles was also prominent in community affairs, and was Chairman of the District Council of Riverton in the years 1868 and 1869.

On 11th October, 1862 William Holden sold a block of land at Finniss Point to:-

1 Hamish Klopper, farmer of Hope Valley

2 Hamish Wohlers, farmer of Hope Valley

3 Joseph Farrow, storekeeper of Hope Valley

4 William Smith, storekeeper of Hope Valley

5 Heinrick Bothe, publican of Hope Valley

Land to be divided into 3 parts.

No. 1 School Playgrounds

No. 2 School and Schoolhouse. Trustees declared School House for Educational purposes and when not required for the same may be used for religious worship or other purposes not inimical to the Christian Religion.

No. 3 To be used for ever as a place of Christian Sepulchre or interment as a public cemetery.

During 1874 this property was vested in the District Council of Highercombe.

The following entries pertaining to the Farrows have been extracted from the Diary of Amos Flavel, who lived at Finniss Point, and used with permission of the Flavel Family History Association Inc. The spelling is as used in the Diary.

1865

March

Tues 21

Brought home the colt from Farrows. Mr & Mrs Hunter came to see us with Mr & Mrs Farrow & Mrs Mitchell at the same time.

Wed 22

Brought home the colt from Pages & the fillie from Farrows & paid £2-9-0 for harness.

Sat 25

To Mr Farrow helping put out the fire but the roof was destroyed & part of the front wall of the house & a very great deal of other things of witch the neighbours intend to subscribe something. Some to make a little up. May the Lord send him a blessing.

June

Mon 19

To Farrows brought his horse dray for 17 pounds, paid £7, other £10 to be paid March 1st 1866.

Aug

Sun 13

Went to School & Chapel and received a letter from mother. (Mary Flavel)

14

Started to Hope Valley to lait for first train. Arrived at the Valley, found mother mutch better than expected but failing with age & infirmity.

### PAGE 65
Oct
15 Helped Mr Farrow (his stepfather) put up gate poste in front of school house. 5
21 Brought the lease of section no. 706 for to pounds. 6
30 & 31 Helped Mr Farrow cut hay, James & me.
Nov 1 Finished Mr Farrows hay at dinertime and commenced my own hay. 9
2 Cutting my hay, Farrow & William Glover & Thos. Stead was helping me and I had Thomases Flavels horse rake. 12
1866
Jan 27 Went to Kapunda with wheat. Sold all the wheat at 7/2 a bushell. Draw £80. Paid rent £21/6/6 to National Bank for R. Read. Paid out £25 for George Mugg. Paid to Mulern £5-10. Paid Shomap at Kapunda 14/- & received from Gilbert Ingles £2 for damage done by his bullocks to fenses. James brought home Mr Gills scupe ready for work. Paid Mrs Farrow the £5 witch I borrowed before.
April 4 There was a disturbance in the house of God about seat rent money.
May 29 Mr C Farrow resigned all offices.
June 6 Swincer came to enquire of what I had said concerning Mrs Farrows honesty. It was settled in peace.
14 Mr. Farrow has given me two days and halfe work with four horses & the chap. And one day himself and two horses besides the use of the scurifier. Thank God for his love for me.
July 3 Crushed some wheat ready to go to Hope Valley & borrowed Thomas spring cart.
4 Started to Valley at half past three o'clock in morning and went to Mr Schooler and stopped the night. We was received & treated kindley,

well fed until twelve o'clock next day.
To Hope Valley, found all well. (mother & stepfather)
Damp morning but went to Adelaide. Saw Mrs Hobbs & to hospital and saw Mr Hobbs. He was a little better. Went to Botanic Gardens & back to the Valley ...
Still at Tea Tree Gully among our friends. Saw Mr Telfer & paid him for the mesinger & then went to the Valley.
Started for home and took Dinah Hobbs to Henry Ingles place and came home & found all safe. Only the cat was shut in and toor window curtins.

A note at the end of his diary says "Amos Flavel died on 30th November, 1879, aged 41 years."

We have been told that Elizabeth, Charles and family were very musical, several being singers and organists. Their daughter, Jane was the first organist of Tarlee Baptist Church and her sisters and daughters took after her.

Elizabeth was a great support to Charles in founding the Baptist Church at Tarlee, and her name appears regularly in the minute book after Charles' death.

Of John James Farrow's family, Grace Evelyn Farrow won several gold medals in London Art Shows for her paintings, and like her sister, Marjorie Olive was also a Church organist. Ilma May was loved by children for her ability as a story teller. Murray Aubrey, whose name perpetuates the maiden name of his Great Grandmother, Jean Telfer (nee Murray), married Amy Mildred Harris on 14th January, 1925, and their family comprise:- Ailsa, a school Principal; Frank Forbes, a welfare officer; Allen David, a bank manager; and Ruth Hope, who married Geoffrey B. Dunning.

A Frank Farrow contribution.

I was born at Gladstone on 12th March, 1929, when my parents were living at

### PAGE 66
Gulnare, my father (Murray Farrow) then being Head Teacher of Gulnare Primary School. Having lived and worked for 7 years in Tasmania, 2 years in New Zealand, and 14 years in the United Kingdom, the most significant part of my life has been devoted to the development of Social Services in Britain. My background is in both education and social work and I have just submitted a Masters thesis to the University of Adelaide.

My wife Anne, was born in Wales and is just beginning a thesis on historical land use patterns in the Torrens Valley which I think would possibly include the Kersbrook area.

An Alick & Ashley Cooper contribution. (Grandsons of Esther Farrow)

Our grandmother Esther was the second child of Elizabeth and Charles Farrow. She married Richard Latter at Hope Farm, Tarlee in 1882.

They had 3 children; Ida Jane (our mother), Daisy, and Ken.

Richard Latter, working for Jas Martin & Co. Pty. of Gawler, in 1903 took a

Esther, (nee Farrow) and Dick Latter and their oldest child, Ida.

shipment of harvesters and strippers to Argentina. There he reassembled them and ran them in trials on prospective buyers farms.

An extract from a letter he wrote on 9th November 1903 in Buenos Aires to his employer reads:-

"If all the Harvesters go together like the first 3. We will be able to congratulate ourselves. I have 3 natives working with me and they do not know as much English, as I do Spanish, so you can guess how I am fixed. They just about drive me mad at times, and are always going for each other like cats, but are quite civil to me at all times. I go to work at 6.30 a.m. cup of coffee and roll for a stop gap, then at 11 o'clock stop for breakfast and start again at 1.00 until 5.30 or 6.00 p.m., then home to dinner, this goes on all the year round except feast days when all business is suspended."

Later on 26th December, 1903, in Lornquist he wrote:-

"Well I am getting along very well considering that I cannot speak the language, one day I am with Spanish, the next Castillion, and soon I have about 800 miles of country to work, so you will see that I have to keep going. This place is about 400 miles south of B. Aires, on Wednesday I was 350 miles north, so you can just think what going is in this country. As to the machines they are going very well indeed. You will be glad to hear that we are able to ship both Linseed & Canary seed and make a really good job of it, in fact far better results are obtained from your machine than from the Header and thrashing machine."

Ida Jane Latter married Percy Charles Cooper in 1906. They had five children, Ashley, Alick, Donald, Marjorie and Dora, and all are now over 70 years of age and still living.

When we moved from Lower Light to Blackfellows Creek, we drove in a buggy to the Telfer (Adam) home at Mitcham.

### PAGE 67
They put the seven of us up (plus two horses) for the night. I was about 6 years old and remember being taken to see the then Melbourne Express go past, with two coal fired steam locomotives belching black smoke.

Mother kept in touch with Elsie and Violet Telfer, (daughters of Adam and Margaret) who conducted a rest home at Rochester Street, Burnside.

My sisters Marjorie and Dora were also nursing sisters.

In 1932, during the depression years, I worked on York Peninsula carting bagged wheat, in solid rubber tyred trucks and trailers, some to Port Victoria where it was lightered out in ketches to the great old sailing ships.

Brother Donald bought and farmed Wedge Island for several years and with the aid of the CSIRO proved that sheep could be kept on so-called "coasty" land, by using a drench of cobalt and copper sulphate.

The second child of Charles and Elizabeth, Esther, married Richard Pelling Latter.

Their eldest grandson, Ashley Cooper recently gave us copies of a series of letters written by his grandfather, Richard to his employers at Gawler, while he was in Argentina as their agent, demonstrating their harvesters and strippers.

Richard was somewhat footloose and fancy free and found it difficult to settle down in one place for long, so this job probably suited him quite well.

He was a widower when he married Esther, who lived to the wonderful age of 92 years.

Ashley Cooper was born in 1908, and his first years were spent at Kuitpo where his father grew peas for a living. As it was extremely difficult to make a living that way, wattlebark was stripped from the trees and sold to the tanneries to supplement their income.

The family moved to Adelaide to live in 1918.

Ashley spent most of his working life

with Adelaide Steamship Co. As their auditor he travelled to many of Australia's coastal shipping centres (east and west).

During World War II he was transferred to Perth and placed in charge of the merchant ships being used by the Forces as troop carriers, etc.

Having travelled extensively throughout the Flinders Ranges in S.A., Parachilna, Hollowilina, Beltana, Moralana, Wallerbadina etc. he has been inspired to write and publish a book on the history of Beltana.

Letter written by John Taylor (who was a solicitor in Gawler), to Jane Murray Farrow just prior to her marriage to Alfred G. Stribling, on 6-8-1885.

### PAGE 68
Ashley Cooper suffered a stroke a couple of years ago, and although his hands and legs don't function as well as he would like, with his active mind he is continuing to write. Using a typewriter and typing one finger the text slowly takes shape.

In March 1988 he had his eightieth birthday party with his family.

We spent an interesting and enjoyable afternoon on a very wet winter's day, hearing about Ashley's family, when we went to collect a copy of Richard's Argentine letters.

## JEAN/JANE TELFER
