## JEAN TELFER (1840-1892) m. (1) RICHARD PAYNTER (1838-1869)
(2) FREDERICK PARKER

Most of the material contained in this section is taken from "FAR CALLED" a History of the Flavel and Associated families in Australia, from 1838-1982, and is used with the gracious permission of the Flavel Family History Association Inc. with whom I shared the search for Paynter family history, and was always made to feel most welcome at their meetings.

E.H. Simmons

Jean was born 7th August, 1840, and nothing is known for certain about her younger years. With her Mother's death while Jean was so young (not quite 4 years old), and having half brothers and sisters of her Father's previous, and subsequent marriages, there would have always been family around her.

She was born Jean, and signed her first marriage certificate as Jean, but at her second marriage she signed as Jane; so some of her descendants may feel confused, but she is the same person!

On 12th September, 1859, at 19 years of age, Jean married Richard Paynter, a 21 year old labourer, of Chain of Ponds.

Richard was the son of Richard Paynter, who came from Calstock, Cornwall, and had died in January 1856, aged 39 years, after a "protracted affliction".

Unlike the two Elizabeths, who were married in Francis' home, Jean's marriage took place in the Chain of Ponds Parsonage. Solemnized by John Kither, the Officiating Minister, the ceremony was witnessed by her father, Francis Telfer and a Henry Whiting, of Chain of Ponds. Jean was several months pregnant at the time.

After the birth of their first child, Thomas, in 1859, the family moved to Koppio, near Port Lincoln. Richard worked as a shepherd there for Jean's cousin, James Telfer. Mary Jane was born there, in 1861, but, after the birth of Francis Telfer Paynter, on 27th October, 1862, they left Koppio.

Having returned to the Chain of Ponds area, Jean gave birth to Elizabeth, on 31st December, 1864. Moving on to Saint Kitts, between Kapunda and Truro, Richard took the job as School Teacher to the children of the area. This change of occupation was possibly brought about by his poor health, as he could no longer tolerate strenuous physical work.

In 1869 Richard died of pleurisy at the age of 30 years. On 13th February, 1869, the Kapunda Herald "In Memorium" read:-

PAYNTER On Sunday 7th February at St. Kitts Creek, Richard Paynter, teacher, aged 30 died. Mr

### PAGE 79
Paynter, the Schoolmaster at St. Kitts Creek, died somewhat suddenly on Sunday last, by which a widow and five children have been left unprovided for. Mr Paynter has for some considerable time past been in very delicate health, but still danger was not considered to be so imminent. We believe he was greatly respected in the neighbourhood and that his family will have the sympathy of the residents of that district, in their bereavement.

In July of the same year, five months after Richard's death his fifth child, Dinah Flavel Paynter was born.

The children of Richard and Jean were:- Thomas; Mary Jane; Francis Telfer; Elizabeth; and Dinah Flavel.

On Christmas Day, 1871, Jean now called Jane, married Frederick Parker, son of George Parker, at Kapunda. Leaving Finniss Point they moved to the far north of South Australia, around Hawker and Oraparinna.

Jane died in 1892, at Queens Own Town (Finniss), from heart disease, and is buried at Strathalbyn Cemetery.

Her husband Frederick Parker, later went to live with his elder daughter Margaret, and son-in-law Edward Liebich, at Wildes Meadow, New South Wales. He died in 1928, and is buried in Bowral Cemetery.

## THOMAS PAYNTER

first child of Jean and Richard Paynter

Thomas, the first child of Richard and Jean Paynter, was born at Ward Farm, near Kersbrook, on 22nd December, 1859. He was 10 years old when his father died, and lived with his mother and step-father in the Mid North, and later, at the age of 22, married Elizabeth Liebich at Tarcowie, on 1st December, 1881.

Elizabeth and Thomas leased a small block of land on the western side of Mount Arden Station. After the land had been divided into mile square blocks and called Arden Vale, they made a permanent home in the district.

They built a stone house with a thatched roof on their block, situated five miles north of Quorn. A well was sunk and potatoes planted. Life became a grim struggle for survival with nine children being born to the family.

When supplies were needed Thomas and Elizabeth had to walk five miles into Quorn, and to protect the children from the wild dogs and dingoes that roamed

the area, they were placed on the roof of the house to await their parents' return.

In 1906, just before the youngest daughter Helena was born, Thomas went south to find employment. Their oldest son Richard had bought a nearby block of land and built a house on it, and after selling the old farm, Elizabeth and family moved in and lived with Richard.

Thomas worked on various properties around Peterborough. As the result of an accident in which he slipped under a train, he had a leg amputated in 1946. He died shortly afterwards at the age of 86 and was buried in the Peterborough Cemetery.

Years later their old farm was bought by their grandson, Douglas and the ruins of the stone house and windmill still exist amongst the peppertrees planted in those early days.

Their children were Richard, Lilian, Gustav, Ethel, Dinah, Agnes, Rose, Thomas and Helena.

### PAGE 80
RICHARD WILLIAM JOHN PAYNTER, the eldest son of Thomas and Elizabeth, was born at Mannanarie on 27th April, 1882, and grew up on the farm at Arden Vale. When he was eighteen he bought his own farm nearby, and survived the lean years by chopping wood from the surrounding area and carting it to Quorn, to be sold for 10/- a dray load. Most of his cattle died in the drought of 1914.

When the old Pichi Richi School closed, he bought the building for a farm shed.

He married Lucy Arabella Hill and they had six children. Dick continued farming for the rest of his working life. He died 23rd July, 1960.

LILIAN ELLEN JANE PAYNTER, was born in 1885 while the family were living at Arden Vale and she grew up there.

She worked on Nonning and Yardea stations until she married Henry Alcock, a railway worker of Quorn. They had one son, Henry, the only survivor of several babies, as Lilian carried and passed on to her son the Rh. blood incompatibility.

After Henry died Lilian left her young son with his grand-parents at Arden Vale, while she returned to station work. She married station hand Herbert Mayer, but was again widowed, and later married Alfred Hatchard of Iron Knob. She died there on 1st March, 1954.

GUSTAV FRANCIS GEORGE PAYNTER, was born in 1888, and grew up at Arden Vale farm. He married Sarah Jane Barbour, and they lived by the bridge at North Quorn. Gus worked for the railways as a train Loading Examiner, being responsible for batteries, and lighting of carriages, as well as a lifter.

Sarah kept hens and grazed cows on the parklands adjacent to their block, selling milk and eggs. She died in 1940, and Gus later married Lily Olive Rowe.

Gus died 1st July, 1946 at the age of 58 years.

ETHEL EMILY ELIZABETH PAYNTER, was born in 1891, at Arden Vale, and spent her childhood there. She

married a railway worker, Frank Smith, and they lived at Peterborough. They had two children, Vera and Francis. Frank died in 1932 and Ethel later married David George Flavel, a railway ganger. She died in Peterborough on 19th May, 1932.

DINAH ALICE MARY PAYNTER, born 27th September, 1894, also at Arden Vale. She left the farm to marry railway employee William Mundy. They lived at Port Augusta and produced 10 children. Dinah died 27th May, 1963.

AGNES LETITIA MAY ROSE PAYNTER was born in 1896, at Arden Vale, and went to school at Pichi Richi and Arden Vale schools, where classes were held in alternate weeks because of a teacher shortage. She married railway worker George Hucks, and they lived at Cockburn and Oodla Wirra with their three children.

On 28th May, 1952, Agnes died, and is buried at Loxton.

ROSE ISABEL MARGARET PAYNTER was born in a Quorn nursing home, on 8th August, 1900, and was educated at Pichi Richi, while the family struggled to survive on their farm at Arden Vale.

In 1918, she married John Hunter Rankin, of Koolunga, son of Scottish migrants Angus and Jane Rankin.

Rose and John lived on, and farmed for 23 years, the property owned by the Fosters of Arden Vale, and in doing so raised five children. In 1942 they moved into Quorn.

After a stroke, John became a semi-invalid till he died in 1960, aged 84. Rose lived at Port Augusta until her death, on 8th July, 1988.

Her youngest son Claude Angas, was a member of the committee which organized the Telfer Family Reunion in 1986. He with his wife Jennifer, and daughter Elizabeth, were in charge of the Souvenir sales. Claude is involved in the Marion District history research and is a member of a Community Service group, which works in co-operation with the Marion District Council.

### PAGE 81
THOMAS FREDERICK EDWARD PAYNTER was born 31st December, 1903, at Arden Vale and educated at Pichi Richi. He married Florence Catherine Donovan and worked on the farm of Mr Dave Stokes at Arden Vale.

When their daughter Margaret, was 12 months old and before the birth of Dorothy, their second child, Thomas died on 16th September, 1940 at the age of 36 years.

HELENA FLORENCE DAISY PAYNTER, born on 19th August, 1906, at Arden Vale, rode her pony daily to Pichi Richi until the school there closed,

when she was 12 years old. A keen horsewoman, she stayed on the farm until she married Harry Richmond Hill, a northern station hand.

As work was scarce in Quorn at the time, they moved to Terowie where Harry worked in the Railways at the strenuous job of ore transhipment, which was necessary because of the differing rail gauges.

Their 5 children were all born at Terowie.

In retirement with Harry at Gawler, Lena still treasures the beautifully preserved wedding dress of her mother, Elizabeth.

## MARY JANE PAYNTER

second child of Jean and Richard Paynter

Mary Jane, born on 30th June 1861, was only eight years old when her schoolteacher father, Richard died in 1869, and along with her brothers and sisters remained with her mother, following her mother's remarriage.

In 1882 she married John Watson.

John's grandfather Charles Watson arrived in Tasmania as a convict on board the "Asia II" in 1827 to serve a term of fourteen years. As an engineer, Charles was assigned to Public Works, and eventually became Engineer in Charge of the building of lighthouses around the coast of Tasmania. These included Bruny Island, Swan Island, Goose Island (off Cape Barron Is.) and Tow Head (opposite Launceston). He was also in charge of the building of the cause-way at Bridgewater near Hobart. Charles was given an unconditional pardon in 1834, after having served only seven of his fourteen year sentence. He was married to Bridget, and they had at least one child before he died on 11th November, 1849, in George Town, North Tasmania.

Their son, Joseph William Watson, became an engineer and helped to lay out

the streets of Hobart. As a consequence of this, the name Hobart has been used by succeeding generations of the family.

Joseph's son, John Watson was born in Hobart on 8th June, 1851, and at 12 years of age, ran away to sea on a whaling ship. He continued his life as a sailor until he was about 28 years old. On 1st March, 1882, he married Mary Jane Paynter. On the marriage certificate John is described as a "lime burner" of Arkaba, and the marriage took place in the home of Mr. Mapleton, also a lime burner of Arkaba. We believe this was near the spot where the former township of Wonaka had been situated before Hawker was established three or four miles further west, so as to be on the railway line when it came through in 1879-80.

The marriage was solemnized by Rev. H. Chapman, the first Wesleyan Minister of Hawker, and the witnesses were Mrs. Anne Mapleton and John Telfer (Mary's uncle).

Mary Jane and John, share-farmed and lived on land at Willow Plains owned by John Telfer. They had 10 children, all born at Hawker except Blanche, who was

### PAGE 82
born at Tarcowie. Their children were educated at Willow Plains School, within a quarter of a mile of their home, along with the Telfer boys from 2 miles away.

In 1910 at the age of 59, John and several of the boys travelled to the Olinda area of Victoria to select a property, and the rest of the family joined them later.

They struggled to make ends meet whilst their orchard became established, and Mary Jane was forced to take in washing and work in a guest house to supplement their meagre income. Her husband John died on 10th September, 1929.

Mary Jane lived to the age of 84 years, and died at Preston, Victoria on 14th July, 1945. Both she and John are buried in the Fawkner Cemetery, Victoria.

JOSEPH WATSON born on 21st March, 1883, was about 27 when the family left Hawker and moved to Olinda. He married May Bailey of Hobart and they had eight children.

Joseph was caretaker-manager of a piggery at Preston. He passed away in 1943.

FLORENCE BEATRICE WATSON was born on 1st September, 1885, at Hawker. She returned to South Australia to live when she married Herbert Benjamin Potter, in 1911. Herbert was a stone mason, builder and farmer. They had two daughters, Goldie and Joan.

Florence died on 21st May, 1954 and Herbert in 1961.

LANCELOT HOBART WATSON was born on 30th January, 1888, also at

Willow Plains School, 1895. L to R, back row:- Hobart Watson, Horace Harvey, Walter Telfer, Wilf Shute, Phil Webb, Joe Watson, John Shinnick, Ted Sanders, Bob McInnis on tanh. Second row:- Paddy Shinnick, Roy Shute, Netta Bailey, Florrie McInnis, May Avery-Cis Shinnick, Eddie Telfer, Carrie Taylor, Walter Webb. Third row:- Frank Hill, May Taylor, Alan Lush, Beattie Hill, Olive Lush, Nellie Malone, Stella Avery, Flo Watson, Miss Bleeby, (teacher), Jack. Front row:- Stanley Hill, Jessie Hill, Jessie Taylor, Abb. Edwards, Angus McInnis, with clock.

### PAGE 83
Hawker. He left his family and moved to N.S.W. where he started the Jam factory in Hay Street, Leichhardt, and spent the rest of his working life there until ill health forced him into retirement around 1940-45.

JOHN FRANCIS PERCIVAL WATSON born 30th March, 1890. He enlisted in the first World War, in the Ambulance Corp. After being seriously injured in Melbourne he was discharged. He was allocated a soldier settlement block of 320 acres of wheat growing land. Then on 23rd February, 1927 he married Sylvia Irene Langley.

Perce sold his block and the couple took up residence at "Silvermead", Horsham where all kinds of horses were bred. The property was owned by the Langley family and was on the bank of Taylors Lake. Perce was a keen fisherman and a lover of football.

Sylvia gave birth to 2 baby boys, but both died within a few days of birth. Sylvia died in 1955 and her brother's daughter, Mrs Clarice Goyne kept house for Perce until his death on 13th May, 1965. She has told us that Perce often talked about his early days at Hawker and about the Telfer family, with whom the Watsons were closely associated at school, sporting and church functions and as friends.

BLANCHE OLIVE MAY WATSON born on 14th September, 1892 at Black Rock near Tarcowie. She was 18 when the family moved to Victoria.

In 1927 she married Colin Samuel Smith Williams of Tullamarine, at Flemington Methodist Church, and in the following seven years of marriage two sons were born to them. Then in 1934 Blanche died, aged 42 years, and was buried at the Bulla cemetery. The two boys, Keith aged 6 years and Lloyd 5 years were cared for by various aunties and uncles.

The following is a contribution by Keith Williams, the eldest son.

After my mother's early death in 1934 (I was not quite 6 years old), I

was placed in the care of various maternal uncles and aunts. My father remarried in 1936, but my new stepmother died of a heart attack in less than 12 months, and I was then cared for by a paternal aunt until my father married for a third time.

On completion of my Primary School education which was obtained in various schools, I completed Technical School and obtained an apprenticeship as a baker and pastry cook. However within 12 months I was advised to give this up as flour was getting into my lungs. I joined the railways as a porter and about this time my grandmother, Mary Jane Watson died (14th July, 1945).

My father had returned from overseas service in the Army. Other than my brother Lloyd, I also had two step brothers, and a half brother and half sister.

I found I could not adjust to home life and so, with my father's permission I applied and was accepted into the Royal Australian Navy. I joined up just prior to my 18th birthday on 9th April, 1946, and served until 17th April, 1958.

I served in various ships and establishments, including the patrolling of the north coast of Australia, also southern New Guinea and New Ireland. I also served on a Troop Carrier for 18 months transporting B.C.O.F. (British Commonwealth Occupation Forces) to Japan.

In 1955 I was sent to England to bring the carrier "Melbourne" back to Australia.

My brother Lloyd had meanwhile also joined the Navy and coincidentally he had also been drafted to the "Melbourne", so we both served on it for 16 months.

Lloyd incidentally, had served on the HMAS "Murchison" which had been sent to the Korean War Zone.

### PAGE 84
We both served our full time and on my discharge I decided to go to Queensland. After 12 months of not being able to settle down, I returned to Melbourne, became a conductor in the Tramways and eventually became a driver and stayed for 12 years.

Meanwhile Lloyd had been discharged from the Navy in 1960 and had gone straight into the Public Service (Australia Post). Eighteen months ago I joined him and we are now both working in the Printing Section of Aust. Post. Both of us agree this will see out the rest of our working lives.

Looking back and thinking about my childhood days, I can recall lots of things.

Although it may appear that I had a tough sort of life, I can honestly say I had a happy childhood — due mainly I think to my grandmother, Mary Jane and to my grandfather Thomas Williams.

I can recall carrying in my hand, cups of tea to my mother who was very ill and always in bed. Also I remember I loved to carry the billy can down to the soup kitchen (during the depression), hanging onto my grandfather's hand. My father was out of work for 2 or 3 years, and although Lloyd and I were well cared for, my father has often told me how my grandmother would go without so as to make sure we never went hungry.

I pray those times of "Sussa Soup Kitchens" and hand-outs never come back.

Lloyd Linton Williams was born in the front room of our rented house and my grandfather John Watson died in the back of the house on 10th September, 1929, without having seen his latest grandson, as he was too ill.

My father has told me that my other grandfather (Thomas William's) death was greatly

contributed to by the death of my mother. Dad said grandfather just adored her and after she died he just fretted away, even though he was only a father-in-law, and had eight daughters of his own.

I remember grandmother Watson as a very gentle and hard working woman, and I missed her terribly when she died in July 1945.

Colin Williams resides at the Inala Village, Box Hill, Victoria.

CECIL CHARLES WATSON born 3rd April, 1894, was sixteen when the family moved to Victoria. In 1912 he went to Horsham and worked on two different orchards until war broke out in 1914 and he enlisted. During his $4 \frac{1}{2}$ year tour of duty with the 10th Field Ambulance, he was wounded three times.

On returning to Australia in 1919, he married his longtime sweetheart Elsie

Cecil Watson, son of Mary Jane and John Taken overseas, 22-12-1917.

### PAGE 85
Euphemia Timmins of Horsham. He worked for a time at the New South Wales Jam factory and then in 1921, selected land at Marnoo, but with the injuries he received during the war, clearing the land became too much for him. His ill health forced him to leave his hard work and take up poultry farming.

Cecil died on 26th July, 1936, and Elsie followed him fifty years later in 1986.

Elsie and Cecil had three children:-

Catherine Elsie who married Errol George Gumley.

Ian Cecil Charles who married Annie Margaret Edwards and lived at Natimuk until his death in 1986. Annie out-lived her husband by only ten weeks and died at Geelong.

Marjory died in 1929 at the age of 2 years.

CLIFFORD WATSON was born 20th April, 1898. He served in the Army in World War I and possibly in the volunteer defence corp in World War II.

He married Kathleen Caroline Arnott and they lived at Dimboola, where he died on 14th October, 1976.

Their only child, Carole married Bert Strauss and they also live in Dimboola near Carole's mother.

VERA MERLE WATSON born at Hawker on 12th May, 1900. She married Frederick John Dodd, who had a milk and bread round, until he joined the P.M.G. department.

Fred died in 1969, and Vera passed away on 4th October, 1978.

They had three children:-

Betty Merle married Henry Desmond Parks and they live at Fern Tree Gully, Victoria.

Marie Jean died at the age of 17 years in 1942.

Lauris Irene married Alex James Ball, and they also live at Fern Tree Gully.

Betty Parks writes:-

My Mother's life as a young person seemed difficult. Born in 1900 on the farm at Hawker she came to Olinda in the Dandenong Ranges of Victoria, in 1908. The contrasts in

Vera Dodd, (nee Watson).

climate must have been a shock, as Olinda is 650 metres above sea level and is heavily timbered country. John Watson had an orchard and Mary Jane took in laundry to assist the family finances, not an uncommon thing then.

I think the most traumatic event in my Mother's life was the death of her eldest daughter, Mary Jean who died of leukaemia at the age of 17 years in 1942. Her Bill (my Father) was in Darwin with the Civil Construction Corps, which made it much harder. My Mother took this very badly and withdrew from all public life (War Welfare Committees, etc.) and from that time her life changed significantly, and she never really recovered from this depressed attitude. She died in 1978 after suffering from Parkinson's Disease for 10 years.

I attended the Olinda State School and Upwey High School and worked for the P.M.G. as a telephonist until my marriage.

We have three children, Trevor an Inspector at Police Headquarters, Leonie a computer operator, and Sandra an Administrative Officer at Knase City Council.

### PAGE 86
ROYAL ERNEST WATSON born on 17th October, 1902. He married Rosalie Emily Hamilton and was a market gardener around Red Hill on the Mornington Peninsula. He died on 27th January, 1945.

ARTHUR TASMAN WATSON born 18th March, 1906 and being the youngest child of Mary Jane and John Watson was only 4 years old when the family made the move to Olinda. He was educated at the Olinda school until he was 13 years old. He then left home to live with his brother Perce, at Horsham, and to work on the farm there.

He later went to N.S.W. to work as a diamond driller. He married Jane Hamilton (sister of Roy's wife, Rosalie), and the couple took up market gardening at Red Hill South. They later moved to Mornington, West Preston and Montmorency before deciding to return to the land at Healesville in 1951, where they set up their own farm, which was the sort of life Arthur really loved. He liked being involved in the local activities, including playing for the tennis club and following the local football teams. He was popular and dearly loved his grandchildren.

Arthur died on 18th January 1973, aged 67, and was buried at Healesville.

## FRANCIS TELFER PAYNTER

third child of Jean and Richard Paynter

Francis, the third child of Richard and Jean Paynter was born on 27th October, 1862 at Koppio, where his father was employed as a shepherd by James Telfer.

Sheep used to be driven from Mount Arden Station to Koppio or vice versa according to the availability of feed and the seasons etc.

By December 1864 the family were back living in the Kersbrook area.

Twenty one years later, in 1885, in the Primitive Methodist Church at Yongala, Francis married Ellen Jane Liebich, daughter of Gustav and Mary Jane Liebich of Terowie. Francis lived and worked on the farm of Ellen's family for seven years, and then with their four children they moved to the Mount Arden area, where the Paynter family now lived.

Ellen suffered from diabetes and in 1894 when the children were aged 9, 6, 5 and 3 years, she died from the complaint, and was buried at Yarrah in the Mount Arden area. The children were placed in the care of relatives who lived in the area.

Sometime during the next six years Francis moved south to Port Pirie where

he lived until his death on the day after his 38th birthday on 28th October, 1900 in the Port Pirie Hospital. The cause of his death is given as paralysis of the brain.

FRANCIS THOMAS PAYNTER born 12th December, 1885, and being the eldest of the children, was only 9 years old when his mother died. The children were cared for by different relatives, and Frank went to the home of mother's sister Alice Voigt of Bruce.

At the age of 21 years he married Emily Louisa Wells (a descendant of the Liebich family), and they lived on their one acre block at Bruce where Frank continued to work on the farms of the Voigt and Liebich relatives. Four daughters and two sons were born to them at Bruce, and later they moved into Quorn, and Frank worked for stock firms.

Sometime in the 1960's he developed gangrene and this necessitated the amputation of both legs. He died in 1973 aged 87 and is buried in Quorn cemetery.

Their children were:- Viva Louisa. Ellen Mavis, Walter Russell, Ella Jane, Lorna May and Oliver Francis.

### PAGE 87
The following is contributed by Kevin Paynter, Grandson of Francis Thomas Paynter.

My father, Walter Russell Paynter was born at Bruce on 20th January, 1911, was reared there and commenced work with my grandfather on the construction of the Wilmington to Quorn Road using horse and drays. Dad met my mother, Florence Ivy McCallum, when she was working as a school teacher at the Bruce School. They were married at Grange in Adelaide on 6th January, 1937, and as work was scarce at the time, Dad took a job with the then Commonwealth Railways, as a fettler at Reid, Western Australia, on the Trans Australian Railway. Dad devoted his whole life to the railways and worked at a number of Stations, in positions ranging from Fettler, to Ganger and Roadmaster, before regressing himself to gain employment in Port Augusta to further my education and that of my four sisters.

I was born on 14th October, 1939, at Unley in Adelaide, while my father was stationed at Forrest, and returned there with my mother. Our first home at Forrest, was a tent bounded on three sides by railway sleepers stacked on their edges to roof height and held in place by stakes.

After Dad shifted to Port Augusta, he spent many hours in his loved pastime as a football Sprigger for West Augusta Football Club, for which he was made a life member. Dad died on 21st November, 1979, after a long illness of repeated heart attacks and strokes.

My Mother is still active at 83 years and currently (September 1988) resides in Ramsay Retirement Village in Port Augusta.

I joined the Commonwealth Railways in 1956 as a Junior Clerk and have worked there ever since,

Paynter section of Port Augusta City Band. From left:- Timothy James, Francis Joy, Kirsty Judith, Kevin Oliver, and Andrew David.

The member of the family missing from the above group, Russel Gordon, in uniform of the Australian Army Band.

and I'm now the Personnel Superintendent of the Port Augusta Branch of Australian National.

I met my beloved wife, formerly Frances Joy Langdon, at Port Augusta and we married on 18th December, 1965. Fran was a school teacher, but gave up teaching in 1969 following the birth of our first child, however she has resumed work now on a half time basis with R.I.C.E. (Remote and Isolated Childrens Exercises) which takes

### PAGE 88
her to all parts of South Australia teaching pre-school children, similar to School of the Air. She is required to make periodic visits to oversee their progress.

I have participated in numerous sporting activities, and still play competitive golf and lawn bowls.

Fran and myself have four children, Russell, Andrew, Timothy and Kirstin, and except Russell, all live at home and attend school. Russell is in the Australian Army Band Corps, and plays trombone for the Sydney Band 3rd Military District. Previously Russell played for the Port Augusta City Band, having joined his mother Fran (who plays the Tenor Horn), when he was 10 years old. Andrew (solo cornet), Timothy (EB Bass) and Kirstin (cornet) all play in the band, and dad who can't play any instrument has been roped in as the Drum Major this year.

MARY JANE PAYNTER born at Tarcowie in 1887 and orphaned at an early age was reared by relatives and received only minimal schooling, but wrote very clearly. For several years she worked at Melbourne Y.M.C.A. and then moved to Broken Hill, where she met and later married Thomas Spratt, in the Church of Christ there.

Tom being fond of opals, became a miner in North Broken Hill mines, where he rose to be foreman.

Mary and Tom had two children and joined in all the social life at "The Hill".

Tom enjoyed gardening, and loved to grow Sturt Peas. When he retired they moved to Adelaide, where Mary died of a heart condition on 2nd February, 1942, aged 54, and was buried in the West Terrace Cemetery.

Tom later married Mrs Barr. He died in 1956.

FREDERICK ALBERT PAYNTER born in 1889, and only 5 years old when his mother died, was cared for by

Frederick and Anne Liebich of Jamestown. He attended school for only 2 or 3 years at Whyte Yarcowie.

At the Methodist Manse in Quorn Fred married Mildred Mary Mahar, daughter of John Mahar of Port Augusta, who was a member of an exploration party which set out to plot the state's watercourses.

Fred and Mildred lived at Willochra Plains, north of Quorn and he worked on various farms in the area. Twin boys were born, and after the family moved to Quorn, they had another son and daughter. The couple loved dancing and card parties in the district.

In 1943 they moved to Whyalla, where Fred was employed at B.H.P. shipyards. In 1947, a steel plate fell from a crane sling, breaking both of Fred's legs. Determined to keep mobile he wore specially built up boots and bought himself a bike. Impressed by his plucky service, the firm allocated him a cleaning job until he retired, aged 70 years.

Fred loved all social events, and loyally followed his sporting teams. Although two of his sons were killed in World War II, he was a member of the Fathers' Association, and helped other families get over the loss of their sons in the war.

Mildred suffered a stroke, and Fred nursed her for a year until his death on 4th August, 1963, aged 74 years. He was buried at Whyalla. Mildred died in Salisbury in 1971, aged 83 years.

WILLIAM HENRY PAYNTER born on 24th July, 1891, like his brothers and sister was cared for by relatives after the loss of his parents. Later he worked as a clerk in the railways and in 1924 he married Annie May Richards at Enfield, N.S.W.

In 1941 Bill, Annie and their two children holidayed with his brother Frank and family at Bruce, after nearly 20 years apart. Their enjoyment was marred by the sad news that their brother Fred's son John Ralph (a "Rat of Tobruk"), had been killed in action.

### PAGE 89
Bill suffered heart trouble, and died on 8th September, 1941, at 50 years of age. He was cremated at Rookwood

Crematorium N.S.W. Annie died on 27th January, 1979, aged 79.

## ELIZABETH PAYNTER

fourth child of Jean and Richard Paynter

Elizabeth was born at Kersbrook on 31st December, 1864. Very little is known of her young years, although there must have been difficulties because of her father's early death, leaving the mother with 5 little children, and then the remarriage with more children added to the family.

Elizabeth married George Langford at Pekina Bible Christian Church on 15th March, 1883. George, a labourer, was born at McLaren Vale, and they lived there after the marriage, and the first 3 of their children were born there. George and Elizabeth moved to Strathalbyn about 1890, where 7 more children were born, 1 dying in infancy.

Although small in stature, Elizabeth worked hard for her large family, particularly as George later suffered from heart trouble. As the boys grew up and gained labouring employment, they eased the burden for their mother, until she no longer needed to work, although George remained a semi-invalid.

Several of the sons bought a home in Melville Lane for their parents, who continued to live in Strathalbyn, until their deaths a month apart in 1928. George on 14th November and Elizabeth on 14th December. Both are buried in the local cemetery with Elizabeth's mother, Jean/Jane.

EVA MAY LANGFORD was born 1st May, 1884 at McLaren Vale, and moved with the family to Strathalbyn around 1890.

She helped look after the younger children and did domestic work locally, and in Adelaide before her marriage to William Osborne. Bill was a milk man

George and Elizabeth Langford and Dorrie.

and later was employed as a Council rubbish collector, in the days when horses and carts were used, and the horse's harness was so shiny and bright.

They lived at Maylands where Eva died on 1st December, 1945, and is buried in the West Terrace Cemetery, Adelaide, as also is Bill who died in 1962.

HURTLE JAMES LANGFORD was known as Hurt or Jack and was born at McLaren Vale on 31st December, 1885, his mother's birthday. He was a stutterer which would have been a problem to him.

Jack worked as a labourer at Sandergrove and district, and later at Strathalbyn did odd jobs of gardening. He did not marry, and lived at the old family home till shortly before his death on 15th August, 1959, when he was at his brother Bill's home.

ALFRED GEORGE LANGFORD was born on 5th June, 1888, at McLaren Vale, and worked as a farm labourer, in

### PAGE 90
the Finniss district and also at Thakeringa, near Broken Hill. Alf saw World War I service in France with 27th Battalion, and was wounded in the arm and back of his head.

On returning home after discharge he worked again as a labourer, later losing the sight in one eye from an injury while grubbing yuccas, or "black boys".

Alf died a bachelor on 16th September, 1962 at Adelaide, where he was then living, and is buried in the R.S.L. section of the Strathalbyn Cemetery.

## WILLIAM RICHARD LANCELOT
LANGFORD was Will to his mother,
Willie to his wife, and Bill to most people,
and was born on 19th December, 1890 in
Strathalbyn.

He worked for the Rankine family at "Braeman" Strathalbyn all his working life, mainly as an orchardist and gardener — except for World War I service with 50th Battalion. He was wounded in the shoulder, was listed as missing-in-action, then reported as being interned as a Prisoner of War in Germany for nearly 2 years.

After Peace was declared and he returned home, Bill resumed work at "Braeman" where he met Alice Curyer, of

Langhorne Creek, who was a domestic at the House, and who had dusted the photos of the soldier who the Rankine family spoke of so highly.

Bill and Alice married, at Strathalbyn Church of Christ, on 29th March, 1922. Three daughters were born to them:-Gladys, Edna and Mary. The family lived for 11 years at Ivy Cottage in Taylors Lane, "across the creek" from the "Braeman" orchard. Ivy Cottage also had an orchard and garden, so there was always plenty of fruit and vegetables, and Alice milked cows and made butter from the separated cream, and kept fowls, so providing good food for their growing girls.

In 1938, Bill bought a home and 4 acres of vineyard next to the Showground, where the family lived after his retirement. Hardly retired, as he was still very much occupied with his vines and drying the currants (until the vines were taken out), and his garden as well as helping other people with pruning and gardening jobs.

Bill was a real outdoors worker, and entered Pruning competitions with success. He was also caretaker of the Showgrounds for a number of years, until

Alice and Bill Langford
at their 45th Wedding
Anniversary, with their
grand children. At back:-
Heather, Brian, Bob and
July Patterson. Front:-
George and John Stevens,
Joy Langford, Jan
Stevens and Roger Langford.

### PAGE 91
due to ill health, it became too much for him.

On 9th October, 1969, he died suddenly in the backyard, when he was doing a little job before his usual weekly visit to the sale yards. He is buried in the Strathalbyn Cemetery.

Gladys Emily — born 28th September, 1925, in Strathalbyn, and attended school there. She worked for a short time as a P.M.G. telephonist, and then did domestic work until her marriage to Western Australian, William (Bill) John Patterson, who she met while he was a patient at the Army Convalescent Camp established at Strathalbyn.

After Bill's discharge from the army, they set up home in Strathalbyn, and Bill had employment as a motor mechanic, and later at Goodyear as tyre fitter, in the district, until his retirement. He has always been able to help the family and friends with their car and farm machinery engines.

Their children Brian, Heather, Robert and Judith live in the town and district with their marriage partners and children.

Gladys's mother Alice lives with Gladys and Bill, so there are four generations living in the town. Gladys has her interests in the family and grandchildren, the Church and community work.

Edna Florence — born on 5th August, 1927, at Strathalbyn and educated there. She was employed as a domestic at the local Hospital for a short time and then went to Adelaide, also working as a domestic. She married William Stevens, who at the time was a P.M.G. motor mechanic, and a competitive swimmer of Saint Peters. He progressed through the years to become Resource Manager of Telecom on his retirement in 1987.

They lived with their children John, Geoff and Jane at Saint Peters before shifting to Brooklyn Park. Edna loved the garden and had "green fingers" like her father, Bill.

She died on 17th April 1978, aged 50 years.

Gladys Patterson's 60th Birthday. In front, her mother, Alice Langford, Bill and Gladys, with their family, Bob, Heather, Brian and Judith.

Mary Elizabeth — born on 23rd August, 1930, is the youngest of the family. She sang at Primary School penny concerts, and played the fife in the School Band. After attending high school Mary worked for a year at the local Paper and Gift Shop, before gaining employment as a P.M.G. telephonist at the local exchange. She worked there for 4 years until her marriage in 1951 to cousin Roy William Langford of Blackwood.

They lived for a few months at Blackwood, before moving to Helmsdale, on Anzac Highway, for twelve months and then to Glenelg North, where their children Joy and Roger were born. Fifteen years later, they moved to Glengowrie.

Roy died in 1970 and is buried at Centennial Park Cemetery. Mary has interest in her family, Family history, and music.

ALBERT ROY LANGFORD was born on 28th March, 1894, at Strathalbyn. He worked as a labourer and gardener at the Doctor's residence, until entering World War I as a member of 5th Pioneer Battalion. He was wounded in France, returned to England for treatment, and several times rejoined his

### PAGE 92
Rose (nee Clements) and Albert Roy Langford, 1920.

unit after injury. Again wounded by gun shot to both knees and a thigh, was treated in several English Army Hospitals before coming home at the end of the war.

On returning, Alb joined the Railways as a ganger, working at Blackwood, where he met and married Rosina (Rose) Mabel Clements, and lived there with her family. Alb continued life at Blackwood where he, Rose and their son Roy lived in a Railways Cottage until Alb's death on 8th December, 1949. He is buried in the Soldier's Section of the West Terrace Cemetery.

Rose died in 1978 after living at Glenelg North for some years, and is buried in Centennial Park Cemetery.

Roy William was born on 3rd July, 1921 at Unley, although his parents Alb and Rose were living at Blackwood. He was in the Scouts, and attended Blackwood Primary School, and later

Roy and Mary Langford with children, Joy and Roger.

travelled each day by train to Adelaide School of Mines and Energy, on North Terrace.

Roy played trombone in the College of Music Band appearing in "On Parade" on several occasions, and played with Blackwood and Glenelg Citizens Bands for a few years.

In 1951 Roy married his cousin Mary Elizabeth Langford and they had 2 children, Joy and Roger.

He was a Mechanic and Technician in the P.M.G. for 5 years before being employed at Hendon in the Phillips Company for 23 years, as technician and later as Electronics Engineering Assistant until his death on 25th March, 1970. He is buried in Centennial Park Cemetery.

He enjoyed working with his hands, and was meticulous with radio wiring and soldering. He was a Ham radio

### PAGE 93
operator for some years, having the call sign VK5LM.

HAROLD LESLIE LANGFORD born on 8th January, 1898, at Strathalbyn. He was a restless person and a wanderer, and worked as a farm labourer on properties out of the town, and remained a bachelor.

He died on 31st January, 1964, and is buried at Strathalbyn.

MYRTLE JEAN LANGFORD was born on 23rd February, 1900, and at the age of 11 months, died of convulsions, on 1st January, 1901.

OLIVE MARY LANGFORD born on 5th February, 1901, 11 days after the death of baby Myrtle Jean. Olive (Ollie) had a fair "English" complexion, and did housework at a home on the outskirts of the town. Ollie was ill for sometime, and was allowed home from the local Hospital for her 21st Birthday. She died in Adelaide Hospital on 15th June, 1922 of kidney trouble, 4 months after that birthday.

FLORENCE ETHEL LANGFORD was born on 25th October, 1903, and did domestic work in the town until her marriage to Reginald Aworth of Belvidere.

They lived in Strathalbyn, Kensington and Plympton where they had a delicatessen, before returning to Strathalbyn. Reg had been a butcher and a P.M.G. driver, but during and after World War II, was a machinist at Horwood Bagshaw.

During and after the war, Florrie drove the Royal-Mail by car, leaving Strathalbyn at 7a.m. for Aldgate and then returning, picking up and dropping mail at towns in between. Several days a week at the finish of the Aldgate run she took the mail to Bletchley, Woodchester and Hartley, and return.

Florrie was a tireless worker for War Funds, Women's Agricultural Bureau (she was made a Life Member), and the local Hospital with fund raising. A hospital ward was given her name because of those efforts.

Olive Mary Langford.

She died on 17th August, 1973, and Reg died in 1981.

DORIS ESTHER LANGFORD was born on 22nd October, 1905, the youngest of the family, and like all her brothers and sisters before her, was born at Strathalbyn.

She did some nursing, and then worked as a domestic/companion in the town before her marriage to Douglas Samuel Wade, at that time a plumber of Adelaide. He was later employed at Horwood Bagshaw as a sheetmetal worker.

They lived at Southwark after their marriage, later moving to Torrensville. Doug died in 1956 and is buried at Centennial Park Cemetery.

Dorrie passed away suddenly on 19th June, 1988, and after a service at the Torrensville Church of Christ was buried with her husband Doug.

### PAGE 94
## DINAH FLAVEL PAYNTER

fifth child of Jean and Richard Paynter

Dinah was born at Finniss Point on 18th July, 1869, five months after the death of her father Richard. Her mother, Jane, remarried, and with her stepfather being an itinerant farm worker, Dinah and her brothers and sisters, grew up in various places, from Finniss Point to Strathalbyn.

Dinah returned to the Booleroo Centre area where she worked for the Brook's family. When her mother died in 1892, Dinah was 23 years old, and three years later, she married Thomas Dennis, of Appila.

The couple settled at White Cliffs, in the lower Flinders Ranges. White Cliffs (renamed in 1906 to Awakurra, an aboriginal name with the same meaning), is in the Hundred of Booleroo. Thomas established a saddlery business for the surrounding area. This was the era of horse-mule-oxen drawn ploughs, hauling equipment, drays and coaches, and a saddler was in demand, making and repairing harness.

Thomas was a very industrious worker on their 3-4 acre allotment. He built an under ground tank for the household water, and dug a well for the stock water, and then a cellar for the storage and preservation of food.

From a shed at the back of the house, Thomas set off for towns near and far. At Tarcowie, he worked in an old blacksmith shop. When the work in that district was finished, he moved on.

Meanwhile, Dinah farmed her poultry and cows for the use of the family, and also ran a Post Office. Magazines, newspapers and mail were delivered three times a week, and the family would all help with the sorting.

All nine children were born at home, at White Cliffs and attended the local school. Thomas died on 5th July, 1914 (start of World War I), aged 52 years, and is buried at Booleroo Centre. Later Dinah

moved to Western Australia, to live with her daughter, Mrs. Lavinia Daisy Williams, at Mukinbudin. She died on 10th June, 1958, aged 89 years, and is buried there.

Their children were Lavinia, Dalby, Amy, William, Kathleen, Gordon, Jean, Hamilton and Mary.

LAVINIA DAISY DENNIS born in 1896, spent her early life at White Cliffs helping with home chores and mail sorting. She married Harold Williams at Booleroo Centre and lived at Murray Town, near Booleroo where Harold was an orchardist.

Later three of their seven children moved to Mukinbudin, a pastoral district about 1000 miles east of Perth and is situated just inside the Rabbit Proof Fence.

Harold received recognition from the Queen for services to the community. He died in 1979.

DALBY CECIL DENNIS born in 1897, was killed in action in France in August, 1918, aged 21 years.

AMY PEARL DENNIS born in 1899 at White Cliffs. She married Wilfred Neville Dempster at Caltowie. Wilf purchased land at Chandada, east of Streaky Bay on the West Coast, but was unable to make a living from this property, so they returned to the Tarcowie area. Bad seasons caused them to abandon the property and travel to the high rainfall area in the South East, and a property at Comaum, on the South Australian and Victorian border.

This property was too small to be a viable proposition, so their next move was to Willunga, where Amy and Alf ran sheep and cows until they retired to Broadview.

WILLIAM DENNIS lived only two days.

KATHLEEN LYDIA DENNIS was born on 16th August, 1902. She married

### PAGE 95
Gerald Gray who was a shearer before he enlisted in World War I. He was taken prisoner and Kathleen could not ascertain his whereabouts. On his release from P.O.W. camp, the couple went farming in Western Australia. They had two children, Patricia and Beverley.

Kathleen died on 11th February, 1945, aged 42 years.

GORDON PERCIVAL DENNIS
born 18th September, 1904, at White
Cliffs. Gordon married Muriel Johnson
and they went to live at Pt. Lincoln where
he was a carpenter. They had two
children. Gordon died on 9th October,
1986.

JEAN TELFER DENNIS was born on 31st October, 1906, died at age 23 years in 1929, and is buried at Booleroo.

HAMILTON HOBART DENNIS
born on 4th August, 1908, and never
married. He sharefarmed at Mukinbudin
W.A. until his death on 4th January,
1974, aged 66 years.

MARY EVELYN DENNIS was born at White Cliffs on 27th February, 1914, died three years later in November, 1917, and is buried at Booleroo.

## JANE'S SECOND MARRIAGE

Following the death of Richard Paynter on 7th February, 1869, his widow, Jean now called Jane (of Finniss Point), married Frederick Parker, an itinerant farm labourer/bullock driver,

and son of George Parker, on Christmas Day 1871. The marriage took place in the Wesleyan Church at Kapunda, and Jane's brother-in-law Charles Farrow, and niece Jane Farrow, were the witnesses.

After leaving Finniss Point, where their daughter Margaret Eliza, was born in 1872, the family slowly grew, with the births of Frederick George, Annie, Martha, Joanna Wittita Cordley, and Ethel May at Oraparinna, Arkaba and Hawker, all in the far north of South Australia. No birth certificate can be obtained for Martha, who presumably was not registered, but her name and age are listed with those of the other children in Queens Own Town (Finniss) School records. The first record of attendance for the family was 1886, and the last 1895. It is believed that Frederick was working as a railway ganger at Finniss.

Jane died 15th July, 1892, at Queens Own Town, from heart disease, and is buried at Strathalbyn Cemetery.

Her husband Frederick Parker, after a number of years, went and lived with his eldest daughter, Margaret and son-in-law Edward (Ned) Liebich, at Wildes Meadow, New South Wales, and he died in Berrima Hospital on 12th August, 1928. He is buried in Bowral Cemetery N.S.W.

At the time of his death, only three of his children survived him:- Margaret, Frederick (known as George) and Joanna.

## MARGARET ELIZA PARKER

first child of Frederick and Jane Parker

Margaret was born on 26th July, 1872, at Finniss Point. On 22nd February, 1893, she married Edward Liebich, a 26 year old farmer of Arden Vale, and son of Gustav Liebich. The marriage took place in the Wesleyan Parsonage at Quorn.

The family left South Australia about 1911, and moved to Handen, New South

Wales, and a few years later to the Robertson and Moss Vale districts.

Margaret Eliza Liebich died, on 27th March, 1938, at Moss Vale, and is buried in the Moss Vale Cemetery.

They had eight children.

JEAN LIEBICH was born on the 6th February, 1896, and married Jack Brown,

### PAGE 96
Their children were Walter, Frank, Kenneth, Alec and Len.

ALICE MARY LIEBICH, born 17th March, 1897, married Gordon Clifford White at Quorn.

They had four children:-

Lorna May White born 25th November, 1925, married Albert Robert Freemantle.

Herbert Gordon White was born on 29th October, 1928, in Sutherland, Sydney, N.S.W., and at 3 years of age moved with his family to Robertson, where his father worked on the land.

Working in Sydney, Gordon joined the Army when the Korean War started. He served with 3rd Royal Australian Regiment in Korea, where he earned the Military Medal for bravery.

While stationed at Woodside, South Australia, he married Eileen Glasson, and four children were born to them:-Cheryl, Gail, Bob and Tricia.

After his Army career, the family moved to Nairne, where in later years Gordon suffered a heart attack, and later had open heart surgery, but as his health deteriorated he lived in retirement. Gordon, also known as Pappy, died at his home on 18th June, 1986, aged 57.

His funeral service was attended, together with family and friends, by a number of Returned Servicemen, (some in uniform) to show their respect and pride

for a good soldier, who was a Life Member of 3rd R.A.R. Association.

Margery Alice White was born 15th September, 1930, and married Arthur Sparkes.

John Austin White was born on 3rd January, 1934, and married Sheelah Merrifield.

MARTHA ELIZABETH LIEBICH
born 28th April, 1899, married Edward Limond. Their children were :- Enid, Ellen and Edward.

EMILY MARGARET LIEBICH
born 6th May, 1901, married Jack
Grimson. Their children were :- Allan,
Dorothy and Russell.

EDWARD HAROLD LIEBICH,
born 24th May, 1903, married Irene Hull,
and they had one child, Frederick.

FREDERICK WALTER LIEBICH
born 7th October, 1907, was the sixth
child of Margaret (nee Parker) and
Edward Liebich. He lived with his
parents at Burrawong, near Bowral, and
remembers his grandfather, Frederick
Parker living with them when he was
about 6 years old. The family lived about
4 miles from school, so he didn't start his
schooling until he was seven.

After his marriage to Vera Dent, heran a poultry farm and sold dressed fowls. Some time later he worked a milk run in Grafton, and was a member of the local Rifle Club. Vera and Frederick had no children.

Marge Sparkes, Gordon White and Lorna Freemantle.

ELLA LIEBICH born 10th June,

## FREDERICK GEORGE PARKER

second child of Frederick and Jane Parker

George was born at Oraparinna, on 5th April, 1874 — his father was a bullock driver at the time. He did not marry, and was living at Wantiool at the time of his

death, on 16th December, 1946, in the Junee Hospital. He is buried in the Church of England Cemetery, Junee, New South Wales.

## ANNIE PARKER

third child of Frederick and Jane Parker

Annie was born at Oraparinna, on 18th November, 1875, and a death certificate, which appears to be for this Annie, gives

her death as 6th November, 1895, in Adelaide.

## MARTHA PARKER

fourth child of Frederick and Jane Parker

We have no birth certificate for Martha, but school records indicate she attended Queens Own Town School with the other

## JOANNA WITTITA CORDLEY PARKER

fifth child of Frederick and Jane Parker

Joanna was born at Arkaba, on 6th February, 1880, and was still living when her father died, in 1928.

## ETHEL MAY PARKER

sixth child of Frederick and Jane Parker

Ethel was born on 4th June, 1881, at Hawker. She was at school until 1895.
