### PAGE 112
## JOHN TELFER

First child of Margaret (nee Wright) and Francis Telfer

## JOHN TELFER m. CAROLINE AMELIA

(1847-1929) MASTERS (1842-1929)

Born on 24th May, 1847, John spent his early years busily engaged in helping his father earn a living by growing and selling fruit and vegetables. They did not just depend on the local market, but John, in his horse drawn van, made regular trips to Kadina and maybe further afield to sell their home grown products.

In the early seventies one of his uncles, (thinking it was time John settled down) suggested that he should call at Tarlee school next time he was travelling through and made the acquaintance of the school mistress, Miss Caroline Amelia Masters. Due to his father's three marriages, John had a number of uncles:- one from the Wright family and several

from both the Murray and Flavel connections, and all appear to have taken an interest in the Telfer Family.

John's Flavel uncles lived at Finniss Point and were close neighbours of Charles and Elizabeth Farrow. They would undoubtedly have known of the high esteem in which Miss Masters (of Tarlee) was held for her dedication and perseverance in a very difficult situation there, and felt she would make an excellent wife for a young pioneer in this new, tough country.

Like a dutiful nephew, John took his uncle's advice and called on Miss Masters, and seemed to have liked what he saw as he kept going back.

John Telfer.

Caroline Telfer
(nee Masters).

### PAGE 113
## THE MASTERS FAMILY

An old Masters' Bible which belonged to her father was treasured by Caroline until her death and was then handed down to her youngest son Edwin. He felt it would be more appropriate for a member of the Masters family to have it and so gave it to Mr. Ross Masters, but retained a copy of the following information which it contained.

Charles Masters, son of Charles and Mary Masters of Brownsover in the County of Warwick, England was born on 30th June, 1809. Married 6th August, 1835, to Martha, daughter of John and Elizabeth Williams, of Morton Park, Northamptonshire. Emigrated to South Australia in July 1845. Martha Masters, my beloved wife was born 21st September, 1814.

<table><tr><th>Children</th><th></th><th></th><th></th></tr><tr><td>Charles Frederick</td><td>B</td><td>18th Feb. 1837</td><td>D 30th Nov. 1845</td></tr><tr><td>William Williams</td><td>B</td><td>28th March 1838</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Martha Ann</td><td>B</td><td>20th July 1839</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Joseph</td><td>B</td><td>10th Feb. 1841</td><td>D 9th May 1841</td></tr><tr><td>George</td><td>B</td><td>10th Feb. 1841</td><td>D 9th May 1841</td></tr><tr><td>Caroline Amelia</td><td>B</td><td>10th June 1842</td><td>(D 24th Aug. 1929)</td></tr><tr><td>George Edward</td><td>B</td><td>26th April 1844</td><td>(D 10th Feb. 1912)</td></tr><tr><td>James</td><td>B</td><td>27th Dec. 1846</td><td>D 3rd Jan. 1849</td></tr><tr><td>Frederick Charles</td><td>B</td><td>14th Dec. 1848</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Jemima Williams</td><td>B</td><td>2nd July 1850</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Mary Ellen</td><td>B</td><td>11th June 1852</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Joseph Robert</td><td>B</td><td>12th Nov. 1853</td><td>(D 13th May 1922)</td></tr><tr><td>Emma Jane</td><td>B</td><td>4th Oct. 1855</td><td>at Wroxton Grange<br/>Angaston</td></tr></table>

Charles Masters, Caroline's father.

Martha Masters, Caroline's mother.

### PAGE 114
The dates of death of Caroline, George Edward and Joseph Robert, I have added since, as I came across them. The others were all on the original document.

Charles established a farm at Flaxman's Valley, Angaston and South Australian Directories show he continued to farm there until 1865. In 1868 his name is replaced by that of Mrs Masters, farmer of Flaxman's Valley, so presumably that was about the time of Charles' death, and Martha, with the help of her family, carried on the farm.

We know that about that time Caroline, with her sister Mary Ellen decided to open a school at the small settlement of Gilberton, next to the Forrester's Arms Hotel. This was a staging camp for bullockies in their long haul of goods to and from Adelaide. Neither hotel nor school proved to be very successful ventures, as the following year the railway from Roseworthy, which was supposed originally to terminate near the Forrester's Hotel, stopped 2 miles short of there, and so the population moved to the rail head and the town of Tarlee was established.

It seems likely that Caroline and Mary Ellen gave up and went home, as the Tarlee School history states that two more attempts were made to start a school at Tarlee (giving teachers names), but both failed as “the children were not keen”.

In 1870 Caroline tried again and was granted a licence by the Tarlee District Council of Gilbert to open a school in Tarlee itself. The booklet says, "Showing dedication and persistence that would do a present day teacher proud", she commenced teaching in the original section of the Baptist Church, with 41 pupils, 18 boys and 23 girls.

This time her perseverance paid off and her school flourished. She continued teaching there until 17th August, 1874, when she resigned to marry John Telfer.

The “persistence, dedication and perseverance” which helped her to succeed there, stood her in good stead many times in her future life.

Caroline and John were married in the Tarlee Baptist Church (her schoolroom for 4 years), on 22nd September, 1874. Caroline was 32 years old and John 27 years. John's brother George and Caroline's sister Mary Ellen were witnesses at the marriage, which was solemnized by Reverend J. Medway Day.

On the marriage certificate Caroline signed as C.A. Masters, but in later years she removed the A from Amelia because she was not keen on the idea of her initials being C.A.T., so she was then C.M. Telfer.

She and John lived in a house not far from Francis Telfer's home, and they probably carried on gardening until 1878.

Their first child, Francis Charles was born on 25th November, 1875. On 1st March, 1877, another son John Ernest arrived, but like so many little ones in those days he lived only 6 months (according to his death certificate) and died on 20th September, 1877, of pneumonia. Caroline wrote a poem at the time of his death, and the following is few selected verses from that poem:-

## ON THE DEATH OF MY LITTLE JOHN ERNEST

who died at Kersbrook at the age of 14 months.

1 "Safe in the arms of Jesus.

Safe on His gentle breast,"

But my arms are empty, my heart is sad,

And my spirit cannot rest.

3 God only knows how I miss him,

As the weeks and months pass by;

My darling, my pure-faced baby,

My own little cherub boy.

4 For me there is weeping and sighing,

And sorrowing day by day;

And oh! such terrible pining

For the wee white face that's away.

9 "Not much to lose a baby,"

The unknowing world may say;

May they never know what my heart has known,

Nor feel what I feel today.

### PAGE 115
10 I know I have other treasures,

And for these I thank my God;

And methinks he'll forgive that I sigh so oft

For the one beneath the sod.

23rd December 1877

Maybe that “14 months” was a misprint, because 14 months after the date of his birth, the family had moved nearly 250 miles away from Kersbrook to the Mid-North of South Australia.

Before leaving Kersbrook, John and Caroline offered to care for two children, a girl about 3 years old and her brother who was approximately 5 years older. Legal adoption was not available in South Australia at that time but they assumed full responsibility for them after their mother died and their father, a Swiss immigrant, not knowing how to care for his four children appealed for someone to care for them. Their surname was Hoceuli (spelling not necessarily correct), the boy's name is not known but the little girl was Bertha and henceforth always known as Bertha Telfer. She was two or three years older than John and Caroline's son, Charlie.

The following material was contributed by my brothers, Bert and Bill Telfer.

When the South Australian Government decided in the 1870's to cancel the grazing leases in the Northern Districts and cut up the land into small farming blocks, John applied for one of those blocks. Caroline told some of the family many years later that it was she who had suggested and then urged "Papa" to apply for some of the land to be allocated in the north. That "persistence" again!

After interviews with the appropriate departments, John was allotted 2 sections, 71 and 72E, in the Hundred of Arkaba. The land allocation was gazetted in 1877 and John travelled to Arkaba later that year. How he travelled we don't know. There was a coach run to the Wonoka Creek settlement, about five miles from his blocks, so it is likely he used that service.

In the settlement were several stores, a boarding house, and all the services necessary for an area in the process of changing from grazing to intensive farming.

On this trip John built a small one roomed wood and iron hut on the western edge of his land. The remains of the stone chimney still mark the spot.

John's brother, George, had been allotted the two sections adjoining John's — 72W and 59N — but it is doubtful if George ever occupied them. Due to a limit of 640 acres of land per person, it seems it was common practice for settlers to apply for land in their own names and then more in the names of wives, and perhaps also sisters, brothers and even children, and then work the complete parcel of land as one farm.

Conditions of the land grants included building a house and clearing and improving the land. The hut which John built was near the boundary dividing his and George's land. If the chimney of John's hut was on the eastern end, the dwelling was partly on John's and partly on George's land. Later on the hut was built on George's block close to the boundary.

John returned to Kersbrook, and in March 1878 set out again in a wagon, with all the conveniences of the day:-camp oven, billy can, water bags, blankets, food, household effects, horse feed, seed wheat and various farm and garden tools, etc.

We know that John spent the night of 31st March at Palmers Creek, about 15 miles from his new home. Mr. Will Sweet and his brother Arthur, and their two bullock teams had decided to camp there "on the water". Years later Mr. Will Sweet told Bert Telfer:-

That was where I first met your grandfather. We were settling in for the night when along came Mr. Telfer with his wagon and horse team, and decided to camp there too.

Next morning, before it was properly light, we were woken up with someone using a shovel. When

### PAGE 116
we crawled out from under the wagon, there was your grandfather digging two trenches through the sand, which was quite deep and soft, for the wagon wheels to track in. We told him not to go to all that trouble — we would pull him across with the bullocks.

So they double banked the teams on each of the wagons, and when they were safely across, they separated and went on to what was to be their new homes.

In discussions with various members of the family, it seems likely that Caroline and the three children didn't accompany John in the wagon, but travelled up later in a spring dray driven by someone unknown, possibly George. One suggestion is that it was a bullock dray, but whoever the driver and whatever he drove it must have seemed a long and tiring journey for that three month pregnant mother and three children. I wonder if she ever suffered from morning sickness or those cravings for exotic delicacies which some expectant mothers indulge in these days.

They settled into the one roomed hut which had a hessian partition to divide "bedroom" from living quarters, and with a minimum of furniture and household equipment this became home. Five months later on 2nd September, 1878, in this hut little Ernest John was born. A local woman who acted as mid-wife in the district was there to oversee his arrival. At one stage while she was out of the room, Caroline, feeling rather distressed, got up and opened the small window in their galvanized iron home to get a little fresh air. The mid-wife came storming in and exclaimed, "You wicked woman! Are you trying to kill that poor little baby by giving it a chill?", and very decisively shut the window.

The child survived and made history as the first white baby born at Willow Plains.

Contribution by R.F. (Bill) Telfer, the present owner of the original Telfer property at Willow Plains.

One of John's first problems was water. There were no permanent springs anywhere near his land and the nearest well which had been dug by the graziers in the station area was about 5 miles away, so much of his time would have been spent carting water for the horse team and also the household. So it was imperative to dig a well, but the first well, right under the fence between John and George's blocks was a failure. The second well near the centre of John's Section 72E was successful. A good supply was struck at about 50 feet and from that moment the centre of the farm began to be established around the water supply. A permanent house was begun. It was added to as finances allowed over the years until it was virtually completed in its basic present form in about 1893. Stables, cowyards, fowlyards, implement sheds, buggy sheds and a barn were erected in close proximity. Both the well and the house are still in use, the house likely to remain so for a long time though the well may not last much longer.

The next most pressing need in establishing a farm was to prepare some land for sowing. John's two blocks were covered with blue bush, a woody plant of the saltbush family growing about 2-3 feet high but George's block 72W contained an area which was more easily cleared and on this a small area was sown

Caroline feeding the chooks, near the newly built stable, implement shed and cow yard.

### PAGE 117
in order to get enough seed for sowing a larger area in 1879.

There was still no railway in the area but it was creeping north and the settlers and been promised that it would be available to carry away the 1880 crop and that may have been the last time a Government project kept to its schedule, for it arrived at that point where Hawker now stands about June 1880. As soon as it arrived the businesses and buildings from the Wonoka creek settlement were moved to the railhead and Hawker township was established.

I have been asked why John and Caroline called their new property Angle Farm. A glance at the accompanying sketch will show that John owned a sort of triangle with the square attached and even including George's blocks its boundary contained all sorts of angles. In 1887 George sold his holdings to John, who had previously in 1883 been granted two leases covering Windy Hill. These sections had not been applied for in the original allocations of the area as no one at that time had any use for non-arable land. Later in 1896 John bought out his neighbour Samuel Webb and the house on the 1000 acres so acquired was used by his step-niece Mary Jane and her husband John Watson for many years. John Watson appears to have worked at many jobs around the district. The block of land

Diagram of the land taken up by John and George Telfer at Willow Plains.

where they lived is still known as "Watson's".

In 1895 what was left of the old Warcowie sheep station was divided into farms, but once again they were too small to be viable and in 1903 John Telfer bought from Frank Webb for £75 a block which Frank had bought from the original lessees, the Moyle brothers, for £10. John sold this in 1923 to his son Eddie for £500 and Eddie sold it to his son Bill for £2,000. This block became the nucleus to which Eddie added another block originally owned by Tom Ryan. Bill has added two more blocks owned by Joe Marron so that now the Telfer holding at Warcowie is much larger than the home block at Windy Hill (as it is now called). One of the Warcowie blocks has a good stone house built by Joe Marron which is let in season to tourists.

## A Bert Telfer contribution.

It was while waiting in the waiting room to give evidence to the Land Board. Grandfather struck up a conversation with a sensible and friendly type of man. They each knew of course that the other was seeking a land allocation, but neither gave any indication as to where. When they later and finally reached their future homesites, had time to look around and see who the neighbours were, they found that their properties joined and their homes about a mile apart.

There, with Sam Shute and John Telfer began a close family friendship, with families of similar ages, that has gone along for four generations. The Shute and Telfer boys would nip across the creek to play together. When an extra hand was needed in an emergency, a Telfer would go to the Shutes for help, and vice versa. Together the families have worshipped, played cricket, football — at one stage croquet, sung in choirs, worked, rejoiced and sympathised.

In the book "Built with Living Stones" by R.F. Telfer in celebration of 100 years of Methodism in the Hawker area, we get some idea of their faithfulness to their God, rather than to any particular

### PAGE 118
denomination. There were Primitive Methodists, Wesleyans, Baptists, Lutherans, etc., but when there was a worship service being held, they were all there to enjoy worship and fellowship together.

In June 1880 a minister of the Wesleyan Methodist Church, who was based at Red Hill, Reverend Thomas Edmeades took a pastoral trip up through the north as far as Beltana and on his way back called at the home of the Shute family and visited Mr. Mapleton (where John and Mary Jane Watson were married) before going on to Wonoka creek settlement. In the report he wrote to the President of Methodist Conference at that time and printed in the above mentioned book, we read:-

"A few miles east of Wonoka Creek, where I had the pleasure of preaching last Sunday week in the plain of Arkaba where there are a good number of Protestant families settled ... (seventeen families named, and one was Telford) ... all of whom came from the south and are not only in sympathy with our religious movement, but would gladly cooperate with us if we could establish regular services."

In 1881, after the railway came to the area, a church was built in the newly established town of Hawker and in various other centres including in 1882,

one on the "plain of Arkaba" mentioned by Rev. Edmeades. In January 1882, at the Quarterly meeting of the Wesleyan Circuit of Hawker it was announced that a wood and galvanized iron church had been built at Arkaba and they asked the meeting to approve the building of same. There was not a lot the meeting could do but approve.

John Telfer, although both he and Caroline were Baptists before coming to the area, was one of the original trustees and later held the positions of Steward, Sunday School Superintendent and Teacher. Caroline was a "Poor Steward" (Communion Steward), and also a Sunday School teacher, and both were loyally and deeply involved in all activities of the Church including Christian Endeavour.

Meanwhile on 16th September, 1880, another son was born into the Telfer family. He was William, but unfortunately he only lived two months. He died in November of that year and was buried near the road not far from the family home. We don't know how the grave was originally marked, but before John and Caroline left Willow Plains to retire to Clare, John placed a little row of stones in the shape of a small coffin above the grave and surrounded it with a white picket fence. Those wooden pickets have long since disintegrated and Bill Telfer has recently cemented around that little

The original Arkaba Wesleyan Church. Built 1882. Demolished 1931.

Replacement Arkaba church, built 1931.

### PAGE 119
Grave of baby William Telfer, who died aged 2 months. Homestead and windmill in top left corner and Windy Hill behind.

row of stones and using a large rock from the nearby hill, made a rustic headstone with a metal plaque secured to it, inscribed with the infant's name — William Telfer, and date of birth and death.

The land for John's first crop was prepared by ploughing with a single furrow plough and then the seed was broadcast by hand from a tub he either carried himself, or more likely he drove a horse and cart around the ploughed area broadcasting the seed as he went.

We don't know if he had, and used, harrows to cover the seed or maybe he dragged a log, with chains from either end, behind the horses. I've never heard any guesses as to how he reaped that first crop, but it did provide sufficient seed for the next year.

For several years he had only 2 or 3 furrow ploughs to prepare the ground. His first impression was that he could grow much better crops at Hawker than at Kersbrook. It was proved over the years, as well as through scientific research, that the land up there did not require artificial fertilizers such as superphosphate to grow good crops. What was lacking most often was moisture.

John had a corn crusher (maybe later on) in which grain was crushed for household use as well as for livestock.

Caroline was not keen on iron stoves in which to bake her bread, cakes etc. She preferred a brick oven and had one built onto her kitchen. The oven itself was built outside the kitchen, but the oven door opened into the kitchen. The oven was about 5 feet square on the outside, half filled with dirt, stones and then a layer of bricks, and when the brick roof was completed it left about a 2 foot high oven. A cast iron door was built into the front. The idea was to build a nice hot fire in the oven, while the bread was rising, to get the bricks thoroughly heated. Next scrape out all the coals and ashes and place in the bread, and wait, and it baked beautifully. She baked cakes, biscuits, pies, and tarts the same way.

14th November, 1881, saw the birth of another son, Albert George. By this time the well had been sunk and with permanent water now for stock and household use a home was being established at Angle Farm.

They were probably in the process of harvesting their fourth crop. Fencing would have been erected to provide yards for the horses and cows. The first cow yard was built with mallee posts and mallee rails, with slip rails for gates. Someone had blistered hands and an aching back before all the mallee posts and rails were cut.

Their adopted boy would have been about 13 years old by that time, and capable of being very useful about the farm. Maybe he was pushed a bit too hard, for we are not sure when, but sometime in his teenage years he left the Telfer family and returned to his father.

On 28th January, 1884, another son was born into the family — Walter William; and then about 3 years later on 7th March, 1887, another son Edwin Gilbert arrived, the last of seven boys to be born, two of whom died in infancy. No wonder they all adored their adopted sister Bertha.

The Telfer family was complete. They walked two miles to school through the week and helped father and mother before and after school and on Saturdays,

### PAGE 120
Front view of newly finished house, 1893. Family L to R:- Charles, Walter, Bertha, John, Cocky on his perch, Caroline in chair, Albert the other side of her, Eddie behind chair, and Ernest with the dog.

Back view of the house, showing detached kitchen.

but Sunday being the "Sabbath", was a day of rest except for Church and Sunday School.

A school was built in 1883 at Willow Plains, with John Telfer as one of the trustees. This school continued to educate the children of the district until 1937, during which time it had had 20 teachers and 264 pupils rampaging through it. Destructive as young children are, they were no match for the white ants, and in 1922 the building was actually pulled down and rebuilt.

Charlie was seven years old when the

school was opened, so he was probably one of its first pupils.

We remember Eddie recalling how he helped his older brothers with the fencing of the "long paddock" on his way home from school. Besides the fencing of that paddock, the boys had a back breaking job of clearing it of small rocks and stones which were liberally scattered all over it. These stones were taken to the edge of the paddock and piled up along the fences.

By 1893 the house was completed, and as shown in the photograph they had a pet cocky who was very talkative. This

### PAGE 121
cocky obviously was an important family member, as Caroline wrote a poem about their cockatoo.

## O COCKATOO

1 Our Cocky is a wonderful bird,
Well worthy of a ditty;
And were his name unknown to fame,
'Twere certainly a pity.

2 When morning dawns and wakes the world,
And all the birds are singing;
Throughout the house you'll hear his voice,
In lively accents ringing.

3 He listens to the cawing crows,
He hears the roosters crowing;
"Boys, boys," he calls; of course, he means
"Now, boys, be up and doing".

4 But if the boys to sleep inclined,
With Morpheus seem to parley;
He calls them each and all by name,
Thus—"Albert! Ernest! Charley!"

5 I think he wants his breakfast, too,
He calls for Bertha, oft;
And "Mother! mother!" too, he says,
In tones both loud and soft.

6 I cannot tell you all his talk
Throughout the live-long day.
But when the horses come from work,
He calls out loudly "Whoa!"

7 He calls the dogs "Watch, Watch, Lad, Lad!"
Then sternly says "Lie down!"
You'd almost fancy from his tone
That you could see his frown.

8 He's social, and a temperance bird,
Not given much to mope;
When strangers call, he'll often say,
"You're going to the Band of Hope?"

9 And when around the social board,
Some tale you might relate;
His voice will fill each pause with "Oh!"
Expressive and sedate.

10 And when the children home from school,
Are coming, far away,
He hears their voices, knows their names,
And all you'll hear him say

11 Is "Walter! Eddie! Eddie! Walter!"
In quiet, constant din;
Until they're home, and through the gate
The boys come rushing in.

12 But if we should forget to feed,
Or bring him in at night;
In mournful tones he'll let you know
How much he feels the slight.

13 "Poor Cocky! Pretty Boy!" he says, "Poor Cocky!" o'er and o'er; Until his perch is carried in, And he is fed once more.

14 E'en Cocky has his place to fill,
In God's great world of beauty;
"Tis to make mirth, and bring a smile,
Amid life's sterner duty.

15 And if he makes us stop and laugh
Amid the work and hurry,
It helps to banish care awhile,
And we forget to worry.

C.M. Telfer.

As soon as they had built the dining room in their new home, they organized a Band of Hope, and the meetings were held in the Telfer dining room, which was 18 feet by 20 feet and usually packed out. Band of Hope meetings were fairly common in those days to promote total abstinence. Such meetings were held there, with a few breaks in between, pretty well right through to just before World War II. Both young and old of each succeeding generation, taking part in presenting the concert with songs, recitations, dialogues and musical items, followed by supper and lots of hilarity. They were much enjoyed by all, and were a great training ground for the future life of the community, when there were calls for people who had become used to hearing their own voices in public, to contribute to councils, committees or just take an interest in, and express opinions on, issues that affected the community.

After the back part of the house, including the dining room, was built, it and probably the other rooms too, had only tamped down clay for the floors. All the loving care they required was a sprinkle of water to keep the dust down while they were being swept clean. At least one year at seeding time, the dining room floor was used as a nice flat surface on which to pickle the wheat each night, ready for sowing the next day. The wheat was emptied out on the floor and turned

### PAGE 122
over with shovels while someone had the easy job of ladling pannikans of bluestone solution out of a bucket and sprinkling it on the wheat, to guard against it developing rust or smut as it grew.

Before, or as soon as the boys left school they were doing a man's work. When Eddie was 14 or 15 years old he was working with the horse teams during the day and when it came bedtime he was expected to go out and feed the horses while the others went to bed. One night he was so tired out that they couldn't wake him. One of his brothers had a bright idea, and brought in the shot gun and fired it off close to Eddie, but even that failed to work. He went on snoring, so they went off to bed and left him. In the early hours of the morning he awoke, went out and fed the horses and then went to bed for the few remaining hours before dawn.

I've wondered where the bullet went, but it occurs to me that in those days guns were loaded with powder and shot, and so they probably omitted the shot and settled for the big bang.

After planting crops for a few years, only to watch them withers and die due to lack of rain, and with rain showers that were short and sharp, with the water quickly running off into the creeks, John decided to take action to help nature water his crops more effectively. He undoubtedly stood on the north verandah of his home and watched the water

Four Telfer sons. Charlie, Albert, Eddie, Ernest. Walter took the photo.

flooding down the water course alongside the "long paddock" and then rushing into a deep and narrow acacia creek running past the house, and wondered how he could make use of all that water to flood the lower part of the paddock.

He rallied his sons, with scoops and single furrow plough, horses and dray.

Loads of those stones which had been cleared off the paddock were carted and a retaining wall and a huge bank built across the watercourse to turn the water out across the lower end of the paddock to completely saturate that area. Many times during the next 40-50 years when there were no crops elsewhere on the plain, neighbours would feel very jealous of "lucky Telfers" having a crop of hay or wheat to harvest. Not always, of course!

There were many seasons when nothing grew and the menfolk had to take the horses and head for greener pastures, to find work to raise money to buy feed for the horses so they would be able to work to raise money. Charlie took horses up near the Diamentina River one year, and Albert took horses to the South East.

We do not believe that George Telfer ever really lived on his Sections, but a micro film of Land Tax records in the Adelaide Archives for the year 1885 shows the Occupier of Sections 72West (640 acres), and 59North (108 acres), owned by George Telfer, as Self.

That same year, for John Telfer of Arkaba, County Hanson, Hundred of Arkaba.

The result of flooding the water course.

### PAGE 123
<table><tr><th>Section</th><th>Area</th><th>Actual Value</th><th>Improvements</th><th>Unimproved Value</th><th>Amount of Tax</th></tr><tr><td>71</td><td>504 acres</td><td>£756</td><td>£254</td><td>£504</td><td>£1-1-0</td></tr><tr><td>72E</td><td>136 acres</td><td>£204</td><td>£ 68</td><td>£136</td><td>5-8</td></tr><tr><td>58E</td><td>360 acres</td><td>£414</td><td>£ 54</td><td>£360</td><td>15-0</td></tr><tr><td colspan="5"></td><td>£2-1-8</td></tr></table>

Underneath that section was a letter John had written and sent in with his return. It contains some unusual spelling, but probably not so unusual for the farmers in those days.

Dear Sir,

In filling up my Land Tax Return I got Mrs. Lake to fill them in for me as I did not properly understand how to do it. I felt quite confused at the time, we could not easily understand how it was reconed between value of land and actual value wether actual value was to be considered what the land would fetch if it was to be put up to be sold or if the value of improvements we put on the land was to be added to it and so make the actual value. If so my returns will not show the amount of improvements I put on the land.

The improvements on Section 71 and 72E as I have put them on would amount to 72E £520 Hundred of Arkaba and on 58E £56.

You will understand by this how the mistake happened and will be able to put it to rights.

I remain

Yours Respectfully,

John Telfer

Willow Plain

About 1888 John Harvey opened a Post Office at Willow Plains, about a mile south of the Telfer home, and remained there until 1908.

In 1898 John Telfer opened a General Store just across the road from the Post Office in the small section 71A (as shown in Bill's map). His son Walter mostly ran the store but he also ran a delivery van to Warcowie, 12 miles north, and then 15 miles south to Yednalue, taking groceries to his widely scattered customers.

Sometimes people who travelled long distances to Church on Sunday, brought their grocery lists with them to save another shopping trip later. John Telfer remembering to keep the Sabbath Day holy, would not sell goods from his store on Sunday, but would look over their list and give them what he considered to be necessities free of charge. The other items had to wait for some other day.

Sometime around 1907-08 the store closed and the building was dismantled. The building materials and I believe, what was left of the stock, was shipped to Port Lincoln where Walter had the building re-erected and he opened his store there.

At the same time John Harvey pulled out of the Post Office job and it was taken over by John Telfer and transferred to a small stone room next to the buggy shed at Angle Farm. The mail probably came to Hawker by train where it was collected by the Telfers and brought out in a leather mail bag to the Willow Plains Post Office, and there it was sorted, ready for collection.

Bill Telfer still has the leather mail bag, and one of Charlie's sons has the pigeon

Telfer's Store, Willow Plains.

### PAGE 124
holes which were used in sorting the letters.

According to the Directories, Telfers carried on the Post Office until 1917. I feel a little doubtful if it went quite that long, but who dares to argue with statistics.

About 1903 John bought what was left of Warcowie station after it had been carved up into farming allotments, and Albert, who was 22 years old then, managed that with his sister Bertha, as housekeeper.

As Walter was going to Port Lincoln, and Charlie decided to branch out on his own, there was a dividing up of the horses and some of the plant, amongst those of John's sons who were interested in setting up their own farms. Charlie with some horses and plant, moved to Ungarra on Eyre Peninsula, where he took up some virgin land and began clearing it to establish his farm.

Ernest and Walter had decided against farming. Ernest left home about 1900-1 and travelled to Sydney to take up Missionary work with the United Aboriginal Mission.

John and Caroline were left on the farm with only their youngest son Edwin (Eddie). When extra help was needed, Albert and Bertha were only 12 miles away, and there were always nearer neighbours who were ever ready to help one another, when need arose.

## THE NEED FAMILY

In the early 1890's the family of Henry and Elizabeth Need, moved from Yongala to live at Cradock, 17 miles south of Hawker. After a year or two they moved to Wilson, a few miles west of Cradock and share farmed there for a few years before moving again in 1907 to Willow Plains. They lived in a house about 2 miles from Angle Farm.

At the time Elizabeth Need's brother, Joseph Edwards and family were living in the house previously occupied by John Harvey when he was Post Master there.

The Edwards and Needs youngsters joined in local activities, (and pranks) with young Telfers and others, and some

Telfer Family about 1911. Back:- Walter, Ernest, Eddie, Charles and Albert. Front:- Stella and Vic, Florence and John, John and Caroline, Amy and Frank, Beatrice and Joyce.

### PAGE 125
life long friendships were formed. As far as Ellen Need and Eddie Telfer were concerned it became more than just a friendship, and although Needs left the area again in 1908 and took up land of their own at Dawson (12 miles north east of Peterborough, or Petersburg as it was called them), the relationship developed and eventually on 23rd October, 1912, they were married at Dawson.

Caroline travelled down to the wedding of her youngest son, Eddie, but John was not able to leave the farm for the 100 mile trip there and back. Ernest Telfer and his wife and child were able to attend the wedding as he was a minister in the Baptist Church at Terowie. He was asked to assist in the service which was conducted by the Methodist minister from Petersburg.

When the young marrieds returned to Angle Farm, John and Caroline moved into the four front rooms of the house, with the addition of a galvanized iron kitchen and another small room built under the verandah. Nearby was a door opening into what became their dining room. This left Eddie and Ellen with the large dining room and two small bedrooms under the main roof, and with a stone kitchen across the verandah.

There was no such thing as bathrooms in the houses at that time. An old song says that "Saturday night is bath night", and so it was. The copper was boiled up and a large wash tub, about 2 feet across, was carried into the bedroom, and a bucket of warm water emptied into it and there, one after the other the family members performed their ablutions. It was not till around the 1920's that people began to have bathrooms with galvanized baths in them, and still carried the water from the copper or stove.

The washing was done in those above mentioned tubs, with warm soapy water (homemade soap), and the clothes were scrubbed up and down on a scrubbing board and then tossed into the copper and given a good boil. When they were well cooked they were lifted out with a poker (piece of broom handle), drained over a

kerosene tin bucket, rinsed in clean water, rung out by hand and hung out to dry.

When farm clothes were really dirty all that scrubbing could be a real back breaker, but it had to be done.

## A Bert Telfer contribution

In submitting a few snippets of the life of our grandparents — John and Caroline — I can only tell stories that others have told me, or of what memory can summon up from the sixty five to seventy years past. Some of the details are hazy.

Within the last four or five years of her life, Grandmother told a group of us at her dining table at Clare, that women were at least, and perhaps more adventuresome than men. It was she who had suggested and then urged Grandfather to apply for some of the farming land shortly to become available for allocation in the North of the State. It was she who had suggested, being over eighty herself, that "Papa" sell Eddie their share of the farm at Willow Plains, and retire.

A second hand story told to me by Jack (Snowie) Reynolds, who I guess was born in the late 1890's.

Something came up about Grandfather, whose voice used to change every few syllables from a husky whisper to the resonant and back again, and as a consequence of this we used to call him "Whispering Jack". Snowie's father, Paddy, who had a farm about six miles east of Grandfather's, was known to family and friends as "Limerick". His ancestry and accent having something to do with that. From memory, in the words of Snowie:-

One day the 'Old man' went down to see Whispering Jack in the spring dray, and took Steve and I with him to open the gates. Limerick and your grandfather went inside and we two boys poked around the sheds and yards amusing ourselves, and then found a good big heap of cocky chaff, all heaped up steep and high for covering with straw, out in the horse yard. We had a whale of a time climbing up and sliding down, till

### PAGE 126
the heap was nearly too flat to be much fun. Then we heard this almighty whisper, roaring out from a hundred yards off, "Get down off that chaff". We hadn't thought Whispering Jack and Limerick would be coming out so soon. In no time we were down, around behind the shed, down the creek, and waiting up the road for the 'Old man'. We had had a great time a few weeks earlier on 'Old man Avery's' chaff heap on the way home from school, but he took after us with the buggy whip, and for a few weeks we used to leave the track, go down into the creek, and out into the bushes, till we got to school. We were a bit nervous for a while about loud noises while we were playing in the cocky chaff.

That same trait in Grandpa's voice used to greatly intrigue me in my early years. It was something special. Not every boy's grandfather had a voice that would change from a whisper to "ordinary" and back again to a whisper in everything he spoke. It was really an accomplishment worthy of acquiring. One day, Dad and Grandpa were replacing a gatepost down at the bottom end of the water yard. More tools were wanted, so Dad set off at a run for the blacksmith's shop. Just as he was rounding the end of the stable, Grandpa thought of something else, and the whisper blared out, "Edwin, bring the long auger". Edwin heard from three chains away — and with intonations, etc still strong in mind, Bertie (me) decided to have a little quiet practice — some distance down the paddock. The practice didn't go too badly, with whispers and resonances repeated in fairly satisfactory order, and as I walked back, so as not to lose the ability, I kept them coming. For some reason Grandpa showed no appreciation of my efforts. A cuff on the ear and something about "being cheeky" and my ability fled. At times there is no justice in this world!

I remember grandpa's vegetable garden — all laid out so meticulously. There were a number of fruit trees — pear, fig, quince, a muscatel grape vine, and probably others that I don't remember, but it was the vegetables which took my eye. It was a very large patch, not far from the well, and fenced in with two heights of wire netting. The garden beds were laid out, and when planting time came for the cabbages or cauliflowers or whatever, he placed a peg in the ground at one end of the bed, with a string attached, walking down the bed he unrolled the string and then attached it to a peg at the other end. This allowed him to plant perfectly straight rows of vegetables. A legacy of being a market gardener.

September 1874 to September 1924 made fifty years of marriage for John and Caroline, since that ceremony in a little Baptist Church at Tarlee. The family decided that their Golden Wedding should be celebrated, so a family reunion was planned at Angle Farm. Caroline was 82 years and John 77 years old, and all their sons were there for the celebration. I believe Bertha was in New Guinea at the time and would certainly have made an attempt to attend had she been nearer to home.

There were 24 members of the family staying at the farm for those few days, and the cooks were kept busy. The barn

Table set in the Dining Room for the Golden Wedding dinner.

### PAGE 127
Family Gathering at John and Caroline's Golding Wedding, 1924. Back:- Charles with Reg., Ernest, Albert, Eddie, Vic, Joyce. Middle:- Millie, Douglas, Beattie, Gilbert, Ellen, Stella, Hope. Front:- Amy holding Murray, Melville, John, Ralph, Caroline, Bill, Keith, (standing) and Sylvia,

had been cleaned out to be used as an assembly hall. The program consisted mostly of swapping stories of the early days, and the usual family banter. Walter, a professional photographer, took a photograph of the assembled group. He had brought his own background curtains and a piece of carpet, and wonder of wonders, he proceeded to take a flashlight photo. He placed a little magnesium powder in a dish, and with the family positioned and ready, lit a match and dropped it into the dish — WHOSH! We all jumped out of our skins, but he got the photo.

I suspect the celebration meal was dinner in the evening. There was a beautiful three tiered cake, all decorated in gold. The dining room was decorated

with gold and white streamers. The large dining room table could seat 11 or 12. The children sat at the table in the corner and as you can see in the photo the flashlight did not penetrate that far.

Earlier in the day the 10-12 year old boys were demonstrating their balancing prowess by walking along the edge of the manger in the stable, which was long enough to feed about 15 horses. They were all performing well until something went wrong, and Doug (Uncle Albert's son), slipped and as he fell got caught up in a piece of wire, which left a gaping hole in his leg, just behind his knee. The Hawker hospital had been opened only a few days earlier, and so Doug was rushed in there to receive stitches, and was back again just as the meal was about to begin.

### PAGE 128
As soon as the Golden Wedding was over John and Caroline packed up their belongings in preparation for the move to their new home in Clare.

Dad drove them to Carrieton in our Model T Ford and they travelled by train to Farrel's Flat, where Albert met them in his Model T and drove them to Clare where they took up residence in Mill Street.

Those of us who remained on the farm, never forgot the heart break suffered by grandfather's poor old dog, Carlo, who stood around forlornly watching as their belongings were packed into the car and then they got in the car and were driven away. With his head and tail down he mooched back into the verandah and laid down by their door, and from then on to the day he died, about the only time he ever left there was when the car drove into the yard. He'd go out and sniff around all the people who got out, in the vain hope of finding his master and then wander back to continue his lonely vigil. Being fairly old he didn't live very long after that anyhow.

The cottage in Mill Street, Clare, was small and unpretentious and was situated near the top of a hill on which the Presbyterian Church stood, so once again John and Caroline changed their denomination and became Presbyterians and worshipped there regularly.

During the drought years of 1928-30, Dad and the team had to go off again in search of feed for the horses and money for family needs, but this time he took the

John and Caroline's retirement cottage in Mill St. Clare.

John's family taken 1949 in Geelong, Vic. L. to R:- Albert, Walter, Ernest, Bertha, Eddie, Charles.

whole family and moved to Clare with Uncle Albert's help. We eventually rented a farm at Hilltown, 10 miles north of Clare, and lived there during 1929-30, so enabling us to see more of our grandparents again for a while.

In mid 1929 grandma became ill and had to be confined to bed, so her three daughters-in-law took turns at staying in Clare to nurse her and grandpa.

Uncle Charlie and family had moved over to live at Saddleworth sometime before this, so there was his wife, Auntie Amy, Uncle Albert's wife, Auntie Beattie, and Mum.

Grandfather, aged 82 years was suffering from loss of memory, and he was very desolate, as he had got it into his head that grandma was away somewhere. He used to wander in and enquire, "Have you heard how mother is lately?" He was sleeping in a back room and didn't remember she was in the front one. Eventually he contracted bronchitis and was sent to hospital. Grandma was gravely ill, and worried that she would have to leave him alone as she knew her end was near. As it happened grandpa had a heart attack and died before her, on the 23rd August, 1929. The family felt she should be told — she looked relieved, settled back and died within 24 hours of his death, on 24th August.

### PAGE 129
They were both buried in a double funeral in the Clare Cemetery on the following Sunday afternoon.

We all felt how lovely that having been together for 55 years, they continued their journey together to the Promised Land.

Family picnic at Christesen Park, Clare in 1929. Standing:- Frank, Walter, May, Bert, Gladys, Eddie, Albert, Charlie, Sylvia, Doris, Joyce, Melville, Jean. 2nd row:- Reg, Hope, Nellie, Beattie, Amy, Murray, Millie, Doug. Front:- Ralph, Bill, Eddie, Keith.

## BERTHA TELFER

Adopted daughter of John and Caroline Telfer

Bertha grew up with the family at Angle Farm. In 1903 when John Telfer bought Warcowie homestead, she was approximately 25 years old, and moved up there to housekeep for Albert, who was managing that section of the property. She was to stay there only a few years.

References to Miss Bertha Telfer have been taken from the book "Amongst Australian Aborigines" by Rev. E.J. Telfer, her brother.

In 1906 at the Burnt Bridge Mission on the Mcleay River, addresses were given by Miss Telfer, Mrs Rudder and Mrs Ingram.

Writing from Yorktown, South Australia in 1907, Bertha wrote a postcard to her mother at Angle Farm, in which she said, "I found Lou all alone and very busy and if I can, shall stay a little while and help her. I stayed at Aunt Emma's on Sunday." Aunt Emma was her mother's younger sister.

Bertha studied at the Rev Lockhart Morton's College in Adelaide, in preparation for missionary service. She was accepted by the United Aborigines Mission in December 1908.

The United Aborigines Mission was set up by the Christian Endeavour movement a few years earlier, and may

### PAGE 130
explain how Bertha and her brother Ernest, heard of, and became involved with the Mission.

After her acceptance by the U.A. Mission, she was sent to the old Burnt Bridge station on the Mcleay River. In a postcard, postmarked Kempsey, 1909 she wrote:-

Dear Ones at Home,

This is just to let you know I am quite well, but very busy. I am a school teacher as well as Missionary, and have such a lot to do just now, will write as soon as I can. I hope you are all well, have not heard from home for a very long time. How are the crops & sheep & cows etc. how is E. and the bullocks. I am going to Sydney in few days D.V. to Conference ... Love to Albert & Beattie & all at home from

Yours lovingly B. Telfer

Her brother Ern had done the ground work for setting up in Perth of an orphanage for aboriginal children, and in 1912 Bertha was appointed to the home, Dulhi Gunga in W.A.

In his book, Ern devotes several pages almost exclusively to reports from "Miss Bertha Telfer, writing from La Grange Bay, beyond Broome, North-west Australia, told in thrilling language of her work in that remote district".

Police had told her of crowds of natives at La Grange Bay and of their need, "so I had determined to come and see for myself". It took three days in a tiny sailing schooner to get from Broome to La Grange Bay — everyone on board

Bertha saw many Aborigines in chains.

Bertha Telfer in her Mission Sisters uniform, about 1912, at Dulhi Gunga Childrens Home, Perth.

intoxicated, except her. Another 12 miles from the Bay to La Grange itself took six hours. There were only four other white people there and she was not very welcome.

"I am the first missionary that has ever been in this area, and looked upon as a great curiosity." She was appalled at the condition of aborigines in prison — leg irons and chains etc. "I have listened to heart breaking stories of cruelty perpetrated upon some of the dark people." She took her little organ into the prison yard on Sunday, visited every cell and at the end was asked for more music, more singing and talking.

One white man permitted her to camp on his property, provided it was not near his well. She persuaded the natives to help build a temporary Mission house. "I wish you could have seen the natives building, under my directions, the first house they had ever built." "We have a Post Office but only two mails a year, —

### PAGE 131
Bertha Alcorn, (nee Telfer).

James Alcorn.

November and May." "I wish you could see my house. It is nice and cool, built of boughs, and guaranteed not to keep out the rain."

Her report ends, acknowledging the needs of the natives and that as far as the whites went, she was only there on sufferance, "but I know if God wants me here He will overcome all opposition and supply all needs. This is the hardest position I have ever been in, but His grace is sufficient, I know."

Bertha was a tiny woman, but she had enormous courage, and went to many places where a white woman had never been before. She loved the Aboriginal people very dearly, was full of fun and had a simple faith to sustain her when there was danger or difficulty. She would "just say a little prayer and the dear Lord will look after me".

At some stage we know that she was at Sunday Island in the Buccaneer Archipelago, about 150 miles north of

Broome. About 1915-17 while in that area, she met and married James Alcorn, who was an official with the Broome pearl fisheries. Their only child was a son, Frank.

They returned to South Australia and spent 12 months or so at Willow Plains and Warcowie about the early 1920's and then moved to New Guinea, where Jim had a government job for a while. At one stage he was engaged in recruiting native labour for the plantations.

They came back and lived at Clare about 1926-27. Jim was by then a very sick man. They lived at Clare for some time before moving to Norwood where they had a small shop.

After Jim's death, Bertha and Frank moved to Edwardstown, another suburb. In due course, Frank married Beryl and a granny flat was built on the property and Bertha lived there until she became too feeble to manage there. The remaining years of Bertha's life were spent in the

### PAGE 132
Bertha and her brother, Walter, 1963.

Serene Nursing Home at Lockleys, and she died there on 10th August, 1965, aged 93 years. She was buried in the West Terrace Cemetery.

Frank and Beryl have two children, Jonathon and Jane, both married.

Jonathon and family live on an 80 acre block of land, near Nuriootpa, called Glen Turrett, which was originally owned by Pultney Malcolm Murray. They have 5 sons, and Jon divides his time between teaching 3 days a week, and farming the rest of the time.

Jane is married and lives with her husband in Canada, as he finds it easier to work there, than in Australia. They have 5 children, four boys and one girl.

## FRANCIS CHARLES TELFER

First child of John and Caroline Telfer

A contribution by May Fatchen. (Eldest daughter of Charles and Amy.)

Father was Francis Charles (Charley). He lived with his family at Hawker, and as a young man drove his horses and wagon down to Eyre Peninsula where he took up land from the government in the Hundred of Stokes (Ungarra). The whole 2000 acres was covered in mallee scrub and father had to cut his way into it with an axe. He tethered his horses and set up camp.

He married Amy Provis who was a wonderful hard working partner and together they set to clearing the land bit by bit. They built a small four roomed house with galvanized iron, and lined with hessian and rubberoid on the dirt floors. I was born here, without the aid of a doctor.

The land was cleared by using a "scrub roller", which was a heavy log maybe a foot or more in diameter and 8-10 feet long with chains around it to which the horses were hitched, and it was dragged through the scrub. The scrub was knocked down and when dry enough, was burnt. Those

Charles and Amy in 1939.

terrible bush fires were very frightening if not controlled properly.

The land was then ploughed and stumps picked up and some grew shoots again in the sparse crops and had to be slashed off. Our school was $3 \frac{1}{2}$ miles away and well I remember walking there and back every day, and after school having to take hay from the hay-stack

### PAGE 133
and carry it to the chaff cutter. After tea, wet and cold or fine, we had to go out by lantern light and hand the hay up to father on the platform to feed into the cutter. The prickles and mice it contained was not funny! Every night we had to bag up 10-12 bags of chaff for father to put in the mangers to feed the horses. Besides this we children also had to go around the sheep, milk cows, turn and wash the milk separator and pump water by hand for the garden.

Our poor mother had no conveniences and a baby every couple of years. She had to bake all our bread made with homemade yeast of hops, potatoes, flour and water. She also made butter and had to drive 18 miles into Tumby Bay in a sulky with one horse, with it, before the heat of the day. With the money she bought what groceries she could.

No refrigerators — food was kept cool in a fireplace or draught with a wet cloth to cover it. No electricity — wood stoves, kerosene lamps and candles. No washing machines — mother had a copper which had to be stoked up with wood, two tubs, and a box iron, full of hot coals for ironing the clothes. A scrubbing board on which she had to scrub all our dirty clothes with homemade soap. No water laid on — she had to carry water often, from the sheds for all the household needs in 4 gallon kerosene tin buckets. Aborigines occasionally called in begging for tea, sugar, etc.

There was no entertainment as such in those days, but plenty of hard work and just enough rest to keep us going. We had a buggy and two horses to take us to Church on Sundays, and this we never missed, unless the creeks flooded.

There were ten of us in our family, Frank (deceased), May, Millie, Jean, Gladys, Doris, Eddie, Reg, Murray and Ruth. Brother Reg still owns and works the "Old Scrub Farm", which has produced many good crops over the last 80 years. I thank God for hard times and good and for great parents, brothers and sisters, all hard working and good Christian citizens.

Daughters of Charles and Amy in 1939. Millie, Ruth, May, their mother, Doris, Gladys, and Jean.

Charles and Amy's sons in 1936. Frank, Reg, Murray, and Eddie.

## SOME CHILDHOOD
MEMORIES

by Jean McMutrie

When we were children, we lived at Ungarra and life was pretty demanding, especially on our wonderful mother.

We lived in a four-roomed wood and iron house with clay floors covered with

### PAGE 134
black rubberoid; there was no wire door or fly screen. We used cheesecloth on the windows which were only about 18 inches square. We also hung cut-down wheat bags at the doors especially in the summer to help keep out flies and the heat. There was no electricity of course, no refrigeration or conveniences of any sort. Our laundry was a copper out in the yard, and two tubs on a wooden stand with a hand-wringer, a wash-board and homemade soap.

Bath night was always Saturday. We boiled the water in the copper and carried the hot water into the kitchen where we had placed the wash tubs. Every Saturday all shoes (when we had them) needed to be cleaned, but dad's shoes always had to be cleaned for him ready for Sunday.

We always had to work pretty hard helping to pick stones and stumps, heaping up and burning bushes, chasing sheep and cows, and milking, feeding calves and pigs. During the harvest period there was the hay to stock and then to cart in for the hay stacks, and then there was chaff-cutting and feeding stock. We also had to winnow the grain and keep the cocky chaff for feed. In order to make the chaff palatable for stockfeed we used molasses.

We also had a vegetable garden down at the dam, and we grew beans, peas, cape gooseberries, carrots, cabbage, cauliflowers and silverbeet. Dad had his own garden of fruit trees and vines from which all our jams and preserves and dried fruit were made, and some of this produce we were able to sell. The summertime was hot and dusty, but mother would wrap butter, milk, jellies, etc. in wet cloths and put them into the big fireplace, where the draught would help keep them cool. The wood stove always had to be kept going in the kitchen whatever the temperature in order to cope with the constant preparation of food, baking of bread, buns, cakes, meals etc.

If we were sick or too young to work in the heat the coolest place was under the beds where we would crawl to have a

sleep. We were 16 miles from a doctor, and only horse and sulky and buggy, so mum was mainly our doctor as well as everything else.

The winter time was also tough, often we had no shoes to wear, but would have to get the cows in the paddock, and the ice would be crackling under our feet, and wherever we went our foot prints would show on the frosty grass. In the mornings after milking, separating, feeding calves and pigs and fowls we would often have to walk $3 \frac{1}{2}$ miles to school. Sometimes we had a horse and cart, and then the horse would have to be harnessed and a nose-bag prepared. We had three creeks to cross during our journey to school, and I remember the risks we took when those creeks were in flood. Sometimes if we were walking mother would have to harness a horse and come to get us in the sulky, and the flood-water would be coming into the sulky.

We had many severe thunderstorms in those days, and I remember one day when we were driving home from school the lightning was jumping along the horse's back, we were scared, and got out of the cart (except Frank). We preferred to walk because we had been taught that horses attracted lightning. We also kept away from big trees and fences for fear of being struck by lightning.

In the summertime there would be many snakes, quite often stretched right across the track. We would either have to step over them or find a stick and kill them. Talking of snakes reminds me of the day we were chaffing hay and a snake came out and went into the engine room, and because we felt threatened we went and got a saucer of milk and my sister May who was a very good mouth-organist played away till the snake emerged and lifted its head swaying to the rhythm of the music, and when it went to the saucer of milk we had a chance to kill it.

There were lovely native flowers and orchids for us to enjoy, and we loved to pick and eat the cranberries and nanberries, also wild peaches (quandongs) were a favourite. These

### PAGE 135
mainly grew close to the creek, and we took big risks to get these peaches which were lovely for pies. In early winter we were often able to pick big buckets of lovely mushrooms for our meals.

There was, of course, no permanent water supply, so when mother had to use the dam water for us, she would boil the water with egg shells and then strain it. I remember we sometimes needed to "cart" water with the dray and two horses all the way from Stokes. We would get the water from soaks in the ground, and when that was brackish we would have to soften it with "water glass" used for preserving eggs.

The church and school was a wood and iron building with church pews one side, and school desks on the other, and a raised platform at the front. We had about 40 scholars at school, with Grades 1-7 and just one teacher. The building was very hot in summer and very cold in winter. The ants would almost always invade our lunch boxes and often we had to pick all the ants off before we would eat our sandwiches. Very occasionally we would be given a penny to buy a bush biscuit, and that was a real treat. The only money we ever saw was a penny we were given for our Sunday School offering or the penny or threepence we would win at the school or Sunday School picnics. Father used to promise a penny for 100 grubs we would catch in his fruit garden, but instead he would buy us a new ribbon for our hair.

For church and Sunday School we would all wear white embroidered dresses with ribbon sashes. We five girls often wonder how mother managed with only one box iron. In those days we always had church and then Sunday School afterwards, and it would be late before we got home, and mother would see we had a bag of yeast buns to eat on the way home "to keep the wolf away" she used to say. Mother was always home looking after babies and working.

Christmas time we would select a lovely shaped mallee tree, bring it in and set it up in the dining room. Next morning

there were always lots of little gifts for us all, and much excitement. On Christmas morning we would get up early, and get all cleaned up, and then we would be given our gifts and a lovely dinner and tea. Usually we had poultry which was only eaten at Christmas as a special treat. One Christmas we all had whooping cough which was pretty miserable for everyone.

Our neighbour who lived 3 miles away came in one day to say they had the measles, and because mother couldn't think of anything worse than all of us having measles in that little house we were all sent out to the paddocks while mother burnt sulphur in the house to fumigate it. We were very upset with our neighbour, as it was a horrible smell to live with, but nobody got the measles!

In 1927 dad sold the farm and we shifted to Clare, going by car which took us two days in our Overland car. My eldest brother Frank had had an accident and had TB on the hip so had to have specialist treatment. My grandparents lived in Clare. We only lived there a few months when dad bought a farm at Saddleworth, and we shifted to a farm there. It was the depression years and things were very difficult, we would only receive four pence for a pound of butter, and three pence for a dozen eggs. Many men were carrying their swags and billy, walking the countryside looking for work. They would offer to chop wood or other chores in return for some food. If they considered your house was "good" they would leave secret signs on the road for their mates.

Our youngest sister Ruth was born while we were at Saddleworth, mother was then 48, and that was very hard for her, but Ruth being so much younger than the rest of us has been a real blessing to us all.

Millie had an accident and had to be in bed for nine months on a hard bed. The doctor from Auburn would come in once a week to see her. My eldest sister May, was married there in 1935, and in 1936 the farm at Ungarra had to be repossessed, so

### PAGE 136
most of the family shifted back there. Frank and I stayed at Saddleworth. May was married and Millie was school teaching, but the rest went back to Ungarra and that made dad very happy. A new house had been built there, and the family were more comfortable, but still there was very hard work for everyone.

A Gladys Pedler contribution (4th daughter of Charlie and Amy)

My mother and father were Amy and Charlie Telfer who lived on a mallee farm on Eyre Peninsula, South Australia, near a small township called Ungarra. I was the fourth daughter of a family of ten children. The adjoining mixed farm belonged to Tom and Annie Pedler with a family of 4 boys and the one girl who died at the age of 14 years.

On 17th September, 1937, I married our neighbour's eldest son Leslie, and we also lived in a house in Ungarra for about 15 months. During that time Les worked hard on his father's property, but after expenses were paid and the profits divided up between his parents and 3 brothers we had not enough to pay our very meagre grocery bills. A friend told Les about the Darling Downs in Queensland and rolling black soil plains. No rabbits to trap, no scrub to clear, no hills with stones to wear out the machinery, just soft, rich, level, black soil to farm. It sounded like a grain grower's dream, so he decided to take a trip and see it.

Our first baby boy Stanley James, was only a few weeks old so he and I stayed at Manoora with my sister May and her husband Verbin Fatchen, while Les, his parents, and brother and wife, drove up to Toowoomba, Qld. in his father's car. A land agent took them 45 miles out to the Bongeen Plains as there was a big sheep station being surveyed into 600 and 800 acre blocks for sale. They found it was all they had been told it was and was selling at £3-15-0 ($7.50) per acre. This beautiful Jondaryan Estate was almost all to be sold. We could only beg and borrow enough money to buy 640 acres.

On Boxing Day 1938 we left Ungarra, our home and families to travel to this little known about (in S.A. anyway) State of Queensland. Les and I packed our few personal belongings in two large boxes made to fit on the back of an old Chev. truck, with wooden spokes in the wheels. Some friends from Ungarra, Mr Bob Jericho and his son Garfield, who also wanted to see this lovely country, decided to bring the little T20 tractor up on their truck for us, so we travelled together, not knowing what was before us or how long it would take. Les' brother Harold and Uncle also travelled on the two trucks with us. Uncle Fred sitting up in the tractor seat, out in the open. We brought drums of petrol with us for emergencies. No diesel trucks then, and towns were far apart.

The trip was slow and one of western New South Wales worst heat waves for January. No radios like today. Our route was to dodge the mountains so we went through Broken Hill to Balranald, where we arrived on New Year's Day 1939. Only a few stray goats roamed the streets, so we pushed on, out into the unknown. Little did we know how hot it could get in that heat wave.

Stanley was now only 5 months old and I was a young inexperienced mother for such conditions. We had to stay at a boundary rider's small home, 50 miles from Cobar as Stan had collapsed from the heat. Mrs Brown was so kind, I have never forgotten how she helped and cared for us all that night, although I had little sleep as all night I kept making sure Stan, my baby, was still breathing.

At 5 o'clock next morning, we left there for Cobar before it got too hot, but when we arrived at 7 am my baby had collapsed again. The temperature at 7 o'clock that morning was 117F., so we were forced to stay there all day at a hotel where I could keep sponging him with cool muddy water to keep him alive. The men camped in the bush, with the trucks, and they were also exhausted from the extreme heat. In those days we were supposed to have a permit to cross from one state into

### PAGE 137
another, but ours hadn't arrived from Adelaide when we had to leave. Fortunately the Police were kind and understanding as we were moving on again in the cool of the evening and made for the New England area and the cooler mountain air. We travelled all night to get out of the heat.

After just over a week of travelling, we arrived in Toowoomba in January 1939. The first night was spent at the Imperial Hotel, now converted to a Roman Catholic Shrine in the heart of the city. Next day we went out to the property Les had agreed by telegram for the agent to secure for us. It was just an oblong, flat, block of grassy, plain country, with the only fence being along the dirt road. Pitching our tents, we slept the first night on what was to become our own property. Next day we went to Mount Tyson township, 10 miles away, and found an unfurnished wooden house to rent for myself and our son while Les camped out on the farm for 6 days and nights, ploughing and preparing the ground ready to plant our first crop of wheat.

We also learnt it was impossible to drive on the black soil roads when wet and often Les would have to walk in for food and wait for the ground to dry enough to work again. He found the little T20 tractor was not strong enough to pull the disc plough to break up the virgin grassy soil. He got a stronger tractor and worked 6 days and 3 nights each week, coming home each weekend for food and

Harvest in Queensland. Les Pedlar in centre.

sleep. He worked more like 110 hours a week, not 40 or 38 hours like today.

In August 1939, after we had planted our first 400 acres of wheat, we bought an old Chev. 4 car (no side curtains) and Les, baby and I set off for home, (Ungarra) as my mother was very ill. However she soon started to improve in health when she realized she hadn't completely lost one of her six daughters. We stayed there 5-6 weeks and then drove back to our Qld. farm.

On 21st March, 1940, our baby girl was born and we named her Mary Ellen. Les had built a barn on the farm, which we were now living in, just four walls, a floor and roof, no lining or ceiling or windows, except a sliding shutter. We managed to get some congeleum (a type of linoleum) at 2/6 a yard, which covered the cracks in the floor, and sewed wheat bags together for a partition between kitchen and bedroom. After we harvested our next crop we were able to build a four roomed house with front and back verandahs.

Our second daughter arrived on 11th September, 1942, and we named her Shirley Irene. On 20th December, 1946, our next baby boy, Allan Lawrence, was born.

The black soil was wonderful to grow all kinds of crops, both summer and winter, without any fertilizer, but the dirt roads became impassable in the wet — no bitumen roads like there are today. When the children reached school age we shifted into Pittsworth, about 20 miles from the farm, for about two years, but shifted back onto the farm, as a school started at Bongeen siding (7 miles from the farm).

As the children got older we shifted into Toowoomba to live and Les drove out to the farm every week (45 miles), coming home at weekends. Then in 1956 Les took seriously ill with cancer. He sold the Bongeen farm and bought a small property only 8 miles out of Toowoomba. His health however, deteriorated fast and on 23rd December, 1956, he went to be with his Lord. My family and I lived on in

### PAGE 138
Toowoomba till one by one they married, so now live here alone.

I am still involved in the Swipe business, but hope to retire in 1988. I keep busy with Church activities as well as business and like to go to Brisbane to visit my family there. It's an 80 mile drive over part of the Great Dividing Range. I wish more of my folk living in S.A. could visit this lovely State, I have empty beds here waiting for you to visit.

SHIRLEY IRENE was born in 1942 and married Gordon Rylance on 9th November, 1974. They live in Brisbane where Gordon is a sales manager. They have 2 children:-

Brett Kenneth John — born on 10th May, 1976.

Julie Evelyn — born on 19th July, 1978.

ALLAN LAWRENCE was born in 1946 and married Lynette Large on

Gladys Pedlar and family, 1986. Adults standing:- Allan Pedlar, Shirley Ryland, (nee Pedlar), Gladys, Mary Hawkins (nee Pedlar), and Stan Pedlar. Children:- Julie and Brett Ryland.

13th November, 1976. Allan is involved in the Taxi business in Brisbane where they live. They have 3 children:-

Shane Daniel — born 13th August, 1979.

Kirrilee Skye — born 27th March, 1981.

Lawrence Leslie David — born 29th March, 1987.

Written by Edwin Roy Telfer — June 1987

My father, Francis Charles Telfer, travelled from the family home at Willow Plains near Hawker, S.A. to settle on a property near Ungarra, Eyre Peninsula, in the year 1906.

He travelled through bush tracks with horses and waggon loaded with necessities for the journey and when he arrived one of the first tasks was to dig a small dam with a pick and shovel to try and catch water for his horses, for although a stream flowed through the property it was very salty water.

The future plan was to clear and develop this land into a productive property. "Just to own his own farm" inspired many a pioneer to tackle years of hard work and poor standards of living.

### PAGE 139
I recall him saying that from the top of the hill in the centre of the property he could see for miles in all directions and he could see only one small area rolled for clearing, the remainder was covered with dense growth of mallee and broom scrub.

This scrub had to be flattened by rolling, using a horse team, offset to walk amongst the flattened scrub, the roller being made from a disused railway boiler and often a wooden frame made from gum rail available from gum trees growing in the Stokes area, a few miles south. Broom was easier to clear but poorer soil than the Mallee which after burning persisted to grow back and the wheat and oat crops grown were infested with shoots which had to be cut by hand so that the strippers could harvest them. On every plowing the mallee land yielded a thick crop of uprooted stumps and stump picking by hand into tip drays was the "in" job for many years after the mallee was killed and thousands of dray loads were tipped and burned.

The stump jump plough had been invented by Richard and Clarence Smith of Arthurton on Yorke Peninsula in 1876. This used a series of couplings and swings to enable the pull of the horses to hold the jumps into the ground. This allowed the jumps (tynes) to rise over a solid obstacle. However "Charlie" Telfer found it rode out of the ground or did not penetrate well on the down hill and also jarred the horses shoulders badly in the very stumpy land.

This inspired him to find a better method and with financial help from a friend, Mr. Eagle, the "Telfer Eagle" patent began. This invention diverted the pressure needed on the jumps of the plough to a heavy compression spring, the amount of pressure being adjustable with a lever within reach of the operator. It is worth noting that soon the use of springs replaced the so called bridle draft and although now individual springs are on every tyne the Telfer Eagle patent was the start of the use of springs for this purpose, at least in South Australia.

Charlie married Amy Ellen Provis on 6th April, 1909. When I arrived on the scene in August 1919, there were already a brother and five sisters to greet me. Frank was the eldest, followed by May, Millie, Jean, Gladys and Doris.

Was I just "1 more" or a bit special being a boy after five girls? I'm afraid I don't remember about that, but I will try and tell you some of the things I do remember. By the time I was old enough to store some memories I had two younger brothers, Reginald and Murray. I remember the home of corrugated iron lined mainly with hessian and this also was used for at least some of the division walls. Transport was by horses and cart or buggy and a pair of horses, and I even remember the horses names — they were "Chibby and Bessie". I recall the harvesting on the poorer and more recently developed areas, a stripper was used which only beat off the heads and threw chaff and wheat into a box to be raked out into a heap, to be cleaned later, and a ground driver harvester was the latest in harvesting.

Back to the home — Can you imagine — no electricity, no radio, no telephone, no motor vehicles, and wildest imagination would not have thought of refrigeration, let alone television. Kerosene lamps, lanterns and candles provided the lighting and the mallee stumps cooking and heating fuel. The copper used for washing, heating water for bathing (in a tub) and making soap etc., was located a short distance from the house. The stand and fire box were built of stone with lime mortar, and the copper built in. Water had to be carried in buckets, often from a tank at the shed some distance away.

Our mother was a wonderful person to raise such a large family under such conditions. It was real pioneering, but she always had a real love and concern for every one of us. I remember the huge batches of bread she made and of seeing her kneading the dough and baking it in the oven in a kitchen almost hot enough to cook anyone. If meat had to be kept it was pickled in brine (a salt solution) and

### PAGE 140
stored in buckets made from 4 gallon kerosene tins.

On one occasion my brothers managed to get hold of a pair of scissors and tried a little experiment. Murray the youngest boy held out his finger and Reg tried to cut it off. The buggy and pair were harnessed in a hurry and off for the 17 miles of rough bush track to the only doctor, at Tumby Bay. Mum went too, to try and comfort the baby boy and most important to stop the bleeding.

Sunday memories are of us dressing up in our best clothes and father and children packed into the buggy and Chibby and Bessie jogged us along the bush track to Ungarra to attend Church and Sunday School at the Church of Christ.
