Mother seldom came as her time was so filled with the endless needs of the family and she was often very tired and suffered with her legs a great deal.

By the time I started school at age 6 years we had a horse and cart to travel the 3 miles to Ungarra School. With four older sisters it meant Doris and I had to sit on the floor in the back of the cart. We didn't like that very much but we were the youngest, so...! School at Ungarra was a one teacher school — about 40 children, and I remember most of the families and many of the children. Most of the families travelled to school by horse and spring cart and the horses were unharnessed and tethered to a mallee and given a nose bag or box of chaff. Accidents happened and when one cart loaded with children capsized, one little girl who was under the cart, but fortunately not hurt, told her mother, "I shut my eyes because I thought I was dead".

About the time I started school father bought a motor car — an Overland Tourer, and somewhere around this time the horse team was supported by a Fordson tractor, which was not a very successful machine. Some amusing stories about the new fangled cars. A neighbour, who got going in his, and didn't know how to stop it drove around until it ran out of petrol. One man drove into his shed shouting "Whoa, Whoa",

but it was the crash that stopped it. Father seemed to manage all right with driving. Brother Frank had the misfortune to badly injure his hip through a fall from a horse. This caused a great deal of pain and trouble and resulted in a limp which he had until his death from cancer on 1st March, 1953.

After my first year at school, we left Ungarra (about 1926) and moved to Clare and then to a farm 3 miles from Saddleworth. It was here that I had the rest of my 7 years of schooling. I didn't like school and looked for the time I could work home on the farm. We walked, ran or cadged a ride to school — no thought of danger, we would accept a ride with anyone. Usually hurried to school to keep out of trouble with a very strict teacher, and then loitered and played on our way home. One night we three boys played sliding down the paved sides of some road culverts, putting sand on them to help us slide. We arrived home with the seats of our school shorts worn out, and were sent to bed without any tea, a punishment by big sister who was in charge of us that time. Please don't tell, but one of us sneaked down and nicked a big piece of sultana cake which we shared with quiet glee.

The home at Saddleworth was a roomy stone building with a long passage. We even had electric lights, but no power points or other electrical use.

The depression of the 1930's resulted in lots of "swaggies" tramping the roads and with our house close to the road they were often calling and asking for a feed from my already overworked mother.

A McCormack Deering 15-30 tractor with steel wheels was the farming power. Father still had a car, but used it less because of the depressed economy. Our other means of travelling was with a horse and spring cart. One evening sister Gladys and I set out in this vehicle to attend a meeting at the Baptist Church in Saddleworth. We had a hurricane lantern tied on the cart for a light and the night was dark. The road was metal and rough on the horse and cart and passengers. We

### PAGE 141
let the horse take it easy up the first hill, but on the down hill run there was a side track over on the natural ground and was softer and smoother, so we thought! We moved onto this and stirred Trixie into a brisk trot down the hill. We didn't know and couldn't see that a wagon load of stone had been dumped in the middle of the track. Well, Trixie saw it and swerved around it, but didn't allow for the cart wheel which ran on top of the stones. We had a surprise contact with mother earth and the cart and horse were both on their side. The only injury was one smashed up lantern and after help from a neighbour we were able to drive slowly home.

Dr. Finney came from Auburn twice a week and consulted at Saddleworth and as I had a lot of ear trouble I often had permission to go from school for treatment. One day he took me by complete surprise by saying "What do you think of having another sister?" Innocent Eddie must have looked amazed, as he quickly changed it to "How many sisters have you got?" It must have sunk in a little as I replied, "Five, so far". Well, when I arrived home I wanted to know, and found Pamela Ruth was born in Auburn Hospital on 26th October, 1931.

One result of the financial crisis was that after five years at Saddleworth we returned to father's original Ungarra farm — about 1933. Costs of farming needed to be cut to a minimum and to try to save on kerosene for the tractor we had a gas producer fitted. We burned mallee stumps in a pit and when they were coals smothered the fire by dropping sheets of iron across and shovelling soil on to seal it and make charcoal for the gas producer. Although quite a few were fitted to tractors they proved a poor substitute and were soon discarded.

Meanwhile Frank, with Millie, Jean and Gladys, in turn had been on a property near Coomandook and were using a team of horses. When this farm was sold the horses were brought over to Ungarra. There was a massive return to horse teams at this time and it was all too

evident that those farmers who had kept their horses throughout were the best off with good horses they had been breeding.

I remember a yard in Ungarra with a number of tractors, mostly a make called "Hart Parr" reclaimed by the distributors, from farmers unable to pay the balance. Our McCormack became the power used to chaff the oaten hay for the horse team. We soon began breeding our own horses, after having experience with wild horses that were rounded up and sold from Sydney Kidman's station in the Far North. We soon became quite attached to our horses, especially those we bred and broke into work. We eventually had up to about 30 horses on the farm, working in teams of usually 12 horses but sometimes 14 or 16 in one team. This involved a lot of work growing sheaf hay, carting and chaffing it and when teams were being worked we would feed up at about 5.30 a.m., harness and start work as soon as daylight made it possible, give them a nose bag of chaff around midday, harness off again about sundown, feed up with chaff, have our tea, and with the help of a hurricane lantern feed up again about 10 p.m. Feeding involved bagging chaff and tipping it into the long manger.

While Reg and Murray were going to Ungarra to school they milked cows by hand morning and night and rode, two of them on one pushbike the 3 miles to school. The Nash car had been made into a "buckboard", as they were called, by removing the back half and making and fitting a $6' \times 6'$ truck tray. Now those who couldn't pack into the front sat on bags of chaff on the back.

Our family shield has the words "Tela Fero", the explanation being "Workers in iron" and there seems to be that inclination in some of our family. Father had had some training in blacksmithing and was rather fond of working with forges and anvil. I found this very interesting and he taught me to make things and to weld steel using a coal fire with bellows and anvil and hammer to temper steel tools and to shoe horses. Years later I invented and patented the

### PAGE 142
"Telfer patent stone rower". Our son David continues this trend as owner and manager of Telfer Engineering in Bordertown, S.A. with a staff of four men is kept busy manufacturing and doing repair work.

Tractors came back with the success of diesel power and rubber tyres in the 1940's and horse teams almost disappeared. The Ungarra property developed into an excellent farm with a good rainfall and is now owned by Reg and his sons, Malvern and David.

At this time of writing, June 1987, our family are still all living except Frank. May married Verbin Fatchen, Millie married Fred Barton, Jean married Eric McMurtrie, Gladys married Les Pedler, Doris (Doll) married Albert Rowe, Ruth married Des Nicholson. Verbin is the only husband living as all of my other sisters are now widows.

Our families are predominantly of the Christian faith, which is very meaningful to those who have accepted the Bible as the Word of God and a real source of strength, peace and joy.

In 1942 I married Lola Patterson and went farming in the Butler Tanks area between Ungarra and Port Neil. Frank was in partnership with me for the first 3 years then he purchased a property in the

hills west of Ungarra. After a period of leasing the Butler property we were able to purchase this farm and remained there until 1961 although the rainfall here was much less reliable than even 15 miles away at Ungarra. We moved to the South East in 1961 having purchased a large partly developed grazing property west of Penola. After 3 years we found a change necessary for health reasons and were able to obtain a smaller well improved farm near Mundulla in the Tatiara. Tatiara means "Good country" and it is just that.

Our family, three married daughters who were all born at Tumby Bay are:-

Judith and Graham Lawrie and children, Stephen, Roxanne, Carolyn and Bronwyn.

Miriam and Tony Biddle and children, Donna and Justen.

Joy and John Commane and children, Matthew, Adrian, Lincoln, Kym and Emma Louise.

David our son, who was born in Penola in 1961 and has his own home and business in Bordertown.

After battling with cancer for 13 years Lola passed from this life in 1970. After 6 years as a widower I married Kaye Copping in 1976 and we are living on the Mundulla property. In 1977 I had a heart

Family of Francis
Charles Telfer, taken
at Telfer Family
Reunion at Hawker,
1978. Shirley and
Murray, Eddie and
Kaye, May and Verbin,
Gladys, Jean, Elma
and Reg.

### PAGE 143
Eddie in front of his property at Mundalla. With him is Michelle Bassett, granddaughter of his sister May Fatchen.

valve replacement followed by a very severe heart infection, causing severe internal haemorrhaging, the doctors at the Royal Adelaide Hospital completely gave up all hope of recovery and I can only say that it was an answer to the prayers of many people and the determination of a very courageous wife that I am here today. It was a very real experience of the nearness of the loving Lord and Saviour which could only strengthen our faith and trust in Him.

My family and their children and Kaye's sisters and their families just love to come and spend time on the farm with us and we thank God for all the experiences that have been our lives, as part of the Telfer family.

## JOHN ERNEST TELFER

second child of John and Caroline Telfer. (died in infancy)

## ERNEST JOHN TELFER

third child of John and Caroline Telfer

(Incidents as I remember hearing them and gleanings from his book "Amongst Australian Aborigines")

By his nephew, G.E. Telfer.

Ernest John Telfer, the third son born to John and Caroline, our grandparents, held the honour, largely unrecognized, of being the first baby born to farming settlers on Willow Plains.

It was no mansion that Ern was born in. A home of one galvanized iron room, and a tent. Nearby or attached was a stone chimney and fire place, with an open fire for cooking, heating water for the family wash, the bath or the cup of tea. This was most likely home to them for 12 months or more. Our mother recalled once some lady questioning grandma- "But Mrs Telfer how could you manage

for so long under those conditions?" Grandma: "Could you do it for one day?" "Yes." Grandma: "And for another day?" "Ye-es, I suppose so!" Grandma: "Then you could manage for a week, and another — just one day at a time."

Such was Ern's birth place. Of his early life not much has been passed on. On one occasion Uncle Watt remarked that "Scobie" (Ern) had "been the biggest larriken out". A neighbour's daughter, in retaliation to his teasing, placed the hot teapot against his arm resting on the table. Ern didn't flinch or bat an eye. Her attention was distracted for a few minutes till she again picked up the teapot with Ern's arm still against it, and a two inch blister sticking up half an inch. On one occasion while having a lone

### PAGE 144
lunch in the bush, he became irritated by the attentions of a blowfly. When the fly settled on a stone five yards away, he picked up the 12 gauge muzzleloaded shot gun and blazed away. Recounting the incident later he was disappointed that he couldn't find the fly to see where he had hit it.

Uncle Ern had a year's training at Rev. Lockhart Mortons College in Adelaide before going to New South Wales to work for the United Aboriginal Mission, run by the Christian Endeavour Movement.

Ern's book "Amongst Australian Aborigines — the story of the United Aborigines Mission", with a preface by Henry Bush and dated 1st May, 1939, offers brief glimpses of Uncle Ern in this work, where he refers to himself only occasionally, and then as "the writer". He refers to the voyage from Adelaide to Sydney in 1900 or early 1901, travelling steerage in the "Woogonga" was an experience to literally "bring out" all the good there was in a young missionary. It seems that his early work was at McLeay River Mission, with something of a

roving commission. He mentions a talk with "this native theologian, Frank Waddy" at Nambucca Heads.

It could be that the nomadic instinct of the natives, also permeated the missionaries. In April 1906, Ern was set apart to pioneer the mission work through scattered camps throughout the State of New South Wales. The mention of "we", occurs over much of the North and East quarter of N.S.W.

In late 1907 came "the call to go West", and the decision was made. With him went Peter Wandy, born near Coolgardie, W.A. where he got into mischief and went to N.S.W. where at La Perouse Mission he was "born again" and engaged in mission work in that state. En route to Western Australia they spent several months in S.A. based in Hawker with Ern's people, but travelling the State enlisting support for the W.A. venture. Wandy spent part of the time working on stations — earning sufficient money to buy a bicycle for the work in W.A.

They travelled by steamer to Albany, W.A. (before the East-West Railway days)

Ern Telfer and his native helper.

The Missionary and his travelling outfit.

### PAGE 145
Ernest's wife, Florence Telfer (nee Nicholas).

and "on landing our united wealth amounted to 2½d". Near Kattaning was a large camp of Aborigines, where some work was done. It seems a year or so may have been spent in and near Perth, setting up a W.A. State Council for the Mission, and a building was purchased for a Children's Home. "On 24th February, 1909 the writer was married to Miss Florence Nicholas of Boulder City, and the following day we commenced work at the Dulhi-Gunga Orphanage in East Perth." Later he mentioned travelling in-land to many districts "while Mrs Telfer remained in charge of the orphanage".

In May 1910, it was decided to travel overland to Derby, over 2000 miles, visiting large and small companies of Aborigines en route, his travelling companion was Mr E.V. Radford (Eustace Radford, his cousin). They travelled by steamer to Carnavon, and by bicycles thereafter. Understandably, Mrs. Telfer remained in Perth. They were warned that, if long dry stages or flooded rivers didn't get them, they could look forward to hostile natives near Derby. At

Carnavon, "we were not favourably impressed with the treatment of native prisoners who were compelled to work in chains, guarded by an armed trooper". Further on:- "Sandhills. Our biggest days journey, toiling from 4 a.m. to 8 p.m. was 12 miles." On thirst :- "One hot afternoon (when) we threw ourselves down in the shade of some bushes, we were horrified to see hundreds of crows drawing closer and closer to where we lay, some coming within a few feet, evidently anticipating a feast. We continued our journey followed by the cruel, carnivorous, crows." Some time later:- "Awakening about 4 a.m. we saw the beautiful sight of Halleys Comet, its brilliance beyond description." Seventy seven years later and 2000 miles to the south, its brilliance wasn't so impressive.

Between Port Headland and Broome, on one day they waded through five rivers, waist deep, carrying bicycles and gear. In one they were almost overwhelmed. "We were able to keep the camera dry and took some interesting photographs of each other in embarrassing circumstances." Throughout the journey, aborigines from small and large groups were contacted — the need for mission stations, schools, housing, etc assessed as far as possible under the language difficulties, and the suitability of various sites for the establishment of missions recommended. Derby, Sunday Island, and places in King Leopold Ranges were recommended as

One of Ern's Aboriginal Congregations.

### PAGE 146
important. They returned to Perth by steamer with their recommendations.

"In March 1911, the writer and Mrs. Telfer resigned from the missionary staff." "Shortly afterwards the writer entered the Baptist ministry and accepted the pastorate of a church in S.A." He accepted the pastorate of the Church at Terowie, S.A. from 1912 to 1916. At a later date held several charges in Tasmania and N.S.W., the last of which was at Waverley, N.S.W.

Of Uncle Ern's later life and ministry I have little knowledge. On the borders of my earliest memory, I believe he, with Aunty Florrie, and their children, John, Jessie, and Lloyd stayed with his parents at Angle Farm, near Hawker, for a while. I can only deduce it was during the 1916-17 harvest. Blistered hands, the result of a minister and a pitch fork in sustained contact, have been mentioned. Churches around Sydney were where he spent his later life.

In 1923 he came "home" again, alone, to his parents' Golden Wedding. Transport by then was revolutionised. Dad (Eddie) was to meet him at the railway station at Carrieton after dark, in the Model T Ford, and drive him the remaining 45 miles back to the farm. Uncle Ern, sick of waiting for the passenger train, caught the earlier "Goods" train, and arriving at Carrieton with time to spare, he hitched and hiked a

The Baptist Manse at Temora, N.S.W. in which Ern and Florrie lived at some time.

few miles along the Hawker road. It was getting dark — head lights coming — must be Eddie. "Let's take a rise out of him!" With suitcase hidden, overcoat collar turned up, handkerchief folder diagonally over his lower face, he stepped out into the head lights. The car stopped. With nickel plated fountain pen held in real bushranger style, he ordered "Hands up, or I'll blow your brains out!" No reply. No word from the "victim", only a large hand reaching out for the "revolver". Panic, had he bailed up the wrong man — and with a humbled "Is-is-is that you Eddie?" and "You rotten old sinner!" identification was established.

Uncle Ern died in 1953 at Waverley, N.S.W. aged 75 years.

## WILLIAM TELFER

fourth child of John and Caroline Telfer. (died in infancy)

## ALBERT GEORGE TELFER

fifth child of John and Caroline Telfer

Albert Telfer was born at Angle Farm on 14th November, 1881. In 1903, his father bought Warcowie homestead and

part of the property, after it was divided into small farming allotments, and Albert took over the management of it.

### PAGE 147
His sister, Bertha kept house for him there for a few years. The Warcowie district school teacher, Beatrice Rowe from Kadina (b. 12/10/1877), boarded there with them for a year or two before being transferred to Moolawurtie, near Ardrossan. Sometime between 1903 and 1906 there was a Christian Endeavour Rally at Quorn and Bertha, Beatrice and Albert rode their bikes to the Rally and then back again to start work on Monday morning. Sixty miles each way. Some time during this period Beatrice climbed Saint Mary's Peak, at Wilpena. She also had a camera and took an interest in photography. At the end of 1907 Beatrice resigned as a teacher, and on 21st February, 1908, married Albert Telfer and took over as the mistress of the large Warcowie home. Bertha by this time had left, and was carrying on her missionary activities.

The homestead was an interesting old building. Built in the early days of the state's history, it was a rather rambling design, narrow dark passages, lots of small rooms, a very large dining room and underneath a big cellar, which was entered by a flight of stairs outside the kitchen door. At the bottom of the steps was a very solid, thick door, which could be bolted from inside. There was another flight of stairs up the other side of the cellar, which came out into an alcove in the passage. It was said to have been built that way in the days when aborigines were fairly numerous in the area, just in case there was any unpleasantness and the occupants needed to take refuge underground.

The cellar was divided into two sections, one section had a raised platform and was used as a wine cellar by the previous occupants. Being cool down there it was an ideal place to keep the food and milk. The Telfer children used to play "Who's that walking over my bridge," by stomping around in the dining room, over the cellar. At one stage young Doug dropped something down the stairwell, and while leaning over to watch it fall,

managed to fall down into the cellar himself, cutting his head.

Most of the family slept out on the verandah, year round, unless the weather was so bad it drove them inside.

Outside was a lovely big Mulberry tree.

## WARCOWIE
by Bert Telfer

The atmosphere, the scenery, the sight and smell of the pines, almost everything about Warcowie has always been appealing and satisfying, and I would still love to live there.

The brightness of the moonlight nights were really something, and have been deceiving to at least two generations of Telfers. While a boy, something under 10 years old, I had stayed many times with Douglas and Melville, (cousins) in age just on either side of me, when they lived at Warcowie. With rabbit traps set the night before, the three of us would be sleeping or talking in a double bed. As time went on and it was getting lighter — yes, quite sure it was — unanimous — time to get up and clear the traps by sunrise. We had dressed and going through the dining room Doug found and struck a match, holding it up to the old “Ansonia” (clock) to check the time. Ten Past One, and Uncle Albert’s voice to the immediate rear of us, “What are you boys doing?”

Albert and his family call at "Angle Farm" to say "Goodbye", as they head off on their way to their new home at Clare.

### PAGE 148
Home of Albert and Beattie, at Hillriver, Clare.

"We thought it was morning." "Get back to bed."

Something like 20 years later I told the story to Uncle Walter and he responded with a story of when he and Albert were camped there ploughing. One morning Walter, who as a farmer was not an enthusiast, was awakened by Albert with, "Come on. It's nearly daylight. We'll be late getting those horses in." So out of bed and out to the horse paddock. After a while it dawned on them — not the day, but that it was still no lighter. One struck a match and looked at his watch. Three o'clock. So they lit a fire and sat by that till daylight really proved itself in the east. The moonlight again!

Albert was a farmer interested in examining ideas and learning from them. At Warcowie he became a foundation member of the Warcowie Agricultural Bureau, and took an active part in its activities — and later at Hill River.

Albert and Beatrice's family were all born while they lived at Warcowie. In 1923, Albert bought a farm at Hill River, 6 miles east of Clare, where the children could attend the High School. Joyce was already attending High School at Unley, Sylvia, almost ready for High School, Douglas and Melville about ten and eight, with Ralph and Keith down another couple of steps.

Everything at Hawker was sold, including the furniture, and the family

moved to Clare in the car. On arriving, Beatrice had her first taste of furnishing their home. As she had been away teaching, by the time they were married, Caroline and Albert had chosen the furniture for the house at Warcowie.

The house at Clare was complete, but without a verandah, even though the foundations for the floor were in place. It was not long before the roof was finished, but many years before the flooring was completed.

Albert found farming at Clare (one of the wetter areas of the State) very different from what he had been accustomed to at Hawker.

The following account of his early experiences is contributed by his youngest son, Keith.

When father sold the Warcowie homestead property in 1923, he

Albert's CASE tractor and Ground Drive SUN header, with cocky chaff catcher attached, which was moistened with molasses and fed to cows.

Albert's FORDSON tractor pulling a BIG E harvester to gather in his first crop at Hillriver. The crop was quite good but the last few acres were destroyed by a severe hail storm, the like of which has not been experienced since.

### PAGE 149
Albert's complete family group, 1959. L-R:- Doris, Doug, Marjory with Philippa, Ralph, Timothy in front, Beattie, Albert, Joyce, Sylvia, Melville and Merle. (Keith is missing.)

bought land in the Hill River Valley near Clare — 540 acres of undulating land, only two thirds of which was arable. He took possession on March 1st, 1924.

It was his intention to concentrate on sheep farming, as he had done at Warcowie, but his new neighbours were so positive that wheat was the only thing to grow in the valley that he accepted their judgments and bought some farming equipment, including a Fordson tractor, which was only the second tractor ever to come into Hill River.

Once again the experienced ones assured him that tractors were no good in such a wet climate. The 1923 winter had been one of the wettest on record, with a number of crops not sown at all. However father went his wilful way and bought his Fordson,

Albert's hay field. Eddie on binder, Dot (Watt's wife) and Albert on tractor.

which unfortunately proved unsuitable for the job, so after about a year it was replaced with a Cletrac. Here again problems arose, the "tracks" wore badly on the sidlings and "ran off".

In 1926 a "Case" tractor was purchased which served him well until 1937, when he looked to rubber tyres, and bought an "Alice Chalmers" tractor, the first rubber tyred tractor in the valley.

The Fordson tractor kept breaking axles, and Albert believed it was due to the jarring caused by the use of large steel spikes on the wheels to give traction in the wet soil. That was fine in the wet, but on harder ground they caused vibration.

In the 1930's the government placed a tax on tractor parts, in an attempt to encourage the use of horse teams. Albert reverted to horses for a while, but after monitoring the situation, decided that the tractor was the most economical.

Albert was a Methodist local preacher, Sunday School Superintendent, and both he and Beattie were Sunday School teachers, at Warcowie and later at Clare, and members of their family followed in their footsteps.

Beatrice died in June 1951. Albert actively continued his involvement in the farm till the end of his life, and was helping with the hay carting when he had

### PAGE 150
Albert's family. L to R:- Douglas, Joyce, Ralph, Keith, Sylvia, Melville.

his 89th birthday. Sadly there was an accident a week later, and his Lord welcomed him home.

RITA JOYCE was born on 12th April, 1909. After completing her schooling, part of which was spent at high school in Unley, she returned home to help on the farm. Having spent her working life attending to poultry, cows, house and garden Joyce now lives in retirement in Clare.

BEATRICE SYLVIA was born on 25th March, 1911, and on completion of her schooling, trained for two years at teachers college in Kintore Avenue, Adelaide. Sylvia was then posted to various schools throughout the state, including Adelaide, Cradock and Melrose. She has made a number of trips overseas. Retiring in 1971, she returned to the farm at Hill River, and then in later years, with sister Joyce, she moved into a house in Clare itself.

DOUGLAS IVAN TELFER was the eldest son of Albert and Beatrice, born 6th October, 1913. It was expected that he would follow his father's example and become a farmer, and so he attended Roseworthy College where he obtained a diploma in agriculture.

The same family years later Melville, Joyce, Doug, Sylvia, Ralph, Keith.

However a call to full time service in the Methodist Church upset that plan and he spent 2 years travelling up and down on the "Ghan" between Hawker and Maree, ministering to the people in that remote area. Then followed 4 years of training for the Methodist ministry and 2 years working in the rapidly developing township of Whyalla.

In 1945 he was ordained, married Doris Young, and they left to take up an appointment at Levuka, in Fiji. Doug found himself responsible for the oversight of the Fijian Methodist Ministers who were stationed on small

### PAGE 151
islands scattered over hundreds of miles of ocean. He had many interesting experiences visiting these islands. Perhaps the most memorable was a trip to Lomaloma, 150 miles from Levuka, on a small sailing boat. The return journey usually took about 10 days. They set sail early one morning, before long a strong wind sprang up and the tiny vessel tacked to and fro. The captain wisely ran for shelter behind a small island where, fortunately, a European planter and his wife took pity on Doug and invited him to stay. The storm lasted two days. Then they set sail again, but now the wind had dropped. They were becalmed. At times they rowed, once a larger powered boat towed them, but they mainly sat, day after day, waiting for the wind to blow. On the tenth day after their departure they reached their destination.

The little boat had to go on to another island and again was delayed because of the light winds. After a few days they returned to Lomaloma and set sail, but once again were becalmed. This was most unexpected as usually the return trip was much faster, because they sailed with the prevailing wind. The crew were unprepared for it and ran out of food, everything except tea. Fortunately Doug had been presented with a quantity of yams to take back to the boys in the boarding school at Levuka. For several days the passengers and crew lived on yams, boiled in sea water, and tea. They eventually arrived home on Doug and Doris' second wedding anniversary, 23 days after setting out.

Their next home was on the tiny island of Bau, the home of Fiji's highest chiefs. It was also the island where the language was spoken in its truest form. This provided Doug with a unique opportunity to master the language, which he did. His ability in this regard was later recognised when he was given the responsibility of training other mission workers and later again Government officers.

Instead of travelling in small craft Doug now had the oversight of one third

of Viti Levu, Fiji's largest island. He travelled by bus, where there were roads, then tramped many miles up hill and down, through mud and slush, over swollen rivers, faithfully visiting the ministers and the villages to which they were appointed. He preached wherever he went and spent much time helping the ministers with their book work and encouraging them in every way.

Then came the most demanding period of his life when for five years he was the Superintendent of Davuilevu. His main responsibility was the training of the Fijian ministers but as well he had the oversight of a large compound with a secondary school as well as a Bible school. This called for all of Doug's gifts and possibly it was there that he made his greatest contribution to the church in Fiji, as many of his students have since held very responsible appointments.

Their last three years were spent in Suva when Doug again had the oversight of a large, including a number of small islands. During this period he was, for a time, the editor of the church paper, and for a short period was a member of the Legislative Council.

At the end of 1959 Doug and Doris returned to South Australia with their four children; Jeff, who was born while they were at Levuka, Helen and twin boys, Andrew and Colin, while on Bau. Another daughter, Judy was born at Victor Harbor, their first Australian home.

Doug served, first the Methodist Church of South Australia and later the Uniting Church, faithfully and well for 21 years before he retired. He and Doris have reason to be proud of their family.

Jeff and his wife Raelene (nee Swift), and their children Ian and Wendy live at Glenelg. Jeff, who is a mechanical engineer, commenced work with Philips as soon as he graduated. He remained with them for 15 years, including 2 years when he worked at Venlo in Holland. In 1983 he joined the Commonwealth Government Department of Science and

### PAGE 152
Technology as assistant director of the South Australian office. He is a keen musician and plays piano, pipe organ and clarinet. Raelene, a former teacher, is now studying accountancy and is actively involved in the community.

Helen, after training as a social worker, married Peter Whittington who was a school teacher. They spent 4½ years in the United Kingdom, living first in Edinburgh where Helen worked as a social worker. Later they studied at the Lancaster University. On their return Peter entered the Uniting Church ministry and Helen worked with Domiciliary Care. Peter was appointed to Windsor Gardens, where they spent 7 years, before moving to Salisbury. They have two sons Philip and Timothy. Helen's training and wide experience enable her to make a considerable contribution to their work.

Andrew entered the Police Force and after completing his training, spent eighteen months on patrol work before being invited to join the Forensic Science Section. He is also a Warrant Officer in the Psychology Corps of the Army Reserve. He and his wife Ruth (nee Clarke), and their daughters, Nicole and Melanie live at Daw Park. Ruth is a trained nurse who now works part time in an aged care hostel.

Colin is a primary school teacher. He trained at Flinders University and has

Albert's grandchildren. Back:- Andrew, Gregory, Jennifer, Philip, Helen, Fergus and Colin. Front:- Jeff, Tim and Neville Simmons (not one of the family). Judy in front.

taught at several metropolitan schools. While at Blair Athol he was heavily involved with Priority Projects, a programme of the Australian Schools Commissions Disadvantaged Schools Programme. He and his wife Alison McDougall are very keen singers and are members of the Graduate Singers Choir. They spent one year overseas. They live at Fullarton with their young son Stuart Andrew McDougall Telfer. Alison is a speech pathologist working in Community Health.

Judy is also a primary school teacher. On completion of her training at Sturt C.A.E. she went to Darwin where she taught at the Marrara Christian School for 4½ years. She married Dale Chesson, from Halbury in S.A., and they are now at Aurukun, an Aboriginal Community in North Queensland, where Dale is working as Community Advisor. They have a baby son Bradley.

MELVILLE BRUCE was born 30th October, 1914. His working life was engaged in assorted farm work, including stockman (on Hill River Station), and

Melville and Merle's family. John and Pamela at back. Seated:- Melville nursing Kevin Nourse, Pam's son, and Grandad Albert with Adrian, son of John.

### PAGE 153
fruit picking (up the Murray), before becoming a dairy farmer in the Clare district.

In 1940, Mel married Merle Crossman, and in 1990 they celebrate their Golden Wedding. Retired Mel and Merle live on a block of land in Clare, and are keen gardeners. They have two children, John and Pam.

John — born in 1941, worked for some years with the Electricity Trust and is now a gardener. He married Lillian Dawson and they have two children, Adrian and Tania.

Pam — born in 1946, works as a cook at the troubled Blyth Hospital. Married to Barry Nourse, they have two children, Kevin and David.

JOHN ALICK RALPH TELFER known as Ralph, the fifth child of Albert and Beattie Telfer, was born on 8th September, 1918. After completing schooling at Clare, he was accepted by the S.A. Education Department and trained as a primary school teacher, taking up his first appointment to a school on Eyre Peninsula in 1941.

In 1942 he married Marjorie Parsons, also a school teacher. They worked in a number of country areas before being appointed to Victor Harbor Primary School, where they taught for 21 years up to their retirement in 1979. They continue to live at Victor Harbor in their retirement.

They have three children — Timothy, Philippa and Fergus.

Tim and his wife Glenda live in Victor Harbor. Tim is self-employed in the building industry. Glenda trained in office work and after years as accountant and finally part owner of a service station, is now managing an agency — Link Introductions — in Adelaide, as well as doing accountancy work for Peter Clarke & Associates, an insurance firm in which he and Glenda are partners.

Philippa and her husband Peter live at Belair. Both work in government departments, Philippa in the accounts branch of the Housing Trust, where she is

Watt Telfer, Bertha Alcorn, Albert Telfer and Hope Simmons.

responsible for salary payments, and Peter a survey-draughtsman for many years in the Marine & Harbours Dept. and is transferring to the E. & W.S. Dept.

Fergus and his wife Cheryl live at Port Lincoln. Fergus, who for a number of years was a professional fisherman at Streaky Bay, is the manager of a Fauna Park, which provides training and work experience for handicapped young people. He and Cheryl also run a small clothing shop which Cheryl manages. They have three children, Karman, Bridie and Jack.

MURRAY KEITH, the youngest son of Albert and Beatrice, was born on 28th January, 1920. Enlisting during the War, he celebrated his 21st birthday in the Army, and served in New Guinea, returning home with a touch of malaria. On 8th July, 1950, Keith married Marie Evans and they lived in their own house on the property at Hill River. They raised their two children, Jennifer and Gregory there, while Keith and his father worked the farm.

Marie died on 17th October, 1976. Keith continues to live and work on the farm.

Jennifer — born 28th November, 1951, and was trained as a teacher at Wattle Park Teachers College. One of her postings was at Bookabie (a one teacher school), after which she was transferred to Wirrulla, and Bookabie was closed. She married Brian Earle in January 1978 and they live in Port Lincoln with their children, Robin, Wendy and Geoffrey.

### PAGE 154
Gregory — born 24th February, 1954, completed an apprenticeship as a motor mechanic, and went on to train on trucks, and farm machinery, and now is a principal mechanic with the Electricity Trust at Clare. Greg married a teacher, Pam Bartholomaeus, and they now live

in the original farm house on the Hill River farm, with their children, Mark, Andrew, David and Paul.

Greg divides his time between helping his father on the farm and his job as a mechanic. Pam continues to study and do some teaching.

## WALTER WILLIAM TELFER

sixth child of John and Caroline Telfer

Walter was born on 28th January, 1884, and spent the first part of his life on his parents' farm at Willow Plains. Here he ran the general store and a delivery service. As a youth he was interested in photography and used a Box Brownie camera or similar, and a dark-room was built for use in developing and printing his photographs. As a young man he became a salesman, travelling between Hawker and Marree with a horse and four wheel buggy, possibly selling light-hardware, etc. He used to take his camera with him on his delivery rounds and for a small fee, take the photographs of his customers and their families. He also wrote overseas to order new equipment and get information on the latest photographic techniques.

In 1907-08 he left home and went to Port Lincoln where he re-erected the old Willow Plains store and once more became a storekeeper.

He married Stella Sophie Vickery, a school teacher, on 29th September, 1908, and their son, William Vickery was born on 16th August, 1909 at Port Lincoln. Walter continued with his photography, and ran a small business, but unfortunately about 1914 their store was burnt down, and they moved to Adelaide and settled in West Street, Torrensville.

Walter had always been a very keen photographer, so changed his occupation and became a professional photographer, doing Home-Studies, making frames and working in the darkroom in their house.

Their son, Vic started school at Thebarton Primary school, but soon afterwards (around 1918) they moved again, this time to Alexander Street, Port Pirie. Here Walter canvassed for copy-enlargements, and eventually bought a Studio, followed by a second Studio, but this one was later closed.

About 1926 they moved to Ellen Street, Port Pirie. In the late 1920's Walter and his wife were divorced.

During Vic's second year at high school, Walter became ill and was unable to work so Vic had to leave school and run the Studio. In 1930 they sold the Port Pirie Studio and purchased one in Goulburn, New South Wales, and it was known as Watt Telfer Studio.

About 1936, leaving Vic to run the Goulburn studio, Walter branched out again, doing Home Portraiture, and covering a wide area, including Geelong and Bendigo. To cope with all the extra travelling a large Packard car was bought. The business possibilities of those two cities, so impressed him, that he opened a studio in Bendigo, followed by a second studio in Geelong. Later he sold the Bendigo Studio and devoted all his time to the Geelong Studio, where he met and married his second wife, Dorothy Richie. They operated the Geelong Studio till his retirement in 1947, when they moved to Queensland.

It was in their home at Port Pirie, that I (Hope Simmons), first experienced the wonder of electric lights — lamps which

### PAGE 155
could be lit, simply by touching a button on the wall. My young brother Bill, was fascinated too. While the rest of us were sitting in the lounge, he wandered off to the bathroom, and soon we noticed our light flickering frequently. Uncle Watt woke up to the fact that Bill was proving that he could perform that miracle too.

While we were in our temporary home at Hilltown, (1929-30) because of the drought at Hawker, Uncle Watt and Vic were frequent visitors. I particularly remember one occasion, they stayed with us on Christmas Eve and next morning we were preparing to go over to Uncle Albert's for the day. Uncle Watt had brought a Father Christmas costume with him, and dressed up in it before going to join the others. Our mother asked him to come out to the front verandah so she could take his photo. Looking at him, his dark hair showed around the edge of his red and white hood, and looked out of character with his white beard. She went inside for a minute and when she returned, said "Just let me straighten your hair a bit" and she dusted a little plain flour into his hair. Vic nearly had stitches, but Uncle was unaware of the procedure performed.

Next time he came to visit us, mother said to him "Walter what have you done to your hair? It's gone so thin." He scowled at her for a moment and then laughed and told her. Knowing nothing about the flour, when he took his Father Christmas costume off, after the Christmas tree ceremony, he just splashed a little water into his hair and combed it through. Next morning, back in Pt. Pirie, he found his hair all clogged up, and couldn't get the comb through it. He couldn't think what was wrong with it, till Vic told him about the flour, which by then had set firm. He'd pulled half his hair out trying to comb it out.

The Life and Family of Walter William Telfer

By his son Vic.

As far as I know my Dad ran a grocery business in Port Lincoln in the early

days, and we shifted to Adelaide when I was about four. Transport has always played a big part in our lives, and a horse and sulky was our first transport when we lived in Adelaide. I can clearly remember my dad making picture frames in the shed in the back yard, and our horse was so tame and quiet, that he used to stand behind dad and look over his shoulder, waiting for a pat and rub.

The horse was later replaced with a B.S.A. motor cycle and side car as our first motorised transport. I remember when we first went to Hawker in the motor cycle and all the horses there were terrified of the noise. Dad rode the motor around and around the paddocks till the horses became used to it and quietened down.

It was about this time that Dad purchased our first car. This was an old Wolsley and was made before the days of batteries, self starters, electric lights and horns. The head lamps were gas operated from a cylinder of gas on the side of the car. The tail lamp was kerosene and the horn was air operated from a rubber squeeze bulb mounted on the door. This car had a rather historic early life as it was owned and used by the engineer when building the railway line across the Nullabor Plain.

Petrol pumps in those early days were non-existent. Petrol was purchased from the general Store with your other requirements. You would buy a "case" of petrol, which would consist of a wooden case containing two square tins of 4 gallons each. You had to knock 2 holes in the top of each tin to pour the petrol into the car's tank. The petrol tanks in those days were usually under the front seat, which had to be removed to refuel. There were no air compressors, so all tyres had to be hand pumped, up to about 60 lbs. pressure. Punctures and blow outs, were frequent occurrences, and a spare tyre was essential.

We shifted to Port Pirie when I was about seven. On my 14th Birthday I obtained my first motor cycle licence and I took over the ownership of the B.S.A.

### PAGE 156
Watt's son Vic, nursing his Uncle Eddie's little son Bertie.

motor cycle, which I rode to High School. I continued at high school until 1925 when I had to leave under rather dramatic circumstances. I was called out of class and told to collect all my gear as I was wanted home urgently. I discovered dad had a haemorrhage and was rushed to hospital for an operation. I had to go to the studio and take his place in running the business. It was indeed fortunate that I had helped at the Studio for several years previously. This was my entry into the photographic profession that I followed for about the next 40 years.

I had my first car licence in 1925 when I was 16 and have held it continuously ever since. I have had my car licence now for 63 years (to 1986). In February 1986 I received a lovely and treasured present from the N.R.M.A. of a gold badge for my car, in recognition of over 50 years membership.

We sold our Port Pirie Photographic Studio in 1930 and purchased a studio in

Watt and his wife and family visited his boyhood home at "Angle Farm". L-R:- Bill, Hope, Nellie, Dot, Watt, Eddie and Vic, with little Freddie in front. Daphne took the photo.

Watt and Dot Telfer.

Goulburn, New South Wales, driving over by car, along the Murray River.

A number of girls were employed on our staff over the years, but one girl was very special. Daphne May Wilkinson started work at the Studio on 5th March, 1934, as a highly qualified colourist and Receptionist. She became so special that I proposed marriage and was accepted. We were married on 22nd March, 1937.

My Dad left Goulburn and went to Geelong about 1936, where he established

### PAGE 157
Vic and Daphne at their home in Wamboral, N.S.W.

Fred and Joan, with their children, Julie and Michael, on the occasion of their Confirmation, Oct. 1985.

another Studio. Once again history repeated itself, and he married his receptionist — Dorothy May Ritchie. They lived in Geelong and ran the studio till his retirement. They then moved to Wynham, Qld. and later to Nambour, Qld. where he passed away on 2nd July, 1966, aged 82 years.

We (Daphne and myself) owned and managed the Goulburn Studio till 1962, when we sold the business. We lived in retirement in Goulburn till 1965, and then sold our home and moved to our present

The house third from the left along the bottom is Vic's home showing his view of the Pacific Ocean.

address at Wamberal (7 miles from Gosford). This makes only 2 addresses and phone numbers since I left South Australia.

For relaxation from business and for holidays we purchased a trailer and large tent and went camping along the south coast of New South Wales. Fred was then only 3 years old. When he was 10 years old, we purchased a small cottage at Durras Lakes, and spent many happy weekends and holidays there. Durras is on the South Coast near Bateman's Bay, a 90 mile drive over the Clyde Mountains from Goulburn. The cottage was small but comfortable, however there were few of the luxuries people expect nowadays. No electricity, no town water — the only shower was under a tall tank stand, and we had an outside toilet. The stove, fridge, and lights all ran on kerosene. We had our own lobster pots and caught plenty of lobsters from the rocks, prawns from the lake and fish from the beach.

We enjoyed this change of lifestyle as often as business would allow. Fred had a lovely pup, named Tim. He was part Alsation, part Cocker Spaniel. The two of them used to roam and romp over the sand dunes and swim in the surf and lake. We had many friends there.

After Fred left High School in 1957, he worked for a local mens wear store. In

### PAGE 158
1959 he joined G.J. Coles in their Goulburn Store. In 1960 he was transferred to their Sydney branch and later was appointed manager of several of their country and suburban stores. On 25th January, 1974, he married Joan Harmer, and they have a son Michael, and daughter Julie. In 1980, due to long hours, etc. Fred resigned from Coles and joined the A.M.P. Society.

An Obituary, printed in 1966 reads:-

Mr. W. Telfer

The death occurred last week of Mr. Watt Telfer, of Mayfield Street, Nambour, at the age of 82. Mr. Telfer had been in failing health for the past 12 months.

A lifetime of industry that began with seeking jobs from door-to-door, to owning three studios and employing a score of assistants, is the success story of the former Geelong photographer.

All this was accomplished with only four years schooling.

Mr. Telfer was one of a family of five sons and an adopted daughter. He was born at Hawker, about 300 miles north of Adelaide.

His schooling, which began at the age of eight, was brought to an end four years later when his father needed extra manpower on the farm. Mr. Telfer had never wanted to be a farmer and his mother thought he would become an artist. Time eventually proved her right.

His first camera, bought when he was

13, cost him three shillings and sixpence.

He sent to the United States for the camera, which arrived in the year 1898. It was a crude contraption which had to be loaded with a single film each time a picture was taken. He developed the film with materials supplied by the makers.

With his "picture box" he eventually took and sold enough photographs to purchase a camera costing one guinea. He never had a lesson and never worked for a photographer. He learned photography by sending to France and America for literature.

At about 22, he left home, rented a house in Adelaide and began the door-to-door search for customers. The charge for the finished work was 6d. a copy. He opened his first studio in Port Pirie and ran it successfully for 15 years.

He moved to Goulburn in 1930, and later to Geelong, where he remained for 11 years. He had taught himself to colour prints and to retouch. He was his own chemist, electrician, mechanic and carpenter, and had reached a high grade of artistry in his work. A mother's prophecy had come true.

After retiring in Geelong 17 years ago, Mr. and Mrs. Telfer lived there for seven years and on medical advice moved to Queensland and resided at Wynnum. About six years ago they moved to Nambour. Mr. Telfer is survived by his wife and one son, Vic, by his first marriage.

## EDWIN GILBERT TELFER

seventh child of John and Caroline Telfer

Edwin was born in 1887 and was the youngest member of John and Caroline's family. On 23rd October, 1912, he married Ellen Need at her parents' home on their farm at Dawson, 12 miles approximately from Peterborough. Ellen or Nellie as she was always called, had never been very robust, and in fact as a young girl it was

feared that she was fading away. Her mother, Elizabeth, with the insight mothers often produce when their children are in danger, put her foot down and said to her husband, Henry and her father-in-law, Richard, who was living with them, "No more smoking inside!" and from that time Nellie's health

### PAGE 159
Eddie Telfer and Nellie, (nee Need), 1912.

improved somewhat, but she was never very strong.

Their first child, Gilbert Eustace (Bert), was born on 12th December, 1913. That year was the beginning of a drought, and horse teams and their owners were dispersed to wherever feed could be found. On this occasion the Telfer team, along with others from the district, were sent north to Lake Frome, where heavy thunder storms had provided surplus feed.

Next year, with the drought becoming more serious, Eddie, with others, went north to "The Frome", mustered the horses and took their teams west, to work on tank construction for the East-West Railway, then being built across the Nullabor. Nellie stayed home, at Willow Plains with her little boy, and in the other half of the house, her parents-in-law.

Out on the Nullabor, Eddie nearly lost his life, falling victim to typhoid fever which was ravaging the camps at the time. He was brought to Port Augusta Hospital, and his brother Albert went out and brought the team home. Nellie went

Eddie Telfer's Christian Endeavour Membership Card, 1901, when he was 14 years old.

down there to be near him and one day decided to take him some little delicacies in the form of some jellies. Eddie, on the mend, was out in the grounds, so they sat talking and after a while they looked at young Bert in the pram, and found he and the jellies, had somehow got together, and what he hadn't eaten of the jellies was spread all over him and the pram covers. Poor sick Eddie didn't get any of the jellies, but he probably managed his usual hearty laugh.

When the family farming partnership was dissolved, the draught horses were apportioned out, with each partner in turn taking a pick. Eddie won the toss and first pick and chose "Champ", an unbroken 3 year old, and that must have been in 1913. Champ remained the apple of his eye until the mid 1930's, when due to old age and drought conditions, Dad handed the rifle to Bert, and looking fairly upset, asked him to see that Champ and Diamond (who had been the two shafters of the wagon team) didn't come home again.

The next few years were the best years known in the district. During these good years Eddie and Nellie, had a daughter, Eleanor Hope, born on 16th September, 1916. Good years meant horse teams were bred up, new machinery purchased, and good crops harvested. Good crops meant

### PAGE 160
A fairly ordinary sort of dust storm, in those days (1943). Horses endure it with their backs to the wind and in the shelter of the shed.

plenty of seed, which in turn led to plenty of mice, and there was a terrible mouse plague.

After the good seasons, in 1919 there was another drought. This time Walter Crossman took the best of John's and Eddie's team to the Crystal Brook — Red Hill area, hay carting and wheat carting, and a few more horses died. On 26th April of that year another boy, Reginald Francis, was born, and has ever since been called "Bill". A run of three good years and a fairly average year followed.

About 1923 with the threatened closure of the Warcowie school, and a desire that all their children should have access to secondary education, Albert and Beattie sold Warcowie. The family moved to the Clare district and bought a farm at Hill River.

From 1923 on the droughts became progressively more severe each year. Albert, now a tractor farmer, offered to put his tractor in the shed and feed and work the Hawker horses on his farm. In 1927 their brother Charlie, sold his farm at Ungarra and purchased a property at Saddleworth, and although a tractor farmer, he took three of Eddie's horses.

In 1928, with the drought still worse, all reserves of feed gone, blackout dust storms twice a week and the wherewithal to pay the grocer negligible, Eddie went to Clare. There he sought and secured

promises of wheat cartage and then travelled home to prepare to move the family south for the duration of the drought. Two unbroken draught fillies were put to harness, as were two or three buggy ponies, bred for the buggy, but with modern means of transport on the place (a T Model Ford), had never looked through a collar, and two utility cart horses were harnessed to the wagon, loaded with chaff, furniture, luggage, etc. With Eddie driving the wagon and Bert droving the relics of the dairy herd and the store conditioned poddies, the week long trek commenced.

I (Hope) recall a few days later our neighbour and friend, Jim Bennie, took Mum, Bill and me in our "Tin Lizzie" (as the Ford cars were called), and drove us down till we overtook the wagon. Before overtaking the wagon Mum mentioned that she smelt something burning. Jim looked down and said, "By Jove! It is burning," as there were flames coming in around the clutch, reverse and brake pedals. He stopped, and grabbing the water

Bert, Bill and Hope propped up against Grandma's brick oven.

### PAGE 161
bag, opened the bonnet and extinguished the flames. After having the car checked at the next town, we continued the journey. Upon reaching the wagon we swapped drivers, with Jim taking the wagon, and dad driving Lizzie on to Snowtown.

Eddie and family lived for a few months in a house belonging to Mr Jim Scott, near Barinia Railway siding, where the carted wheat was delivered. Bert having left school that year was able to secure a few jobs around the district, while Hope and brother Bill attended the White Hut School.

The cows were paddocked on a resting farm nearby, on which was an old farm house, with a wilderness of a garden and scores of garlic heads in flower. Bert used to milk the cows, separate the milk and save the skim-milk for Jack Roach (Eddie's cousin) to feed to his pigs. When the cows found the garlic the situation changed. The vile smelling milk couldn't be used, so it was all put into Roach's milk cans for the pigs. When poured into the trough, the pigs tumbled in after it, took one sip, had a sniff and turned and stalked off. Fencing was then required to keep the cows and the garlic apart.

In the following autumn Eddie rented a farm near Hilltown for two years. The horses were put to regular work, the few remaining sheep were trucked down from Hawker and were retained as at least a nucleus of a flock for when the drought broke.

In early 1931, with “the Depression” at its worst and wheat growing in high cost country not lucrative, and with some rain having fallen at Hawker, the family returned there. The horses, sheep, wagon, Model T Ford and a secondhand Chrysler (the Ford being largely unserviceable), all returned home. Two or three fairly good years followed, and then another lesson about trying to grow wheat with horses in drought prone country. Twice during the 1930’s the horses had to be sent away again on agistment, and “loaned out” to work for their keep. Once to Melrose and

then once again, it was Albert to the rescue at Clare.

After this most of the draught horses were sold, a few of the best going to farmers, the rest, heart breakingly to the zoo. A secondhand "Case" tractor was bought to do the tillage, although a few horses were kept for odd jobs — for about another 20 years.

Eddie and Nellie moved into Hawker in 1923, to a house that they'd built. Nellie died there on 15th August, 1954. Eddie died in the Hawker Hospital on 17th July, 1958. Both are buried in the Hawker Cemetery.

## REMINISCENCES
by Hope

Bill's birth is almost my earliest memory. I can remember after having been banished with Bert out into the backyard for what seemed a long time, we were called inside by Mrs Huskison, the midwife, to see our baby brother. I don't remember seeing the baby. All I can remember was a line of white towels across the foot of mum's iron bedstead. What were they there for? They had never been there before. I can still see them as they were then, and feel something of the unanswered questioning of my $2 \frac{1}{2}$ year old mind.

Grandfather had planted a row of Tamarisk trees across the back of the house, which he regularly trimmed up to keep them in good shape. One day while engaged in pruning them, Bert (still quite young) was out with him and Grandfather trimmed up a nice straight tamarisk stick, handed it to Bert and said, "Here take this in to Mummy and say, 'Here's a nice stick to hit me with!'" So a rather bemused little boy did as he was told, and said, "Grandfather said, 'Here's a nice little stick to hit him with!" and probably waited for the action to begin. It didn't, of course.

My schooling started in 1923, when I was $6 \frac{1}{2}$ years old. I enjoyed Grade I so much that I stayed on for another year. At

### PAGE 162
the time brother Bert was in Grade IV, and we had a 2½ mile walk to the Willow Plains School, from home.

The school, which was built in 1883, was pulled down and the new G.I. building was erected during the school holidays, and was ready when school reopened in 1924. The builders had used most of the water in our small rainwater tank, so for sometime each child had to carry their day's supply of water to school with them.

I carried a small billy can, which probably held a pint when full. As I stumbled across the furrowed and stony paddock, trying to keep up with big brother, he kept saying, "Take longer strides, and you won't have to take so many." On several occasions I slopped some water on my clothes, and was told I'd better go home and change or I might catch cold. I was not altogether reluctant to do so as usually Mum would drive me to school in our "Tin Lizzie." Unfortunately after the first few times, she became a bit hard-hearted, and after changing my dress, I was sent off on "shankes pony," to walk that long distance and be very late for school. After that I took a chance on getting a cold, muttering that "It would be her fault, anyway!"

In 1927 Bert went to Clare High School with Uncle Albert's family, and younger brother Bill started school at Willow Plains. He had been a very sick boy for a good while, and was not up to walking far, so we were promoted to riding in a spring cart, pulled by a dear old chestnut mare called Fairy. She was a very heavy-hoofed fairy, who managed only a step every second, and no intention of being stampeded by any little female driver.

When we were youngsters, after the Willow Plains Post Office had closed, the only news from the outside world, (anywhere beyond Hawker) was brought by Mr Harry Ward in his buggy and pair. He jogged along the 20 mile trip to Warcowie every Saturday, delivering mail bags to everyone along the way, hanging them on a post at a point nearest to their house. Ours was hung under a

gumnut tree in the creek about a ¼ mile from the house. While grandfather was still living there he would mount our chestnut mare Fairy and she'd struggle over for the mail, never getting out of walking pace. After he moved to Clare, we children would be sent over to the creek for the mail.

The mail included our only newspaper, the weekly Chronicle, which apart from the news of the world, also had a Childrens Page. This page contained serial comics, such as "Harry Hip — The Teddies and Bunnies" and "Bib and Bub — The Gum Nut Babies". As well as the Chronicle, and bills, there were usually a few letters, as people wrote more in those days, it being the only way to keep in touch with parents, brothers, sisters and friends.

One day, when I was about 7 years old, a man came in a motor car to see Dad. After the stranger had departed and Dad had joined us in the kitchen, I discovered that we had become the owners of a beautiful, black, shiny Model T Ford car. I remember as though it was yesterday, springing to my feet and throwing my arms around his neck and exclaiming, "Oh — you're the most wonderful Daddy in the world!"

I had ridden in a car only once before. Uncle Albert had one and as he was travelling south, (probably to Clare) our mother took the opportunity of getting a lift for herself, Bill and me (Hope), to Peterborough, to see her parents. I didn't enjoy the trip much, as the unfamiliar motion of the car meant I was car-sick for most of the 100 mile journey. After having to make several stops to allow us to get out and walk around, Uncle Albert probably regretted having consented to take us with him.

Driving the Tin Lizzie was interesting as on steep inclines the petrol in Lizzie's fuel tank used to run to the back of the tank, away from the outlet pipe and the engine would die. To overcome this, Dad would turn the car around and back up the hill. After she had seen out her usefulness as a car, Dad had the back half

### PAGE 163
of the body removed, and replaced with a tray, to make her into a buckboard. The removed back part of the car, proved to be a very comfortable seat, and stood on the front verandah of the farm at Hilltown. Living with us at the time was Grandfather Need (Mum's father), and that old car seat was his favourite spot. He would sit there and watch the traffic go up and down the Clare to Spalding road, only a few chains from the house.

As Christmas time drew near, Mum decided we would have a special treat for Christmas. A couple of weeks before Christmas, she bought a goose, which was let loose to wander around the house yard, and became very attached to Grandfather. Sitting beside him most of the day on the verandah, either at his feet or up on the car seat beside him. At morning and afternoon teatime, he was always given a drink of tea out of Grandfather's saucer. When the time came for this "goose to be cooked", Dad was away somewhere, and the question was put to Grandfather that he kill the goose. Quite decisively he replied, "NO. I'm not going to cut his head off. Might hurt the poor fellows feelings." The reprieve only lasted a few hours, until Dad returned.

Our time at Hilltown was right in the middle of the Depression, and as we were living right alongside the main road from Clare to Spalding, we were constantly having visits from "Swaggies." Tramping the roads, with their swag and black billy on their backs, they looked for work, and were always wanting a feed. Although we were not in a very financial position ourselves, Mum and Dad couldn't turn them away without a meal, but thought it only right to ask them to chop a bit of wood or some similar job in return, which most of them were glad to do.

After the completion of the 1930-31 harvest at Hilltown, we returned to our home at Hawker and a few weeks later it started to rain, and kept raining, and the floodwaters came the nearest they had ever come to flowing in our front door. All that stopped them was the only bit of dry soil for miles — a few kerosene tins of soil, in which Mum's geraniums were growing, were emptied out across the doorway.

Within weeks the country was a waving mass of green feed — as far as the eye could see. Anyone who has not lived through years of drought can't possibly imagine what that means to someone

Henry Need in the old Ford car seat, with his cuppa and his goosie friend.

### PAGE 164
who has struggled for years to keep his or her, family and stock alive. Our Dad, a 6'1" farmer, was seen with tears running down his cheeks as he looked out over what had been a dry, drifting desert, at waving, lush, green feed. The country responds magnificently when it does receive rain, but unfortunately that is all too infrequently.

Our first radio was bought about 1934. The Australian cricketers were playing in England and as not many of our cricket-crazed neighbours had "wireless" sets at the time, several of them spent many a night at our place until 3 a.m. listening to the cricket through the raspy, static and whistle noises which accompanied it. Some of the neighbours had crystal sets with head phones before we bought our "Wireless".

## BERT REMEMBERS

My sister, Hope, brother Bill (legally Reg) and I, all spent our early years with the Grandparents just around the verandah or through the wall. I always felt that Hope was much more appreciated by them, than I was — but they had had seven sons and their only daughter was adopted at the age of three. So a little girl was more of a novelty than a little boy.

Immediately after breakfast each morning Grandpa and Grandma would have a reading from the Bible, or the Book of Psalms, and a few words of prayer together to start the day. Our father would lead our family worship as a regular feature on Sunday morning.

It always seemed — and was my mother's observation also, that Dad (Eddie) and Uncle Watt (Walter) being the two youngest of their family, and in age closer together than the older boys, had a special affinity for one another. Yet it was with Albert and his family (the A.G.'s) that we (the E.G.'s), were destined to become more closely involved through the years, and family bonds still strongly remain.

The first 10 years or so after my father and mother married, though living in the same house as the Grandparents, which was about halfway between Warcowie and Hawker, it became a staging point on Uncle's trip to Hawker. My father had a block of sheep country adjoining Warcowie, and as access to it was via the Warcowie homestead contact there was relatively frequent. Our mother and Aunty Beattie also seemed to hold a great respect and liking for one another. Christmases were alternately spent at "our" (to leave Grandfather's ownership in abeyance) place and Uncle Albert's. School holidays with Douglas and Melville, as mentioned earlier, roughly my age — either at our place or their's, was an exciting event eagerly looked forward to, and looked back upon with pleasure.

That cosy setup was in for disruption though when Uncle Albert and Aunty Beattie wanted something better education-wise than the Warcowie Primary School, which was threatened with closure. Joyce, their eldest daughter, was already of high school age, Sylvia, fast approaching it, Doug and Mel, down the road a bit, with Ralph and Keith just about ready for primary school. So Warcowie was sold, and a farm was purchased at Hill River, about six miles (by road) from Clare, where secondary education was available and a new High School being built. Our family setup was further disrupted in the following year when Grandpa and Grandma decided to retire, bought a house in Clare, and there lived out the remainder of their lives.

Two years or so later Uncle Albert and Aunty Beattie offered to board me with them while attending the Clare High School. This offer was gratefully accepted by my parents and in 1927 I virtually became a member of their family. At that time five of Uncle Albert's family were attending either the Clare Primary or the Clare High Schools, besides me. Generally four of us travelled in a one horse buggy by road, 6 miles, and the other two, after helping with the milking,

### PAGE 165
rode across country, only 4 miles. The extra horse that was required for a spare, besides hack or cart work on the farm, meant there were four horses Uncle had to keep fed, shod and in working order.

During that year Uncle Ern's eldest daughter, Jessie, from Sydney also spent some time staying with the family at Hill River. To go to Church or Sunday School or any other trips to Clare (like the Show), it meant a car load of 10 in the Model T Ford, and everyone inside with the doors shut. There was a place for everyone and everyone had to stick to it. This was complicated by the fact, as Joyce once remarked, that Melville and I being temperamentally much alike and with some rough corners, had to be kept separated if possible, in conditions like 10 in a Ford.

(Albert's family remember that if there was an accident, it was usually Bert who came away with the bruises. Like the time the front wheels came off the phaeton buggy, and Bert was thrown against the support for the reins, collecting a black eye.)

The close associations between the two families continued when late in 1927 most of Eddie's horses were sent to Clare, and the following year, with the worsening of the drought, our whole family moved to Barinia, four miles from Uncle Albert and later to Hilltown another nine miles on. Throughout our stay the two families frequently met and mingled, and it was during this period that the Grandparents died, and the whole of their family gathered for perhaps a week.

While we were at Hilltown our mother had an operation, and following this went and stayed at Uncle Albert's, and Aunty Beattie nursed her through a rather trying convalescence.

On returning to Hawker in 1931, following an absence of two or three years, the farm, fences, sheds and country generally, had deteriorated to the point where another pair of hands would be welcome. Douglas and Melville had both left school, so first Mel and then

Doug came to live and work with us, on a share farm/wages basis, until the next drought only a year or so later. A further link between the two families came some four years later, when Doug was accepted as a Home Missionary, and travelled the railway line from Hawker to Marree regularly, and occasionally went on to Oodnadatta. The wheel had turned, and he then boarded in Hawker with the family of Will Sweet, who had pulled Grandfather across the Palmer Creek in 1878.

After 1932 for five or six years drought years were the order of the day. We were able to maintain a rather less than moderate flock of sheep, largely due to sporadic summer rains — the feed residues from these just tiding the sheep over the winter. With no harvesting work and Bill home with Dad, to get a little pocket money and make myself useful, I put in a fruit harvest, and a wheat harvest in the Clare district, where Uncle Albert was frequently visited.

Another harvest at Wilmington and other work locally, like drought relief road work, shearing sheds, etc. Then in 1939 everything fell into place for rain and wheat growing, and an excellent year. It was spoilt by the outbreak of war.

In early 1940 Bill volunteered for the RAAF, but was refused due to poor eyesight. A month or so later, with the war starting to get serious, I joined the rush, enlisted in the 2nd AIF and spent $5 \frac{1}{2}$ years in the 2/27th Infantry Battalion.

Bill, Hope, Bert, Eddie and Nellie. Late 1940.

### PAGE 166
As the war and labour situations became still more serious, Bill volunteered for work in industry and secured a job with Miller Engineering in Adelaide where he worked for a year or so. That left our sister, Hope, to help Dad run the farm and look after Mum, who was at the best a semi-invalid.

About that time the manpower authorities organised a Women's Land Army Unit, and in their wisdom decided to call all young women who were still on their home farms, and place them elsewhere, to help in production of food, etc. Hope received a letter calling her to report for such a service, as she was not "gainfully employed," according to them. At the time Dad was in bed with a very bad back, and Hope was trying to keep the wheels turning on the farm. Fortunately our local State MP paid a visit to Hawker and Mum and Hope sought his advice and help. He was able to get the call reversed. Later Bill was released from industry to go home and help Dad.

Hope and her mother, 1942.

During shearing Bill broke his leg while about 3 miles from home. He had tried to stroke a calf which had previously been a pet, but decided he was too grown up for that and tried to break away. Bill jammed his foot against a post to try to hold the calf, but with a snap his leg fractured between ankle and knee and gave way. He had to stand on his right leg, on the right side (wrong side) of the horse and spring as best he could, to lay across the saddle and drag the broken leg over the horse's rump. This particular horse would normally jump yards if anything touched her ribs or legs, but strangely, she stood still till he was settled. Bill's hat was chewed to tatters by the time he reached home. The leg developed gangrene, but was saved, and eventually knitted and healed, but it took six months before he could walk without crutches.

## GILBERT EUSTACE

(Bert)

On discharge from the AIF, and after a month or two at home, I got a job with the Telephone Department in Adelaide, and a few months later, on 27th July, 1946, married Edna Walkington, after an engagement of nealy four years. Edna was secretary to a business firm and continued in that position till the birth of our first born, Peter in 1949.

Being an applicant for land under the War Service Lands Settlement Scheme, and the probability becoming evident that I wouldn't qualify while working in Adelaide, coupled with an offer by Dad to finance us into a small grazing property near Hawker, we moved back there for seven years in mid 1950. There our second son Donald was born in 1952.

In 1957 the Lands Department wrote and said they were running out of land on mainland South Australia, but Kangaroo Island was still an option. So after coming over and having a look, we pulled up our stakes at Hawker and came to Kangaroo Island. The family lived in the

### PAGE 167
Bert and Edna, following their wedding, 1947.

"L.D.E. Camp" near Parndana, in common with the other hopeful families. The men worked on land clearing and development for anything up to 40 miles away from "home", returning for one mid-week night, and the weekends to see their families and replenish the tucker boxes for the next week.

After just over two years, with the allocation of a War Service block, the farm at Hawker was sold and we moved out to Gosse about 20 miles west of Parndana on 30th January, 1960.

We had been on the Island about a month when the Gosse War Memorial Community Centre held its 1957 Annual General Meeting, to which I was enticed to go, and promptly found myself on the committee. With the exception of two years I was a member of that organization till the end of 1980 when it was absorbed into a larger organisation, and Edna and I left the Gosse area and were living at Parndana. The objectives of the Gosse Community Centre were to promote the well being of nearly every aspect of a community.

The farm which we had been allocated, (and in due course were allotted) when we moved into it, had 860 acres of the total 1470 acres cleared, a house, a shed and 3 dams. Not a fence in sight. The cleared land had been seeded to pasture, but had to have another one and a half seedings to

come up to minimum requirements. The cost of fencing, seeding etc. was met by the Government, under the War Services Lands Act, and had to be repaid over a period. Then followed 20 years of cleaning up stumps and stones from the size of a tennis ball to that of a 40 gallon petrol drum.

By 1964 the Gosse Community Centre had a hall built where every organisation in the district held its meetings.
