Prior to that, meetings were held in our garage — Church, C.W.A., E.F.S., etc. Edna was a foundation member of the Gosse C.W.A. and when we semi-retired to Parndana in 1980, was made a Life Member. She also spent time on Community Centre Committees, was pianist for the Methodist Church, and concerts, and painted China and landscapes. I was an officer in the Methodist Church (later an Elder in the Uniting Church), was Secretary, Chairman and truck driver for the Duncan and Gosse E.F.S. and involved with Pioneer Bend Agricultural Bureau, Stockowners Assoc. etc.

Peter was 10 years old and Donald nearly 8 years old when we took over our block. They attended the Parndana Area School by school bus, which went past the front gate. A trip of about 20 miles by the most direct route, but the meanderings of the bus pick-up route added another 10 miles to that. After finishing primary school both boys completed their secondary education at Westminster School in Adelaide.

Bert and Edna's children, Donald and Peter.

### PAGE 168
Peter Gilbert left Westminster and came home to the farm for a while, but times were difficult and a lot of his time was spent on off farm work, in woolsheds, on the Agricultural Research Farm, etc. In busy periods, or when other work was unavailable, he worked home with us. He also worked for some time on abalone boats, and loved diving on and around old wrecks, and exploring the glories of underwater life and scenery. He bought himself a 19 foot "tinny" (an aluminium boat) which he used for his diving and did some crayfishing in a small way.

In 1970 Peter took a working holiday in England, with some trips to Europe and North Africa. While overseas he became engaged to Cathy Walsh, a local girl also travelling abroad. She proved a better correspondent than was Peter. On 8th March, 1975, Peter and Cathy (now Kate) were married in the Edmund Wright House, in Adelaide and lived near Parndana, and then Middle River, before moving to Kingscote and buying their own home, overlooking the sea. They bought a small crayboat "Ralphie" and Peter went crayfishing off the South Coast of the Island, while Kate continued teaching school. The treacherous Southern coastline with much travelling meant a larger boat was required, so "Ralphie" was replaced by "Tiger" which also proved not quite adequate.

In 1981 they had a 48 foot aluminium hulled boat built to specifications in

Peter and Cate after their wedding. 1975.

Perth. At about that time their first born was due. By consulting the calendar and doing sums, they worked out that Peter could go to Perth, do the sea trials, load the boat on a semi-trailer, and overland it to Adelaide in time to welcome their infant and hold Kate's hand.

This doing of sums was like the "famous last words" combined with "Murphy's Law". The sea trials proved some alterations were needed, then more trials, and a few days were lost and instead of being in Adelaide by the weekend, Peter and the convoy were just leaving Perth on the Friday. In W.A. wide loads on semi-trailers were not permitted on the roads on Saturdays and Sundays, so Peter had to put in the weekend stumping around Norseman, while Samuel Peter, the individualist, was making his entry into the world in Adelaide — on 15th November, 1981. The new boat "Wave Dancer" has done sterling work since, but the crayfish industry is in some difficulties, so they sold the licence and craypots. Branching out into the tourist industry, they have an interest in a Solomon Islands venture and developing another at Middle River, Kangaroo Island, and planning to retain "Wave Dancer" for charter work, etc. Sam now five, has not yet made his mark, but with his volatile temperament, I forecast that life around him will at least be interesting.

Donald Victor was nearing the end of his schooling at Westminster. He determined to get a degree in Agricultural Science, the better to fit him for running a farm, so we decided to put him through the required university course. At that time it was considered that getting a university degree was something that was worth working for and investing in, and not just one of those things that the world owed one. We started off financing him, but when the time came that the farm income for a year or so was zero, Don was able to raise a loan to be repaid after graduation, and by working through the vacations with a seed wheat grower,

### PAGE 169
Hanafords Seed graders, E.T.S.A. contractors, and university gardening was able to keep going. He paid off the loan.

He met another student, Nancy Middleton, and they were married on 12th April, 1973. Nancy gave up her course and took a job to help finance Don through his course, and was then to continue her studies when Don graduated. Those plans had to be rearranged with the arrival of Melissa Kate on 4th December, 1974. On completion of his Ag. Science course, and with the farm being incapable of adequately keeping two families, Don secured a position as Rural Officer with the A.B.C., and was sent to Brisbane for a while and then to Rockhampton, where Nancy was able to continue some part-time study.

After about three years in Queensland, they were transferred to Perth. Both at Rockhampton and in Perth, Don's work involved widespread travelling and gave him practical insight into most types of Australian agriculture. While in Western Australia his job took him as far north as Kunnanurra, and south to Albany and well into the wheatlands in the east. Still anxious to come back to the farm at Gosse and apply himself there, in 1980 he decided (with some reason) that I was too old to be farming, so relinquished his job with the A.B.C. and came home with his

Bill (left) and Bert. 1987.

Bert and Peter, (standing), and Cate and Edna, at Bert and Edna's 75th birthday party, Dec. 1988.

family, furniture and the cat, acquired in Queensland days. With Don and Nancy living and working on the farm, Edna and I moved out — into the township of Pardana, from where I travelled to the farm two or three times weekly, with decreasing frequency.

On 19th March, 1981, they had an addition to the family, Cassandra Marie. The Soldier Settler Schemes were planned that one family be able to make a living per farm. To provide a living for two families was to put a strain on living standards which was tolerable while we could get a part age-pension, but when the Assets Tests were applied by the government, Don and Nancy decided that to sell the place was more reasonable. We regretfully acquiesed to that.

With his Agricultural Degree as a base, together with his experiences in managing the farm, his public relations background as a rural officer over much of Australia, he applied for and was accepted as Farm Manager at the Agricultural Research Station near Longford, Tasmania.

## ELEANOR HOPE

Our mother had been a semi-invalid ever since I was at school, and not

### PAGE 170
surprisingly by the 1940's she was becoming increasingly worse. The droughts and the war, with my brother Bert in the thick of it, did nothing to help the situation. By 1948 she was suffering a lot of pain and unable to eat most normal foods, so the local doctor sent her to a Specialist in Adelaide, who whisked her into hospital for an operation.

Brother Bill had been to Adelaide sometime earlier and had met a young nurse, Gwen Nolan, and they had been corresponding regularly. Gwen was nursing in the River Murray area, and when he told her that Mum's condition made it impossible for us to stay in places of public accommodation, she contacted her parents in Adelaide and arrangements were made for us to share the bedroom she had vacated. After Mum's operation, she had to stay down in Adelaide for a couple more months, and so we spent some of that time staying with friends across the other side of town.

Soon after our arrival in town, Harry Simmons (who had been married to a favourite cousin of mine), now widowed, and whom I had met before, came to visit us. We saw a lot of each other during the following months, and eventually announced our engagement. We were married the following year, on 30th March, 1949, at South Arkaba Church, about a mile, from the farm at Hawker, then shared the house on the farm with the rest of the family, to enable me to take care of mother.

Home of Hope and Harry in Hawker.

Harry and Hope with their small son, Neville. 1950.

Harry obtained a job as overseer of the Hawker District Council road works. While in this job the Council gang was seconded to the Highways Department, to build the graded road from Hawker to Wilpena Pound, this being outside the Hawker Council area.

The following January, Bill married Gwen Nolan, and as Bill's future lay on the farm, they too settled in the house, which was now beginning to bulge at the seams. Bill and Gwen had the back half of the house while Mum, Dad, Harry and myself settled into the front four rooms. This was crowding things a bit, especially as Harry and Mum were both red-heads and both felt they had first option on my time and attention.

On 11th November, 1950, our son Neville John was born, and the house seams bulged further. It was decided that Mum and Dad would build a new stone house in Hawker, and Harry would obtain a pre-fabricated house from a firm he had previously worked for in Adelaide, and have them erect it on the block adjoining Dad's.

While all this was going on it was discovered that Harry had cancer and

### PAGE 171
Cousins Jim and Neville cool off on the grass, at "Windy Hill".

after three operations in Adelaide it became clear that it was incurable. We were living in our house in Hawker, and our 15 month old red-head was toddling around the place and continually handing his Dad a story book and saying, "Daddy wead," so Harry read those stories over and over, as he sat by the wood stove for warmth, until the books were worn out and he didn't need them anyway — he could recite them all through.

Bert and Edna lived on a farm 4 miles away, and both Edna and Gwen took turns in caring for Mum, which I appreciated very much. When it became clear the end was near, Neville, now eighteen months old went and spent a few days on the farm with cousin Jim, fifteen months old and Gwen. Harry was relieved of all pain, and welcomed into the presence of the Lord he loved, in the early morning of 3rd August, 1952.

Mum and Dad moved into their new home the following year, and Mum improved for 12 months and enjoyed being in town with friends nearby. Then her health went down hill again and she died on 15th August, 1954. Neville and I, then moved in with Dad, and when his health started to deteriorate I nursed him until three days before his death, when he was hospitalized. He died in the Hawker Hospital in July 1958.

We stayed at Hawker until Neville had completed primary school, and as his

The morning Hope and Neville left Hawker to live in Adelaide, in 1964.

natural leaning seemed to be more towards subjects taught in technical school, than high schools, we moved to Adelaide in 1964. I obtained a number of jobs (which included accommodation), looking after first one elderly woman and then another and later a family, while the wife went overseas for twelve months' study. That involved three moves within about 12 months.

Deciding it was time to have a house of our own, I finally bought one at Thebarton. I took on cleaning jobs for elderly folk, through Meals on Wheels. I did a couple of hours a week for two or three people each week. After about two years the house began to show signs of deterioration, rising salt damp, rotting floor boards and sagging ceilings. Time to move.

A land agent sold my house and after several months searching found another suitable and sound house in the Richmond area, which I bought. We attended the West Richmond Methodist Church, and soon I was teaching Sunday School, as well as involved in Womens Fellowship, Parish Council and later an elder in the Uniting Church. The friendships I have made here have deepened into a real Church family of loving, caring people.

I had three eye operations in three years in 1984-86, and in an attempt to

### PAGE 172
Hope's main hobby, growing Bonsai trees. A Second Prize Winner in the Adelaide Royal Show, 1984.

help me prepare this manuscript for publication, Neville bought a computer. Otherwise I think I may have had to say, "I'm sorry I can't do it!" and wasted all the information we have gathered over the years as well as all you have sent us.

Neville John born in 1950, and attended Hawker primary school. Year 8 was spent at Goodwood Boys Technical School and then he completed his schooling at Thebarton Boys Tech. Having completed an apprenticeship as a Fitter and Turner with Simpson Pope he moved on to making and repairing speedo-cables and then to splicing and repairing conveyor belts around Adelaide. After being retrenched in 1984 due to the shortage of work, he was employed by the Royal Adelaide Hospital as a clerk in Accident and Emergency.

## REGINALD FRANCIS
(BILL)

Reginald Telfer was the youngest son of Edwin and Ellen Telfer and from an

At Back:- Bert and Bill, Front:- Edna, Hope and Gwen.

Cousins at Grandpa Eddie's place in Hawker about 1956. L-R:- Jim, Peter, Neville. In front:- Donald and Lorna.

early age has been known as Bill. He was born on 26th April, 1919, and remained on the farm working the place in partnership with his father. In 1950 he married Gwen Nolan of Adelaide and as had happened a generation earlier the newlyweds lived for a time in part of the house built by John and Caroline, with parents occupying the other part.

Eddie and Nellie moved in 1953 to a house they had built in Hawker. Bill and Gwen still live in the homestead basically unaltered since 1893, though with modern conveniences that John and Caroline could not have imagined.

Bill and Gwen have four children. They were all educated at Hawker area school

### PAGE 173
for the ten years that the school offered secondary classes, then continued their secondary schooling in Adelaide, the boys at Wesminster School and the girls at Adelaide Girls High School. From there they branched into their chosen paths.

Bill and Gwen, January, 1950.

James Alexander, the eldest, born 1951 is a barrister in private practice after having worked for the State and Commonwealth public services in Darwin as a prosecutor, Legal Aid in Alice Springs, and the Attorney Generals Department in Adelaide. He married Janet Caroline Daenke in 1974, and thereby hangs a tale. The original leases taken up by John and George Telfer in 1877 required that they pay the Government one pound per acre. This requirement was later changed to an annual rental fee, but George did freehold his land by paying one pound per acre for his 764 acres. To do so he borrowed the money from one Thomas Magary. Janet or Janie as she is known, is the great grand-daughter of Thomas Magary. Jim and Janie live in Adelaide.

Lorna Fay was born in 1953 and married Dean McEvoy in 1973 and they have a son Warren James born in 1977. Lorna trained as a registered nurse at the Queen Elizabeth Hospital in Adelaide. In 1981 the family left for a working holiday in Texas U.S.A. and stayed for four years. After spending two years back in South Australia they returned to the U.S.A., this time to Virginia and in their spare time tour the country.

Bill and Gwen's family, 1987. Back row:- June, Jim, Dean, Bill and Andrew. Front:- (each in front of their spouse), Tom, Janie, Lorna, Gwen, Sarah. And Warren in front.

### PAGE 174
Shown above is the result of the efforts of three generations, and one hundred and ten years of labour on the "Angle Farm", (now "Windy Hill"), property. The large shearing shed, left, was originally John's stable, with stone walls and straw thatched roof. The small galvanised iron shed next to it was the hay chaff shed and beside it the chaff still grow across the back. The old barn at right, built about the same time as the house, has now been demolished and a smaller more vermin proof one built in its place. The view of the country side is much the same as that which met the eye of John and Caroline as they stepped out each morning from their small hut (slightly to the right of where this was taken), and turned their eyes to the rising sun as it appeared over Mt. Plantagenate, in the background.

### PAGE 175
June Telfer.

Andrew enjoys a snack.

June Elizabeth was born in 1955. She met and was married to Thomas William Cooper while they were both working in the Haematology laboratory at the Queen Elizabeth Hospital. They later shifted to Tom's home town of Dubbo, N.S.W. Tom continued in similar employment and June found work as a laboratory assistant at a local high school. They have a daughter Katherine Elizabeth, born on 18th April, 1988.

Andrew Laurence was born in 1959. He graduated in Applied Science (Geology) at the S.A. Institute of Technology, and has worked in the outback, and also spent a couple of years working in Tasmania. Andrew is currently employed as a hydrogeologist with the South Australian Engineering and Water Supply Department. Andrew married Sarah Hartshorne in 1987 and they live in an Adelaide suburb.

### PAGE 182
## GEORGE WRIGHT TELFER

Youngest son of Francis and Margaret Telfer

## GEORGE WRIGHT TELFER m. RUTH PENNA

(1850-1913) (1863-1908)

George Wright was the youngest son of Francis and Margaret Telfer, and was born at Macoota, Kersbrook on 22nd November, 1850. He grew up in Kersbrook and worked on his father's farm during boyhood. When older he bought land of his own in the area, and on it built himself a home, and then on 17th September, 1884, he married Ruth Penna, whose parents were also market gardeners. George ran a grocery van to Pt. Augusta.

George appears to have engaged in a good deal of land speculation. As well as his land at Kersbrook, he bought a number of blocks at Kensington (which had previously been owned by his mother), and a mining lease at Kadina. He also bought several sections of land at Hawker, which he later sold to brother John.

In December 1885 George and Ruth were welcomed to the fellowship of Salem Baptist Church at Gumeracha, where he later was elected secretary.

While they were living at Kersbrook three children were born to them, William Francis on 21st August, 1885, Olive Millie in March 1890, and Lesley Thomas born in 1892. Olive only lived about 2 months and was buried in the Gawler Cemetery on 5th May, 1890. Her parents' address was given as Mount Pleasant, 16 miles from Kersbrook.

After George's mother died in 1892, his father, Francis came and lived with them, and in 1893 they all moved to Willaston, near Gawler, where George ran a butter

George Wright Telfer with his wife, Ruth and son, Frank.

### PAGE 183
factory. In June 1894 Ruth gave birth to another daughter, Olive Netta.

Francis went to spend a few weeks with his daughter Elizabeth Farrow, at Finniss Point in 1895. While there he became ill and died of "Necrosis", on 2nd September, 1895. He was buried in the Tarlee Cemetery.

Some time during the following year George, Ruth and their three children moved to Western Australia. They lived for a few years in Albany, then moved on to Perth where they ran a boarding house overlooking the Swan River, before establishing a Steam Laundry in Leederville, Perth.

Information from the Registrar General's Office in Perth, and taken from Post Office Directories, shows "City Steam Laundry (Geo. W. Telfer, manager)" in 1901, and in 1902-03 "City Steam Laundry (Telfer & Penna, proprietors), Office and works, Newcastle Street, Leederville. Tel. no 464. Special quotations for Hotels." The Penna mentioned here was Thomas Penna, (brother of Ruth) who, with his wife lived at Oxford Street, Leederville from 1907 to 1920. Thomas was a J.P., newsagent and

registrar, but was not mentioned in the Steam Laundry after 1903. It continued in the name of George W. Telfer.

In 1908 a branch was opened in Hay Street, Perth and the name of the plant at Leederville was changed to City Steam Laundry & Dye Works. George's wife Ruth died in 1908. The 1909-1911 period changed things, and the business was split, with W. Frank Telfer becoming proprietor of "Telfer's City Steam Laundry" which included Newcastle St., two Hay St. branches and one in Central Arcade, while G.W. Telfer was proprietor of the "Telfer's Cleaning & Dye Works" at Newcastle Street, Leederville.

When they disposed of the Laundry (which although modernised was still in operation as the Monarch Laundry in 1981) about 1912, they moved east, along the railway line and opened a store in Merredin, and also bought a farming property at Nungarin, nearby. George and his sons ran both businesses for a year or so.

In 1913 George died, and the following year his younger son, Lesley Thomas, was killed in an accident on the farm.

## WILLIAM FRANCIS TELFER

first child of George and Ruth Telfer

On 17th March, 1913, Frank married Ethel Wisher.

In January 1914 Frank and Ethel had a son, Geoffrey Francis, and also that year, Frank was made a Justice of the Peace, and then came the drought and World War I. Financial difficulties caused Frank to sell the farm, but he carried on the store in Merredin. In 1915, their daughter Ruth Margaret was born, and two years later in 1917, Stewart Alan was delivered, to be followed by Basil William, born in 1919.

In 1920 Frank was instrumental in the starting of the Merredin Agricultural

Society, and for 11 years he was its secretary. He was a foundation member of the Merredin Club (Inc.) and the Masonic Lodge in which he was Master in 1921 and secretary until 1942.

In 1943 Frank Telfer, as a member of the Labor Party, decided to stand against the Country Party sitting Member, in the State election and won by a margin of 15 votes. Mr Boyle the sitting Member appealed and a new election was held which gave Frank a majority of 298 votes. He was defeated at the next election.

In August 1944 Frank's wife Ethel died suddenly. This was a great blow to him,

### PAGE 184
but he continued serving the community until he suffered a severe stroke in May 1955 and died on 25th August the same year. An article in "The History of Merredin" from which much of this information was obtained, ends by saying, "The biggest driving force Merredin has experienced, was spent".

**Geoffrey Francis** — born 9th January, 1914, married Elaine Burdett Sheridan on 8th November, 1939. Geoffrey Francis died on 25th August, 1975. They had four children:-

Claire Denise — born 27th August, 1940.

Helen Frances — born 16th January, 1942.

Dean Geoffrey — born 17th May, 1945.

Lynn Elaine — born 21st February, 1951.

**Ruth Margaret** — born 22nd November, 1915, at Merredin. After schooling in Merredin and Perth, returned to Merredin and worked in her father's office until she married Gilbert Hadley Carson on 1st July, 1941. Gilbert was the great grandson of Alfred Carson Senior, who was the first mechanical engineer to come to Western Australia in 1834.

Gilbert was champion athlete at Guildford Grammar School, and played most sports successfully. He was later employed by Dalgety's Stock and Station Agents, and had a good knowledge of north west station country and southern agricultural areas.

After the fall of Singapore he joined the army and served in Australia and on the landing craft going up the New Guinea coastline. Returning home after the war, he bought out the Agency part of W.F. Telfer's business in Merredin and in time held the franchise for General Motors (Holden cars etc.) and also held franchises for John Deere, Chamberlains etc., for agricultural tractors and implements and this business is still being run under the management of his son Max.

They had four children:-

Adrian Ethel — born 2nd September, 1942, married Dr. Michael Quilter on

18th February, 1979, and they have two children, Jacqueline and Hugh.

Bruce William — born 20th November, 1945, gained colours for rowing as he rowed in the Head of the River in 1963, when Guildford won. He married Marilyn Dench on 14th September, 1974. Bruce died on 10th December, 1987 and is survived by his wife and three children: Joanna, Christopher and Zoe.

Margaret — born 20th June, 1947, gained school colours for swimming, tennis and athletics, and later married Christopher Oakley on 19th February, 1973, and they have four children, Andrew, Timothy, Paul and Caroline.

Maxwell — born 17th May, 1950, married Elizabeth Piesse on 15th September, 1973, and their children are: Vanessa and Anneliese.

**Stewart Alan** — born on 3rd December, 1917, married Kathleen Dawson, and they have two children: Julie and Philip.

Basil William was born on 20th January, 1920, and married Eileen Louise Harrison and they had three children, Vicki, John and Debbie. John writes:-

Dad was born in Merredin, went to school there and worked with his family on their farm and business and finally came to Perth working with the Caltex Oil Company.

Joining the Army at the outbreak of World War II, he transferred to the R.A.A.F. in 1943. Passed out from the R.A.A.F. as a flight Lieutenant, and flew Boomerangs, and Kittyhawk aircraft, and was posted with 76 and 80 Squadrons in New Guinea.

After the War dad returned to Merredin and in partnership with his father re-opened the flour mill, Merredin Milling Company. After the fire in 1962 we all moved to Perth when my father all but retired.

The flour mill itself was built 1910-12, and in 1961 won First Prize at the Meredin Show for the Best Baking Flour.

In the early hours of Saturday, 8th September, 1962, on the eve of the next Show, the mill was burnt to the ground in a raging inferno. The following day was

### PAGE 185
Show day so cleaning up had to wait until Monday.

Basil died suddenly on 12th July, 1981, while playing golf.

John Alan — born 9th November, 1945, married Marian Janette Fleming-Babbage on 28th November, 1970. They have three children: William, Michelle and Nicola. In 1988 Michelle was the current Australian Junior Champion in gymnastics, and was a W.A.I.S. (W.A.

Institute of Sport) scholarship holder and part of the gymnastic programme at the Superdome, under coach Liz Chetkovich. In August 1988 in the Daily News it was reported that Michelle would go to China at the end of the year with the representative squad. Western Australian coach Larry McDonough said, "She (Michelle) still has work to do before she is at Olympic standard, but that is well within her capabilities."

## OLIVE NETTA TELFER

fourth child of George and Ruth Telfer

Olive born at Gawler, South Australia in 1894, married Harold Thomas on 11th April, 1930. Although living at Finsley, W.A. he was formerly from Penong in South Australia. We believe they lived at Merredin. On 26th April, 1932 they had a daughter Ruth Marie.

After Harold's death on 4th April, 1974, Olive Netta lived on at Merredin for many years. She remained alert and was very interested when we made our first contact with the family in 1981, although at that time she was almost blind. She died in her 93rd year on 12th September, 1986.

Ruth Marie — married Charles

Garrett on 14th April, 1952. They had three children:-

Robert Harold — born 25th July, 1953, married Vicky Ann Treffone on 30th October, 1976, and they have three children, Christy, Ashley and Tenielle.

Peter Raymond — born 28th June, 1955, single.

Jennifer Ruth — born 31st May, 1957, married Vivian Dixon on 6th November, 1976, and they have two children, Elizabeth and Charles.

Charles Garrett died on 4th December, 1972, and Ruth married again, to Brian Stagg, in October 1973.

## IN CONCLUSION

This story of the life and influence of Francis Telfer and his three wives, (Jean Murray, Dinah Flavel and Margaret Wright) and their descendants must come to an end.

We are proud of the contribution they made to the Faith, stability, justice and freedom of this country, and we trust that some day in the future, someone will be able to write the continuing story of the TELFER FAMILY, with the same sense of pride in the Faith, achievements and character of those who carry on from this time, into the unknown years ahead, building on these solid foundations.

### PAGE 189
## PART 2

The Stories of James, Elizabeth, Robert, John, Adam and Margaret Telfer.

By Barbara J. Lodge
(with help from many family members)

### PAGE 190
## James Telfer

James Telfer was born at Greenholm in 1796, the first child of James and Betty (nee Hutton) Telfer. He married Elizabeth Beattie in about 1830. James was the eldest brother of Francis Telfer who emigrated to South Australia in 1838, and four of his sons and one daughter were to follow their uncle in future years.

It has been difficult to obtain reliable information concerning their family, as the children, with the exception of the youngest son, Adam, were apparently not baptised. This probably means that the family attended a non-conformist church during those years. Many births were not registered because of the need to pay a Government tax of one shilling which was a great deal of money at that time.

However, from facts known and verified concerning the sons and daughter who came to South Australia, and comparisons with the Scottish records, it would appear that James and Elizabeth had the following children:-

<table><tr><td>James</td><td>b. 1829</td></tr><tr><td>Ellen</td><td>b. 1831</td></tr><tr><td>Elizabeth</td><td rowspan="2">Twins b. 1832</td></tr><tr><td>Margaret</td></tr><tr><td>Bellie</td><td>b. 1836</td></tr><tr><td>Robert</td><td>b. 1837</td></tr><tr><td>John</td><td>b. 1840</td></tr><tr><td>Adam</td><td>b. 1842</td></tr></table>

The Jedburgh Census of 1851 lists the household as follows:-

<table><tr><td>James Telfer</td><td>Head</td><td>Married</td><td>Age 52</td><td>Ag. Labourer</td><td>Born Roxburghshire</td></tr><tr><td>Betty Telfer</td><td>Wife</td><td>Married</td><td>Age 48</td><td></td><td>Born Newcastle</td></tr><tr><td>Ellen Telfer</td><td>Daughter</td><td>Unmarried</td><td>Age 19</td><td>Servant</td><td>Born Newcastle</td></tr><tr><td>Bellie Telfer</td><td>Daughter</td><td>Unmarried</td><td>Age 14</td><td>Servant</td><td>Born Newcastle</td></tr><tr><td>John Telfer</td><td>Son</td><td>Unmarried</td><td>Age 13</td><td>Scholar</td><td>Born Newcastle</td></tr><tr><td>Adam Telfer</td><td>Son</td><td>Unmarried</td><td>Age 9</td><td>Scholar</td><td>Born Jedburgh</td></tr><tr><td>Robert Beattie</td><td>Visitor</td><td>Widower</td><td>Age 77</td><td>Labourer</td><td>Born Newcastle</td></tr><tr><td>Robert Beattie</td><td>Nephew</td><td>Unmarried</td><td>Age 11</td><td>Scholar</td><td>Born Newcastle</td></tr></table>

It seems likely that Robert Beattie, widower, age 77, was Elizabeth's father.

James, Robert, Elizabeth and Margaret were not living at home at the time of the Census. The same Census lists a Robert Telfer, age 16, Agricultural Labourer, born Newcastle, living at Falside Stable. This could be Robert, the son of James and Elizabeth, as the family home at that time was in the village of Falside.

James Telfer died at 4 p.m. on 9th December, 1863, within his own dwelling house at Falside, Southdean, Roxburghshire, "from injuries received in consequence of being squeezed between the shafts of a cart and a granary wall". The informant was his youngest son, Adam Telfer. The death was recorded at Jedburgh on 18th December, 1863.

Elizabeth died on 28th December, 1891,

### PAGE 191
at the age of 89. The cause was listed as "natural decay". The informant was her grandson, Robert Telfer.

Four of the children of James and Elizabeth emigrated to South Australia. James, Elizabeth and Robert left on the ship "Oliver Lang" in 1854. They were

followed by John, who sailed on the "Morning Star" in 1859, and Adam who travelled on the steamer "London" in 1865.

The stories of each of them, and of their descendants, follow.

## JAMES TELFER

JAMES TELFER (1829-1914) m. JANET WELSH (1826-1885)

James Telfer was the eldest son of James and Elizabeth Telfer (nee Beattie). He was born at Hermitage Castle, Liddesdale, Roxburghshire, Scotland, on 3rd September, 1829. He came to Australia at the age of 25 on the ship "Oliver Lang", in company with his brother, Robert and his sister, Elizabeth.

According to the official shipping list, the "Oliver Lang" departed Liverpool on 25th July, 1854, and arrived in Melbourne on 25th September, 1854. The Master's name was Henry Manning, the tonnage 1736; and the ship carried 445 passengers. James, Robert and Elizabeth were listed as "adults" and "English". The fact that the ship sailed from Liverpool could account for the fact that they were described as English and not Scottish (as they should have been). However, there were so many folks emigrating at that time that officials were probably not very fussy about accuracy in spelling or origins!

After landing in Melbourne, the three made their way to South Australia where they made contact with their Uncle Francis Telfer who was living at "Macoota", Kersbrook, in the Adelaide Hills.

James was under contract to Mr. Alexander Borthwick Murray, and he

Hermitage Castle, Liddesdale, Roxburghshire.

worked for him as a shepherd at Tungkillo for five years before taking over the management of Koppio Station near Pt Lincoln for Dr. John Harris Browne.

While still working as a shepherd at Tungkillo, James married Janet Welsh, the daughter of John and Margaret Welsh. Janet was born at Nisbet, Scotland. On the marriage certificate, James is described as a "shepherd" and Janet as a "spinster". The marriage was conducted at "the Manse", Wakefield Street, according to the rites and ceremonies of the Church of Scotland by

Marriage Certificate of James Telfer and Janet Welsh.

Ruins of Pondanna Station, Gawler Ranges (taken 1987).

the Rev. Robert Haining. Witnesses at the ceremony were Robert and Elizabeth Telfer. The date of the ceremony was 6th November, 1855.

Their family of seven children grew up at Koppio Station, near Port Lincoln, where James was Manager for 39 years. In addition he managed "Talia", "Kalka" and "Pondanna" Stations in the Gawler Ranges. This meant many miles of arduous travel over unmade roads in all weathers.

James was recognised as one of the State's leading experts in wool production. Koppio Station carried 25,000 sheep, and the wool produced there and on the West Coast and Gawler Ranges stations, was noted for its very high quality.

### PAGE 195
One other letter written by James Teller to his son, James, also survives. This one describes how James and his daughter, Janet, had some difficulty in finding a "lying-in" place and mid-wife

for his daughter-in-law, Fannie. The precautions he took to ensure that the letter reached its destination would suggest that the mails were none too reliable in those days!

James retired from Koppio Station in 1900, and lived for the rest of his life at "Cliffside", Port Lincoln. He died on

6th July, 1914, and his obituary pays tribute to his fine character.

### PAGE 196
## MR JAMES TELFER

The death occurred at "Cliffside", Port Lincoln, on Sunday, July 6, of one of the oldest residents of the West Coast, viz.:—Mr James Telfer. The deceased gentleman was born at Hermitage Castlo, Liddiedale, Roxburghshire, Scotland, on August 12, 1829, and was thus nearly 84 years of age. He came to Australia in September, 1854, and was first in the employ of Messrs. A. B. Murray and Angas, with whom he served five years. On his severance he came to Port Lincoln in 1859, and took over the management of Koppio Station for the late Doctor John Harris Brown, with whom he worked until 1900, when he retired and came to live in Port Lincoln. Besides managing the Koppio station Mr Telfer had the oversight of several other stations on the West Coast as well, which fact necessitated a lot of travelling. During his term as manager the Koppio station used to be considered the show place on the Coast, and the first olive oil plant was established there, being sent out from France. Koppio used to carry 1200 head of cattle, but this was altered until 25,000 sheep were on the property, and the stations in the Gawler ranges also were noted for their wool producing capabilities. He knew the country from end to end—few have greater claims of being one of the pioneers of this vast country than had the deceased gentleman. Mr Telfer never had an enemy in the world, being loved and respected by all who knew him. He was one of the oldest members of the local branch of the Manchester Unity Oddfellows' Lodge; one of the oldest trustees for the Institute; many years member of council; Justice of the Peace and member of the Licensing Bench for many years; and took a practical interest in all matters pertaining to the improvement of the town. Mrs Telfer died about 28 years ago, and there are two sons—Messrs James Telfer, Portana, West Coast, and J. K. Telfer, Big Swamp, and three daughters, Mrs Andrew Watherstone, Port Lincoln, Mrs E. J. Barraud, Lipson, and Miss Telfer, "Cliffside," Port Lincoln,—surviving. There are 17 grandchildren, and 11 great-grandchildren. The funeral took place on Tuesday afternoon, the Rev. A. B. Lloyd conducting the service at the graveside. The members of Manchester Unity Lodge acted as pall bearers.

where he became proficient in all aspects of the sheep and wool trade. After his marriage he went to the South East of South Australia for some years. Following his return to Eyre Peninsula he managed Hillsea Station for Mr. J.T. Morton and then took up land at Pearce in 1885.

He specialised in wool growing and his station "Portana" was renowned for the high quality of its wool. James sold the property in 1929.

He took an active interest in all community affairs. He was a Justice of the Peace for 45 years and served on the District Council of Elliston for twenty years, two of them as Chairman. James and Frances had a family of seven daughters.

## OBITUARY MR. JAMES
TELFER

"Mr. James Telfer, who was in his 86th year, died at his residence, Sherbourne Road, Medindie Gardens. He was one of the State's most successful pioneer pastoralists. Mr. Telfer was born at Tungkillo in

1858. He afterwards lived at Koppio near Port Lincoln where his father was Manager on Dr. Browne's station.

After his marriage to Frances Oswald of Big Swamp, Port Lincoln, Mr. Telfer spent several years in the South East, returning later to Eyre Peninsula to manage Hillsea Station for Mr. J.T. Morton. He then acquired the well known Portana Station near Sheringa where he remained until his retirement in 1928. His wife died in 1931.

Mr. Telfer was a member of the Elliston District Council for many years, and acted as Chairman. He was an ardent supporter of the Anglican Church. Up to the time of his death he was a regular attendant at St. Cuthbert's Church, Prospect.

Of the family of seven daughters, two died before him — Mary in infancy, and Netta (Mrs. Vigar) of Mt. Hope. The surviving members are Mesdames Fraser (Barmera), Hibbard (Gawler), Hardingham (Hay, N.S.W.), A.S. Urch (London) and Miss Birdie Telfer, of Sherbourne Road, Medindie Gardens. There are also four grandchildren."

Elizabeth Beattie Telfer grew up at Koppio Station, and when her father retired, she went to live with him at "Cliffside", Port Lincoln. After her father's death in 1914, she lived with her sister, Agnes Barraud, at "Kinross", Lipson. She was a regular attender at the Lipson Church of England, and she is buried at the Lipson cemetery.

John Koppio Telfer was born at Koppio in 1862. He married Ethel Maud Julia Dutton on 5th February, 1898, and in 1899 moved from Koppio to Big Swamp, about 12 miles west of Port Lincoln, where he built a new house. "Big Swamp" was the first farm in the area to have a telephone exchange for the district.

John built one of the first wool sheds in the district, and the neighbouring farmers used to bring their sheep to Telfer's to be shorn and dipped. The local dances were held in Telfer's woolshed.

He was very interested in sport, and tennis courts and a cricket pitch were established on the Telfer farm. John was one of the founders of the Wayback Football Club in Port Lincoln.

Ethel Julia Maude Telfer (nee Dutton).

John Koppio Telfer died on 12th September, 1922, at the age of 59. After John's death the family continued farming at Big Swamp. They moved to Port Lincoln in 1939, leaving Douglas and William to run the farm. The rest of the family lived at No. 8 New West Road, Port Lincoln. Ethel Telfer died in 1956. The farm was divided in 1952. Douglas retained "Big Swamp" and Bill established his own farm on his section.

Douglas Dutton Telfer was the first child of John Koppio and Ethel Telfer. He was born on 9th February, 1899, at Koppio Station, near Port Lincoln. Douglas and his brothers Robert and William, later owned and worked the family farming property at "Big Swamp".

In 1935 Douglas married Vera Lucy Gore in the Methodist Church at Norwood in Adelaide. They lived on the property at "Big Swamp", and their son, Douglas Brian Telfer, was born on 18th July, 1937.

Brian started school in 1942. At that time classes were held in the Big Swamp Methodist Church and hall. However, in 1942 the tiny school was closed and a bus service was provided to take the children to Port Lincoln Infant, Primary and High Schools.

Douglas and his brother William worked the "Big Swamp" farm together until 1952 when the partnership was dissolved and the property split in two. Douglas remained at "Big Swamp", and William built a new house on his section and named it "Swandale".

Church at Big Swamp where school classes were conducted. Eva Hookings (nee Farrow) played the organ for church services.

Brian left school and came home to the farm. A dairy was later set up and 15 to 20 cows milked each day, the milk being picked up daily and taken to Port Lincoln. The property also bred merino sheep. The Telfers were among the first farmers in the district to bale hay in rectangular bales.

In 1962 the "Big Swamp" property was sold and another purchased at Pearlah. Douglas and Vera retired in Port Lincoln.

Brian Telfer married Joy Valerie Pope in 1962, and they lived on the property at Pearlah in the Vanilla area. Sheep, beef cattle and cropping provided the main income, and later a piggery was set up and pork and bacon produced for the local market.

Brian and Joy have four children — Janice Beverley, Kym Douglas, Graeme Neil and Heather Anne. The children were all educated at Port Lincoln until 1981. Janice played netball for Vanilla in the Port Lincoln Association, while Kym and Graeme played junior football for Marble Range.

The Pearlah property was sold in 1981, and Brian and Joy and family moved to

Natimuk in Victoria, where they purchased a farming property called "Grandview".

Graeme continued his education at the Horsham Technical School for two years where he gained a Wool Classing Certificate. Heather went to the State School at Natimuk for two years and then to Horsham High School. Kym and Graeme continue to work on the family farm as well as doing outside work such as shearing and crutching, wool classing, and other seasonal work.

In November, 1981, Janice enrolled as a nurse at the Tumby Bay Hospital, South Australia, where she worked until November, 1982, when she married Philip John Parsons, of Port Lincoln. Matthew John was born on 23rd March, 1984. Philip, a qualified mechanic, worked at a garage in Port Lincoln until the family moved to Wudinna late in 1984. A second son, Michael Philip, was born at Wudinna on 28th May, 1985.

Ethel Rose Telfer married Henry Schlink in 1928, and they lived at "Hillsea" Station near Sheringa, until 1973, when they retired and lived in Port Lincoln. They both died in 1974, Henry in May and Rose in July, just eight weeks apart.

Their son, Donald, lives in Port Lincoln. Their daughter, Lorraine, married Wally Jue. They live in Adelaide and have one daughter, Shana, who loves school and sport. The second daughter of Rose and Henry, Anne Schlink, married Gary Miller. Anne and Gary also live in Adelaide and they have four children. Roxanne works with a Travel Company in Adelaide, and Craig works with E.T.S.A.

Robert Welsh Telfer was born at Port Lincoln in 1903. He was brought up at "Big Swamp" and worked on the family farm, sharing in the management of the property after the death of his father in 1922. His mother, and other members of the family, moved to Port Lincoln in 1939, and Bob left the farm too. He worked for many years for Hopping Bros. in Port Lincoln, and even after his retirement he

### PAGE 203
was often called upon to help at the store during busy times. He was a great gardener, and there was always a plentiful supply of fruit and vegetables from the garden at 8 New West Road, Port Lincoln.

Bob served in the Army during World War II, and on his return he was an active member of the Port Lincoln Branch of the R.S.L. He did not marry, and he died on 28th August, 1979.

Rita Millicent Telfer was born at Port Lincoln in 1905. She too moved with the family to Port Lincoln in 1939. After the death of her mother in 1956, Rita and her sister, Eva, and brother, Bob, opened their home to some young people who needed accommodation, and looked after "boarders" for many years, thus providing a much-needed service in Port Lincoln. Rita was a great favourite with her nieces and nephews, all of whom were frequent visitors. She, together with Eva and Bob, ensured that everyone was made welcome. Rita was a keen bowler. She died on 30th May, 1978.

Eva Millicent Telfer was born at Port Lincoln in 1906. She married George Watson Neate on 29th August, 1952. Unfortunately George died a short time after their marriage, and Eva returned to live in the Telfer family home at 8 New West Road. Her last years were marred by failing health, and she died in 1988.

James William Telfer (known as Bill) was born at Big Swamp on 31st May, 1908, the youngest son of James Koppio and Ethel Telfer. After his father's death, Bill managed "Big Swamp" with his brothers, Doug and Bob. He married Nancy Roberts on 6th April, 1940.

When the family farm was divided in 1952, Bill and Nancy built a new home on their section, using local limestone. Their property overlooked the western side of the Big Swamp which was a haven for black swans and other water birds, so they named their farm "Swandale".

Bill and Nancy had three sons — Rodney William Telfer, born in 1942; Eric Robert Telfer, born in 1945; and Peter Spencer Telfer, born in 1952.

"Swandale" was sold in 1973, and the family retired in Adelaide. Bill died on 17th March, 1983. Nancy lives in retirement at Seacliff Park, where she continues to pursue her interest in painting. She also spends much time at her son's farm where she is able to help during the busy times.

Rodney William Telfer was born in Adelaide in March, 1942. He lived at "Big Swamp" until his parents built their new home at "Swandale". Rodney was educated in Port Lincoln and later at Scotch College, Adelaide.

In 1965 Rodney married Anne Sorby Adams. They first farmed at Wirrabara and in 1967 moved to Clare where Rodney manages a property.

Rodney and Anne have two children — Robert James, born on 16th March, 1968, and Susannah Mary, born 21st October, 1970. Both children were educated at Clare. Robert then moved to Adelaide to study Electronics, and Susannah is doing a course in Child Care.

Anne is the Co-ordinator of the Lower North Domiciliary Care based in Clare, and she also plays the organ at St. Barnabas Anglican Church in Clare.

Eric Robert Telfer was born at Port Lincoln on 25th May, 1945. He was educated at Port Lincoln and Brighton High School. At the completion of his education he returned to the farm at "Swandale" for some years, and then joined Elders G.M. at Strathalbyn. After his brother, Rodney, left "Swandale",

Eric (known as Ric) returned to the family farm.

In 1972 he married Bronwyn Smoker, a senior teacher at Port Lincoln High School.

He left the farm again in 1973, and joined South British United Insurance Company as their Field Representative. This entailed a lot of travelling from Port Lincoln to Ceduna and all points in between. After several years he moved from Port Lincoln to Adelaide where he continued in the insurance field. He is now Manager of J.M. Insurance at Collonades, Christies Beach and Noarlunga.

Bronwyn was appointed Senior Teacher at Immanuel College, Novar Gardens, in 1981. The family live at O'Halloran Hill.

Jane and Catherine are students at Woodlands Church of England Girls Grammar School, and John attends Pulteney Grammar School.

The family spent some years in Alice Springs and Darwin where Bronwyn continued her teaching career. They returned to Adelaide in 1981.

Agnes Lily Telfer and Edwin James Barraud, August, 1904.

Peter Spencer Telfer was born on 10th May, 1952, at Port Lincoln. He began his education at Port Lincoln and then attended Pulteney Grammar School, and later Urrbrae Agricultural High School. He returned to the farm until it was sold and the family retired in Adelaide in 1973.

Peter joined the staff of the National Bank for a short time; and after that the Department of Agriculture in Adelaide. He purchased a property at Geranium, jointly with his brother, Rodney. Peter manages the farm with assistance from Rodney who travels from Clare at weekends and other busy times to help.

Peter married Rosslyn Mary Kitts on 27th September, 1979. They have one son, Luke James Telfer, who was born on 18th August, 1982.

Doreen Lucy Telfer was the youngest child of Robert Koppio and Ethel Telfer. She was born on 14th August, 1910.

Doreen (known as Dorrie) married Alick W. Byles at Port Lincoln, on 16th July, 1938. They had one son, John,

who married Jennifer Bowden, of Agery. John and Jennifer have three children.

Dorrie died on 3rd December, 1976.

Agnes Lily Telfer was the youngest child of James and Janet Telfer. She lived with her parents at Koppio Station, and together with her sister, Elizabeth Beattie Telfer, cared for her father after the death of her mother. Agnes was just 19 years old when her mother died, and she did not marry for another 19 years, in 1904.

Her bridegroom was Edwin James Barraud, who had taken up farming land in the Lipson district. Upon his marriage to Agnes, he built a large stone house, and they lived there until Agnes died in 1937. The farming property at Lipson was named "Kinross" by Agnes Lily Barraud because of the associations of the Telfer family with Scotland.

Agnes was the organist at the Lipson Church of England for many years. After her death Edwin moved to Tumby Bay,

leaving the management of the farm to their son, Murray Alwyn Telfer Barraud, who was born in 1905.

Edwin James Barraud died on 8th November, 1950, and his obituary bears testimony to the high regard in which he was held.

## OBITUARY
THE LATE E.J. BARRAUD

Died 8th November, 1950.

Tumby Bay district lost one of its oldest and most highly respected residents in the passing of Edwin James Barraud on 8th November at the age of 87 years. He had resided in the district for 67 years.

He was born at South Rhine on 17th March, 1863, and came to Tod River with his parents a few years later. Having acquired land in the Hundred of Yaranyacka, near Lipson, he and his brother, Frank, moved up to farm there. He also tried his luck at Teetulpa when

### PAGE 206
Agnes Lily Barraud with Murray Alwyn Telfer Barraud.

gold was discovered, but with only moderate success, and returned to Lipson. He was one of the first to use superphosphate and drills, and conducted super experimental plots for the Government.

He served for four years in the Lincoln District Council and represented the Lipson Ward until a severance was obtained from Port Lincoln. He assisted in the formation of the Tumby Council, was its first Chairman, and held that office for 15 years. He also assisted in building the Tumby Bay Hospital and was Chairman of the Board for ten years.

Great Flinders Agricultural and Horticultural Society came into being in 1903 and he became its first Secretary, later being President. At one time he was Secretary and later Chairman of the Lipson Agricultural Bureau and subsequently a Life Member. He was Chairman of the Board of Advice and local school committee; he assisted in

building the Lipson Institute, and was the first Secretary and then President of the Committee.

He was largely instrumental in establishing the Port Lincoln Freezing Works, and was Chairman of Directors of the Company that saw the beginning of that great enterprise, which has been such a boon to the producers in Eyre Peninsula. He was the first Chairman of the Liberal Union District Committee and later Chairman of the Flinders Country Party Association.

Since 1891, a period of 59 years, he was a member of the Oddfellows Lodge. In 1938 he enjoyed a Farmers' Tour to New Zealand, of which he was made the leader. In 1939 he travelled overland to Western Australia, driving his own car, and was accompanied by Mr. Geo. Wishart. He was of a strong constitution and enjoyed good health until the last few hours. No-one could have been more public-spirited in their lifetime than the deceased gentleman.

The late Mr. Barraud was a J.P. for many years.

On 31st August, 1904, he was married at Port Lincoln to Agnes Lily Telfer, of Koppio. She died in 1937. One son survives, Mr. Murray Alwyn Telfer Barraud. He also left two grand-daughters.

Murray Alwyn Telfer Barraud was educated at Adelaide High School and Roseworthy College, and then returned to work at "Kinross" with his father. In 1929 he married Ethel Violet Carr, who was born in Lipson in 1904.

After his father's retirement Murray and Violet moved to the "big house" and took over the management of the farm. They were both very active in the community affairs of Lipson. Murray supported the Agricultural Bureau, the Show Society, sporting bodies, the Institute, the Methodist Church and later the Uniting Church, and in every committee he held the position of either President or Secretary for many years. Violet was very supportive of her

### PAGE 207
"Kinross", Lipson, in the 1930's.

husband's community activities and also belonged to many ladies' groups.

In 1969 they retired and went to live in the enlarged house of Murray's father on the Esplanade in Tumby Bay. Murray died in 1976, and Violet continued to live in Tumby Bay until her death in 1986.

Murray and Violet had two daughters, Margaret Jean (b. 1929) and Pamela Agnes (b. 1933). Both girls were educated at Methodist Ladies College where they were boarders for their secondary education.

Margaret Jean Barraud married Norman Stanley Solly in 1955. They live at Yednalue Station, Cradock, where they have a grazing property. They have four children — Rosalyn Maud, Meredith Gaye, Darren Ashley and Kathryn Jean.

Rosalyn Maud Solly, born in 1957, married David Watkins in 1981. Both are Agricultural Science graduates, and Rosalyn has also graduated in Economics at Adelaide University. Rosalyn and David have one son, Benjamin David, who was born in 1985.

Meredith Gaye Solly, born in 1959, is studying Business Management on a part-time basis, and works as a Company Secretary and Office Manager.

Darren Ashley Barraud Solly, born in 1961, attended Prince Alfred College and Urrbrae Agricultural High School, and is now working on the home property. He married Debera Grace Retallack in 1985.

Kathryn Jean Solly, born in 1962, is a graduate in Economics from Adelaide University and is an accountant.

The three girls were all students at Methodist Ladies College (later Annesley College).

Norman and Jean Solly are active in community affairs. Norman has been a member of the Hawker District Council since 1979 and a member of the Hospital Board since 1965. For eighteen years he has been a delegate to the United Farmers and Stockowners. Both Jean and Norman are active members of the Uniting Church of Australia, Hawker Parish.

Pamela Agnes Barraud married Ivan Ralph Solly in 1961. Ivan and Norman are brothers. Pamela and Ivan lived at Cradock until Pamela's parents retired in 1969. They then moved to "Kinross", Lipson and took over the management of that property. They have four children.

Brenton Roderick Solly, born 1961, attended Urrbrae Agricultural High School and is now farming with his parents at "Kinross". Brenton is the fourth generation of the family to work there.

Andrea Lynne Solly, born 1964, graduated in Early Childhood Teaching in 1985, and in 1986 married Raymond Carson.

Robert Stanley Solly, born 1966, and John Barraud Solly, born 1972, are the two other children of the family.

### PAGE 216
## ELIZABETH BEATTIE TELFER

## ELIZABETH BEATTIE TELFER m. JOHN CHARLES DILLON

(1832-1896) (1827-1906)

Elizabeth came to Australia in 1854 with her brothers, James and Robert, on the ship "Oliver Lang", landing in Melbourne and coming overland to South Australia where she visited her Uncle Francis Telfer at "Macoota", Kersbrook. It seems likely that she made her home

Elizabeth Beattie (nee Telfer) and John Charles Dillon.

there as her marriage on 18th December, 1856, to John Charles Dillon took place in Francis' home.

John's occupation was given as "Stockkeeper" and his residence as "Wall — Hundred of Murray". He was the son of John Boswell Dillon, who in turn was a descendant of Viscount Dillon of Ireland.

Elizabeth's residence was "Macoota", Chain of Ponds. Witnesses at the wedding were Elizabeth's brothers, James and Robert, who were "labourers" of Tungkillo. William Vercoe was the officiating Minister. The place in which the marriage was solemnised was described as "House of Mr. Francis Telfer, 6146 Chain of Ponds — Hundred and District of Para Wirra and Registration District of Talunga & known as Macoota".

Early in their married life Elizabeth and John had a property on Yorke Peninsula, but they later moved to Tea Tree Gully, and eventually settled at Millbrook.

Elizabeth was a very generous and kind-hearted woman, and if neighbours were sick or in trouble, it was Elizabeth to whom they went for help and assistance. It has been said that she would put the side saddle on her horse and gallop down the main street of Millbrook with her clothes flying in the wind. Some unkind

### PAGE 217
The Marriage
Certificate of
Elizabeth Telfer
and John Dillon.

person is reported to have said, "There goes that wild Irish woman!" but that person was promptly silenced when told that she was Scots not Irish!

John took a very keen interest in church affairs. It is on record that he was one of the twelve trustees who bought a piece of land "being allotment No. 4 in the Township of Maidstone ... to permit a chapel to be erected on said land".

He was a very big man and very devout, and insisted that his boys attend Sunday School and church. The story has been told that on one occasion when they were living at Tea Tree Gully, the boys decided to play in the creek at the front of the house instead of going to Sunday School. John gave chase along the creek, taking his belt off as he went, so that he would be ready to administer the appropriate punishment, quite forgetting that his pants would fall down — which they did! Legend has it that the boys escaped that time!

At one stage the entire enrolment at the Millbrook School was made up of Dillon children. The school was later destroyed by fire.

## The Family of Elizabeth and John Dillon

Elizabeth Esther Dillon
John Dillon
James Dillon
Robert Telfer Dillon
Francis Telfer Dillon

Henry Hatch Dillon
Adam Telfer Dillon
Septimus Dillon

Elizabeth died on 17th February, 1896, at the age of 63, and John Dillon died on 28th April, 1906. He was 79 years of age.

The grave of Elizabeth and John Dillon in Houghton Cemetery.

### PAGE 218
Both are buried in the Houghton Cemetery.

Elizabeth Esther Dillon the only daughter in the family was the eldest, who married Elijah Giddings. They had five children — Bessie, Nellie, Amy, Francis and Beatrice.

John Dillon had two children — Beatrice Dillon who married Harry Patten; and Leslie Dillon.

James Dillon married Susan Alcorn, whose father was a surveyor in the Tea Tree Gully area. Mr. Alcorn was responsible for the planning in that district when it was still very much a rural area.

James and Susan were orchardists who lived in the Millbrook area. Their children, John, Rettie and James Ralph Dillon, all went to the Millbrook School. Their land, together with that of others in the neighbourhood, was taken over by the Engineering and Water Supply Department in about 1920 to enable the construction of the Millbrook Reservoir. The family home was a short distance away from the area to be flooded, but nevertheless it was demolished. However, the white lilies which grew in the garden clung to life, and have continued to flower each year to this day.

When forced to leave Millbrook, James and Susan moved to Adelaide where they had a grocery store in Myrtle Street, Prospect. Susan died in the late 1930's. The house was sold and James went to Victoria to live with his daughter, Rettie. He died there approximately ten years after his wife. James and Susan are buried in the Houghton Cemetery.

Their son, John, went to live in Kalgoorlie and later in Perth. Rettie, who died in the mid 1970's, lived in Melbourne. Her eldest son, Lex, was killed in the Second World War. She had two other sons, Kenneth and Malcolm, who live in Pascoe Vale South, Melbourne.

James Ralph Dillon (known as Ralph) married Victoria Gosben. Ralph died in 1971. There were four children — James Ray Dillon; Harley Kintore Dillon; and Judith Lynette and John Lyndon Dillon

Robert Telfer Dillon.

(twins). John Lyndon Dillon is deceased. Judith married Robert Keith McPherson.

There are nine grandchildren — Simon James Dillon, Christopher Paul Dillon and Adam Lindsay Dillon; Tara and Natalie Dillon; and Sally-Ann, Amy and Anna McPherson. There are also two great-grandchildren — Stuart and Brianna Dillon.

Christopher Dillon was tragically killed in a road accident in 1988.

Robert Telfer Dillon was born in 1862. He married Matilda Langley on 30th April, 1895. They lived on an orchard at Millbrook, South Australia, and they had one daughter, Elva May Dillon. Matilda died at the age of 35 and she is buried with her parents in the Cudlee Creek Cemetery.

Elva May Dillon married Albert Raymond Southwood and they had four children — Audrey, John, Anne and Richard. John Southwood lives in Maitland, Yorke Peninsula, where he established a very successful automotive and farm machinery business which is still carried on by members of his family. John is a member of the District Council of Central Yorke Peninsula.

Robert's mother (Elizabeth Dillon) died in 1896, and his father then came to live

### PAGE 219
with him. Robert was a widower at the time.

Robert was a chaff merchant at Millbrook. When the Government required the land for the new Millbrook Reservoir in 1920, Robert bought a property at Paracombe.

Robert's second marriage was to Mary Ann Gilligan. There were four children — Robert Frank Dillon, Leslie James Dillon, Henry John Dillon, and Beatrice Mary Dillon.

Robert died in 1942 at the age of 79. His wife, Mary Ann, died in 1954, aged 68. The inscription on their tombstone reads

"In memory of Robert Dillon, dearly loved husband of Mary A. Dillon. Died Sept 9th, 1942, aged 79 years. Also Mary Ann, beloved wife of Robert Telfer, died Sept. 5th, 1954, aged 68 years.

Also to the memory of Private Leslie James, son of above. Lost his life in action, Tobruk. July 9th, 1941, aged 25 years."

Leslie James Dillon married Elizabeth Charlton, and they had one son, Trevor. Sadly Leslie was killed at Tobruk in 1941, and Elizabeth later married again. Trevor Dillon is Chief Agronomist with the Department of Agriculture at Kadina, Yorke Peninsula.

Henry John Dillon married Phyllis Charlton and they had one son, Barry. Henry later married for the second time. His second wife was Christine Patrick, and they had two children — Harry and Mitchell.

Beatrice Mary Dillon married Joseph Robert Murray and they had four children — Robert Anthony, Sandra Helen, Richard John and Jennifer Anne. Beatrice and Robert Murray live in retirement at Glenelg.

Francis Telfer Dillon was born at Kersbrook in 1864. He married Sabina Triplet, the daughter of Thomas Cleveland Triplet of Cornwall, and his wife, Martha Triplet (nee Coleman) of Glasgow.

Francis was a sharebroker and had a seat on the Adelaide Stock Exchange. He was a gentleman with a great sense of humour, but he was incapacitated by a stroke and was nursed at home for some years before his death. His wife, Sabina, was a very energetic lady, and worked tirelessly for many charities. Their home was at Cambridge Terrace, Unley, and they were known as "Granny" and "Popsy" to the grandchildren.

Francis and Sabina had five children, the youngest of whom (Adam) was stillborn. The other children were Martha Beattie Dillon who married Carl Jorgensen; Thomas Cleveland Dillon who married Osberta Loretta Odgers; and twins, Inez Dillon who married Horace Sampson Chappell, and Vera Dillon who married Jack Corbin Williams.

Vera Dillon trained as a nurse at the Royal Adelaide Hospital. During the Second World War she taught Home Nursing for the St. John Ambulance Brigade. She was an excellent cook, and made hundreds of sponge cakes to raise money for charities and the local church. For recreation she played croquet and bridge, and on the evening before she had her last major stroke, she enjoyed a game of bridge with her friends at Resthaven, Malvern. She died in 1986, the last of her family.

Vera's husband, Jack Corbin Williams, was employed by Elder Smiths Stock and Station Agents all of his working life. He worked firstly in Adelaide, and was then transferred to Melbourne when Elders and Goldsborough Mort amalgamated.

He enjoyed sport and played Pennant Bowls at Unley Park and later at Brighton Beach, Victoria.

Their daughter, Josie, married Dean Southgate. Josie is a Registered Nurse who trained at Melbourne School of Nursing, Victoria, followed by Midwifery training at the Queen Victoria Hospital, Adelaide. Josie works part-time as a nursing sister at Resthaven Homes for the Aged.

### PAGE 220
Josie and Dean were married at Malvern Methodist Church, the Church attended by both their families. Dean is a General Practitioner in a partnership practice on South Road, Clovelly Park, where he works half time. He is Associate Professor, Department of Primary Care and Community Medicine, at Flinders Medical Centre.

Josie and Dean have four daughters. The eldest, Anne Southgate, is a secondary school teacher who has taught at Naracoorte High School for several years. In 1987 she obtained study leave and attended Adelaide University to study science. She enjoys bush walking and canoeing.

Kathryn Southgate married Leon Cutting in 1984. She is a Junior Primary Teacher and is presently teaching at Loxton North School. Leon is an Ambulance Officer at Loxton. Kathryn enjoys playing netball.

Mary Southgate is a Secondary Home Economics Teacher at Meningie Area School. Her chief interest is in cooking and she attended a short course at the Cordon Bleu School in London. She too enjoys playing netball.

Rosemary Southgate is studying Law at Adelaide University and she plays tennis and netball for recreation.

Rosemary Vera Williams lived with her parents first at Unley Park and then moved with them to Melbourne in 1949 when her father was transferred by Elder Smith & Co.

Rosemary was educated at Firbank Church of England Girls Grammar School, and then trained for three years as a Kindergarten teacher. She married Ian Fred Burston in 1964, in the Were Street Methodist Church which the family attended. Ian is an Engineer, and since their marriage they have moved house sixteen times! They have lived in Darwin, Leigh Creek, Broken Hill, Sydney, Tom Price, Paraburdoo and Dampier in the Pilbara Region of Western Australia, Perth and Melbourne. Their three children were born in different States. Sally Anne was born in Leigh

Creek, South Australia, in 1967. She is undertaking a three year course in Primary Teaching at Claremont College of Advanced Education in Western Australia. Joanna Louise was born in Sydney, New South Wales, in 1969, and is studying Pharmacy at Curtin University in Western Australia. Nicholas Ian was born at Tom Price in Western Australia in 1973. He is a student at Ivanhoe Grammar School in Melbourne and has yet to decide on his chosen career.

Henry Hatch Dillon was born in 1866. He married Elsie Ada Williams and they lived at Sunninghill. Their first years were marred by tragedy as their eldest son, Henry, lived for only one day, and then twin daughters also died at birth.

The family built a house at Millbrook and lived there until 1931 when they moved to Hampstead (now Manningham) which in those days was an outer Adelaide suburb. Henry was an orchardist and nurseryman. He was a faithful worker in the Methodist church, serving in the Sunday School, the Christian Endeavour and general circuit work. His interests also extended to community affairs and he was a member of the Gumeracha Council for many years. Henry Dillon was a fine, upright and godly gentleman.

Of his family of six children, only two survived. The first three died at birth, and

Millbrook and the surrounding areas were flooded when the Reservoir was constructed. This photograph was taken in 1900.

### PAGE 221
The graves of Henry Hatch and Elsie
Dillon, and their son, Charles Dillon, in the
Houghton Cemetery.

his youngest son, Charles, died at the age of nine years, suffering from what we now know as leukemia.

Henry died in 1936 at the age of 70 and his wife, Elsie, died at the age of 77.

Dorothy Dillon, their elder daughter, spent many years on the mission fields of India, working faithfully there until her "retirement" at the age of 65. She then lived at the Assemblies of God Aged Home in Brisbane where she continued her Christian work and service in spite of a failing memory. Dorothy died in 1988.

In 1921 the younger daughter, Inez, married Ray Thomas Pitman, an orchardist of Chain of Ponds. They had one daughter, Valmai Inez Pitman, and four sons. The family lived first at Chain of Ponds, but changes came when Dorothy went to India. Inez then moved with her family to Millbrook so that she could care for her mother, Elsie, who was in poor health. In 1931 they moved to Manningham and Ray (known as Tom) continued to do the nursery work there as well as manage his own hills property. Depression times were difficult, but with careful management, the family and business prospered.

During the war years Tom worked in essential services, and after the war he purchased a small cycle business which flourished and expanded. All four of his sons became involved in the cycle business which is today well known as

"Yamaha Pitmans". The families of the sons are also involved in this enterprise.

Valmai Pitman married Alfred Russell Britcher, and they had five sons. All are married with families and all are successful in their chosen careers.

The children and grandchildren of Inez and Tom Pitman have continued in Christian work, and all belong to the Assemblies of God Church.

Tom Pitman died at the age of 88.

Adam Telfer Dillon married Edith Jolly and they had three sons — Roy Colin Dillon, Reginald Telfer Dillon, and Alfred Laurence Dillon (known as John).

When Adam Dillon married Edith Jolly he thought he had arranged his affairs so that they could live comfortably on the income from his orchard. They had their honeymoon, but on returning, very keen to begin the big job they had set for themselves, Adam found that he had only one shilling (10 cents) in his pocket — and he had forgotten to provide a stove for their house! They managed somehow, and later were the proud owners of a thriving orchard.

Sad to say the Government stepped in all too soon, and advised the orchardists in the area that their gardens were to be compulsorily acquired for the purpose of building the Millbrook Reservoir. Most of the fruitgrowers very reluctantly accepted the amounts of compensation offered to them by the Government, but Adam was not satisfied. This was his life's work, and would have been so for as long as he could have managed it. He pursued the matter through the courts and was eventually given a much better deal than first offered.

He looked at many fruit gardens, even as far away as Kangaroo Island, but nothing seemed as good as the garden he had built up at Millbrook. He purchased a house and several adjoining blocks, as well as those opposite, planted fruit trees on all of them, and lived in semi-retirement at South Payneham.

Twenty or so years later South Australia had a very serious drought, and the old Millbrook home, with chimney

### PAGE 222
still standing, was uncovered — in fact quite dry. Adam and Edith were taken for a drive to Chain of Ponds and Millbrook. They were able to walk across the dry reservoir bed and actually through the house. Great was their excitement, especially Edith's, when she found (at the back) two little rosemary bushes growing. She had planted one each side of the back door all those years ago and the seeds had taken root after having been covered by the water for twenty years. Edith brought the rosemary plants home and replanted them. One died very soon but the other survived and lasted for a long time.

Reginald Telfer Dillon married Gwendolen Taylor in 1926. Reg died in 1933 from bacterial endocarditis, leaving Gwen with two young children, John Graham, aged $4 \frac{1}{2}$ years, and Margaret Rosemary, aged 4 months.

John married Margaret Campbell and they have three children — Gary John Dillon, Bruce Campbell Dillon and Peter Graham Dillon. There are two grandchildren — Kate and Emma Dillon. Margaret married Moreton Mitford and they have two children — Jennifer and Philip.

Alfred Laurence Dillon (known as John) went to school at Millbrook, and later in Adelaide. In his youth he was a keen and proficient tennis player and was

at one time chosen to participate in the Davis Cup Training Squad. After leaving school he joined the South British Insurance Company Limited as a Clerk. He was transferred to Sydney where he met and married Katherine Winifred Benham. He became Assistant Manager of the Company in Sydney, and then Manager in Townsville where his title was Manager for North Queensland. He was subsequently transferred to Brisbane as Manager for Queensland, which position he held until his retirement. After his retirement he held many Directorships of Building and Housing Societies in Queensland.

Alfred Laurence and Katherine had one son, William John Dillon, who was educated at Brisbane Boys' College and later at the University of Queensland where he obtained the Degrees of Arts and Law. William John married Leith Heather Taylor, and they have two children, Alexander William and Katherine Elizabeth.

Alfred Laurence was a great lover of sport, particularly tennis and golf. He played golf at Indooroopilly Golf Club in Brisbane until he was 80 years of age. He died in 1987, aged 84 years.

Septimus Dillon the seventh son of John and Elizabeth Dillon. He had two children, Ivy and Robert.

### PAGE 228
## ROBERT TELFER

ROBERT TELFER m. SUSAN BURTON

(1837-1907) (b. 1845)

Robert, second son of James and Elizabeth Telfer (nee Beattie) was born at Jedburgh, Scotland in 1837. He came to Australia as a lad of sixteen on the ship "Oliver Lang", landing in Melbourne in 1854. He was accompanied by his brother James, and his sister, Elizabeth.

They all travelled overland to South Australia and made contact with their Uncle Francis Telfer, who lived at "Macoota" Kersbrook, in the Adelaide Hills.

During his first years in South Australia he worked for the late Dr. J.W. Browne on a station in the Gawler Ranges in the north of South Australia. Later he transferred to Dr. Browne's station, "Moorak" near Mount Gambier. He was living at Moorak at the time of his marriage to Susan Burton on the 11th April, 1865.

The marriage certificate is almost illegible, but Robert was 28 years old and Susan was 20. She was the daughter of James Burton, of Claraville, and the marriage took place at the private house of Mr. Burton, father, at Claraville. The names of the witnesses are very indistinct, but one of them was James Burton, of the building trade, of Mount Gambier. The Rev. Needham performed the ceremony.

Robert and Susan had a family of five sons and four daughters.

Robert managed the German Creek section of Moorak Station for several years and then became Overseer and later Manager of Moorak.

Ill health finally forced him to resign as Manager of Moorak. He built a house "Carlowrie" at Yahl, near Mount Gambier, and lived there until his death in 1907 at the age of 72 years.

Mr. Robert Telfer's Obituary appeared in "The Chronicle" on 19th January, 1907.

"Death of Mr. R. Telfer, Mount Gambier, Jan. 14. At his residence, Carlowrie, Yahl, Mr. Robert Telfer died on Saturday evening. The deceased was 72 years of age and was highly respected. He had been ailing for several years. Born in Jedburgh, Scotland, he, at the age of 16, came to South Australia. Therefore he was a colonist of 52 years standing. He was engaged by the late Dr. W.J. Browne on a station in the north of South Australia and later was transferred to the owner's Moorak Station at Mount Gambier. He managed the German Creek section for some years and then became stock overseer at the Moorak homestead section under the management of the late Mr. T. Williams. When Mr. Williams died, Mr. Telfer was appointed manager but ill health soon forced him to resign his post. He was engaged constantly during the whole of this 52 years in South Australia by the late Dr. Browne and his successor

### PAGE 229
Col. P.J. Browne. Upon leaving Moorak four years ago, Mr. Telfer built a residence in Yahl and has since resided there, his sons working the farm. The deceased was stricken with paralysis and frequent subsequent attacks left him in a low state of health. The last four months of his life he was bed-ridden. He left a widow, five sons and four daughters. Rev. Caldwell of the Presbyterian Church conducted the funeral."

## The Family of Robert and Susan Telfer

James Telfer
Elizabeth Telfer
Helen Telfer
Susan Telfer
William Telfer
