Edith Telfer

James Robert Telfer the eldest son of Robert, who married Hannah Peacock. James was a station hand at Moorak Station. There were three children — Leslie Robert, Ethel Edith and Jean.

The family attended Mount Gambier Primary and High Schools. Leslie Robert Telfer served in the first World War and was wounded in France. He married

Jessie Farrow and they had two sons, Bob and Les.

Ethel Edith Telfer married James Gordon Steele and they had two sons and two daughters. Phyllis Steele married Mr. H. Ploenges and they live in New South Wales. Joan Steele, who married Lionel Dutschke, lives in Maitland, South Australia. Alex married Lorna Hinton and they have two sons. This family lives in Mount Gambier. Colin Steele's first marriage was to Joan Watson. He later married Maureen Johnston. Colin had two sons, James and David.

Elizabeth Telfer married Charles Rowbotham and they had five children — Edith Mary, Robert, John Telfer, Charles and Emily.

Helen Telfer (known as Nell) married George Renfrey. There were four children — Agnes, Arthur, George and Helen. George Renfrey was Mayor of Mount Gambier from 1907-1909. He was the son of Richard Renfrey, an early settler and a well-known pastoralist. He was a cabinet maker by trade and was manager of the largest furniture factory and ironmongery business in the South East. He showed a great interest in public affairs in Mount Gambier and was elected a member of the Town Council in

The family of Robert Telfer. Back:- James (Jim), William (Willie), Francis John (Jack), Lindsay Burton (Burt). Front:- Edith, Robert, Bessie, Susan, Helen (Nellie).

### PAGE 230
1905. He held a commission in the Light Horse and Infantry in Mount Gambier. His interests included the Freemasons, the Australian Natives Association, the Order of Oddfellows and Foresters. He held the position of President of the South Eastern Football Association and was on the committee of management of the Presbyterian Church. He married Helen Telfer in 1897.

Susan Telfer did not marry. She lived in the family home, "Carlowrie" with her mother until 1924, when she moved to North Adelaide, becoming a companion to a Mrs. Walker. After retiring from this position she lived at Gilberton. Susie had a very close friendship with her cousin, Violet Telfer, daughter of Adam and Margaret, and was a frequent visitor at the Telfer home at Rochester Street. She died in 1954.

William Telfer married Mildred Willson. William was a farmer at Glenburnie in the South East. There were no children.

Lindsay Burton Telfer (known as Burt) had varied occupations. He was a produce merchant and later a clerk. He held the rank of Staff Sergeant at the Mitcham A.I.F. Camp from 1915-1919, and then served in the Pay Corps from 1919-1921. Prior to his retirement he had a tobacconist's business in Pirie Street, Adelaide.

Burt's first marriage was to Isabel Thomson. They had two children, Keith and Bessie. Isabel died in 1916.

Keith Telfer, born in 1904, married Gwendoline Nancy Williams in 1933. He was first employed at S.E. Beach Sharebroker, and later joined Mr. Beach to form a partnership, Beach and Telfer, Sharebrokers. Later he commenced his own Real Estate business, Keith Telfer Ltd.

Keith was keenly interested in local government. He was a member of the Enfield Council during the war years, and became the first Mayor of the City of Enfield. He died in 1964.

Keith and Gwen had three children. The eldest was Josephine Eva Telfer who

died from polio in 1948. Susan Ann Telfer married John Taylor and had three children — Karen Josephine, Julie Jane and Michael John. Margaret Isabel Telfer married Michael Williams, and they have two children — Christopher Wellesley and Andrew Keith.

Bessie Josephine Telfer, born 1906, worked at Anderson Potter, Seedsmen and Florists and later for E. & W. Hackett Ltd., also seedsmen. She enlisted in the W.A.A.F. in 1941, and served as a cook in the Officers' Mess, R.A.A.F., Laverton. She attained the rank of Flight Sergeant, and received a Certificate of Commendation.

After the war she became the proprietor, in company with Miss Phyllis Vincent, of a catering and home-made cake shop.

Now retired, Bessie lives at Seacliff Park.

Lindsay Burton Telfer's second marriage was to Sophia Schwab. There were three children — George James Telfer, Lindsay Allan Telfer and Clarice Jean Telfer.

George James Telfer, born in 1918, worked at the Savings Bank of South Australia from 1934 until his retirement in 1979. He was Manager at Cummins, Peterborough, Findon, Woodville and Rundle Mall Branches. He held the position of Accountant in the Adelaide Office from 1971 to 1974. His last appointment was as Assistant Manager, Adelaide Office, from 1977 until his retirement in 1979.

George served with the 27th Battalion A.I.F. (Scottish Regiment) from 1941-1945, and saw service in Darwin, New Guinea, Solomon Islands and Bougainville. He attained the rank of Warrant Officer Class II, Regimental Quartermaster Sergeant 1943-1945.

He married Betty Joyce Malcolm in 1943, and they had three children — Peter James Telfer, Elizabeth Jane Telfer and Jonathon Robert Telfer.

Peter Telfer is a dentist and holds the position of Director of Primary Care at Adelaide Dental Hospital. He married

### PAGE 231
The four Telfer brothers who came to South Australia. From left:- Adam, John, James and Robert.

Susan Joy Dewhurst and they have four children — Karie Louise, Emily Fleur, Josephine Amy and Hamish Alexander.

Elizabeth Telfer is a teacher at Cabra College, Adelaide. She married Peter Bevan Swift and has two children — Georgina Jane and Benjamin Daniel.

Jonathon Robert Telfer is a Senior Parole and Probation Officer at the Department of Correctional Services. He married Julie Bungay and they have two daughters, Kristin Myung-eun and Mikella Eun-Mee.

George and Betty Telfer live in retirement at Kingswood.

Lindsay Allan Telfer married Lorna Higgett and they have three children — Barry, Dianne and David.

Clarice Jean Telfer married Harold Edward Mattson in 1943. Harold was employed by the State Bank of South Australia. He enlisted in the 2nd A.I.F. in 1941 and served with the 2/27th

Battalion in the Middle East Campaign and two New Guinea Campaigns. He was discharged from the Army in 1945.

Following the war he returned to the State Bank, working mainly in country branches. He retired because of ill-health after 38 years service, and died in 1978 as a result of war-caused injuries.

Jean and Harold had four children — Christine Joy, Wendy Elizabeth, Julie Josephine and Jeffrey Ian. There are seven grandchildren.

There is very little known of Robert Allan Burton Telfer and Francis John Burton Telfer (known as Jack). Robert died in Sydney in 1940.

Edith Mary Kathleen Telfer did not marry. She worked at Heaver's Shoe Store in Mount Gambier for many years and later came to Adelaide where she was a companion to the Whitbread family at Klemzig. She lived in retirement at North Adelaide and died in 1960.

### PAGE 235
## JOHN TELFER

## JOHN TELFER (1840-1913) m. (1) MARTHA ALLEN (1842-1876)
(2) MARTHA HENSTRIDGE
(1856-1921)

John Telfer was born at Peel, Roxburghshire, Scotland, in 1840, the third son of James and Elizabeth Telfer (nee Beattie), and he followed his brothers James and Robert, and sister, Elizabeth,

to South Australia, in 1859. He left home with a reference from the Rev. John Fergusson, of the Parish of Edgerston, dated 15th June, 1859.

### PAGE 236
This must have been one of the last documents obtained by John prior to his departure, as he sailed on the ship "Morning Star" on 18th June, 1859, for a journey of 140 days to Melbourne. The ship carried 111 adults and 13 children, and John Telfer was listed as being aged 19 years, a Shepherd, from Scotland.

He landed in Melbourne and made his way to South Australia where he contacted his Uncle Francis Telfer at "Macoota", Kersbrook, and no doubt had a very happy and exciting reunion with his brothers and sister whom he had not seen for five years. John came to South Australia under contract to Mr. Alexander Borthwick Murray, and for seven years he worked for Mr. Murray as a shepherd at Tungkillo in the Adelaide Hills.

On 19th January, 1865, he married Martha Allen. The ceremony took place at the private residence of Mr. Robert Boyd in Synagogue Place, Adelaide. On the marriage certificate John is described as a Shepherd of "full age", and Martha as a spinster, likewise of "full age". Martha was the daughter of William Allen. She was born in 1842, and her

parents are believed to have come from Glenmaquin, County Donegal, Ireland.

John and Martha had four children — James, Elizabeth, Susan and Martha.

The family moved to the South East of the State in 1874, and settled at Tantanoola (then known as Luceton). Sadly Martha died on 9th March, 1876, leaving John with four children aged 10, 8, 5 and 3 years. Martha's death was recorded in the "Border Watch" on 11th March, 1876 —

"Death in Hospital: On Thursday morning, Martha Telfer, wife of Mr. John Telfer, of German Flat, died in Hospital in Mount Gambier from cancer. She had not been there long. She was 34 years of age."

It is a brief and blunt statement, and we can only guess at the pain and suffering which Martha must have endured before her untimely death.

James Telfer the eldest of their four children was born at Honeysuckle Flat on

Martha Telfer (nee Allen) with son, James.

### PAGE 237
18th October, 1866. He accompanied his parents to Tantanoola where he showed considerable interest in community affairs. He was the first Secretary of the Tantanoola Agricultural and Floral Society, and the Secretary of the Committee which was formed to organise the building of a Presbyterian Church in Tantanoola.

In 1917 he married Alma Ida Sporer, whose parents were Wilhelm August Sporer and Bertha Augusta Bottroff. The wedding took place in Mount Gambier. James and Alma had a store in Glencoe West. The Store incorporated the local Post Office, and as well handled groceries, hardware, drapery, and a multitude of other goods required by the residents of a farming community.

James attended the local Presbyterian Church where he held the office of Elder. He was a quiet, gentle person who enjoyed reading, particularly poetry. For many years he was Patron of the Glencoe West Tennis Club and acted as its Auditor. He was an enthusiastic supporter of the Cricket Club and very active in community affairs. He was always willing to help people when help was needed. James died in 1946. He is buried in the Lake Terrace Cemetery, Mount Gambier. His wife, Alma, died in 1965.

James and Alma had one daughter, Vida, who was born on 8th March, 1919. Vida Telfer married Kenneth Lionel Templer on 16th February, 1946. They live at South Plympton, an Adelaide suburb. Vida and Kenneth have two children — Peter James Templer, born on 21st December, 1947, and Julie Ann Templer, born on 1st August, 1950. Peter Templer married Christine Margaret Tomkins and they live at Crafers in the Adelaide Hills. Julie Ann Templer married Edwin Adrian Wilson Kermode, and their home is at Glengowrie. Julie and Adrian have two children — Paul Adrian Kermode, born on 21st September, 1973, and Debra Leeanne Kermode, born on 25th March, 1976.

The wedding party of James Telfer and Alma Sporer, 1917.
From left:- Roland Telfer, Alma, James, Meta Ey and Flower-girl Ena Aitken.

James Telfer in front of his General Store at Glencoe West.

Elizabeth, Susan and Martha Telfer.

Elizabeth Telfer was the second child and daughter of John Telfer, who was born on 2nd October, 1868. She married a Mr. Clarke and they did not have any children.

### PAGE 238
The family of Robert and Susan Agnew.

Susan Telfer was the third child, who was born on 9th June, 1871. She married a farmer, Robert Agnew. They farmed first at Millicent and later at Glencoe in the South East of South Australia. They had two sons, Keith and Gilbert, and six daughters, Myrna, Gladys, Doreen, Thelma, Marjorie and Betty.

Robert Agnew was a member of the Mt. Muirhead School Board of Advice at the same time as Adam Telfer.

Susan died at the age of 81 years, on 29th July, 1952. At that time she was living at the home of her daughter, Myrna, (Mrs. Arthur Goodes) at Glencoe West.

Myrna Agnew married Arthur Goodes who was a farmer at Glencoe West. They had two daughters, Barbara and Elizabeth.

Barbara Goodes married Harvey Stribley in 1949. Harvey served in the 2nd

A.I.F. during World War II. The family live in Adelaide and have close connections with Scots Church in Adelaide. Barbara and Harvey have three children — Geoffrey, David and Anne. They are all married. Geoffrey and Anne live in Adelaide and David now resides in Brisbane.

Elizabeth Goodes married Stanley McKay and they have three children — Ronald, Graham and Susan. Their home is in Mount Gambier where they have a close association with the Uniting Church. Stanley McKay served in the Royal Navy during World War II. Susan McKay now lives in Adelaide, and Ronald has moved to Sydney with his family.

Marjorie Agnew, the seventh child of Susan and Robert Agnew, grew up on the family farm at Glencoe West, but left there in 1929, to undertake a training course at Adelaide Teachers College.

### PAGE 239
Her first appointment in 1931 was at Largs Bay Primary School, and in 1932 she returned to Glencoe School to teach a composite class of Grades 1, 2 and 3.

In 1937 Marjorie married Arthur Robert Bleechmore. Their home was at Largs Bay, an Adelaide suburb. They had two sons Robert George Bleechmore (born 1938), and Kenneth Malcolm Bleechmore (born 1943).

Marjorie was affiliated with the Presbyterian (now Uniting) Church and was an active member of the Parish of St. Luke's at Peterhead. Marjorie and Arthur retired at Wesley House, a retirement complex managed by the Port Adelaide Central Mission. Marjorie died in 1987.

Robert Bleechmore became a Wool Classer, but he left that occupation and studied accountancy. He is now Secretary/Accountant in a winery at McLaren Vale. He married Eleanor Kathleen Hare and they have three children — Samantha, Heather and Tasman. The family lives at Willunga and the children all attend Willunga High School. Tasman is a Scout and he was one of the survivors of the canoe tragedy on Lake Alexandrina in 1987.

Kenneth Bleechmore began his career as an apprentice fitter and turner at Glanville. He is now a qualified chief engineer with the Mobil Oil Co. His first marriage was to Patricia Godfrey and they had two children Valmai and Brenton. He later married Margaret Vera Wills, and they live at Para Hills, an Adelaide suburb.

Betty Agnew, the eighth and youngest child of Susan and Robert, married Archibald William Mitton, and they live in Adelaide. They have one son, Andrew, who married Margaret Graham. Andrew and Margaret have three children — Nathan, Jason and Stephanie.

Andrew Mitton is a police sergeant, a member of the Accident Investigation Squad. In 1984 he was sent to the North Western University in Evanston, Illinois, U.S.A., to study American accident investigation techniques. He was required to sit for an examination which

Andrew Mitton.

dealt almost exclusively with American traffic laws. He was not very confident about the test and was therefore not surprised when the examiners asked to see him. Everyone else on the course had received their marks, when a worried Andrew was called to the front of the class. He was amazed to learn that he was the first person in the history of the University to obtain a one hundred per cent pass mark for the examination. He had beaten the U.S.A. at its own game!

Martha Telfer, who was born at Mount Pleasant on 18th April, 1873, was the fourth child of John and Martha (Allen) Telfer. She married Malcolm Duncan Cameron in 1899. Malcolm was the son of John and Margaret Cameron who had come from Scotland in 1855 and settled at German Creek.

Martha (known as "Mattie") and Malcolm took up land at Glencoe in 1902, and they lived there for the rest of their lives.

Malcolm was a very public spirited gentleman and was Clerk of the District Council of Tantanoola for twenty years. In 1922 he was elected a Member of Parliament, a position he held for twelve

### PAGE 240
Malcolm and Martha Cameron (nee Telfer).

years. During his time in Parliament he was a member of the Works Committee and later Chairman. He was a member of the Liberal Party all his life and an Elder of the Presbyterian Church for thirty years.

Mattie and Malcolm had five children — Murray, Ivan, Lorna, Mavis and Jack.

Malcolm died on 1st March, 1935, and Mattie died at Mount Gambier in 1964.

John Telfer married for the second time on 28th December, 1876. His second wife was Martha Clara Henstridge, daughter of Frederick Henstridge. The marriage took place at John's private residence at Tantanoola Creek, the officiating Minister being the Rev. Charles Tresize. John was described as a "Stockholder", aged 34, and Martha as a Spinster, aged 23.

Martha was descended from a family with a long history, as the following extract from an old letter explains:-

"When John Telfer took Martha Henstridge to wife, he married into a family with a great historic heritage. I say with some pride that I have in

The family of Malcolm and Martha Cameron.

From left:- Murray, Jack, Martha, Mavis, Malcolm, Lorna and Ivan.

Martha Cameron (nee Telfer) and her family, taken on the occasion of her 80th birthday in 1953.

From left:- Lorna, Ivan, Martha, Mavis and Murray.

my possession a book of some 400 pages, written by an English professor and archeologist, written about the family Henstridge from manuscripts in the London Museum where he used to lecture in the 1800's. We are descended from the Viking Hengest who came to England as a mercenary to assist King Vertigen of Kent. He overthrew Vertigen and ruled Kent for a number of years.

His descendants fought the Normans in Sherwood Forest with Robin Hood. The heir to Hengest-Ridge Castle was given a barony by

### PAGE 241
Martha Clara Telfer (nee Henstridge).

King John. The barons kept the manuscripts to Magna Carta secretly in Hengestridge Castle until he was forced to sign them.

Baron Henstridge (first name corruption) on his demise, left money for the building of an abbey. When the ruins of the abbey were excavated much later, the inscription on his tomb was found. Today, the Church in the town of Henstridge is built on the ruins of the abbey.

The town is now part of the estate held by the Duke of Bedford."

The first town blocks in Tantanoola were auctioned in 1879. John purchased Lot 106 for £2.15.0. In 1888 he purchased Lots 103 and 104, also for £2.15.0 and in partnership with his brother, Adam, and Heinrich Kornor, he purchased several other allotments in 1889. The three were listed as Directors of the Tantanoola Cheese and Butter Venture.

John opened a butcher shop in 1880, but closed it in 1887. He was appointed as the first Postmaster at Tantanoola in 1881.

The Post Office operated a daily service to Adelaide. In 1890 a telegraph service commenced, followed in 1891 by a money order service. From March, 1893, the Post Office was a Savings Bank Agency.

John Telfer was the Postmaster from 1881-1888; his son James held the position from 1888-1890; and his wife, Martha, and daughter, Tressie, from 1919-1921.

In 1894, when Chairman of the Directors of the Tantanoola Cheese and Butter Venture which apparently failed, he purchased the factory, the land on which it was built, and the plant, for the sum of £170. The property had cost £1,000, but as the venture was being wound up, there was no competition at the auction. At that time the price of milk was 2½d. per gallon. The factory was declared "black" as a neighbouring factory paid 3d. per gallon, and farmers chose to cart their milk by horse and dray an extra five miles in order to obtain the extra ½d. per gallon.

John Telfer was Chairman of the Tantanoola Drainage Board which first met on 26th January, 1884. He was a public spirited gentleman, and a member of the Tantanoola District Council. He was a member of the committee which was appointed to form a Presbyterian Church in Tantanoola, and the church was built on an allotment given by John.

He purchased grazing land near Tantanoola, named his property "Brackenridge", and farmed there until his son, Mark took over the land. John retired and built a home in the township where he lived until his death in 1913. Some of his descendants still live and farm in the district.

The following obituary appeared in the South Eastern Times on Friday, 10th January, 1913.

"Mr. John Telfer, who died at Tantanoola last Sunday, was the third son of Mr. James Telfer, and was born at Peel, in Roxburghshire, Scotland, in 1840, and came to Australia in 1859. For several years he was in the employ of Mr. A.B. Murray at Tungkillo. He came to Tantanoola in 1874 and for many years

### PAGE 242
The grave of John and Martha Telfer at Tantanoola Cemetery.

The inscription reads:

In loving memory of John Telfer who died 5th January, 1913, aged 72 years, and Martha Clara, his loved wife, died Nov. 13th, 1921, aged 68 years. Blessed are the dead who die in the Lord.

Eric and Jean Telfer.

was butcher on the drains, and then took over the general store in the early days of Tantanoola. For a number of years he carried on a butchery and storekeeping business very successfully, but of late years he had been in poor health. He was a member of the first district council of Tantanoola. Two brothers are left — Mr.

James Telfer J.P. of Port Lincoln and Mr. Adam F. Telfer J.P., of Mitcham. Deceased leaves a widow and large family of sons and daughters."

Martha Telfer was very supportive of her husband in all his activities. She was an active member of the Tantanoola Red Cross. She died in 1921.

A black card, printed in gold, was produced following the death of Martha Telfer (nee Henstridge). It depicts an angel, surrounded by the doves of peace, and the following words appear:-

The Lord giveth and the Lord taketh away; blessed be the name of the Lord.

IN LOVING REMEMBRANCE OF MARTHA CLARA TELFER

Relict of John Telfer

Died November 13, 1921.

Peaceful be thy silent slumber,
Peaceful in thy grave so low

Thou no more wilt join our number,
Thou no more our song will know;
Yet again we hope to meet thee,
When the day of life is fled,
And in heaven with joy to greet thee,
Where no farewell tears are shed.

Charles Telfer was the fifth child of John and Martha (nee Henstridge), who married Amelia Fitzpatrick. They had six children. Charles died at Port Augusta in 1954.

Their sixth and youngest child was Eric John Telfer. He married Kathleen Jean Faul and they had five children — Robert, Marjorie, Elaine, Anne and Kenneth.

The family grew up in the South East but moved to Port Augusta where Eric was employed by the Commonwealth Railways. He was very involved with the Port Augusta (now St. John) Ambulance, and both he and his wife, Jean, worked very hard for that cause. Eric was Secretary of the organisation for many years, holding the position until his death. Jean acted as "his secretary" helping with the letter writing, social gatherings and fund raising, to keep the ambulance going.

### PAGE 243
Eric was a very keen fisherman.

Robert, Marjorie and Anne all live in Port Augusta, and Elaine and Kenneth live in Adelaide.

Mark Telfer (1877-1946) was John Telfer's sixth child. He married Julia Matilda Seebohm (known as Tillie) in 1902. The ceremony took place in the Public Hall at Tantanoola on 9th July, 1902, the Rev. James Davies officiating. Mark was 25 years old, a Farmer, of Tantanoola, and Tillie was also 25 years old, the daughter of William Seebohm, of Tantanoola.

Both Mark and Tillie were involved in Sunday School teaching at the Tantanoola Presbyterian Church, and they were presented with a large family Bible by the Church on the occasion of their Silver Wedding.

They took over his father's farm, "Brackenridge" at Tantanoola when John Telfer retired. The farm produced crops, and ran sheep and cattle.

Mark lived there until about 1940 when he purchased a house in Millicent and retired there with two of his daughters, Jean and Maifie.

Mark was an excellent sportsman and played football and cricket. When his days as an active footballer were over and his sons were playing, he acted as Timekeeper for the Club. In cricket he was a very good batsman and a competent

Eric Telfer with daughter, Ann, 1961.

slow bowler. He won a medal for the highest batting average in the Tantanoola Cricket Club.

Mark and Tillie had seven children — Floris, Ken, Mel, Jean, Joyce, Maifie and Lindsay.

Mark's daughter, Mrs. Joyce Downs, relates the story of a hold-up on Glencoe Hill. Some time in 1927 Mark and Tillie, accompanied by their daughter, Jean, and Mrs. Annie Telfer, (who was the wife of Ray Telfer, son of Adam) were on their way to Mount Gambier to do the shopping. It was race day and it was well

Marriage Certificate of Mark Telfer and Julia Matilda Seebohm, 1902.

### PAGE 244
known that some land agents would be going to the races with their pockets full of cash for backing the horses.

As they drove up the hill they found the road blocked by branches. The women got out to shift them from the roadway, but suddenly a masked man appeared from behind the trees at the side of the road. He pointed a gun and demanded that Mark get out of the car and empty his pockets, and that the women empty their handbags. Having no alternative, they did this and threw everything on the ground. The bandit then gathered up the valuables and told them that they could now move all the branches and proceed on their way!

By watching in the rear vision mirror they saw the robber jump on to a motor bike and ride off down the hill. Mark drove on to Glencoe West and called at Mr. Vic Cram's garage where he phoned the Mount Gambier police. He was told to call at the Police Station to make his complaint, which he did.

In the meantime the bandit had gone into Tantanoola and called at the hotel. While he was inside a young lad with a very keen interest in motor bikes, took a good look at it and was later able to describe it to the police. The robber was soon caught. He admitted that he had been waiting for the land agents with all the money. He said that he needed the money to bring his mother out from England to Australia. He was an English migrant; he was tried and found guilty, and the sentence was life imprisonment.

Tillie died on 26th April, 1939, aged 62 years and Mark died on 23rd July, 1946, aged 69 years. Both are buried in the Tantanoola Cemetery.

The following Obituary appeared in the South Eastern press:-

"Mr. Mark Telfer, who died at Parkwyn Hospital on July 23rd, was born at Tantanoola 69 years ago, and lived at Tantanoola until retiring last October.

He was the son of the late Mr. and Mrs. John Telfer, of Tantanoola, and worked on his father's farm until his marriage to Julia Matilda Seebohm. He then took

The grave of Julia and Mark Telfer at Tantanoola Cemetery.

The inscription reads:

In loving memory of Julia Matilda, Beloved wife of Mark Telfer, died 26th April, 1939, aged 62 years. Also Mark Telfer, beloved husband of above. Died 23rd July, 1946, aged 69 years.

over the farm, and remained in occupation of same for over 40 years. He carried on mixed farming, and was very interested in dairying. He also owned a depot shearing shed and shore many thousands of sheep for his neighbours.

In his early life he was proprietor of the Brackenridge limestone quarry, of which many houses on the drainage area were built.

During Mr. Telfer's term as chairman for the Board of Directors for the Tantanoola Co-operative Butter, Cheese and Produce Factory, the building was improved from a make-shift to one of the best factories in the South East.

He was a life member of the Tantanoola Show Society and was President for some years. He was also President of the Tantanoola Football and Cricket Clubs. He was a keen sportsman and played cricket until he was over 60 years of age.

His wife predeceased him by seven years. There was a family of seven from the union. They are Mrs. T.C. Bunston (Floris) Tantanoola; Mrs. C. Downs (Joyce) Beachport; Misses Jean and Maifie Telfer (Millicent); and Messrs.

Ken, Mel and Lindsay Telfer, Tantanoola.

Mr. Telfer's remains were brought from Adelaide to the Presbyterian Church at Tantanoola, where a short service took place before the burial, the church being crowded. His remains were interred in the Tantanoola Cemetery on July 25. The Rev. N. Cowley officiated at the grave, and the pall bearers were Messrs. R.G. Serle, W.C.B. Haines, J. Chant and C.C. McPherson."

The eldest child of Mark and Tillie was Floris Ethel Victoria Telfer, who was born in 1904. She married Harry Bunston and they had one daughter, Jennifer, who married Alwyn Chant. Jennifer and Alwyn had three children — Terry; Graham, who married Gaye Frost; and Belinda.

The second child and eldest son was Kenneth William John Telfer, who married Jessie Sargent. Their son, Murray, who married Susanne Maloney, had five children — Joanne, Mark who married Maryann Harrap, John, Richard and Neville.

Ken played football for Tantanoola for many years. He was Captain of the team and played at centre. He also played in

Jennifer Bunston.

the Tantanoola cricket team, being a useful bat and a slow bowler. In later years he played bowls, both indoor and outdoor. He held the position of President of the Tantanoola Pastoral, Agricultural and Horticultural Society from 1953-55.

Gilbert Melville Telfer (known as Mel) married Jessie Allan and they had three children — Beth, who married Clive Lipscombe, Ian, who married Sally Taylor, and Allan who married Christine Marshall.

Mel was also a good footballer. He played at centre wing and proved formidable as a fast player and a good ball handler.

Mel was opening batsman for Tantanoola Cricket team and made many centuries. He was a most useful member of the team and he excelled as the wicket keeper.

When his eyesight no longer permitted him to play football and cricket, Mel played bowls, both indoor and outdoor, and proved to be an excellent player.

Like his father and brothers, he was interested in local affairs and was a Life Member of the Tantanoola Pastoral Agricultural and Horticultural Society.

Jean Maryann Martha Telfer did not marry. For some years she lived and worked at "Pleasant Hill", Glencoe West, the home of her father's cousin, Frank Telfer and his wife, Clara. Jean was a foundation member of the Tantanoola Women's Agricultural Bureau which was formed in 1931. When her father retired in Millicent, Jean accompanied him. She was a very skilful bowler, both indoor and outdoor.

Mark's fifth child was Joyce Elizabeth Telfer. She attended the Mount Gambier Technical School where she gained an advanced Grade III Certificate in Dressmaking in 1935. Joyce was a member of the Tantanoola Women's Agricultural Bureau until 1937, when she married Clarence Walter Downs. Her cousin, Frank Telfer of Glencoe West, recorded in his diary that he and his wife attended a Kitchen Evening for Joyce at Tantanoola on 30th January, 1937.

The marriage took place at the Presbyterian Church at Tantanoola, on 6th February, 1937. Joyce and Clarrie have six children.

Joyce is an active member of Beachport Country Women's Association and was a handicraft instructor for some years. After serving the Branch as Vice President, she was elected President for a three year term from 1983-1986. In 1982

Clarrie is a very keen shell collector and occasionally he allows the general public to view his collection, which is housed in a shed at the rear of his home. On Wednesday, 8th January, 1986, the

Advertiser interviewed Clarrie who described himself as an "oceanophile". His collection numbers possibly millions of shells and includes starfish, sea horses, shark jaws and numerous rock specimens. Clarrie's interest began in 1963 when he and Joyce were holidaying in Broome, Western Australia. They quickly learnt that shell collecting involved more than a leisurely stroll along the beach. Shell collectors were not interested in shells washed up along the shore because the dead shells lose their colour. Clarrie has exchanged specimens from as far away as Fiji, the Red Sea and California, and he has personally collected more from Queensland to

Western Australia. He can identify and name all the shells in his collection, and this is truly amazing, as Clarrie is almost completely blind. He admits that this handicap is a big frustration. He is unable adequately to police the collection so it is seldom on display to the general public. Clarrie was a fisherman at Beachport and has a great love of the sea. He in a conservationist and takes only a few shells for his collection and a few to exchange.

Joyce and Clarrie had six children. Sadly, the eldest, Gary John Downs, lived for only one week.

## ONE LESS AT HOME

The charmed circle broken — a dear face Missed, day by day, from its accustomed place

But cleansed, and saved, and perfected by grace —

One more in Heaven, One less on Earth. Its pain, its sorrow and its toil to share; One less, the pilgrim's daily cross to bear, One more the crown of ransomed saints to wear —

At home in Heaven!
DEEPLY REGRETTED

Isobel Lucieton Telfer eighth child of John Telfer married Alexander Thomson, and they had three children — Roma, Trevor and Murray.

Roland John Telfer ninth child of John Telfer did not marry. He was a very

great friend of his sister, Clara Lane, and her family, and would spend every Good Friday and Christmas Day with them. He died in 1975.

Clara Blanche Telfer tenth child of John Telfer who was born on 18th November, 1883, married Francis Edward Lane, and they had ten children. Sadly three died in infancy, but seven survived.

The marriage took place at the Tantanoola Presbyterian Church on 6th April, 1903. The marriage certificate shows Francis Lane's occupation as "Hotel Keeper". He was the Licensee of the Tantanoola Tiger Hotel from 1902-1905, together with his brother, William Lane.

Clara had a very close relationship with her brothers and sisters and often visited Tantanoola. In her 90th year she again visited her beloved Tantanoola and made a special request to be driven to the Presbyterian Church where she had been married, and to other places which held very special memories. She died five

### PAGE 252
months after this visit, on 5th June, 1974. Francis Lane died at the age of 74 in 1940.

The eldest child was Nina Amy Lane who married Walter Marks. Walter was a bricklayer by trade, and a part-time Rawleigh dealer. He played the French horn in the South Australian Symphony Orchestra. They had two sons — Brian and Ian. Brian was an Instructor at the Trade School and retired from the Regency Park College. Ian held the position of Secretary of the Timber Merchants Association of South Australia, and was later transferred to Victoria.

Ethel Jean Lane married Clarence Hugo Elix in 1934, and they had three children — Brenda, Glenn and Marilyn. Clarence Elix served in the R.A.A.F. during the Second World War. He was a Rawleigh dealer, and he died in 1984.

June Lane was the youngest member of the family of Clara and Francis Lane. At the age of 12 she contracted the dreaded

infantile paralysis (poliomyelitis) when the severe 1938-39 epidemic was raging. At that time there was no vaccine and no known cure. She spent a year in hospital and became a paraplegic. Because of the help of friends and the unfailing, devoted support of her mother, Clara, who massaged her limbs to try to bring back the life to them, June lived as near a normal life as possible.

With the help of a Hansard reporter, she studied Shorthand and became a stenographer with the Railways Department, a position she held until her marriage to Reginald Selwyn Morris in 1950.

In 1967 he became interested in municipal affairs and was elected a Councillor and later Mayor of the City of Nunawading, Victoria, from 1969-70.

Betty had a long association with the Victorian Association of Benevolent Societies. She is a Justice of the Peace and is interested in many hobbies which include oil painting, pottery, gardening, growing orchids and genealogy.

Donald Bruce Telfer is a teacher who worked for four years in high schools in Zimbabwe. He has now taken up an appointment at a College in Western Samoa.

Colin Roy Telfer was the thirteenth child of John Telfer's family. He married Elsie Cecelia O'Shea and they had three children — Colin Cyril, Phyllis May and Ruth. Colin died in 1964.

Allan Dale Telfer, the fourteenth child, was born in 1890 and died in 1955. There was no family.

Alma Tressie Telfer, who was born on 27th November, 1893, was the fifteenth child. She assisted at the Tantanoola store and post office, run by her parents. Her marriage to George Lewis Cullen took place in 1921. They had two daughters, Audrey Avis Cullen and Kathleen Ila Cullen.

George Cullen was a soldier during the First World War. He served with the 11th Australian Infantry Battalion at Gallipoli in 1915. Tragically he was killed in a motor cycle accident in 1923, leaving Tressie with two tiny children. Tressie later married Roland Moss and had a son, Gilbert Keith, who was known as Keith Cullen.

To support her family, Tressie worked as a laundry assistant, and then as a cook at a private hospital in Camberwell, Victoria.

Audrey Avis Cullen was educated at a State school and then at Swinburne Technical School. She was a Girl Guide with the 1st Hawthorn Guide Company where the Captain was Miss Gwen Swinburne, the daughter of the founder of Swinburne Technical School.

Avis enlisted in the A.W.A.S. during the Second World War and was in the Second Australian Signals Training Battalion, starting in July, 1942, and finishing in October, 1945. She enjoyed her time in the Army.

In 1948 she gained a bronze medallion for swimming and the next year obtained an Instructor's Certificate.

Avis married Eric Allan Frame in 1945. Eric served with the R.A.A.F. during the Second World War. They have two daughters — Gillian Leith Frame who was born in 1946, and Carol Margaret Frame, born 1954.

Gillian and Carol were educated at Heathmont Primary School and Ringwood High School. Gillian was a member of the 1st Heathmont Guide Company, and then of the 1st Ringwood Rangers, and with the Extension Guides. She has been in charge of a Cub Pack in Heathmont. She was a Dental Nurse and came fourth in the State Examinations. She went on to do her nursing training and gained her general and midwifery certificates. Her hobbies are ceramics, dressmaking and the local garden club. Gillian went to Singapore, West Malaysia and Thailand in 1970. She married Michael Zidlickey and they have two children —

She married José Castiello in 1978 and they have two daughters, Anna, born in 1980, and Kimberley, born in 1984. Carol and José (who is Spanish) went on a trip to Spain, via the Philippines, and then on to Rome and through Europe, in 1981. They were abroad for four months.

Kathleen Ila Cullen was born at Renmark in 1922. She was educated at the State and High School and worked with a dry cleaning firm. She married Alan Frederick Washusen on 24th August, 1946 and they lived on an orchard property. They had five children. Kath and Alan lived at Heathmont for twenty years, growing fruit, and then moved to Baddaginnie, where they farm and run Polwarth sheep.

Margaret Lynette Washusan was born on 22nd October, 1947. After primary and secondary school she was employed at the Royal Women's Hospital in Melbourne where she trained as a Radiographer. She married Kerry Ronald Davis in 1969 and they have two sons, Glen, born in 1971, and Heath, born in 1973.

Kerry is a farmer near Violet Town and they run merino sheep, Murray Grey cattle and Angora Goats. Margaret worked part time as a Radiographer at Benalla Hospital. She died on 29th October, 1986, from cancer, at the very early age of 39.

Jeffrey Alan Washusen was born on 23rd February, 1949. He was educated at Heathmont Primary School, Ringwood Technical School, Swinburne College of

Technology and Melbourne University. He did his Doctorate at the University and is now employed with the State Electricity Commission of Victoria as a Mining Engineer. He is married to Robin Elizabeth Harmsworth, and they have two sons, Daniel, born in 1978, and Thristen, born 1981. Robin is a potter and makes jewellery.

Lindsay Raymond Washusen was born on 27th July, 1950. He is not married. He works as a Telephone Technician with Telecom Australia. His hobbies are motor bikes, old cars, books and records. He lives at home with his parents.

Russell Kenneth Washusen was born on 18th March, 1952. After primary and secondary schools he attended Dookie Agricultural College where he passed a Diploma Course. He is now on a farm south west of Benalla where he runs Polwarth sheep. He is a part-time shearer and wool classer. He married Janine Elizabeth James in 1976, and they have one son, Samuel who was born in 1980. Janine is a school teacher at the primary school in Benalla.

Gary John Washusen was born on 6th December, 1956, and attended primary and secondary schools. He is employed with the Road Construction Authority as an experimental Officer. He married Adele Margaret Carpenter in 1978, and they have two children, Clarissa, born in 1980, and Christopher, born in 1983.

Gilbert Keith Cullen was born on 6th May, 1923. He was a hairdresser until ill health forced his retirement. He died in 1985. He married Betty Christina Washusen in 1947 and they had six children.

Pamela Christine Cullen, born in 1948, is married to John McCallum, and they have two children, Kate and Brett. Pam works as a nursing aide (part time).

Janet Linda Cullen, born in 1949, is married to Anthony Claxton, and they have two children — Meghan and Rebecca.

Robert Keith Cullen is not married.

### PAGE 258
John Martin Cullen, born in 1954, is married to Christine Evans and they have two children — Joel and Jessica.

Richard Alan Cullen, born in 1956, is not married.

Tressie Anne Cullen, born 1957, is married to Chris Fitzpatrick, a Bank Officer, and they have two children — Sarah and Emily.

Marion May Telfer was the youngest child of John Telfer, who married Ken Cornish. Their home was at Port Elliot. They had four children — Maurice, Kathleen, Grantley and Brenton.

### PAGE 269
## ADAM FRANCIS TELFER

## ADAM FRANCIS TELFER m. MARGARET DOUGAL
(1842-1925) TELFER (1849-1936)

Adam Francis Telfer was born at Jedburgh, Roxburghshire, Scotland, on 14th September, 1842, the youngest son of James and Elizabeth (nee Beattie) Telfer. His father died in 1863 as the result of injuries received in an agricultural accident, and it must have been a hard decision to make to leave his mother and sisters, and follow his brothers to South Australia.

He came to Australia on the steamer "London" on her second voyage. The ship sank on her next voyage! The "London" was a steamer of 1752 tons which carried 250 passengers. The Master's name was J.B. Martin. The ship left Gravesend on 30th May, 1865, bound for Melbourne, a journey of 140 days. Adam Telfer's name appears on the Shipping List as a Steerage passenger, age 22, and his nationality is given as "Irish". This is, of course, an error, probably made by the shipping clerk in his haste to process all the passengers. "Near enough was good enough" and considering the numbers of people who were emigrating during that period, it is not surprising that mistakes were made. Adam was a Scotsman through and through!

Adam landed in Melbourne and travelled overland to South Australia where he made contact with his Uncle Francis Telfer at "Macoota", Kersbrook, and with his brothers and sister who had

preceded him to Australia. He worked as a shepherd, and for a short period, joined his eldest brother, James, at Koppio Station near Port Lincoln.

In 1868 he returned to Kersbrook, and married his cousin, Margaret Dougal Telfer, the daughter of his Uncle Francis.

Adam Francis and Margaret Dougal Telfer shortly after their marriage.

### PAGE 270
The Marriage
Certificate of Adam
Francis and Margaret
Dougal Telfer.

"Edgerston" in 1903.
Lillian, Eva, Hazel,
Margaret holding
Millie, Adam, Ronald,
Elsie, Violet and Effie.

Margaret was eighteen years old at the time of the marriage. Margaret's mother was Margaret Wright who came to Australia with her parents in 1839. The story of the Wright family is included in the chapter describing the life of Francis Telfer.

The marriage of Margaret Dougal Telfer and Adam Francis Telfer took place at her father's home, "Macoota", Kersbrook, on 25th March, 1868. Adam was 25 years old and Margaret 18. The witnesses at the wedding were Margaret's cousins, George and Mary

Robertson, of Marion, and Adam's brother, John. Adam was described as a "Stockholder", of Koppio, Port Lincoln. The officiating Minister was the Rev. John Kither.

After their marriage, Adam and Margaret returned to Koppio for several years. They moved to the South East of South Australia in the early 1870's, and Adam's first recorded community appointment was as Vice President of the Millicent Show in 1878. He opened a store in Tantanoola (then known as Lucieton) in 1880.

### PAGE 271
Adam purchased land in 1888 at a cost of £2.15.0, and in 1889, in partnership with his brother, John Telfer, and Heinrich Korner, acting as Directors of the Tantanoola Cheese and Butter Venture, he shared in the purchase of more allotments.

Adam named his property "Edgerston" after the parish he had left behind in Scotland. He lived there with his family until 1908. Although he was the proprietor of a store and a partner in the cheese venture, Adam's main interest was always in sheep grazing and wool production.

The early settlers at Tantanoola, including Adam Telfer and his family, experienced all the trials of the pioneering days. The land had to be drained and cleared before crops and pastures could be sown, and this had to be done the hard way without the benefit of modern machinery. Transport was always a problem and horses were used extensively. Some crops were grown at Edgerston, but Adam's main interests were in sheep and wool. He was involved in dairying and took the milk to the local cheese factory with a horse and trolley.

Adam and Margaret had eight daughters and four sons. The eldest, Margaret Wright Telfer, died at the age of twenty, but the others lived at Edgerston, and they all went to the Tantanoola School. All the children were expected to help with the farm chores, and Eva and Effie had to milk 18 cows by hand each morning before going to school, and again at night. Violet was a particularly good cook which is not surprising as she would have had plenty of practice while feeding the large family, the working men, and the many visitors to the home.

Shearing was a particularly busy time. As well as the large family, six shearers were employed, all of whom lived on the premises for several weeks. The day commenced with early morning cups of tea served at 6 a.m., then work; a pause for a cooked breakfast; then a succession of work periods interspersed with morning tea, lunch, afternoon tea, the evening

"Edgerston" in 1986.

meal, and finally at the end of the day, supper. The amount of food needed to supply all these meals must have been enormous. And there was no electricity and no refrigeration!

The Telfers of Edgerston were renowned for their hospitality to family and friends. Visiting clergymen of all religious persuasions were always welcome at Sunday lunch, and a constant stream of visitors found a cordial welcome. There was always room for one more!

Margaret planted a tiny Norfolk Island pine tree in the front garden and nurtured it with tender loving care, covering it each night to protect it from the frost. The tree still stands to this day, towering above the house, a well-known Tantanoola landmark.

Adam and Margaret were both people of strong character with deeply religious feelings, and following the Scots tradition, they were keenly interested in the Presbyterian Church. Adam has been described by his grandson, Rex Telfer, who knew him well, as a "God-fearing man" who was "very religious". He spoke with a decided Scots accent, and he was quite capable of losing his temper when sufficiently provoked.

In 1890 a Committee was formed to undertake the building of a Presbyterian Church in Tantanoola, and Adam Telfer was appointed as its Chairman. Services had been held in the Hall, and later in the

### PAGE 272
Presbyterian (now Uniting) Church, Tantanoola.

Methodist Church. Adam's brother, John, gave the land for the proposed church. After much hard work, the foundation stone was laid by Mr. John Livingstone, M.H.R., on 15th April, 1902, and the Church was opened on 10th November, 1902.

Adam Telfer showed a keen interest in community and local government affairs. He was a member of the Tantanoola Drainage Board which met for the first time in 1884. His brother, John, was the Chairman. The Drainage Board was the fore-runner of local government in the area. The last meeting of the Drainage Board was held in December, 1887, and the first meeting of the District Council convened in January, 1888, with Adam Telfer as Chairman. He served as a Councillor from 1884-1889 and again in 1891.

With a large family to educate, his interests extended to the local school. The provisional school at Lucieton (later renamed Tantanoola) was opened in 1883 when classes were held in a wooden building. Forty-three pupils were enrolled, but the average attendance was only 26. Provisional schools were those in the charge of uncertificated teachers, and classes were usually held in halls or buildings erected by the local residents. They were not owned by the Education Department, and parents paid fees.

Education was an expensive business even in those days!

The Lucieton (Tantanoola) School, and others in the district, were supervised by the Mt. Muirhead School District Committee. During the first year of Lucieton, Adam Telfer was the Chairman, and later he was a member of the Mt. Muirhead School Board of Advice.

Adam was admitted as a member of the Mt. Gambier Freemasons Lodge No. 1207, on 24th April, 1883.

The first Tantanoola Show was held on 9th December, 1885, in the woolshed at Edgarston. The sheep were shown in the yards adjacent to the shearing shed, and the horse events were conducted on "Telfer's Road" adjacent to the property. The cattle were yarded in the railway yards. Adam was the first President of the Tantanoola Pastoral, Agricultural and Horticultural Society from 1885-1888, and again during the years 1890-1893. He was known as "Father of the Show". He also took a turn as Secretary of the Society from 1896-1898 and in 1900.

Adam's brother, John, was President from 1894-1897. Other Telfers with a commitment to the Tantanoola Show were James Telfer, Mark Telfer (a Life Member), and Messrs. Mel, Ken and Lindsay Telfer, as well as Mrs. Jessie Telfer (Life Member) and Mr. G.M. Telfer (Life Member).

At the time of the inaugural show the pioneers were still very busy building their houses, establishing their farms and clearing the land in very wet country. Self-sufficiency was their aim and most homesteads had a garden, orchard, milking cows and laying hens. Adam Telfer, who had been Vice President of the Millicent Show, was responsible for the idea of staging a local Show at Tantanoola, but because the area was sparsely populated, and travel was difficult, the idea received mixed reactions. However, there was considerable support from residents of Mount Gambier and Millicent who also exhibited their produce. Money was collected and prizes offered in many

### PAGE 273
categories. The Telfer family featured prominently in the Prize List.

Adam Telfer won Second Prize for the best 12 pansies, and Bessie Telfer won Second Prize for the best 6 verbenas. The First Prize for the best collection of wildflowers went to Lily Telfer and Bessie won Second Prize for the best table bouquet. Adam was First Prize winner for the best 10lbs. of new potatoes, and he won Second Prize for the best cheese. Bessie won Second Prize for Scones and First Prize for pastry. The two best entries in the Ironed White Shirt (Man's) section were those of Bessie and Maggie Telfer. They also excelled at Fancy Wool Work, Cushion Work and Basket and Wreath arrangements.

The Show went from strength to strength and much of its success was due to the enthusiasm and hard work of Adam Telfer, supported by his wife, Margaret and his family.

The ladies were responsible for the catering at the Shows. All their equipment had to be brought to the Showground, the water heated over open fires, and washing up done in tin bowls using homemade soap. Cold meat, hot vegetables cooked in boilers or camp ovens, homemade bread and butter, plum pudding and dairy cream formed the usual bill of fare — a busy time for the wives and daughters.

Adam Telfer was a consistent prize winner in the Sheep Section of the early Shows. One of the extra attractions displayed by Adam Telfer was a collection of eagles' eggs brought from Scotland.

The high regard in which Adam Telfer was held in the community is indicated by the fact that he was appointed as a Justice of the Peace, and he discharged the duties of this office with dignity and impartiality.

It is safe to assume that Adam could not have accomplished all that he did, nor could he have been involved in so many community activities, without the loving support of his wife, Margaret. She deserves great praise and respect. She

bore twelve children and supervised the home and Telfer's Store, as well as acting as hostess to many visitors. In the country tradition she would have spent many hours in baking for community functions and supporting her husband in many many ways. Her life was one of extremely hard work, without the benefit of any modern conveniences or transport. As medical help was not readily available, she had to be doctor and nurse to the family as well. She also took the responsibility for her little grandson, Rex, when his mother died shortly after his birth.

Margaret was a serene and gracious lady, and while no records remain of her daily life, little imagination is needed to gain some idea of the role she played at Edgerston. She was rewarded by the great respect and high esteem in which she was held by her children and her many friends.

Adam returned to Scotland to visit his family in 1902. To mark the occasion, he was presented with a leather kitbag to which was affixed a silver plaque, inscribed —

"Presented to A.F. Telfer, Esq., J.P., as a token of esteem from his friends in Tantanoola

March 28th, 1902."

The formal photograph of six of his daughters in the grounds of Edgerston was doubtless taken to be shown to his family and friends back in Scotland.

The Telfer family joined with others in the neighbourhood to make their own entertainment. Dances were held in woolsheds or barns, and Ray Telfer would take his sisters there in an old buggy. He had considerable difficulty in getting them home, for no sooner would he collect one, than another would dance off again. It was not easy to get them all together at once. By the time he did get them all home to Edgerston, it would be morning and time to milk the cows again.

The "Cyclopaedia of South Australia 1907" describes the locality where the Telfer family lived.

### PAGE 274
The Telfer girls of "Edgerston" in 1903. Hazel, Violet, Eva, Effie, Lillian, Elsie.

"Between Beachport and Mount Gambier — a railway run of fifty one miles — besides smaller stations, there are three interesting towns about ten miles apart — Rendlesham, Millicent, and Tantanoola ...

Tantanoola, which is about the same distance from Millicent on the one side as Rendlesham on the other, has the appearance of having been established for a longer period, and is at least equal in the number of buildings and present importance. It lies in the midst of a great plain, which sooner or later will be divided into farms ... Millicent is a kind of focus for all the towns and settlements within an extensive radius ...

Mount Muirhead, a smooth and verdant cone from base to summit, now looks down on an immense and prolific plain dotted with substantial homesteads. The soil will grow anything, and its capabilities for carrying stock are described in terms that sound like a fairy tale. Beyond this plain on a low ridge at the foot of which the railway has been built, the town has been laid out with excellent judgement. Considering the generally

level topography of the country, it has provided for the production of scenic effects that are charming, the white stone buildings showing through or above the foliage and ornamental trees to great advantage. Within the Millicent and Mount Muirhead districts, both of which have their Council offices in the towns, there are about 200 miles of drainage works. The drains are grouped into three systems, the outfall of them being into Lake Bonney, a sheet of water about 8 miles from Millicent..."

The Telfer pioneers could be justly proud of the contribution which they made to the development of the town and district which was described in such glowing terms.

Adam and Margaret, and the family members who were still living at home, left Tantanoola in 1908, and went to live at Mitcham, an Adelaide suburb. The whole family gathered for a photograph, probably the last one ever taken of the whole group.

A farewell social was held at Tantanoola on 14th December, 1908. The people of the district presented Adam and Margaret with a silver tea service which

### PAGE 275
is still in the possession of their great grandson, Bruce Watson, of Millicent.

Many friends gathered at the railway station to say farewell. As the train entered the platform, they all sang, "God be with you till we meet again". It must have been a very moving and emotional experience, and it was all too much for Eva, who gathered Rex in her arms, and quickly boarded the train.

The family went to live at "Waverley Park", Wattlebury Road, Lower Mitcham. The house had an interesting history. It was originally part of one of the sections granted to George Fife Angas, Henry Kingscote and Thomas Smith in 1839. In 1851 Philip Levi purchased 77 acres at £5 per acre, and he later sold part of the land to William Townsend who migrated to South Australia in 1853. Townsend started work as a shoe salesman, but he soon opened his own business, and he was eventually elected as Member for Onkaparinga in the South Australian House of Assembly.

He took up residence in Lower Mitcham and built a fine home which he called "Pellatt Villa" in honour of an English friend. He entertained his Parliamentary friends and many of the colony's important visitors. The ballroom was used either as a conference room or for lavish parties. Townsend died at Pellatt Villa in 1882.

Grand-daughters of Adam and Margaret at morning tea, using the silver tea service (1986) — Audrey Day, Gwen Burdon and Barbara Lodge.

Family group at "Edgerston" in 1908. Back:- Herb, Effie, Ted, Violet, Ray, Elsie. Seated:- Lillian, Hazel, Bessie, Eva, Frank.

### PAGE 276
The next owner was Thomas Dodd who made extensive additions by building vast stables and a coach house. It was to this house that Adam Telfer and his family moved in December, 1908. Adam re-named it "Waverley Park" because of his love of the "Waverley Novels" of Sir Walter Scott. Many of Scott's novels dealt with the social history of the Border areas of Scotland and described the valleys and mountains of Liddesdale which Adam had left behind. Scott wrote about the peasant farmers and gathered stories and ballads from the people themselves. As he lived from 1771-1832, his tales would have had special significance for Adam Telfer, who owned the complete collection of Scott's novels.

"Waverley Park" was reached from imposing gates, by a circular driveway through rows of trees. There was an attractive garden, with an organgery planted from Parramatta seedlings, and pear, fig, peach and apricot trees, grape vines, and four cherry trees which produced fruit over a period of three months. Rex Telfer recalled that the front lawn was large enough to accommodate the entire Salvation Army band which would play there occasionally, and afterwards be entertained with tea and cakes. The ballroom was used for dances, and as all the girls played the piano, there was never a shortage of music.

Adam ran sheep on the 50 acres attached to the property. There were also pigsties and a large fowl run.

During the time they lived at Mitcham, the family attended the Hawthorn Presbyterian Church. Eva Telfer was married from "Waverley Park" and the reception was held in the grounds.

Although he was 66 years of age when he moved to Adelaide, Adam had by no means retired. He carried on a business in Adelaide as a General Produce Merchant and Land Agent, and the South Australian Directory of 1910, lists him as "Sheep Farmer, of Bulls Creek Road, Mitcham", and in 1912 as a "Justice of the Peace, Produce Merchant and General Agent, Santo Building,

The gates of "Waverley Park", Lower Mitcham. Effie watching Rex on his tricycle.

"Waverley Park", Lower Mitcham.
Standing:- Herb, Adam and Hazel.
Seated:- Elsie, Margaret, Effie, Eva.

Waymouth Street, Adelaide, Telephone: Central No. 1443; private residence "Waverley Park", Lower Mitcham". (Bulls Creek Road is now Unley Road.)

At about this time Adam had a sheep station at Truro which was managed by his son, Herb. However, the severe and prolonged drought of 1914 was a disaster and this property was lost.

He continued to take an interest in the South East properties of his sons, and made frequent visits there.

Hospitality to family and friends continued at "Waverley Park". On one occasion, Margaret's great niece, Ida Latter (grand-daughter of Margaret's sister, Elizabeth Farrow), together with

### PAGE 277
"Edgerston House", Knightsbridge, about 1920.

her husband, Percy Charles Cooper, and their five children, were accommodated overnight while they broke their journey between Lower Light and Blackfellows Creek. Stabling was also found for their two horses.

The family lived at Mitcham until 1916, when they moved to Rochester Street, Knightsbridge (now Leabrook). Adam named this house "Edgerston House", and its size was more in keeping with the reduced size of the family. The nine-roomed house was set in large grounds with a creek meandering through the garden. A wooden bridge, covered with wisteria, gave access to an extensive orchard and an area for fowls and ducks. There were stables and a coach house.

When they moved to Rochester Street, the family became involved with the Knightsbridge Baptist Church. They regularly attended services, the girls taught in the Sunday School and joined the Christian Endeavour Society, and Hazel sang in the Church Choir.

When the move to Knightsbridge took place the First World War was in progress. A letter written by Adam to his son, Frank, at Glencoe West, on 5th December, 1916, makes reference to the War and the need for wool. The letter was written on what must have been a "bad day" for Adam, as the text shows. The paper is thin and folded and the writing almost illegible, but this is what he said:-

Edgerston. Decr. 5 1916

Dear Frank,

There is not much to write about but I may tell you that our potatoes are all gone with the Irish Blight. Indeed every one about here complains of the same. They looked all right when I left for the South East and when I came back they were all black as if frosted and when you dig them you get about one out of four all right.

I am enclosing the Cheese Factory Shares. You can sell them if you like, but if not sold I will try to redeem them some day.

I have had a real bad time since I came home with Influenza but I am getting better. At least I think so.

How are you getting on with the shearing? I hope you have got it safely over, and what is to do with the wool? I hear the British Government want it all. Things look very bad just now with the War and other troubles.

We had a nice day yesterday. We are all well at home except myself and to use an old Scotch Fraze I'm no that ill.

With kind regards to all at Glencoe West.

Your afft. Father,

A.F. TELFER

Adam suffered a stroke in 1919, but recovered to some extent. Two letters which he wrote to his daughter, Effie, in Perth, throw some light on the life he lived. The following are extracts from those letters:-

Edgerston June 26 1919

Dear Effie,

I have written such a long list of letters today so think I will finish up with one to you. I have written to Ted, Frank, Ray, Tom Holloway and Elsie at Broken Hill, and now if I write one to you that will be na sae bad for a blind man.

I am much the same as usual. My left hand and arm is useless but I think

### PAGE 278
Adam Francis Telfer.

Margaret Dougal Telfer.

when the warm weather comes again it will get better, but the cold weather is very bad just now and I feel it in my left arm and hand. My sight is rather improved but not quite well yet. However you can see it has improved and I can write without glasses again and you may just try and read it the best way you can. I often make a mistake and miss a line or a word or two and then I have to call Mother to correct mistakes ...

(Father has left this so I will close it for him.)

Best love from us all.

M.D.T.

On 24th March, 1920, he wrote another letter to Effie, and this indicates that the family were still very hospitable and caring.

"... I suppose you have seen Lilly before this and will know all about matters at home. I can assure you that we miss her here very much.

We expect Ted over here to see Kathleen and we also expect Ray and Herb and Elsie from Broken Hill, so will have a house full.

Yesterday was a great day in Adelaide — the return of Sir Ross and Sir Keith Smith. There was about 20,000 people to meet them. Mother and I saw them come over Mount Lofty but we did not leave the verandah to see them.

I have had an attack of lumbago this last week but I am getting over it alright.

We had a very dry season and our garden does not look well. The trees are nearly dead for the want of rain but I think they will get over it if only we get plenty of rain.

This short letter is from your affect.
Father

A.F. TELFER"

Margaret nursed him for several years. He died on 12th January, 1925, at the age of 82.

### PAGE 279
The following death notice appeared in "The Advertiser" on 13th January, 1925:- TELFER: On the 12th January at Rochester Street, Knightsbridge, Adam Francis Telfer, loved husband of Margaret Dougal. Aged 82 years.

Several days later, the following obituary appeared in "The Chronicle":

"Mr. Adam Telfer, whose death was announced in "The Advertiser" on Tuesday, was born at Jedburgh, Scotland, on September 14, 1842. He came to Australia on the steamer "London" on her second voyage to the colonies in 1865. This ship sank on her next voyage to Australia. He was engaged on Dr. Browne's station, Koppio, Port Lincoln, for a short period, afterwards taking up land in the south-eastern districts. He went in largely for grazing which he carried on successfully for 40 years, afterwards retiring to Adelaide where he lived until his death. He had been in poor health for the last six years. He leaves a widow and grown-up family of four sons and seven daughters. The sons are Mr. F.A. Telfer, of Glencoe West; J.E. Telfer, of Bool Lagoon; V.R. Telfer of Scotts Creek; and Herbert Telfer of the South East. Daughters are Mrs W.J. Beaton, Tantanoola; W. Easter, Underdale; Mrs. R. Wilson, Knightsbridge; Mrs. C. Lawrie, Enfield; Mrs. C. McElhone, Western Australia; and Misses V. and E. Telfer, of Knightsbridge. There are 28 grandchildren."

Margaret lived at Knightsbridge for the rest of her life. Unfortunately she developed cataracts and was blind in her latter years. Violet cared for her mother and helped her to her special chair by the dining room window. She wore a long black dress, and that is the way many of her grandchildren remember her. On fine days she would sit on the verandah.

During those years the home at Knightsbridge continued to be the base for members of all branches of the Telfer Clan whenever they visited Adelaide. The

On the verandah at Knightsbridge, 1931.
Grandma Telfer, Aunt Vi and Barbara
Lawrie.

traditional hospitality continued and there was a constant stream of visitors.

Several of the grandchildren boarded there while studying at the Adelaide Teachers College, and sons, daughters, grandchildren, cousins, friends and neighbours, were always welcome.

In October, 1936, Margaret broke her hip when she slipped beside her chair, and the last days of her life were filled with pain and most distressing. Violet wrote three letters to her sister, Effie, in Perth, and these tell the sad tale far better than any other words could do.

Margaret Dougal Telfer died on 7th November, 1936, at the age of 87. Her passing was mourned by her family and many many friends.

Adam and Margaret are buried at the Mitcham Cemetery. On the stone are engraved the words "God is our Refuge and Strength", the words from Margaret's favourite Psalm No. 46. This is indeed a fitting tribute to the strong characters and quiet strength of Adam and Margaret Telfer.

### PAGE 280
Acheler/Sheet

Knightbridge

October 1936

Dear Office

You will know before you get this about mothers accident: it does turn a great shoot to no as I know at will be to you, we don't know exactly how it happened whether her knee gave way (she had a weak knee) or whether she stepped, but it happened in the dining room? we had to get her to her room after that - get her to bed, blein - I think her into the rocking chair when we got her into the bedroom she had a put her bed and for the wall, but office he says he can't do anything go it he can't eat it, so we had to get sand bags a pot inside her leg to keep it in position of some every time we move her she has terrible pain, and it just about

takes the heart out of no when she calls out, a sort of dreadful to think that just when we are ready to take patients that this should happen, for we could now look after anybody else at present for mother takes all our times, then was only blue? in the house when it happened, everybody has been so good to me with offers of help. In her room is like a rose above everybody things her flowers, but comes up a dose what she can't try you will all know tonight except though we wrote to her? she won't get it until tomorrow afternoon. She will be glad she came to me. Her last week, I know you will receive a short letter tonight for me are both very tired, blue day with her part of the night when I stay the next, last night she had scarcely any club. She all send love to you? I'm not

Recheal's

Knightbridge

1302nd 1336

Dear Office

I was pleased to get your letter on Saturday, and I can just imagine how you would feel when you got the telegrams, but I just felt that you all should know as soon as possible about the accidents, poor mother she has had a lot of pain and very bad nights, the trouble is that it was so much. You were asking how it happened, well there was helping her into the dining room and when she got to her chair she sat down before she got quite up to it, blue called out to her not to sit down yet but she had gone too far I couldn't stop of course it would have happened who ever had helped her we are all very upset about

id, it seems a dreadful thing to have happened to mothers, but I will let you know how she's perhaps I must write again on Thursday all the balls are fine and talking and it is just about impossible to write mother I do her love to you? I'm a and he do blue? No.

Your sister
Michael
