### PAGE 282
## MARGARET WRIGHT TELFER

first child of Adam and Margaret Telfer

Margaret Wright Telfer.

Margaret Wright Telfer, who was named after her maternal grandmother, was born at Koppio in 1869. She was the eldest child of Adam and Margaret Telfer. Unfortunately Margaret contracted pneumonia and died just before her twenty-first birthday. Her parents had planned to give her a piano as a birthday present, but she did not live long enough to receive and enjoy it. Margaret Wright Telfer is buried in the Mitcham Cemetery.

## ELIZABETH BEATTIE TELFER

second child of Adam and Margaret Telfer

The second child to be born was also a daughter, Elizabeth Beattie Telfer. She was named, as was the custom, after her father's mother. Elizabeth, known always as "Bessie", grew up in the family home, "Edgerston" at Tantanoola. In 1895 she married William John Beaton and the story of her family was recorded by her son, Ken, shortly before his untimely death in 1986. Ken's account is reproduced here without alteration or addition.

"The Beaton clan is descended through Adam Francis Telfer, son of James Telfer and Elizabeth Beattie, and Margaret Dougal Telfer, daughter of Francis Telfer and Margaret Wright. Adam and Margaret had twelve children — Margaret, Elizabeth (Bessie, our mother),

Lillian, Francis, Ted, Ray, Herbert, Violet, Eva, Effie, Elsie and Hazel.

Bessie married William John Beaton in the 1890's and there were eight children of the marriage — Olive, Ivy, Margaret, Bill, Harold and Frank (twins) Ken and Colin.

William John Beaton and Bessie lived in Tantanoola — the Adam Telfers also lived there — and he was a Postmaster, Clerk of the Council, and Chairman of the Council before settling on a farm about five miles from the town. He was also a foundation member of the Tantanoola Football Club.

It was a mixed farm with crops of barley and oats, a few sheep and 15 or 20 dairy cattle.

The eldest in the family did their schooling in Tantanoola, and the

### PAGE 283
The Beaton Family in 1908. William and Bessie nursing the twins, Frank and Harold, with Ivy, Olive, Margaret and Bill.

youngest went to Buchan's Creek school and finally Tantanoola. Ken and Colin rode a pony five miles each way to school, and were both rewarded with exhibitions (scholarships). Ken got 670 marks out of 700, and this was beaten by Colin the next year with 677 out of 700 in the Qualifying Certificate (Q.C.) examinations. This entitled them to a small boarding allowance when they attended Mount Gambier High School — a big help in the days of little money.

All the boys were keen sportsmen. Harold and Frank played football and cricket or tennis in the Riverland and at other centres where they were stationed. Ken, when still at High School, played football for Mount Gambier North, and when at Adelaide Teachers College, played for the College team in A.I Amateur League. He also played in the country centres where he was stationed. He also played cricket for the College A team and made several centuries in the Country Associations of South East, Yorke Peninsula and Lameroo District. His highest score of 175 Not Out for Hatherleigh still stands as a record.

Colin played most of his football for Tantanoola and was a member of the Inter-Association teams on several occasions. He was also a useful cricketer for Tantanoola.

Six of the eight children — Olive, Ivy, Margaret, Harold, Frank and Ken — were members of the Education Department and taught in many parts of the State. All were primary school teachers.

Ken later branched out and was appointed by the Technical Section of the Department to pioneer education for adults in the country — the fore-runner of the present T.A.F.E. College which he developed for twenty years before his retirement in January, 1979.

Harold, Frank and Colin joined the A.I.F. at the outbreak of World War II and saw service in New Guinea and the Asian mainland. Colin was a prisoner-of-war at Changi prison in Singapore and was on the Burma Railway with his inhuman captors. He died while on the project.

Harold and Frank returned, but war had an influence on their deaths, as Harold died of a stroke in Snowtown, and

### PAGE 284
Frank was bed-ridden for some years with lung troubles until his death.

Ivy married Clarrie Cadd, but there were no children. Margaret married Alfred Cornish, a veteran of the First World War, but there were also no children. William (Bill) did not marry.

Frank married Jean McKay and had one son, David, who is also a school teacher. David married Celine Coghlan and has three children — Darien, Jacqui, and Caela.

Harold married Blanche Longmire, of Snowtown, and they had two children — Elizabeth and Gregory. Elizabeth married Bruce Proud, a member of one of the Riverland's best known sporting families. Bruce played football for South Adelaide at League level and some of his brothers played for other Clubs in Adelaide. Elizabeth herself was a very good tennis player. They have three sons — Brett, Andrew and Philip. Gregory married Jill Corey and they have two children — David and James.

Ken married Jeanne Stacey and they had two children — Judith and Colleen. Both are married. Judith married Ronald Hicks and lives on a farm between Kybybolite and Apsley over the Victorian border. They have two daughters — Cathryn and Michelle. Colleen married Peter Redman, a Police Officer, and they reside at Mount Barker where Peter is Officer-in-Charge. They have a daughter and son — Karen and Gregory.

Colleen and Judy were both very good netball players, and Colleen played Pennant Tennis for Woodville on occasions when in the Commercial Bank in Adelaide. The children of both are also very sports minded.

Colin did not marry and his life was cut short when a prisoner-of-war of the Japanese."

William John Beaton died on 18th June, 1942, and Bessie died on 1st July, 1948. At the time of her death Bessie was staying at Rochester Street, Knightsbridge, where she had gone to help her sister, Elsie, while Violet had a short holiday.

Olive and Colin Beaton.

William and Bessie are both buried at the Tantanoola cemetery, and a memorial to their youngest son, Colin, also appears on the stone. The inscription reads — In loving memory of William John, beloved husband of Bessie B. Beaton. Died 18th June, 1942, aged 73 years. In God's Care. Also a loving wife and devoted mother, Bessie B. Beaton, died 1st July, 1948, aged 77 years. Peacefully sleeping.

On the other side of the stone appear the words — Colin L. Beaton. Died whilst Prisoner of War 14th May, 1944. Aged 29 years Lest We Forget.

Olive Clarice Beaton was a primary school teacher and she taught in many one-teacher schools in South Australia. She had the full responsibility for teaching Grades 1 to 7 as well as all the administrative duties to perform. She

### PAGE 285
was a very skilled needle-woman and embroiderer, as well as being very accomplished in knitting and crochet skills. Every new baby in the family was sure to receive some beautifully knitted baby garments. The supper cloths, doyleys and other "fancy work" are highly prized by those who were fortunate enough to receive them.

Olive retired from teaching as a result of ill-health, and she lived for some years with her sister, Margaret, at Broadview. Her last years were spent at Kirkholme Presbyterian Rest Home, Goodwood, where she died in 1987, at the age of 91.

Ivy Beaton married Clarrie Cadd, a farmer from Boors Plains near Kadina. Ivy and Clarrie worked the farm until they retired and went to live in Kadina. Both were very keen bowlers and played for the Wallaroo Mines Club. Clarrie was the greenkeeper for many years. He was a leading top division skipper and had the distinction of representing the State in interstate bowls in 1956.

Ivy and Clarrie travelled extensively throughout Australia by caravan during their retirement years. Many of their trips included bowls carnivals. One such visit on their calendar was the annual Victor Harbor Bowling Club March Carnival which they entered each year for 25 years. They liked Victor Harbor so much that they finally moved there from Kadina and spent their last years doing what they so much enjoyed — playing bowls for the Victor Harbor Club. Ivy died in 1980 and Clarrie died in 1985.

Margaret (known as Marge) married Alfred Cornish and they lived at Lameroo. She did not have children of her own but cared for her niece, Nell Fleming, as if she were her own daughter. Marge was widowed early in life. She bought a house in Broadview, an Adelaide suburb, and resumed teaching. Even after she had officially retired, her services were much in demand as a relief teacher. She was a very bright and lively person, but the years finally took their toll and she entered a retirement home in Adelaide. In the meantime, Nell had married and,

Margaret Beaton.

with her family, moved to Western Australia. Rather than leave Marge on her own in Adelaide, they moved her to a Rest Home in Western Australia so that she could be nearer to them. Her memory failed with the passing of the years, and she died in January, 1989, the last of her family.

Francis Telfer Beaton and Harold Edgerston Beaton (known as Frank and Hal) were born in Millicent on 14th August, 1908. They attended Tantanoola Primary School and Mount Gambier High School. They both trained as teachers and while studying at Adelaide Teachers College they boarded with their Aunts, Vi and Elsie Telfer, at Knightsbridge.

Frank's first school was in the River Murray district, and he later taught at many other schools in South Australia.

### PAGE 286
The Beaton sons. Colin, Harold, Ken. Frank and Bill.

He opened the primary school at Mount Burr in the South East of the State.

Frank was a good cricketer and played in matches in the South East. He was very interested in all sports, but ill-health restricted his sporting activities after he returned from service in the Second World War.

He married Jean McKay in 1938. The ceremony took place in Scots Church, North Terrace. He was then appointed to the Sebastopol School and was teaching there when he enlisted in the Army in August, 1940. After training for some time as a signaller with the 2/14 Field Regiment, he left for Singapore. However, the Regiment had only got as far as Darwin when Singapore fell to the Japanese. They were stationed in Darwin for 18 months and were there when Darwin was bombed by the Japanese.

While Frank was stationed in Darwin he received word that his son, David, was born, on 14th March, 1942.

The regiment later returned to Sydney, and then left for New Guinea. Frank served in New Guinea for the remainder of the War.

After the War was finished, he returned to the Education Department in spite of ill health. He taught at Reeves Plains, Croydon, Paringa Park, Henley Beach and Oaklands. While he was at Croydon School he wrote the Pathfinder Grade 4 Arithmetic Book.

His health deteriorated greatly and he was forced to retire from teaching because of this. He was too ill to participate in his other great interests – sport and gardening. Sadly he was bedridden for six years with Emphysema, and he died in 1977.

His son, David, is also in the Education Department. He married Celine Coghlan and their three children, Darien, Jacqui and Caela, were a great joy to their grandfather, Frank.

After he had passed his examinations at the Adelaide Teachers College, Harold (Hal) taught at Concord, Salt Lake, Walloway, Yakilo, Salters Springs and Ponde, all small schools where he too had responsibility for multiple classes. His last appointment was at Snowtown Area School where, being musically inclined, he produced many school concerts of very high standard. He was a keen sportsman and played football, tennis, cricket, bowls and golf. He won the Snowtown Golf Club Championship. During his time at Snowtown he married Blanche Longmire.

Hal enlisted and served for four years in World War II. He was a Gunner in the

Elizabeth Beattie (Bessie) Beaton.

### PAGE 287
## Called to Higher Service

## In Loving Memory

of

## PRIVATE COLIN L. BEATON

Died of illness while P.O.W. in Japanese hands.

June 1st, 1944

Rest on in peace, oh, warrior brave,
Now your talk is o'er:
All your best you gladly gave
To help us win the war,
But the "last part" now has rounded,
You're laid aside your sword,
And God has called you from us
To your nobly-unt reward.
And this prayer we all are breathing,
Though our hearts are wrong with pain,
Rest on in peace, brave soldier boy,
Till the bugle sounds again.

Memorial Card for Colin Beaton.

2/2 Australian A.A. Regiment. After the war he returned to Snowtown Area School where he was recognised as a devoted teacher who was highly respected by his students.

He was a faithful member of the Presbyterian Church.

Hal died suddenly at Snowtown at the age of 57. He was cremated at Centennial Park and his ashes are interred in the R.S.L. rose garden.

Blanche (known to the family as "Barney") continued to live at Snowtown where she cared for her aged parents and two Aunts. There were two children — Elizabeth and Gregory. Elizabeth married Bruce Proud and made her home at Loxton.

After the death of her Aunts, Barney moved to Loxton where she has been very involved in many community activities.

When Ken Beaton was appointed to organise the first country adult education division, he was stationed at Murray

Bridge. He was responsible for a very large area of South Australia, stretching through the Riverland, the Murray Mallee and the South East. The results of his hard work are very evident today as community colleges have been established in many centres of the State, and adult education has become part of our way of life.

Ken had a block of land near Murray Bridge, and after his retirement from the Education Department, he ran beef cattle there as a paying hobby.

He died suddenly on Father's Day, 1986. His wife, Jeanne, had been in failing health for some time, and she now lives at a Rest Home in Murray Bridge.

Ken's granddaughter, Cathryn Hicks, married in 1987. She lives only ten miles from her parents, and goes to work in Naracoorte. Her sister, Michelle Hicks, still lives at home on the farm. She is an apprentice landscape gardener at Edenhope in Victoria.

### PAGE 288
## third child of Adam and Margaret Telfer

Lillian May Telfer was born in 1872, the third daughter of Adam and Margaret. Lillian (affectionately known as "Lil") grew up in the family home at Tantanoola. Shortly after the family moved to Adelaide, Lil went to Western Australia where she worked for some years as a nurse in and around Kalgoorlie and Norseman.

She met Charles McElhone, an Irishman, who had served in the 16th Battalion A.I.F. He returned to Western Australia after the First World War and worked as a foreman on the pipeline being constructed from Mundaring to Kalgoorlie.

They were married on 16th April, 1922. In 1921 the first Group Settlements were started in the south-west of Western Australia, the brainchild of the then Premier, Sir James Mitchell. (He was affectionately known as Jimmy Moo-

Lillian May Telfer.

Cow.) In 1922 the Lanark Group 19 was started, about 16 miles (approx. 25km) out of Manjimup, and Charles and Lil were part of that group. They were allocated approximately 85 acres to start their dairy farm. As time went on they acquired more land until they had in all, 300 acres.

Lil was no stranger to dairying as she would have been involved with milking the cows at Edgerston, Tantanoola when she was growing up.

Charles and Lil built their dairy herd to 30 milking cows, with lots of pigs to drink the skim milk. In those early days the milk was separated and only the cream went to the Butter Factory.

They had fowls and a large vegetable garden, and the work was hard. Lil made her own bread and churned the butter. She was quite famous for her fruit cakes, and usually made all the "special occasion" cakes for the district.

She washed the clothes in an old kerosene tin, soaked overnight and boiled next morning on an open fire out in the yard, transferring the clothes by copper stick into a large tub, then washed and rinsed by hand.

Lighting was by kerosene lamp for the main room, and at bed time a candle lit the way.

Lil helped to clear the land, often taking one end of the cross-cut saw to fell the trees after they had been previously ring-barked and allowed to die. She was often called on for her nursing experience to help with a birth or to patch up a nasty accident. With no doctor or hospital for miles, it was often a matter of life and death.

Lil often laughingly referred to their property as "Slave-On Farm", and by today's standards, it was! But life improved and after living in tents to begin with, they had a house built. It was a standard "Groupie" house with no

### PAGE 289
Home of Charles and Lil McElhone at Manjimup.

bathroom or laundry, no water laid on, and the toilet a trek of some distance from the house. This was, no doubt, more comfortable than a tent.

By this time Lil had planted a garden and a wonderful assortment of fruit trees. In season, the fruit was made into preserves and jams. The bacon was smoked and cured in the kitchen, hanging in cheese cloth bags from the rafters, and how wonderful it tasted!

By the 1930's the mail was delivered a couple of times a week, brought out to the farms by a neighbour who had a contract for the mail. He was an obliging fellow and one could usually get a lift into Manjimup in the back of his truck.

Lil and Charlie owned a radio, and Charlie enjoyed the market reports each day after his mid-day meal. The only other entertainment was the Saturday night dance in the local hall. The ladies took a plate, and as most of them were members of the C.W.A., a roster was put together for the preparation of the supper. Everyone enjoyed the exchange of news and views. The babies and young children were put to sleep under the seats in the hall. There was always a rousing band made up with whatever talent was available. The men stood outside around a huge fire and consumed quantities of alcohol — then it was into the cart and home.

In the late 1940's they sold their farm which, by then, they owned. This was a

Effie Wilson and Lillian McElhone.

great achievement as many people were forced off the land by hardship or mismanagement in those depression years.

Charles and Lily bought land in the township of Manjimup and built a house for their retirement.

In 1950 they paid a last visit to South Australia, and spent some weeks visiting Lil's brothers and sisters. It was a very happy time for all.

Lil died in 1951 at the age of 79. Charles lived a lonely life for many years after Lil's death. He managed on his own with the help of some kind neighbours and friends, and while his health was generally good, he suffered badly with arthritis and was forced to use crutches or a stick. He was always pleased to see Lil's family and made them most welcome. He died in 1971 at the age of 86.

### PAGE 290
## FRANCIS ADAM TELFER

fourth child of Adam and Margaret Telfer

Francis Adam Telfer.

Francis Adam Telfer was the fourth child and eldest son of Adam and Margaret. He was named after his father and also Margaret's father, Francis. He was born on 17th August, 1875, and by law was duly vaccinated against the dread disease of smallpox.

Frank spent his early years at Edgerston and could remember his mother planting the Norfolk Island pinetree in the front garden. She would cover it each evening to protect it from the frost. The tree survives to this day.

In 1899 he married Clara Ann Millicent Dunn. The marriage took place on 23rd October, 1899, at Edgerston, Tantanoola, the home of Frank's father, Adam Telfer. The witnesses were Frank's sister, Lillian, and his cousin, Mark Telfer.

Frank and Clara settled at Glencoe West on a farm which they called "Pleasant Hill". Frank kept a daily diary of his farming activities and the volumes from 1908 until his death in 1955 have been preserved by his son, Ronald.

His early activities included growing potatoes, raising sheep, cattle and pigs and growing wheat and barley. On 25th May, 1908, he sold two pigs for £4.10.0 ($9). The price of potatoes in that year was £3.5.0 ($7) per ton and heifers in top condition brought £4.5.0 ($8.50) per head. Seed barley was 4/3 (43 cents) per bushel. In August, 1908 he purchased 37 ewes with lambs at foot for 9/- (90 cents) per head. The price of a good milking cow was £5 ($10). "Nugget" the horse was sold at the Millicent sale for £26 ($52). It must have been rather disconcerting because the next day Nugget found his way home to Glencoe West!

Besides managing his own property, Frank assisted other local farmers with harvesting, shearing, etc.

The working week was six days from before daylight until after dark. The horses had to be fed and cared for as they played a most important part in the work of running the farm. The family depended on the horse for work and for transport to church, for shopping, visiting and entertainment.

Clearing the land, chopping firewood and trapping rabbits all occupied a great deal of time. The preparation of the ground, planting, harvesting, loading onto waggons and carting of the potatoes to the railway sidings, then loading on to the railway trucks, were all done the hard way without benefit of machinery and loading equipment. Farming at that time was a matter of long hours and arduous labour. Sunday was the day of rest and the only activities recorded were occasional visits by friends or relatives or

### PAGE 291
Compulsory Vaccination — Francis Adam Telfer, 1875.

Marriage Certificate of Francis Adam Telfer and Clara Ann Millicent Dunn, 1899.

visits to them. Weekly visits were made to Mount Gambier for shopping.

Clara and the children had an annual holiday at Beachport. In order to get there they were taken by horse and buggy to Tantanoola to catch the train.

In 1909 Frank built a dairy. He carted the stone for the walls and floor and helped in the actual building.

There was a very wet winter in that year and the bullock waggon became bogged while carting the potatoes. Considerable time and effort were necessary to extricate the animals.

Each year Frank went to the Adelaide

Show. This entailed a two day journey each way. He exhibited fat lambs and Lincoln ewes in the 1909 Tantanoola Show and won the 1st and 2nd prizes for the fat lambs.

He sold meat to his working man — quarter of mutton (10lbs or 5kg) for 2/6 (25 cents). On 1st December, 1909 he purchased a new pair of boots for 13/9 ($1.40).

The Cyclopaedia of South Australia 1909 described the Glencoe district in glowing terms:-

"One of the richest portions of the fertile district by which Mount Gambier

### PAGE 292
Frank and Clara Telfer with Millie, Dorothy and Ronald, 1908.

is surrounded lies to the north-west and in the heart of it, about eighteen miles from the Mount, the thriving township of Glencoe has been established. It is reached by a branch line of less than ten miles in length from the Naracoorte line, the junction being at Wandillo. This part of the country was originally occupied for pastoral purposes, but when it was surveyed and divided into smaller holdings suitable for agriculturists, there was a rush of applicants, and marvellous statements were made of its productiveness. It is an almost ideal dairying district, and the yield per acre of root crops is considered, even in the prosperous South East as exceptional. Land has frequently changed hands at from £30 to £35 per acre since the capabilities of the locality were tested, and as much as £40 has been paid for small holdings that are especially adapted for potato growing. Glencoe is fully supplied with the customary aids to moral and mental culture, churches, school, etc. and social organisations. Its chief manufacturing industry is the Edendale Cheese Factory, which is fitted up with the most modern appliances, and capable of handling a thousand gallons of milk per day. The product has already obtained a more than local reputation,

and there is good prospect of the output being largely increased."

When Frank decided in 1910 to build new stables, he went to the forest and cut the timber himself, and then assisted in the construction of the building.

There was an extensive garden at Pleasant Hill and Frank and Clara grew most of their own fruit and vegetables. The old buggy was sold for £15 ($30) and a new one purchased for £34.10.0 ($69). At this time the price of wheat was 3/- (30 cents) per bushel.

Frank was a keen cricketer and played for the Glencoe team in the local competition. His other love was football.

He added to his land holdings in 1913 by purchasing a block of land at Koorine and this enabled him to carry more sheep and cattle. In February, 1914, there was a fire at Koorine. The block was completely burnt out and 74 sheep were lost. More land was acquired at Bool Lagoon and disaster struck there in 1925 when another fire occurred. Frank's son, Ronald, was overseeing the Bool Lagoon property at the time.

Frank purchased his first wireless set in 1926, having cut and erected the necessary wireless pole himself.

He purchased another block of land at Wepar and once again was able to increase the carrying capacity and grow more potatoes.

Frank, Clara and family were all members of the Presbyterian Church at Glencoe West.

Frank did a lot of contract reaping for neighbours and later undertook a lot of cropping work for many in the district. He purchased a tractor in 1929 and had a lot of teething problems with the machine. There are many references in his diaries to visits by the “tractor expert”, and he must have wondered on many occasions whether the new fangled machine was worth all the trouble. Horses continued to be used for many years.

In between milking cows, growing potatoes, wheat, barley and oats, caring for sheep and cattle, shearing, growing the family's fruit and vegetables, and

### PAGE 293
maintaining fences and equipment, he managed to find time to take an interest in community affairs. His main interest was in the building and maintenance of the Glencoe West District Hall. He attended many working bees and fund raising functions for this project and the "Hall Fair" was an annual commitment for the family.

Other community projects included the planting of pines in the Recreation Ground and carting the stone for the Glencoe West tennis courts.

He took a petition round the district in a bid to get a bitumen road, and then went to Tantanoola to meet the Commissioner of Highways.

Rabbits were a constant problem in the days before myxomotosis and he spent much time and ingenuity in trying to eradicate this pest.

Cricket was a great love. In 1937, he and his son, Ronald, went to Melbourne and saw Australia win the Fifth Test and the Ashes, with Don Bradman the star. Frank and Ronald stayed with his sister, Hazel, who was living in Melbourne at the time.

Regular visitors at Pleasant Hill were Mr. and Mrs. Joe Lane, of Tantanoola. Mrs. Lane was Clara's aunt and visits between the two families occurred frequently. Joe and Polly Lane celebrated their diamond wedding on 30th April, 1943, and as several Telfers were involved

"Pleasant Hill", Glencoe West.

in the celebrations, and as Joe Lane was closely connected with Francis Edward Lane who married Frank's cousin, the report of the Golden Wedding which appeared in the local paper, is reproduced here:-

"On Friday evening, April 30, a gathering of friends and relatives at the home of Mr. and Mrs. J.A. Lane joined them in the celebration of their diamond wedding. A pleasant evening was spent. The two tier wedding cake, which was made by Mr. Lair and prettily decorated by the Misses M. and J. Telfer was given to Mr. and Mrs. Lane by the former's niece and nephew, Mrs. Frank Telfer (Glencoe) and Mr. Steve Dunn (Tantanoola). Mrs. M. Klem presented a very beautiful posy and buttonhole to the guests of the evening. They also received many gifts and congratulatory telegrams. Speeches appropriate to the occasion were made by Messrs. F.A. Telfer and James Corcoran, and the reply on behalf of the happy couple was made by Mr. C.W. Haines. Mr. Lane is the son of the late Mr. Aaron Lane, first licensee of the Somerset Hotel at Millicent, and later of the Bay View Hotel at Beachport. He was born at Mt. Rouse (now Penshurst) nearly 85 years ago, and came to the South East at the age of eight years. He has resided here continuously for 77 years.

Mrs. Lane is a daughter of the late William Varcoe and was born in Adelaide 83 years ago. Her parents were then living at Goolwa. When she was 12 years of age (71 years ago) Mr. Varcoe took up land at Kongorong and brought his family to the South East. Later on they settled at Millicent ...

It was in the year 1883 that the wedding of Mary Ann Varcoe and Joseph Aaron Lane was solemnised by the Church of England minister, the Rev. Hartley Williams, at the Bay View Hotel, Beachport. The wedding cake was a large three tier confection made and decorated by the late Mr. A. Kieselbach of Mount Gambier. Mr. Lane, at the time of his wedding, was the proprietor of the Railway Hotel, Tantanoola (now known as the Tantanoola Tiger Hotel), and a rather unique happening was that Mr.

### PAGE 294
Lane had to attend (as a witness) a court case at Millicent on his wedding day. It was 12 o'clock before he could leave the court house, and he had only three hours in which to travel to Beachport, get married, and catch the 3 o'clock train back to Tantanoola. There were no motor cars in those days. He found it necessary to ride to Beachport. He changed horses at Rendelsham, and although he forced the pace, he arrived at Beachport half an hour late for his wedding, with the result that the bride and bridegroom could not be present at their own wedding breakfast which was held at the Bay View Hotel in their absence. They have lived at Tantanoola ever since and have endeared themselves to the Tantanoola people. Although Mr. and Mrs. Lane are advanced in years, they are young in spirit. Mr. Lane was honorary secretary to the Tantanoola Turf Club for 25 years, and took an active part in everything affecting the welfare of the district in which he lives. He relinquished hotelkeeping many years ago."

A serious fire broke out at Pleasant Hill on 14th January, 1944, a hot, north-windy day. A spark from the kitchen chimney ignited grass in the garden and the fire quickly spread to the hedge and then through the adjoining paddock. Desperate efforts to halt the fire at the road failed; the fire jumped the road, burnt through the paddocks and into the pine forest where it burnt all night. At Pleasant Hill the fire burnt right up to the house and flames licked the verandah posts before they were extinguished.

Frank celebrated his 70th birthday with a family party on 19th August, 1945. In that year he purchased a block of land in Mount Gambier with a view to eventually building a house and retiring. In the meantime his busy life on the farm continued. In that year, too, Clara suffered a serious injury when her hand was caught in the wringer of the washing machine. She spent a month in hospital.

The building of the house in Mount Gambier presented all the problems which were common after the Second

World War. Shortages of materials and skilled labour caused many frustrations, but Frank and Clara moved into their new home on 21st October, 1949. Two days later they celebrated their Golden Wedding with a party attended by all the family.

They were given a farewell social at Glencoe West, and the big attendance at that function was indicative of the high esteem in which they were held.

After moving to Mount Gambier their home became the base for the family to visit when they were in town for shopping. The grandchildren were frequent overnight visitors, and almost every day some member of the family called for lunch.

Frank took up bowls, and continued to take an interest in cricket and football. He worked hard establishing a fruit and vegetable garden for himself and growing flowers for Clara.

He still had cattle at Koorine and had to inspect them regularly. The cattle sales of the district interested him and he went to most of them. On one occasion he purchased a bull for 25 guineas. His niece, Barbara, asked him, "What could I do with 25 guineas?" to which he replied with a grin, "You could buy a bull!" In his "spare time" he helped Gordon, Ronald and Millie in their gardens.

Frank's busy life continued right to the end. On 7th May, 1955, he had an x-ray, followed by a visit to the football match.

During the following two weeks he fed the cattle, changed the oil in his car, worked in the garden, cut the lawns, did the shopping, went to Glencoe and Koorine, mended a fence and a gate and shifted the cattle. His last diary entry in his handwriting reads — “May 21 Home in morning. Went to football afternoon.”

His daughter, Millie, completed the record, which is quoted verbatim.

May 22 Took Dad to Hospital. Very sick all day. Uncle Ted, Steve and Eric came.

23 Dad had a blood test. Still very sick. Mum and I went up to see him.

### PAGE 295
24 Dad had his operation — a very big one. Called up at night.

25 Dad still very sick. Estelle and Bert came from Geelong.

May 26 Ronald and Gordon came up. Went up to see Dad. He was still unconscious. Looked very sick.

27 Dear old Dad passed away — never gained consciousness. Ronald and Gordon here nearly all night.

28 A very sad day for us all. Ronald and Gordon made arrangements for the funeral.

29 Dad's funeral. A very big one and a lovely lot of wreaths.

Francis Adam Telfer died just before his 80th birthday. His wife, Clara, continued to live in Mount Gambier until her death in 1966.

Frank and Clara had five children —
Ronald Edgerston Telfer
Millicent Joyce Telfer
Dorothy Margaret Hope Telfer
Gordon Francis Telfer
Estelle May Telfer

Ronald Edgerston Telfer was born in 1900. He grew up at Pleasant Hill and farmed at Glencoe West all his working life. He was involved in dairying, potato growing, cropping, and sheep and cattle husbandry.

He married Olive Von Duve in 1929, and they built a house on land adjacent to Pleasant Hill. They had one daughter, Maureen, born in 1936.

Ronald and Ollie shared an interest in all the activities in Glencoe West. Ollie was a keen gardener and a very good cook. Her garden provided most of the fruit and vegetables needed by the household, and she grew beautiful hydrangeas as well as other flowers and shrubs. Both Ronald and Ollie attended the Glencoe West Presbyterian Church.

Ronald worked closely with his father in all aspects of their farming operations. He owned a block of land at Wepar where he ran cattle for many years.

After his retirement from active farming life he continued to live on his

Wedding party of Ronald and Olive Telfer. From left:- Gordon Telfer, Olive, Ronald, and Iris Beuck.

Mark, Tania, Haydn, Maureen and Rohan Dow.

farm at Glencoe West, but finally made the move to Mount Gambier in 1987. His health deteriorated and he died in 1988 at the age of 88.

Maureen Telfer married Ivan Lester Dow, a farmer of Glencoe West, in 1957. They had four children —
Haydon Ronald Dow
Mark Stanley Dow
Rohan Scott Dow
Tania Michell Dow

Tragedy struck the family when Lester was killed in a road accident in 1984. Maureen continues to manage the farm with the assistance of her family.

Millicent Joyce Telfer was born in 1901. She grew up at Pleasant Hill and went to

### PAGE 296
school in Glencoe West, and in 1926 she married Leslie Gordon Case.

Millie and Les had a farm at Glencoe West. They milked cows, had sheep and cattle, and grew potatoes and other crops.

There were two children — Bruce Francis Case and Roma Joyce Case. Les Case died in 1953 at the age of 59. The esteem in which he was held was evident when a very large crowd of friends attended his funeral.

Millie continued to live at Glencoe West for some years, but after the death of her father in 1955, she went to live with her mother in Mount Gambier.

She was an excellent cook and a very keen gardener. She joined in all women's activities in Glencoe West and was a member of the Presbyterian Church. She was often called upon to ice cakes for special occasions.

Her chief recreation was croquet and she was a championship player, travelling all over South Australia and interstate to play the game.

Following the death of her mother in 1966, Millie bought another house in Mount Gambier, and later moved to a comfortable unit.

Unfortunately advancing years and deteriorating health forced her to give up her independence, and she now resides in a home for the aged in Mount Gambier.

Bruce Francis Case managed the Glencoe West farm for some years, but this was later sold and he moved to another property at Nangwarry. He married Maureen Lynch in 1963, and they have three daughters — Carol Lesley Case, Jenine Marce Case and Denise Francis Case. Disaster struck Bruce and Maureen in the 1983 Ash Wednesday bushfires, when their home and property were destroyed. They have since rebuilt their house.

Roma Joyce Case was born in 1934. Her childhood was marred by ill health but she did not allow this to spoil her enjoyment of life. She grew up in Glencoe West and went to school there. One of her

Millie and Les Case in 1926.

Millie, Roma and Bruce Case at Roma's 21st Birthday in 1955.

### PAGE 297
great interests was popular music and it was through this common interest that she met her husband, Colin Schultz. Colin is a farmer at Jabuk, South Australia. He has a 220 acre property and he grows some crops and raises cattle.

Dorothy Margaret Hope Telfer, the third child of Frank and Clara Telfer, was born in 1905. She helped her father on the farm and fed the calves and milked the cows. She too is an excellent cook. In 1928 she married Allen Ferdinand Koop, of Glencoe West.

For some time they lived at Glencoe West, then moved to Mount Schank, then back to Glencoe West, then to Naracoorte, and finally to Geelong in Victoria where Dorothy still lives.

During their time at Glencoe West Dorothy and Allen had many visitors. They had a large truck which was necessary for Allen's business, but when it was not required for that purpose, it became the conveyance to take the young folks to the movies at Mount Gambier. Boys and girls wrapped themselves in rugs for warmth on the freezing South East nights and travelled on the back of the truck to "the Mount". Usually the girls went to the movies and the boys to the boxing. After a hot snack supper the long cold journey home was undertaken.

There were four children — Beryl Margaret Koop, Jeffrey Allen Koop, Laurie Koop and Trevor Francis Koop.

The children joined in the sports at Glencoe West and often went fishing in the Tarque, a water hole a few miles from home. During the war years they rode miles on their bikes, collecting for Red Cross and other wartime charities.

Beryl married Desmond Vivian Havelberg in Mount Gambier on 24th March, 1951. They lived first at Naracoorte and later in Geelong. They had three children — Heather Margaret Havelberg, who married Russell James Bowley and had two children, Robert James Bowley and Craig Russell Bowley; Lynette Kay Havelberg, who married Terry Andrew Mortimer, and had two children — Melissa Claire Mortimer and

Nicole Maree Mortimer. The third child of Beryl and Des was a son, Peter Desmond Havelberg.

In 1985 Beryl and Des won a substantial prize in a lottery. They purchased a caravan and went on an extended holiday around Australia. Shortly after their return Beryl was diagnosed as having cancer and her most untimely death occurred in 1987.

Jeffrey Allen Koop married Norma Thornton. Their son, Douglas Jeffrey Koop married Sharon Lavars. They also have a daughter, Karen Lorraine Koop.

Laurie Koop did not marry. He lives with his mother in Geelong.

Trevor Francis Koop works with the Railways Department at Tailem Bend. He married Isobel Pengelly, and they have two children — Jacqueline Anne Koop and Shannon Leigh Koop.

Dorothy and Allen Koop celebrated their Golden Wedding in 1978. Allen died suddenly in 1983. Geelong is still home to Dorothy. She is a very keen bowler and gains a lot of pleasure from this activity.

Gordon Francis Telfer was born in 1911. He grew up at "Pleasant Hill" until he married Emily Alice Michell in 1938. Gordon and Emily lived at "Pleasant Hill" for the first few years of their marriage, but in 1941 they purchased a property of their own and moved there.

From left:- Heather Bowley, Des Havelberg, Beryl Havelberg, Peter Havelberg and Lynette Mortimer.

### PAGE 299
Golden Wedding Celebrations, 1978.
Laurie Koop, Beryl Havelberg, Allen Koop,
Trevor Koop, Dorothy Koop and Jeffrey
Koop.

They farmed the property "Holloway's" as it was called, dairying and growing potatoes, and sharing the Koorine property with Gordon's father, grazing Hereford cattle. They grazed sheep too, the breeds being Corriedale and Romney Corrie X.

In 1947, Gordon's parents, Frank and Clara, built a house in Mount Gambier and handed "Pleasant Hill" over to Gordon to manage. The family then moved to the old home.

Gordon worked very hard to build up the property. He and Emily milked cows and Gordon and his brother, Ronald, grew potatoes and grazed beef cattle at the Wepar property.

There were three children — Marie Frances Telfer, Glenys Alvina Ann Telfer and Gordon Michell Telfer.

Gordon was an outstanding wingman in the Glencoe Football team, winning many trophies as the best and fairest player. After the war he became an umpire for the Mid South East League, a position he held for many years. As the

years went by, he relinquished this position and became the Trainer of the Glencoe team, and "carried the bag", attending to the players during the matches. He did this until 1964 and was honoured with a Life Membership of the Club in 1965.

Sadly, by this time he was already quite ill, and he died from cancer in 1965 at the age of 54. Emily nursed him at home until the last three days.

Gordon loved fishing. He had a log cabin at Southend, and he and his son-in-law, Alistair Pegler, shared a little boat with an outboard.

Their son, Gordon, was only 20 years old when his father died. He and his mother managed the farm until "young" Gordon married Janice Sporer. "Young" Gordon played football too and it was a proud day when he won the trophy as the B Grade Best and Fairest. However, his increased responsibilities and work load after his father's death made it impossible for him to continue with his football career and he did not play again.

After Gordon and Janice married, Emily moved into Mount Gambier. For some years she worked as Librarian at the Mount Gambier High School, and after her retirement she undertook several courses at the T.A.F.E. College at Mount Gambier.

Emily has always been very interested in wool and loved shearing time. It was natural that she should join in the Rural

Glenys, Marie and Gordon Telfer in 1958.

### PAGE 300
Four generations. Clara Telfer, with her son, Gordon; grand-daughter Marie; and great-grand-daughter, Lisa ... 1962.

Studies Wool Knowledge course which she passed with credit. She is a spinner and still loves working with wool. She has won First Prize for a homespun crochet jacket and second prize in homespun home furnishing with a crochet cushion.

Emily still belongs to the Glencoe C.W.A., an association she has had for more than 45 years.

"Young Gordon" and Janice still live at Pleasant Hill with their children, Simon Gordon Telfer and Trina Faye Telfer. Simon is the fourth generation of Telfers to live on the property. At present he works as a mechanic for the Woods and Forests Department at Mount Burr. His spare time activities centre around music. He is an exceptionally gifted guitarist and he and his band, called "The Mob" are in constant demand at functions throughout the South East.

Marie Telfer married Alistair Pegler, a professional fisherman. They live at Southend with their two children, Drew and Lisa.

Glenys married Peter Mitchell and they had four children. Glenys is a teacher.

Gordon and Emily
Telfer with their
family on the
occasion of the
wedding of Glenys.
From left:- Emily,
Gordon, Glenys, Marie
and "Young" Gordon.

### PAGE 301
The wedding of Estelle Telfer and Bert Morris, 1937. From left:- Jean Telfer, Estelle and Bert, Beryl Koop.

The family are very good tennis players. Susan Mitchell is the No. 1 South Australian Table Tennis Player, and holds second position in the whole of Australia. In December, 1987, she went to China to compete at table tennis. There are three sons — Peter, Guy and Darcy.

Estelle May Telfer, the youngest child of Frank and Clara Telfer, was born in 1914. She married Bert Morris in 1937, and they lived at Geelong. After Bert's death, Estelle moved to Queensland, where she lives a very active life "in retirement" at Coolangatta.

## JAMES EDGERSTON TELFER

fifth child of Adam and Margaret Telfer

James Edgerston Telfer (known as Ted) was the fifth child, and second son, of Adam and Margaret Telfer. He was born in 1877 and spent his early years at Edgerston. Like his brother, Frank, he could remember his mother covering the pine tree at the front of the house to protect it from the frost.

Ted married Della Richards and they had one son, Claude Rex (known as Rex), who was born in 1902. Della died when Rex was only three days old, and he was cared for by his grandparents, Adam and Margaret Telfer. There was never any

shortage of "baby sitters" with five Aunties to help out!

After Della's death, Ted went to Western Australia for a short time, in company with Isaac Carthew, a friend and neighbour from Tantanoola.

On his return from Western Australia, he married Elizabeth McMorron. The marriage took place at the Presbyterian Church, Mount Gambier, the Rev. Robert Alexander Caldwell officiating. Elizabeth, described as a "Dressmaker" was the daughter of James Wilson McMorron, of Mount Gambier. The

### PAGE 302
witnesses at the wedding were Ted's brother, Herb, and Elizabeth's sister, Lillian.

Ted and Elizabeth (known as "Liz") lived for a short time at Holloway's Siding, but when Adam and Margaret left Tantanoola to live at Mitcham, they moved to Edgerston.

In 1911 the Edgerston property was sold. Ted purchased land at Glencoe West for £1.7.0 ($2.70) per acre and he and his family lived there for ten years. After that they went to Mount Gambier for a short time and then to Bool Lagoon for five years.

Wedding invitation, 1908.

The wedding party of James Edgerston Telfer and Elizabeth McMorran. From left:- Effie Telfer, Sam McMorran, Elizabeth, Herb Telfer, James (seated) and Lillian McMorran.

### PAGE 303
Golden Wedding
celebrations, 1958.
From left:- James and
Elizabeth Telfer, Bill

The opportunity to buy back the Edgerston property came in 1928. Ted purchased it and lived there until his death in 1959.

Ted was a farmer all his life. He milked 60 cows and fed 80 calves on whey which he procured from the Tantanoola Milk Factory. He took the milk to the factory by means of a horse and trolley.

One New Year's Day he went to get the horse to take the milk to the factory but was dismayed to find the horse missing. A New Year's Eve prankster had removed the road gate and the horse got away. There was no telephone in those days, and no refrigeration either, so Ted had to walk some distance to the road gate to meet his cousin, Mark Telfer, who would be on his way to the factory. Ted had one thousand gallons of milk in ten cans. Mark was unable to take all that quantity as well as his own load, so another neighbour, Mr. Walter Haines, helped out. The factory was two miles from Edgerston.

Ted and Liz had six children — Kathleen, Daphne, Eric, Jessie, Gwen and Audrey.

During the war, Ted broke his leg and was forced to spend a long period of inactivity while it healed. He wrote in a letter to his sister, Effie, that his daughters, Gwen and Audrey, had been a wonderful help to him during his enforced

rest, and had made light of all the extra work.

It was about this time that he installed milking machines, and that lightened the load for the whole family.

Liz was a wonderful cook, a skill she has passed on to her daughters!

On 11th March, 1958, Ted and Liz celebrated their Golden Wedding at Edgerston with a party for family, relatives and close friends.

Ted died on 2nd June, 1959. Liz spent her last years living with her youngest daughter, Audrey, at Millicent, where she died in 1967.

After Ted's death, his son, Eric, took over the management of the Edgerston property, and it is now owned and worked by Eric's son, Noel James Telfer, who is,

Telfer's Road, with "Edgerston" in background.

### PAGE 304
of course, a great grandson of Adam and Margaret.

Telfer's Road runs past the Edgerston gate and leads to the farm once owned by Mark Telfer and now worked by Mark's grandson, Ian Telfer.

Ted's eldest child was Rex Telfer. He was born in 1902, and as his Mother died shortly after his birth, he went to live with his grandparents, Adam and Margaret Telfer, who looked after him during his early years. His Aunts too shared in his upbringing and treated him with great affection. Eva in particular was devoted to Rex, as his mother, Della was one of her closest friends.

Among the gifts which Adam brought back from his visit to Scotland was a kilt for Rex. This he wore proudly as his photograph shows.

Rex left Tantanoola with his grandparents and went to live at Mitcham. He attended Miss Ralph's kindergarten. He was so upset when his Aunt Eva was married, that he hid in the pigsty at Waverley Park. When eventually found he explained his distress by saying that Uncle Dick was taking his Aunty away!

He returned to Edgerston at Tantanoola and worked on his father's farm for some time, rising early in the morning to milk the cows and take the milk to the factory. He later decided that he was not a farmer at heart and he left Tantanoola. He kept in close touch with his grandparents and was always welcome there.

Rex married Edna Bronson and they had seven children — Dawn Natalie Telfer, Shirley May Telfer, Margaret June Telfer, Donald Rex Telfer, Beverley Rose Telfer, Susan Kaye Telfer and Sandra Ann Telfer. There are 24 grandchildren and 13 great grandchildren.

During the war Rex served in the A.I.F.

Rex and Edna lived in retirement at St. Peters in Adelaide, but Rex died in 1988 at the age of 86. In spite of poor health, Edna continues to live alone. Her main recreational interest is playing bowls.

The grave of Kathleen Telfer, Tantanoola Cemetery.

The eldest child of Ted and Liz was a daughter, Kathleen. She was injured in an accident at a very early age, and was crippled for most of her life. She died at the age of 49.

Daphne Pearl Telfer was the second daughter of Ted and Liz Telfer. She was born in Millicent on 11th December, 1910, and grew up at Edgerston, Tantanoola. When Daphne was 17 months old, the family went to live at Glencoe, and it was there that she started school. When she was eleven years old they moved to Mount Gambier for a short time, and then to Bool Lagoon for five years. The next move was back to Edgerston where her father lived for the rest of his life.

Daphne left the farm and went to work at the Cave Garden Cafe in Mount Gambier. At this time her Mother's sister and her husband took over the Park Hotel so Daphne went to live with them for five years. After her Uncle and Aunt left the hotel, Daphne went to live and work in Naracoorte.

She married Ivan Archer Prider on 21st September, 1940. Eighteen months later, Ivan went away to World War II, so Daphne returned to Naracoorte for a short time, and then went home to Edgerston where she helped with the milking of the cows and other farm chores.

After the War Daphne and Ivan lived and worked on the farming property which belonged to her aunt and uncle,

### PAGE 305
The wedding party of Daphne Telfer and Ivan Prider. From left:- Vern McIntyre, Audrey Telfer, Daphne and Ivan Prider, Flower-girl Jennifer Bunston, Gwen Telfer and Jack Hood.

Bessie and Bill Beaton, near Tantanoola. They were there for two years and then moved to a dairying property at Mount Schank where they milked cows for five years.

Daphne and Ivan had three sons, Graham Ross Prider, Rodney Prider and Jim Prider. After leaving Mount Schank the family went to live in Mount Gambier. Ivan died from cancer in 1976. Daphne had a very bad fall and suffered extensive injuries. She broke her arm in three places and shattered her shoulder blade. This has restricted the movement in her arm. She also suffers with arthritis in the legs and so is confined to a wheelchair. She now lives at Boandik Lodge, Mount Gambier. Daphne is not idle. She makes tartan rugs and recently donated one of them to be raffled to raise funds for the Lodge. She was very pleased when one of the nurses won it!

Graham Prider is a chef in the Army. He went to Vietnam at the age of 20 and took part in the Vietnam Veterans March in Sydney in October, 1987. He won the Certificate of Merit in the Professional Chefs and Cooks Associations of Queensland Chef of the Year Competition, competing in the Armed

Forces Division. He was presented with the award by Sir Bruce Small, Mayor of the Gold Coast, Surfers Paradise. Graham married Judy Morrish and they have two daughters. Joanne works in a shop and Tanya is at school.

Rodney Prider married Sandra Forrest and they have two sons, Mark and Adam. Both boys are still at school. Both Rodney and Sandra are very interested in Tenpin Bowling, and Sandra is a well known sportsperson in the Netball and Softball Associations. Mark and Adam both play basketball and tennis.

Jim Prider married Shirley Walker and they have three children, Daniel, Carlee and Briony. The children are very musical and sometimes go to Boandik Lodge to play for the entertainment of the residents. Daphne, their grandmother, is very proud of them. When Briony was eight years old she competed in the Under 10 years section of the Warrnambool Eisteddfod at the Performing Arts Centre, playing the organ.

As part of the Jubilee 150 Celebrations in 1986, an old-time fashion parade was held at Boandik Lodge. The wedding dresses worn by some of the residents were modelled by relatives and friends.

### PAGE 306
Sandra Prider wore Daphne's wedding dress, which was the "something new" when she was married in 1940. Daphne recalled her wedding day. The ceremony took place at the Tantanoola Presbyterian Church at 2p.m. on Saturday, 21st September, 1940. It was a beautiful day and the ceremony was followed by a sit-down dinner for 60 guests, held at Edgerston. There was a two tier wedding cake, and the couple received lots of vases and cutlery as wedding presents. The compere at the fashion parade described Daphne's dress as being "made of satin with a flower print, a 'heartline' neckline and a high Empire style waist with buttons at the back extending from the neck to waist and from elbow to wrist".

Rodney and Sandra took Daphne to Wodonga in New South Wales to visit her son, Graham, for Christmas, 1987.

Eric James Telfer was born in Millicent in 1912. He went to school at Glencoe, Mount Gambier and Bool Lagoon, and after leaving school he worked at home on the family farm at Bool Lagoon.

Eric Telfer, aged 2 years.

The family of James and Elizabeth Telfer. Back:- Jessie, Gwen, Daphne, Audrey and Eric. Front:- James, Kathleen and Elizabeth — at "Edgerston".

### PAGE 307
Phyllis and Eric Telfer.

The family left Bool Lagoon in 1928 when they purchased the old Edgerston home in Tantanoola. Eric remained at Bool Lagoon for some years, sharefarming on Struan Station. After he had finished sowing the crops, he would return to his parents in Tantanoola, travelling by horses and waggon. The journey would take three or four days and he would sleep on the side of the road each night.

He finished share farming at Struan in 1931, and then went share-farming at Hatherleigh, a district about 16 kms. north of Millicent. In 1936 Eric purchased a property at Nangula (between Millicent and Tantanoola), and another property in Millicent in 1946.

Eric married Phyllis Walker and for a time they lived at Edgerston. They later moved to Phyl's family home at Millicent where they still reside. They had five children — Lynette Erica Telfer, Noel James Telfer, Margaret Joan Telfer, Mary Elizabeth Telfer and Neville Bruce Telfer. There are sixteen grandchildren and three great grandchildren.

The family of Eric and Phyl Telfer. From left: Mary Higgins, Margaret Gray, Neville Telfer, Noel Telfer, Eric Telfer, Lynette Peckham (seated) and Phyl Telfer.

### PAGE 308
Winner of Queen Competition at Tantanoola School, 1929. Jessie Telfer, attended by her sisters, Gwen and Audrey.

Eric continued farming until 1980 when he retired and his two sons took over the properties.

Noel Telfer married Sandra Joan Bellinger and they have two children. Stephen is a very accomplished sportsman who plays squash, cricket and football. He has trophies for all of these sports, and he has recently taken up golf. In 1986 he was "cricketer of the year" for his Club. Angela is studying at Underdale College of Advanced Education for a Bachelor of Applied Science Degree. This was formerly the Home Economics Course. At the end of 1985 she went to New Zealand as a Lions Club Exchange student.

Jessie Beryl Telfer grew up in Tantanoola. She went to the Tantanoola School where she won a scholarship.

She married Allan Edward Ey at the Presbyterian Church at Tantanoola. The day was especially eventful as the Minister forgot about the wedding and Jess, Allan and the guests were forced to wait while someone drove to Millicent to remind him! The ceremony eventually took place, followed by a reception at Edgarston.

Jess and Allan lived for a short time in the South East and then in 1946 they went to Queensland where they planned to stay for about two years, shearing sheep and bird watching. When they arrived in Ayr they were stranded for about ten days by flood waters, which made life very difficult for Jess who had two young sons at the time.

They finally went to Townsville and over to Magnetic Island but they were not very impressed with Townsville as it was at that time. So they went back to Ayr where they bought a sugar farm at Airville.

Allan's life-long hobby has been studying birdlife. It began when he was a child in Millicent and has continued ever since. He has recognised and catalogued many different species in South Australia and in Queensland, and has written many articles for ornithological magazines. While he possesses no formal qualifications, his work has been recognised and experts have come to him for advice and help.

In 1956 Jess and Allan began collecting shells, a hobby which they found most relaxing. They have travelled many thousands of kms. in pursuit of their specimens.

Their two sons, Brian and Kerry, took over the running of the farm in 1964 and Jess and Allan purchased a house in Ayr. They added a display room to house their collection of shells. Friends urged them to open the display to the public. The idea appealed, and they then turned their attention to collecting insects.

The display was opened in 1966 and proved so popular that the display room had to be extended to cater for the ever increasing numbers of visitors.

### PAGE 309
Jess and Allan Ey, Ayr Nature Display, North Queensland.

Ayr Nature Display, featuring the Hercules Moth, largest in the world.

Ayr Nature Display, showing giant sea crabs.

Ayr Nature Display. Bird scene made from 1067 moths and butterflies.

The Ayr Nature Display is of world class standard. It was put together by Jess and Allan and with the exception of about five per cent of the shells, the whole collection is their own. The work is entirely Australian in a bid to show the public the true native fauna.

Jess and Allan have three children — Brian, Kerry and Lorraine. Brian now operates the sugar farm; Kerry is the proprietor of a group of holiday units in Ayr; Lorraine married a teacher, Graham Buchan, and lives in Townsville.

Keeping their display open for visitors is a full-time job for Jess and Allan. They are continually adding to their collection and in recent years Allan has cut many thousands of slabs of rocks to make feature walls on their house and display room. All the stones featured were

gathered in Queensland, and over half came from the Burdekin River district.

The display includes a selection of aboriginal artifacts — common implements which were used by the indigenous peoples before Europeans ever came to the country. There are also some fossil sea shells found in North Queensland, estimated to be about 70 million years old.

Beetles and other insects are caught when they are in plague proportions. Allan has spent many weeks in the Cape Yorke Peninsula area catching butterflies and moths. Jess and Allan can be justly proud of their work, as their expeditions have filled the Ayr Nature Display with many unique specimens. There are over 60,000 specimens artistically arranged under glass and this

must surely be Australia's finest personal collection of fauna, displaying butterflies, moths, beetles, crabs, sea shells, reptiles, rock fossils and aboriginal artifacts.

Gwendoline May Telfer was born at Mount Gambier on 19th March, 1922. She lived with her family at Glencoe for a short time, and then moved with them to Bool Lagoon where her father had a farming property. They all returned to Tantanoola when the Edgarston property was bought back. Gwen went to the Tantanoola School, and after leaving there, she undertook sewing classes.

During the war Gwen worked with Red Cross, raising money and knitting for the soldiers. One of the major Red Cross efforts during the war was a Debutante Ball. Gwen and her sister Audrey were debts on that occasion.

Gwen was always interested in music. She learnt to play the piano, and used her skills in playing the organ at the Tantanoola Presbyterian Church for six and a half years prior to her marriage to Colin John Burdon on 22nd December, 1943. She still plays the organ at the Millicent Presbyterian Church when asked.

The home of Jess and Allan Ey in Ayr. The wall features the many-coloured rocks of North Queensland. Thin slabs are cut with a diamond saw and fixed to the wall with tiling glue. All 9,400 slabs have been lacquered to counteract staining.

After her marriage Gwen lived at Mount Burr for 18 months and then moved to Millicent. In 1954, Gwen and Colin bought a farm called "Argyle", a few miles from Millicent. They milked cows and reared calves for several years and then changed to rearing sheep and cattle. Gwen still lives at "Argyle".

Her community activities have been many and varied. She was President of

### PAGE 311
Gwendoline May Telfer.

Colin and Gwen Burdon.

the Hatherleigh School Welfare Club, President of Hatherleigh Football Ladies Committee and President of the Mothers Club of the Millicent Presbyterian Church. She is still President of P.W.A. of Millicent Church and Convener of Fund Raising for the Church. This involves catering for many functions which is a time-consuming task. Gwen taught Sunday School for 24½ years and she is an Elder of the Church.

Colin unfortunately contracted multiple sclerosis and as his health deteriorated, Gwen nursed him devotedly for many years. He died in 1984.

Gwen and Colin had three children — Beverley May, Geoffrey Colin and Peter Robert.

Beverley trained as a nurse. She married Francesco Fabris, a fisherman of Southend, in 1963. Frank has an interest in Fabris Marine, manufacturers and

The family of Gwen and Colin Burdon. Standing:- Peter Burdon, David Fabris, Gwen Burdon, Beverley Fabris, Geoffrey Burdon, Mark Fabris, Linda Fabris. Seated:- Naomi Burdon, Simone Burdon, Heather Burdon, Frank Fabris.

### PAGE 312
suppliers of fishing equipment, at Southend.

They have three children — Mark Jay and David Francis, who are both qualified Fitters and Turners, and Linda Michelle, who is an Apprentice Hairdresser. All the children attended Rendelsham Primary School and Millicent High School.

Geoffrey lives at "Argyle" with his Mother and works on the farm.

Peter Robert Burdon, the youngest child of Gwen and Colin, left school at the age of 16. He worked on the farm and went shearing for two years, and then purchased a truck with a view to undertaking super-spreading. He did this for two years and then purchased a semi stock-crate. The business is run from Peter's home about five miles from Millicent on Argyle Road. The business is named "Argyle Transport", the name originating from the Burdon family farm.

The stock cartage business has continued to grow until Peter now owns five trucks which are used to transport stock from the south east areas to Adelaide or anywhere else in Australia. Peter attends the local markets to organise work for his trucks.

Peter married Heather Ella May Sutherland at St. Andrews Presbyterian Church, Millicent, on 5th October, 1954. There are two children — Naomi Lee and Simone Marie.

Heather plays an important part in the family business as she acts as the business secretary, working from home.

Besides the transport business, Peter has acquired some land on which he runs sheep for the live export market.

Naomi and Simone travel by bus to the Millicent North Primary School.

Audrey Isabel Telfer was born in Mount Gambier on 10th May, 1923, the youngest child of James Edgarston and Elizabeth Telfer. When she was six weeks old, the family went to live at Bool Lagoon for five years. They moved back to Tantanoola in 1928 when they bought back Edgarston which had been the home

## Debutante's Memento

U. Altschwager, President.
Gladys Bishings, Hon. Sec.

A memento of the Tantanoola Red Cross Ball, 1940.

Debutantes Audrey and Gwen Telfer, 1940.

of Audrey's grandparents, Adam and Margaret Telfer.

Audrey was educated at the Tantanoola School. She went to Sunday School at the Tantanoola Presbyterian Church where services were held once per fortnight. She would often walk to the

### PAGE 313
Wedding party of
Andres Telfer and
Leon Day. From left
Mary Ross, Flower girl
Margaret Telfer,
Andres and Leon,
and Sul Day

The Millicent home of Audrey and Leon Day.

church with the Methodist Minister as both clergymen would travel together to Tantanoola from Millicent in the interests of petrol economy.

When she was sixteen years old, she made her debut at the Red Cross Ball at the Tantanoola Institute. Her partner was Lindsay Telfer, and her sister Gwen's partner was Mel Telfer, making it a family foursome.

In January, 1950, Audrey married Leon Paul Day at the Tantanoola Presbyterian Church, the Church which was built through the efforts of Adam Telfer and

his brother, John. The wedding reception was held at Edgerston.

Audrey and Leon went to live in Millicent where they built two rooms and conveniences, and two years later completed the home where they still live.

Her community activities included the Presbyterian Mothers Club, of which she was a member until it closed. She is a Life Member of the Women's Agricultural Bureau which she has served well, having held the position of Treasurer for eight years. Many of the Bureau meetings are held in her home. During the years her children were at school, Audrey was a member of the Mothers Clubs at both the Millicent Primary and High Schools. She worked at the school canteens for many years.

Leon worked for the Railways. When he commenced duty at the Millicent Station in January, 1942, he followed a family tradition, as his father, Mr. George Day, had been a porter there for over thirty years. Leon commenced work as a junior clerk, and then continued his studies in Adelaide, with four years at Ovingham, Islington and Bowden. He was then appointed to the clerical staff and went to Cammins for a year before returning to Millicent. Leon has seen the railway

### PAGE 314
Leon Day at his desk at Millicent Railway Station.

activity at Millicent decrease from a scene of bustling activity, to a daily rail car service, and then an even more restricted service.

Leon retired from the Railways in 1986.

After Leon's father died, he and Audrey purchased 277 acres which his father had owned near Millicent. The land was in two blocks, with the block in the middle being owned by Leon's brother, Sid.

When Sid's block was offered for sale in 1984, Audrey and Leon were able to purchase it, thus consolidating the area which had been owned by Leon's father before he died. Strangely enough, this was the way his father originally acquired the land, purchasing the middle block last. The farm is now 433 acres, and Leon runs beef cattle.

A trip to America, Canada and Honolulu gave Audrey and Leon a taste for international travel, and in 1982 they went to the Philippines, South East Asia and China, and in 1985 to New Zealand. They have travelled in all States of Australia.

Audrey and Leon had five children. The eldest was Dianne Elizabeth. Sadly she died at the age of 19 months. Then followed four boys. The eldest was Kevin Leon Day who was born in 1954. He went to the Presbyterian Sunday School, attended gymnasium, and played tennis. At primary school one of his teachers had taught his father, Leon, when he was at

The grave of Dianne Day at Tantanoola Cemetery.

school. When he was at High School he entered a National Heart Foundation Essay competition and won $100 for his contribution. Kevin won two scholarships, one in his Leaving year for $150, and the other from Matriculation worth $250. This enabled him to attend the University.

In order to earn extra pocket money, Kevin worked at a motel for about two hours before school and at weekends. This employment began when he was about twelve years of age and continued for about six years.

Kevin is Manager of Sales and Service at Telecom in Maryborough, Queensland.

He married Heather Mullan and they have four daughters. Belinda Holly, Stacey Camilla, Rochelle Larissa and Sally Rowena.

William James Day (known as Bill) was born on 10th January, 1956. Like his brother, he attended the Presbyterian
