Tony, Allen and Leon Day.

Kevin Day with Rochelle, Sally, Stacey and Belinda.

Sunday School, went to gymnasium, and played tennis. He too worked at the local motel to earn extra pocket money. His duties included carrying trays to the units and washing the dishes. He worked there for six years until he left school and went to Adelaide.

Bill studied at Sturt College of Advanced Education where he gained his Diploma of Teaching. He then went to the United Kingdom and Europe for twelve months. He has now joined Sealey Bros (air conditioners and plastics) where he has been promoted to Supervisor. He lives at Mitchell Park.

Anthony George Day (known as Tony) was born on 12th September, 1959. He

attended the gymnasium, but his favourite sport was hockey. He won several trophies for "best and fairest" player. Tony went to the Presbyterian Sunday School, and like his brothers, he worked at the motel for about six years. When he and his younger brother, Allen, were about 13 and 12 years old, they had to cook thirty breakfasts as the motel cook failed to arrive. They boiled, scrambled, poached and fried eggs, bacon and chops, and managed to supply all the motel guests with their breakfasts.

Tony went to the Millicent High School. He matriculated at the age of 18 and then joined the South Australian Railways (now Australian National). For the first two years he worked in Mount Gambier, but he was then transferred to Mile End where he still works.

In 1979 he went to America and in 1982 travelled overseas again, this time visiting England, Scotland and Ireland and every country in Europe.

Tony lives at Mitchell Park.

Allen Leigh Day was born on 10th July, 1960. Like his brothers he went to the Presbyterian Sunday School, attended gymnasium and played hockey and tennis. At the conclusion of his primary schooling he was presented with a travelling clock as he had a record of unbroken attendance for seven years. This was a surprise to the family and to Allen, as they had not realised that he

### PAGE 316
The family of Audrey and Leon Day.
Back:- Bill, Heather, Kevin, Allen and Tony.
Front:- Leon nursing Rochelle and Stacey; Belinda, and Audrey nursing Sally.

had not missed a single day. After leaving high school, at the age of 16, he joined the Railways. His first appointment was at Millicent where he worked with his father for two years. He was then transferred to Mt. Gambier where he worked in the same office as his brother, Tony.

He is now at Mile End, working in the same office as Tony.

He went to Europe in 1982 and spent two months travelling extensively.

Allen bought a house at Mitchell Park where he now lives.

The Day family has clocked up about 120 years of service to the Railways. Leon's father, George Day, spent 30 years; Leon 40 years; and Leon's sons Tony and Allan have also been part of the family affair.

Audrey and Leon are extremely hospitable people and have many visitors

Grave of James Edgerston and Elizabeth Charlotte Telfer at Tantanoola Cemetery.

at their home. During January in the last few years they have held a "reunion" of many of the Telfer cousins from near and far, creating a splendid opportunity for members of the wider family to renew acquaintance.

## VERNON RAY ST. CLAIR TELFER

sixth child of Adam and Margaret Telfer

Vernon Ray St. Clair Telfer was the third son of Adam and Margaret Telfer.

He grew up on the family farm at Edgerston, Tantanoola and married

### PAGE 317
Vernon Ray St. Clair Telfer.

Annie (Rogers) and Ray Telfer.

Annie Telfer with Ruby and Reg in 1915.

Reg Telfer.

### PAGE 318
Annie Jane Rogers. They owned a mixed farming property, Corindah, which adjoined Edgarston, and they lived there until 1920, when they moved to Adelaide.

Ray and Annie owned a dairy farm at Lockleys until the spread of the suburbs overtook such properties. He then moved to Norton Summit where he worked at market gardening on a share basis for a few years.

They then purchased their own small orchard and market garden at Scotts Creek, but this was eventually taken over by a larger orchardist. Ray then went to Hackham as Manager of a stud sheep property, and when this changed hands, he managed a dairy farm at Oakbank until he retired and went to live at Bridgewater.

Ray and Annie had two children. The eldest was Margaret Ruby who married Reg Williams. There were no children. Towards the end of her life, Ruby suffered ill-health, and she spent her last years in a wheel chair as a patient at the Home for Incurables.

Ray's son, Reginald Vernon Telfer, taught at Scotts Creek School for one year. He then went to work at the Monreith Bakery of his uncle, William Richard Easter, who married Ray's sister, Eva. He was there for ten years, and then the business changed hands. His next position was in the glass trade where he worked for forty years until his retirement. Reg served in the A.I.F. in New Guinea and New Britain.

Reg married Nora Edina Symons in 1935. They were both on the Committee of the Calisthenic Association of South Australia and are both Life Members. Reg served on the Association for 25 years, six years as a Committee member, sixteen years as Vice President, and three years as President. Nora was Treasurer for ten years.

Their daughter, Raelene Margaret Telfer, married Richard Bauman in 1962. Raelene's main hobby was Calisthenics, where she won two State and one Australian title. Richard is a schoolmaster at Prince Alfred College.

Nora and Reg Telfer.

Ray Telfer with grand-daughter, Raelene, in 1943.

### PAGE 319
Lynette, Raelene, Richard and Gregory Bauman.

Richard's main hobby is sailing. He joined the Sea Scouts as a junior and went on to become an instructor. He also sailed as a crew member on several Sydney-Hobart yacht races.

Raelene and Richard have two children — Gregory William and Lynette Michelle. Gregory and Lynette are both keen swimmers. They both hold State records and are members of the State Swimming Squad. They have travelled to every Australian State for swimming events, and have acquitted themselves with distinction in their chosen sport.

## HERBERT DOUGAL TELFER

seventh child of Adam and Margaret Telfer

Herbert Dougal Telfer, the fourth son of Adam and Margaret, was born in 1881. He was engaged to be married but the young lady died just before the wedding and Herb remained single all his life. He was a quiet, reserved, gentle man and a great reader. One of his favourite books was "Jane Eyre".

He worked on the land for most of his life, beginning with his father at Edgerston. He managed Adam's sheep station at Truro until losses in the devastating drought of 1914 forced its sale. Afterwards he worked on properties at Wattle Range and Hatherleigh in the South East of the State, and at Karoonda. Often he stayed at outcamps alone for many weeks at a time.

As a young man he spent some time in Western Australia, and when he finally retired in 1952, it was to that State that he went. He bought a property at Greenmount in the foothills of the Darling Ranges. It was a comfortable little cottage with enough land for fruit trees and a vegetable garden. This was Herb's retirement home.

Later he was joined by his sister, Effie, who managed the household while Herb

Herbert Dougal Telfer.

### PAGE 320
tended his garden. That happy arrangement lasted for some years, but ill health eventually caused Effie to return to Perth.

Herb continued to live alone at

Greenmont, but advancing years and deteriorating health finally forced him to sell his property and enter a nursing home.

He died in 1974 at the age of 93 years.

## VIOLET HOPE TELFER

eighth child of Adam and Margaret Telfer

Violet Hope Telfer was born on 4th August, 1883. She grew up at Edgerston, Tantanoola, the eighth child of Adam and Margaret Telfer. She helped milk the cows and performed other farm chores, but she was especially helpful in the house as she grew up to be an excellent cook.

She came to Waverley Park when the family moved to Mitcham. She was engaged to be married during the First World War, but her fiance became

seriously ill on his way home from the War, and died at Fremantle. Violet did not marry but assumed the role of home-maker for the rest of the family, and became the respected and loved "Aunt Vi" to all the nieces and nephews.

At the big house in Rochester Street, Violet did the cooking and much of the gardening. There was a constant stream of visitors as all of the Telfer clan used the home as their Adelaide base when they were in the city. Nieces and nephews boarded there while attending Teachers College.

The grounds around the house were extensive, and as well as a large orchard, vegetable garden and flowers, Violet kept fowls and ducks. She was always extremely patient with all the children, who delighted in collecting the eggs and feeding the poultry each day.

A creek ran through the garden, another source of pleasure for the visiting children, as tadpoles were plentiful and

Effie, Violet and Eva Telfer.

Violet, Eva, Lil and Elsie in 1950.

### PAGE 321
Violet and Elsie Telfer in the garden at Knightsbridge.

racing paper boats was a popular amusement. Many were the jars of tadpoles left on the back verandah!

Aunt Vi had an ice chest in the passage, always filled with "goodies" — cakes, biscuits and gem scones. She usually turned a blind eye or pretended to be deaf when the children raided the ice chest.

As the other members of the family left home to marry or to undertake employment, Violet remained at home. She was the constant companion of her mother, Margaret, and cared for her during the last years of her life when her sight failed and she became increasingly dependent.

As well as her skills as a cook, Violet was an excellent needle-woman, and many examples of her skill with the crochet hook remain in the possession of family members to this day.

She was a member of the Knightsbridge Baptist Church for many years and when younger taught a Sunday School class and attended the Christian Endeavour Society. She always arrived early at church for the evening service as she had a favourite spot in the back pew where she liked to sit.

After her mother died, Violet and her sister, Elsie, opened a private hospital. Vi

"Aunt Vi" on her 80th birthday.

was the cook. She worked extremely hard all her life and managed to continue with the garden even when she was so busy with the hospital. Every Thursday afternoon she had her "time off" and she would go to Adelaide to meet her sister, Eva. They would do their shopping and finish the afternoon with a cup of tea at Balfours cafe.

When her sister, Hazel, died at a very early age, Vi and Elsie brought their niece, Barbara, home to live with them at Rochester Street. They cared for her in a loving way and brought her up as their own.

Visitors continued to come as Vi and Elsie held a very special place in the affections of the wider family.

Summer time was especially busy with jam making and preserving. There was a large cellar under the house and this was the store house for all of the preserves. Vi would go down the cellar and tap on the ceiling (which was the floor of the

### PAGE 322
"Auntie Vi" on her 80th birthday, with Christopher and Peter Lodge 1963.

kitchen). All the youngsters thought this was a great game.

Vi was a very placid lady and she hated arguments. One of her pet sayings was, "If the missus says it's black, well then it IS black".

As the years went by, the constant demands of running a private hospital became too great, and the role slowly changed to a Rest Home. Vi and Elsie cared for a number of elderly ladies and provided them with a comfortable home. The workload was further reduced when they sold a block of land at the side of the house.

They continued to live and work at Rochester Street until 1956 when the big house and grounds became just too much to look after. They then sold the property and moved to a smaller house on Knightsbridge Road, where they lived for another ten years. This house, too, became the favourite visiting place for all the out-of-town relatives.

On 4th August, 1983, Violet celebrated her eightieth birthday. A large gathering of friends and relatives helped her to have a very happy day.

In 1965 Violet had a bad fall and broke her toe. It was sad but only too obvious that she would not be able to live independently and care for her sister, Elsie, so the very hard decision was taken to sell the house, and they both moved to a nursing home at Norwood. This proved to be the beginning of the end as the loss of her home and independence was too much for Vi to comprehend. Shortly afterwards she had a stroke from which she recovered, but her memory failed and her health deteriorated very quickly. She died in 1967 and was buried in the Mitcham cemetery in the same grave as her parents, Adam and Margaret.

Violet Hope Telfer was loved and respected by all her family and friends, all of whom mourned the passing of a great and gracious lady.

## EVA ROWENA TELFER

ninth child of Adam and Margaret Telfer

Eva Rowena Telfer was the fifth daughter and ninth child of Adam and Margaret Telfer. She was born on 5th June, 1885. Her early years were spent at Tantanoola in the South East of South Australia where her father had a farm. She often milked, by hand, up to twenty cows before going to school.

She later moved with the family to Waverley Park, Lower Mitcham. Eva was

a dressmaker, and with her sister, Effie, she conducted a small business from premises in Fashoda Street, Unley.

Eva met William Richard Easter (known as Dick) a young Englishman who had come to Australia to seek new opportunities. He was a baker by trade and he worked at a bakery in Nailsworth which belonged to a Mr. Buckingham. He later obtained work at a bakery at Clare

### PAGE 323
Dick and Eva Easter with Bob, 1915.

Eva Easter with Joyce and Bob, 1917.

in the mid-north.

Dick and Eva were married from Waverley Park on 11th April, 1913. The marriage took place at the Hawthorn Presbyterian Church. After the wedding, Dick and Eva moved to Clare to live, and their first child, Ernest Richard (known as Bob) was born there the following year.

When World War I broke out, Dick joined the army. He was stationed at the Military camp at Mitcham at first, but was sent overseas. He was a Warrant Officer and served in France and Egypt with the Field Bakery. He was away for almost four years. When he left Australia his hair was black, but when he returned it was snowy white.

During the war years Eva and her young son, Bob, returned to live with her parents at Knightsbridge. The second child, Marjorie Joyce, was born on 13th March, 1916, while her father was serving overseas with the Army. She did not see her father until she was nearly three years old.

After the war ended, Dick returned to Australia, and with Eva and the children, moved to Holbrooks Road, Underdale, where he and a colleague from the army, bought a bakery business which they ran in partnership for six years. Eva and Dick built a new home next to the bakery, and whilst they were at Underdale, three more children were born. These were John Douglas (Doug) on 14th May, 1920; Mary Mavis (Mavis) on 25th February, 1922; and Geoffrey William Rex on 13th July, 1924. Unfortunately Geoffrey died on 7th August, 1925, a victim of diphtheria, aged one year.

In 1926 Dick returned to England to visit his parents, and on his return, once again went into business, this time at Monreith. Eva worked hard raising the family and helping her husband run this and subsequent businesses. She looked after the shop which was attached to the Monreith house. The business was a family affair as Bob drove the bread delivery van and Reg Telfer, a cousin,

### PAGE 324
Joyce Easter, aged 2 years.

drove the horse and cart delivery. Every Wednesday afternoon Eva would close the shop so that she could visit her mother and sister at Rochester Street.

The next move was to Port Elliot where Dick operated another bakery and shop. Joyce worked in the shop and Bob drove the horse and cart delivery. These were happy years spent in a pleasant place. The younger members of the family spent much time at the beach.

From Port Elliot the family moved to Moonta. Joyce married and went to live at Victor Harbor; Doug helped his father in the bakehouse and Mavis managed the shop and tea rooms which were in the next street.

The war years were especially difficult. Bob joined the Air Force and labour was extremely difficult to get. Eva worked long hours helping in the bakery.

Dick retired in 1946 and the family returned to live at Underdale. Bob stayed in Moonta and opened an electrical shop.

Doug married and continued to work with the new owners at the bakehouse in Moonta.

Dick had a stroke shortly after the return to Underdale, and died suddenly in 1948 at the age of 63. Eva lived at Underdale until her death from pneumonia at the age of 89 in 1974.

Ernest Richard Easter (Bob) worked in the family businesses until after the war. He became a qualified electrician and opened a shop in Moonta. After his father's death, he came to live at Underdale with his mother and sister. For a time he worked as an Insurance Agent, but his great interest in books prompted him to establish a library on Henley Beach Road, and later to operate a mobile library, travelling around the suburbs of Adelaide. It was while he was engaged on this work that he suffered a heart attack and died suddenly at the age of 69 in 1983.

Marjorie Joyce Easter (known as Joyce) was born on 13th March, 1916. She was the second child of Eva and Dick Easter and she was born while her father was serving with the Army overseas in World War I. Her first years were spent with her mother and brother, Bob, at the home of her grandparents, Adam and Margaret Telfer at Rochester Street, Knightsbridge.

On her father's return from overseas the Easter family moved to Underdale where Dick was in partnership in a bakery business. That business was sold, and Dick returned to Huntingdonshire, England, to visit his parents. After her father's return from England, he bought another bakery business at Monreith (now Toorak Gardens).

Joyce started school at Underdale and continued at Rose Park when the family lived at Monreith.

On 14th May, 1938, Joyce married Ronald Eric Watson, of Middleton. Their early married life was spent at Middleton where Ron was share-farming. Joyce helped her husband milk the cows (up to thirty) by hand. In those days there were no milking machines, and the separator

### PAGE 325
had to be turned by hand and the cream sent to the butter factory in Strathalbyn.

Ron and Joyce later bought a small dairy farm of their own at Victor Harbor, on land bordered by the Hindmarsh River.

Three children were born of the marriage — Bruce Ronald Watson, on 15th March, 1939; Lauris Joyce Watson on 29th May, 1943; and Jillian Heather Watson on 3rd September, 1948.

Joyce and Ron rented the property adjoining their farm. This gave them another four acres and a six-roomed house which incorporated the Hindmarsh River Tea Rooms. While Ron worked on the farm, Joyce would bake and serve scones to the people who came up the River in the rowing boats.

Finally, when the tea rooms were sold, Joyce and Ron and family moved to an adjoining grazing property owned by Mr. Paul Cudmore who built them a new home and engaged Ron as resident Manager.

Sadly, in May, 1966, Ron suffered a heart attack, but after a month in hospital, resumed his duties. However, on 30th January, 1967, he had a second heart attack and this time it proved fatal.

By this time Bruce was married and working in Millicent as a carpenter, Lauris was married and living in Woomera, and Jill was working for Telecom in Adelaide.

Joyce continued to live in the house at Adare Heights. She worked as a shop assistant in Victor Harbor.

Her daughter, Jill, married Paul White, of Daw Park, and they settled at Morphett Vale.

Joyce now lives in a unit close to Jill. Her family are a great comfort to her. She has been a keen gardener all her life, and this life-time hobby still occupies much of her time. She loves to knit and crochet garments for the family.

Joyce also does some voluntary work as a "Lavender Lady" in the kiosk at the Royal Adelaide Hospital for four hours each week, an occupation which gives her much pleasure and satisfaction.

Joyce Watson with her grandchildren, 1985. Arlene and Kerri Lauri; Trudi Watson nursing Kathryn White, Sonya Watson. Front:- Ben and Damien White.

In 1984 Joyce realised a life-long ambition, which was to visit England to see her father's birthplace. She managed to do this during a busy itinerary, and with the help of local people was able to find out much about his early life.

Joyce's son, Bruce, lives at Millicent with his wife, Carole, (nee Cabot) and two daughters, Sonya Gay and Trudi Ann. He trained as a carpenter, but is also a successful builder. His most interesting hobby is the restoration of old horse-drawn vehicles which he takes to shows and exhibitions.

Lauris Watson married Riha Lauri. They have two daughters, Kerrilee Ann, and Arlene Evette. Lauris lives at Gawler.

Jillian's three children are Benjamin James, Damien Craig and Kathryn Linda.

John Douglas Easter was also a baker and worked with his father until the Moonta business was sold. He then continued to work for the new owner. In 1938 he was a victim of the infantile paralysis (polio myelitis) epidemic which swept through the State, and he spent many months in the Royal Adelaide Hospital. He suffered badly, but fortunately made a good recovery and

### PAGE 326
Mavis Easter, Joyce Watson and Douglas Easter, 1985.

returned to his work at the bakery at Moonta.

He married Kathleen Arthur, a Moonta girl, in 1949, and they had one son, Peter John Easter.

Doug was a local preacher in the Methodist Church, and he was a very keen golfer, Kath helped in many community activities.

Kath fell ill with a serious and incurable lung complaint, and her last years were marred by frequent visits to hospital. As her condition deteriorated, Doug nursed her devotedly until her death in 1985.

Doug has used his expertise in baking by helping to raise money for the recently completed Retirement Village at Moonta. He baked hundreds of hot cross buns and gave the proceeds to the building fund.

Their son, Peter, worked with the Western Mining Company in Kalgoorlie, Western Australia, for several years. In 1989 he was transferred to a senior position with the Company in Townsville, Queensland.

Mary Mavis Easter (known as Mavis) lived with her family at Underdale, Monreith, Port Elliot and Moonta. She went to the Rose Park Primary School and used to ride on the bus for a one penny (1 cent) fare.

Mavis worked in the family business at Moonta where she managed the shop and

Violet Telfer and Eva Easter, 1963.

tea rooms. Extra duties included catering for weddings and other functions when she would set up the tables, provide the food, and do the waitressing.

When her parents returned to Underdale to live, Mavis accompanied them. She worked in the city store of Woolworths for 32 years and was one of their most respected and valued employees. While she was employed at such a central location it was inevitable that she should be the one whom all the out-of-town relatives would contact, and she was the one who kept all the family members in touch with one another.

The family and all its activities have always meant a great deal to Mavis, and she loves nothing more than to chat about her memories of grandparents, aunts and uncles, and the good times she had had. Her own nieces and nephews regard her with very special affection.

### PAGE 327
As her mother, Eva, became increasingly incapacitated with advancing years, Mavis cared for her unstintingly, coping with all the household chores as well as going to work.

After her retirement Mavis continued to live at Underdale, where she looked after the home and garden. She was a member of the local Church of Christ, and took an active part in the work of the church. She was a volunteer helper with

Meals on Wheels and a member of the local Senior Citizens Club.

In 1988 it became increasingly difficult for Mavis to live alone. After several attempted break-ins at the Underdale home, she very reluctantly decided to sell the house and move to Moonta, where she now lives with her brother, Doug. She has renewed acquaintance with many friends whom she knew when she lived at Moonta, and she has joined in some of the social activities of the town.

## EFFIE OLIVE TELFER

tenth child of Adam and Margaret Telfer

Effie Olive Telfer, who was born in 1886, spent her early years in South Australia. She grew up in Tantanoola, the tenth child of Adam and Margaret Telfer. She moved with the family to Waverley Park, Lower Mitcham, and with her sister, Eva, she helped to operate a dressmaking establishment at Fashoda Street, Unley. When Eva married Dick Easter, Effie was the bridesmaid.

Effie went to Western Australia to join her older sister, Lillian, who was working as a nurse in the Kalgoorlie district. Effie and Lil worked together in Kalgoorlie and Norseman.

She met Robert Wilson, a Scot, in the hospital where she was working. He had been injured in a cave-in while working in one of the gold mines. They were married on 20th November, 1918, and for a time they lived in Boulder. Later they moved to Fremantle. When work became more difficult to find, Robert joined a ship going overseas. He was a boiler maker who loved the sea and he worked for most of his life on ships.

Effie was pregnant and alone, so she returned to South Australia to await her baby's birth. The child, a daughter (Mona Margaret), was born in January, 1921. Effie spent many years in South Australia, living sometimes with her

Effie Telfer.

mother and sisters. She worked in a variety of jobs to support herself and her small daughter.

Eventually Robert returned from his travels and the family settled down together. Work was still hard to find in those depression years. Robert, always restless, went away several times. By 1933 Effie was very unhappy. She wanted a better life for herself and her daughter, so she left South Australia with the help of her sister, Eva. Shereturned to Western Australia and went down to her sister, Lillian, in Manjimup. Effie left her young daughter with Lil and her husband, Charles. She went back to Perth to find

### PAGE 328
employment, and after a while she was able to afford a small flat.

Mona joined her and found work, and life was easier and happier for them both.

In 1936 came the news that Effie's mother, Margaret, had passed away. This saddened her, as she had always been very close to her mother and her death was very hard to accept.

In 1938 Effie suffered a dreadful stroke. She was paralysed down her right side and unable to speak. It was a terrible blow to Mona, who was just seventeen years old. Effie's life hung in the balance for many weeks. Mona, determined to have a home for her mother if and when she recovered, stayed on in the flat. Recover she did, and after many weeks in

hospital, a convalescent home was suggested. So, with the help of a neighbour, Mona took her to this place. She was horrified at what she saw and so was Effie. That night Effie decided that she was not going to stay there, so she walked six miles back to the flat. Even

Mona Reddall at the front of the old Telfer home at Knightsbridge, 1986.

Mona Wilson.

Jillian, Mona, Les and "Young" Les Reddall.

### PAGE 329
Home of Mona and Les Reddall, South Perth, showing portion of their prize winning garden.

Troy Brown, Les Reddall, Adam Brown, Jillian Brown and Mona Reddall.

Colin and Jillian Brown in 1965.

Jillian and Colin Brown, 1986.

though she was very very worried about her mother, Mona had to go to work each day. Then Lily came up from Manjimup and took her sister home with her. With Lily's love and support Effie grew stronger, and in time learnt to write with her left hand. She also learnt to speak again, never so fluently, but enough to make herself understood.

Mona married Leslie Walter Reddall in 1941, and while Les was away at the War, mother and daughter shared a wonderful closeness.

In 1944, Effie's first grandchild, Jillian Margaret Reddall, was born, and in 1947, a grandson, Leslie David Reddall arrived. The children were a source of great pride and delight.

When her sister, Lily, became very ill in 1951, Effie went down to Manjimup to

help nurse her. She stayed with Lily until she died in August of that year.

The following year, Herb Telfer, Effie's brother, retired and came to Western Australia to live. He purchased a small property in Greenmount and Effie went to live with him for many years. Eventually ill health forced her to return to Mona in Perth.

Effie died in 1969 at the age of 83. She had survived for 33 years after that dreadful stroke.

Her daughter, Mona, said:-

"I salute this lady for her courage, her gentle love, determination and her wonderful sense of humour. I knew her well. She was my mother."

Mona and Les Reddall owned and operated an upholstery and furniture renovation business in South Perth until

### PAGE 330
their retirement some years ago. They live in the very pleasant suburb of South Perth. Muna's hobbies are china painting and Lee is a great gardener. His garden has won first prize in the local garden competition for the last three years.

Jillian Reddall married Colin Harry Brown in 1965. They have two sons Troy and Adam. Jill is a very talented artist.

She teaches the subjects and also has had many exhibitions of her work. Some holds an extensive position in Woodcarving in Perth.

Laurie Reddall's known as "Young Lee has two children. Alice and Annie. They live in a new house in Perth. Young Lee has almost completed an Spinn & Books in Western Australia.

## ADELAIDE ELSIE PEARL TELFER

eleventh child of Adam and Margaret Telfer

Adelaide Elise Pearl Telfer (known as Elsie) was born in Mount Gambier. The eleventh child of Adam and Margaret Telfer. She grew up at Edgerston and went to school in Tantanoola. She used to recall that her father Adam would reate

"Adelaide Elise Pearl

Is a good little girl

but no doubt she had her moments too! She and her younger sister Hazel were very close friends and shared many interests.

Elsie moved with the family to Waverley Park and for a time worked in

an industrial practice in city offices. However, this type of work was not to her liking so she trained as a nurse at the Royal Adelaide Hospital. Being admitted as a member of the Australian Nursing Federation (General) on 12th August 1930. She then did the same three months at the Queen's Home, now the Queen Victoria Hospital. At the time of her training at the Queen's Home she had to supply all her own uniforme, gauze for her tuition and instruction, and work long hours for nothing — a factor from under a condition.

Elsie Telfer

Elsie and Violet Telfer 1964

### PAGE 331
Elsie and Hazel Telfer.

After her training was completed, she worked in various hospitals throughout the State and at Broken Hill, and also did a lot of private nursing.

She returned to live at Rochester Street towards the end of her mother's life, and helped to care for her after the accident in which she broke her hip.

The Great Depression made considerable impact on the lives of the Telfer sisters, and they almost lost the family home. In common with most others, their savings dwindled, and they found the upkeep of the large property a great burden.

Elsie and her sister, Violet, took a great step when they decided to turn the family home into a private hospital, Elsie supervising the nursing and Violet acting as cook.

The Rochester Private Hospital came into being at the end of 1936, following Margaret's death. Elsie and her small staff cared for medical patients, and

Hazel and Elsie Telfer in the garden at "Waverley Park" November, 1910.

many babies were born there. Violet ensured that the meals served to patients were first-class.

As it became more difficult to get staff, the role of the Hospital changed to the Rochester Rest Home for long term guests. Many elderly ladies were assured of comfort and care while they lived there.

The house at Rochester Street had a cellar which was reached by a flight of steep steps. Elsie had the misfortune to slip on the steps, fell and broke her leg. She was laid up in great pain for several weeks, and this made the running of the Rest Home very difficult. The last patient left in 1956 and the property, which had by then become too large and burdensome for Elsie and Violet, was reluctantly sold, and they moved to a smaller house in Knightsbridge Road, Hazelwood Park.

Elsie was very interested in literature, poetry in particular. She had an extensive collection of the works of the various poets, all bound in leather. She used to write poetry herself. She loved reading.

She was a lady of very high moral character and very definite views. She was a member of the Knightsbridge Baptist Church which she attended regularly.

When her sister Hazel died in Melbourne in 1939, Elsie brought Hazel's daughter, Barbara, back to Adelaide, to live thus adding to the responsibilities which she and Violet already carried.

### PAGE 332
Rochester Rest Home, 1955.

The back verandah at Knightsbridge.

Part of the garden in Knightsbridge, 1955.

Elsie had an excellent memory, particularly for anything to do with the family. She could always remember the names and birthdays of her many nieces and nephews and their children, and she loved to reminisce about the old days at Tantanoola and Mitcham.

After their retirement, Elsie's health deteriorated. She had a stroke which

disabled her to some extent, but sheer determination enabled her to walk again and to remain as independent as possible. However, when Violet too was forced into inactivity because of an accident, it was clear that they would not be able to manage for themselves for much longer.

The sad and very reluctant decision was made to sell their home, and Violet

### PAGE 333
and Elsie took up residence at a nursing home in Norwood in 1965. Violet died in 1967, and Elsie then transferred to the Home for Incurables at Fullarton. Her health gradually deteriorated and each slight stroke left her increasingly weaker and more dependent. Her memory

gradually failed too and she slowly "faded away" in 1976.

Elsie was a very determined lady and valued her independence which she struggled to maintain as long as possible. She was buried with her parents, Adam and Margaret, at the Mitcham cemetery.

## HAZEL ROSE TELFER

twelfth child of Adam and Margaret Telfer

Hazel Rose Telfer was the youngest child of Adam and Margaret Telfer. She was born in Mount Gambier on 23rd June, 1891, and spent her early years at Edgerston, attending the Tantanoola School.

When the family moved to Waverley Park, Hazel worked in city offices where she performed secretarial duties.

She was a member of the Knightsbridge Baptist Church, and used her talents as a soprano as a valued member of the Church Choir. Hazel had an extensive collection of music. She played the piano and studied singing and she was much in demand as a soloist at various functions.

Hazel married Arnold Cuthbert Lawrie (known as Cuth) on 28th June, 1924. Cuth was the son of John and Elizabeth (nee Nunan) Lawrie of Nelshaby near Port Pirie. He was a compositor in the printing trade and a skilled artisan.

During the early years of their marriage they lived in Adelaide, but the Great Depression forced them to leave the city and move to the country to seek work. They went to Ceduna on the Far West Coast where Cuth was employed at the gypsum works.

It was there, at the small bush nursing home, that their first child, Barbara June Lawrie, was born on 16th June, 1928. Hazel's sister, Elsie, a trained nurse, went to Ceduna to help her sister after the birth of the baby. Barbara was given the second name of "June" because both

Hazel Telfer.

parents were born in June, they were married in June, and Barbara herself was born in June.

Cuth was a keen sportsman — an excellent footballer, cricketer and tennis player. He loved fishing and would travel to Streaky Bay and Smoky Bay where there was an abundance of fish. He would shoot rabbits, too, to help supplement the family's meals.

As the economic conditions improved, the Lawrie family left the West Coast and went to live in Murray Bridge where Cuth resumed his trade, working as a compositor at the Murray Valley Standard newspaper.

A second child, Malcolm Roderick Lawrie (known as Rod) was born at Rose Park Hospital on 24th February, 1931.

### PAGE 334
Marriage Certificate of Hazel Rose Telfer and Arnold Cuthbert Louise, 1924

Hazel Telfer.

Both children started school at Murray Bridge Infant School, where Hazel was an enthusiastic member of the Mothers Club. They attended Sunday School at the Murray Bridge Presbyterian Church.

There was a huge mulberry tree in the backyard, and a home-made swing provided hours of fun. "Pleasant Sunday afternoon" activities included walks across the narrow footway on the road bridge across the Murray River, and visits to the nearby Sturt Reserve, a popular picnic place. The excitement of the day was the whistle approach of the express train as it passed through Murray Bridge on its way to Melbourne. Murray Bridge was a refreshment stop for passengers so there was plenty of

Arnold Cuthbert and Hazel Rose Louise, 28th June 1904

activity at the station as the steam engine was replenished with water after the long haul through the Adelaide hills. The town children would gather to wave to the lucky passengers!

Cuth found a stray kitten in the woodshed — a black Manx — which was promptly named "Stumpy". Stumpy became the family pet and accompanied them to Melbourne where it settled down

### PAGE 335
Barbara June Lawrie, aged 1 year.
(Photograph taken at the Telfer Studios,
Port Pirie.)

for several years before being run over in the street.

The River flats were famous for their dairy herds which produced the milk and cream for the local market. Each Sunday morning Cuth would ride his bike across the bridge and out to a local dairy to obtain some cream. Usually Barbara accompanied him, "donkeyed" on the bar of the bike. On one memorable occasion, Cuth turned rather too quickly at the gate, and upended Barbara into a large patch of stinging nettles! The memory of the pain has remained to this day, and stinging nettles are still treated with exaggerated "respect" and loathing.

Murray Bridge at that time was a small country town. There was no sewerage system, so the toilet was an "outhouse" at the end of the yard. The furnishings were not luxurious — a bucket, a seat, squares of newspaper threaded on string hanging on the back of the door and a tin of ashes! On one night each week a Council employee would do the rounds emptying the contents of the buckets into the town's

Barbara and Roderick Lawrie.

“honey cart”. Hazel named him “the dub man”!

Whenever the opportunity presented itself, Hazel and the children would travel to Adelaide to visit her mother at Knightsbridge. They would sometimes travel by train and enjoy the trip through the tunnels in the hills, and eagerly await the first great landmark — the old home at Waverley Park, which was quite close to the Mitcham Railway Station and clearly visible from the train. On other occasions they would all travel in the family's Oakland car, winding down through the hills on the then narrow road. Both children suffered from car sickness, so those journeys would have been none too pleasant.

There was always a great welcome from Grandma and Aunt Vi. Grandma would sit by the window in the dining room, and although she could not see, she would listen patiently while the children recited or sang for her. Aunt Vi would take them to feed the ducks and the fowls, and to play on the swings at the playground opposite the house. Barbara always went home with a bunch of soursobs!

Hazel did not have good health. She contracted pneumonia and pleurisy and later tuberculosis for which there was no cure at that time. It was a dreaded killer disease. Cuth built a sleepout on the front verandah of their house at Murray Bridge and Hazel spent much time there — fresh

### PAGE 336
air was the only known means of alleviating the symptoms. A maid was employed to help run the house and look after the children.

In 1936 Cuth obtained employment with a Melbourne firm and the family moved to Victoria. Hazel and Barbara travelled by the Melbourne Express, and Cuth and Rod went with the driver of the furniture van. They lived in suburban Essendon and the two children attended the Essendon State School. One of the teachers at that school was Alison Wishart, the daughter of Hazel's cousin, Daisy Wishart, who was in turn the grand-daughter of Elizabeth Farrow (nee Telfer).

The infantile paralysis epidemic raged through the country in 1938 and all the Victorian schools were closed. Children were given lessons to complete at home, and Hazel assumed the role of teacher and supervisor.

The family joined the Glenbervie Baptist Church and Barbara and Roderick attended Sunday School. Hazel was a member of the Women's Fellowship.

Cuth bought a wireless set, the first in the street.

There were always children from the neighbourhood visiting in order to listen to "Dad and Dave", "Martin's Corner" and other popular radio serials of the day.

Cuth was a great gardener and grew all the vegetables for the family. The display of flowers in the front garden always brought favourable comments from passers-by.

Hazel felt the separation from her family very much. Melbourne was a long way away. Unfortunately her health continued to deteriorate to such an extent that she had no alternative but to enter the T.B. Sanatorium at Heatherton, an outlying suburb of Melbourne. Cuth and the children went to board with friends at North Fitzroy, but they undertook the fairly long train journey to visit Hazel every Sunday afternoon.

Life at the Sanatorium was made as pleasant for the patients as possible. At

first Hazel was able to get up and walk and sit in the gardens, but eventually she was confined to bed. The staff and patients entered into the spirit of Christmas and other occasions and decorated the wards accordingly. At one of these special events, Hazel's ward was decorated as Snow White and the Seven Dwarfs. Hazel was the dwarf, "Sleepy". When the doctor made his rounds and spoke to her, she answered with a loud snore! Her sense of humour did not desert her, even when she was so ill.

Hazel died in May, 1939, at the age of 47. She was buried in the Cheltenham Cemetery in Melbourne. On her grave are the words, "The Lord is my Shepherd".

Cuth and Rod continued to live in Melbourne, boarding with friends. Barbara went to Adelaide where she lived with her Aunts Vi and Elsie at Rochester Street, Knightsbridge.

Cuth died in 1963.

Barbara June Lawrie was born at Ceduna on 16th June, 1928. Her earliest years were spent on the Far West Coast where her father was working during the depression years. She was the proud owner of a doll called "Betty Blue" and a toy "Kanga". When she was three years old the family moved to Murray Bridge where she started school. She took part in the Grade 1 production of "the Teddy Bears' Picnic" at the school concert.

Employment opportunities were better in Melbourne, so Barbara moved with her parents and brother, and lived in the suburb of Essendon. New experiences included electric trains and bitterly cold winters when water lying in the gutters would freeze every night and clothes left on the line to dry would be stiff with frost and ice.

Barbara and Roderick attended the Essendon State School, but during the poliomyelitis epidemic they had to do their lessons at home.

During their time at Essendon, Barbara and Roderick went to the Glenbervie Baptist Sunday School.

On Fridays the children were allowed to buy their lunch. They each were given

### PAGE 337
fourpence (three cents) and this would buy a fritz roll, a buttered bun and a piece of fruit! A favourite weekend activity was catching yabbies in the creek near their home. This was accomplished with a minimum of equipment, viz a piece of string and some pieces of meat. Both Barbara and Roderick were very adept at removing the yabbies from the bait without being nipped. The resultant catch would be cooked in a kerosene tin over a fire in the back yard.

Cuth built a "billy cart" for Roderick. It was a rather grand vehicle with rubber wheels and performed very well when raced against any opposition from other children.

A visit to the Royal Melbourne Show was an event to be remembered. Spending money amounted to sixpence (5 cents). In those days sample bags were free and a ride on the merry-go-round cost one penny (1 cent).

On Friday evenings the family would go to Puckle Street, Moonee Ponds, to take advantage of the late night shopping. Barbara's greatest wish at this time was to own a pair of patent leather shoes. Alas, the wish was never granted! Black calf shoes were much more serviceable and easier for the home handyman to repair.

Barbara was nine years old when her mother was forced, because of deteriorating health, to enter the Heatherton Sanatorium. Cuth and the children then boarded with friends at North Fitzroy and the children attended the Falconer Street central School, North Fitzroy, where Barbara topped the Grade 5 class in 1938.

Early in 1939 the family changed their boarding place and went to live at Caulfield. Three months later, Hazel died. Cuth and Rod continued to board with friends, but Barbara went back to Adelaide with her Aunt Elsie to live at Rochester Street.

At this time the Rochester Private Hospital was operating and it was no small sacrifice for the two Aunts, who were not young women, to accept the

Barbara Lawrie, Grade 5, 1938.

responsibility for a ten-year old girl in addition to all their other duties.

Barbara went to Marryatville School and then to Norwood High School for four years. During the time at Norwood High she played hockey in the A team and was elected a Prefect in her last year. She gained the Leaving Certificate, topping the State in Modern History at the Public Examinations.

A year at Miss Mann's Business College in Adelaide studying shorthand, typing and book-keeping, was followed by six and a half years teaching at that establishment. Barbara instructed students in the skills of shorthand, typing book-keeping and office procedures. She herself attained a shorthand speed of 130 words per minute.

The next employment move was to the Public Service of South Australia where she worked as a stenographer in the Public Service Commissioner's Office, and later as Secretary to the Commissioner of Highways.

### PAGE 338
Scripture Examination Certificate, 1943.

Many of her social activities were centred around the Knightsbridge Baptist Church. She was a member of the Christian Endeavour Society and a Sunday School scholar until the age of 16 when she became a Sunday School Teacher, and later the supervisor of the Primary Department. She sang in the Church Choir as an alto. She was an enthusiastic member of the Senior Girls Club and joined in all their social and sporting activities. These were the war years, and petrol was short, so all outings were taken by tram, on bikes, or on foot.

Moonlight and daylight hikes were features of the social activities, as well as the Saturday night socials run at the Church for all young people.

At the age of 12 Barbara joined the 1st Knightsbridge Girl Guide Company. She eventually became Patrol Leader of the Kookaburra Patrol. During the war years much time was spent in collecting scrap materials and old newspapers for the war effort. Both the Schools Patriotic Fund and the Girl Guides participated in these efforts, and mighty were the loads carried on the bicycles! At 17 years of age, she was asked to help as a leader in the 1st Rose Park Guide Company and carried out these duties for a year.

Barbara held the position of Assistant Secretary of the Baptist State Youth Council for several years, and helped to organise the annual Easter Youth Camps

Baptismal Certificate, 1945.

which were held at the Clare Showgrounds.

Sporting interests were tennis for the Church team, and hockey where she played left wing for Wirrawarra in the A grade S.A. Women's Hockey Association; and table tennis. Hockey was played in the South Parklands and the only way to get there was to ride a bike about $3 \frac{1}{2}$ miles. Going there was fine, but the ride home was quite a struggle as the going was all uphill!

Barbara, in company with four friends, went overseas for fourteen months, sailing on the S.S. "Arcadia" on 8th December, 1955, and arriving in London on 2nd January, 1956. There followed a year of hectic activity, working in offices during the worst of the winter months, and travelling extensively during the better weather. Many and varied were the

### PAGE 339
office positions held in London. The first was with the Jewish Blind Society, then a shoe manufacturer and importer, a tobacco company, a music retailer, a building company, and lastly with the Church Commissioners for England Legal Department. The Church Commissioners were responsible for the management of the vast property and assets of the Church of England.

The five girls had a flat at Hammersmith Road, West Kensington,

Aunt Vi, Colin and Barbara Lodge, and Aunt Elsie, with Jamie and Alison Lodge, 1959.

and lived there during their stay in London. During the winter months they rode on the London buses in every direction and visited as many theatres as possible, often spending many hours in the queues for seats in "the gods". It was possible to see the famous stars of stage and screen for a very small outlay. Perhaps the biggest thrill was to see Dame Margot Fonteyn dance, and the price of the ticket was five shillings (50 cents). The only other price to pay was a hard seat.

During the summer months she travelled to Wales and many parts of England; then had two trips to the Continent, visiting many countries. Most travel was done by means of hitchhiking, the usual means of travel in those days.

Barbara Lawrie and Colin Lodge, 1958.

Marriage Certificate of Colin Lodge and Barbara Lawrie, 1959.

### PAGE 340
Barbara, holding Christopher, and Peter Lodge, 1963.

The farmhouse at "Mine Gully".

An aerial view of "Mine Gully", Ardrossan, 1982.

Accommodation was in Youth Hostels. The amount allocated for six weeks travel in Europe, including all fares, accommodation, films and souvenirs, was £60 ($120)!

The second trip to the Continent was done in comfort in a hired car. Another month of temporary employment in London replenished the coffers, and then a month touring in Scotland. This was perhaps one of the highlights of the whole tour, and how much more interesting and exciting it would have been had the interest in family history been as strong then as it is now! A lot more time would have been spent in the Border districts, but Jedburgh and Hawick were visited.

The last three winter months were spent working in the legal department of the Church Commissioners, and then it was boarding the "Arcadia" for the voyage home. The cost of the voyage to

England, lasting three and a half weeks, in a four-berth cabin was £125 ($250), and the voyage home in a six-berth cabin was £100 ($200).

Barbara returned to her job with the Highways Department for two years, and then married Colin Stanley Lodge, a farmer of Ardrossan. Yorke Peninsula, South Australia. The marriage took place at the Knightsbridge Baptist Church on 7th March, 1959. At the wedding reception, the question was asked,

"How are they going to keep her down on the farm,

After she's seen Paree?"

but there were no problems as she adapted to country life very quickly.

The farm, known as "Mine Gully" has been in the Lodge family since 1877 when Colin's great-grandfather made the first purchase of the land. The farm produces barley, wool and fat lambs.

### PAGE 341
Two sons were born — Peter Colin Lodge in 1960 and Christopher Lawrie Lodge in 1962.

There have been many community interests in Ardrossan, many centred around the children's activities. As they grew up Barbara supervised the activities of the Primary Department of the Uniting Church Sunday School, and acted as President of the committee that was formed to build a kindergarten in Ardrossan. The Committee raised the money to build and equip the kindergarten to the standards then required by the Kindergarten Union by holding many fund raising functions. When school days began Barbara joined the School Welfare Club and was President of that body for five years, and later served as Secretary/Treasurer of the School Council for four years. She was Secretary of the Ardrossan Meals on Wheels group for four years.

Besides teaching at the Sunday School, she was President and later Secretary of the Ardrossan Women's Fellowship.

For ten years Barbara had a part-time position as Secretary of the Ardrossan Area School. During those years the school celebrated its centenary, and she co-operated with a friend and researched and wrote the Ardrossan School Centenary Book. She also undertook the compilation of the Ardrossan Area School Centenary Cookbook, a large project which earned a lot of money for the school.

After relinquishing the position at the school office, Barbara and Colin travelled throughout Australia on camping holidays. They bought a Toyota four-wheel drive station wagon and ventured into many parts of the outback, living for three months at a time in a tent, and later in a caravan.

Present interests include taking an active part in raising funds for the Ardrossan Hospital. Barbara has been President of the Ardrossan Hospital Auxiliary for six years and ex officio a member of the Hospital Board. Other interests include the Ardrossan

Retirement Homes, the "Share and Care" Group, the local Branch of the National Trust and Red Cross where she has a special responsibility for the medical loans department.

Colin is a Life Member of the Petersville Agricultural Bureau and has held the positions of Secretary and President. He too is interested in the Retirement Homes project and the National Trust. His chief recreational interest is fishing, both from his boat and at night with the light and spear. He played cricket and football for the Ardrossan teams, and won many trophies as the best left arm spin bowler.

Outback Travel — corrugations on the Gunbarrel Highway, 1986. Colin Lodge.

The Lodge Angling Barley Roller.

### PAGE 342
"Mine Gully" Tractors, 1980. In 1980, Colin Lodge had six tractors — 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6 cylinders. From the right of the picture, they are:- Field Marshall (1), John Decre (2); Chamberlain Super 90 (3); International W.6 (4); Deutz DX85 (5); and a Case 500 (6).

Australian Bicentennial Historic Records Search Certificate.

He is a very inventive farmer and has designed and built much of the equipment used on the farm. He holds the Australian patents for the Lodge Angling Barley Roller which he designed and built. The implement is designed to assist with controlling wind damage in the barley crops.

Jubilee 150 Certificate.

In 1987 Barbara and Colin decided to hand over control of the farm to their sons and to semi-retire in the Ardrossan township. They purchased a house on the cliff top, overlooking St. Vincent Gulf and left the boys to "batch" on the farm at Mine Gully.

Colin accepted a position as Greenkeeper at the Ardrossan Bowling Club, and they both decided to try to learn to play the game. Both play socially.

Researching family history has become a fascinating and time-consuming hobby or occupation which has given a lot of pleasure. It has been the means of keeping in touch with the wider family and of making the acquaintance of many hitherto unknown relatives. Not only the Telfer connection, but all other branches of the family are receiving attention, and a lot of social history is being recorded.

### PAGE 343
Peter Lodge, 1972.

Peter Lodge, 1982.

Gardening and making "home brew" are Colin's other interests, and after winning two free lessons in a competition, Barbara now attends classes in ceramics.

Peter Colin Lodge was born on 31st January, 1960. He grew up on the family farm at Ardrossan and went to the Ardrossan Area School until Year 9. At the age of 14 he went to Adelaide for three years to attend Westminster School as a boarder. While there he was a member of the school A grade soccer team, and took part in other sports. During those years he played Australian Rules Football for the Ardrossan Senior Colts team whenever he was home for weekends, and he received special permission to come home for the Grand Final in 1976 when Ardrossan were unfortunately defeated after having to play extra time at the conclusion of the match.

Peter gained his Matriculation certificate at the age of 16 and then went to Flinders University to study Earth Sciences. After two years he decided that

Cradle Roll Certificate — Peter Colin Lodge.

### PAGE 344
city life was not for him and he returned to Ardrossan to work on the farm which was much more to his liking.

As a child he attended the Ardrossan Methodist Sunday School and was a member of the 1st Ardrossan Cub Pack. He was one of the first children to attend the Ardrossan Kindergarten.

Each school vacation from the age of 16 years, he earned extra pocket money by working at the Ardrossan Terminal Silo for the Australian Barley Board. This seasonal work has continued each year, and the knowledge gained has been most helpful in determining which varieties of barley are best suited to the farm.

During his matriculation year he wrote his main Geography assignment about the family farm at Ardrossan, and gained an "A" rating for his effort.

Peter attended a wool classing school and gained his certificate. He works in local shearing sheds during the shearing season, classing wool for the local farmers. He is very interested in sheep husbandry and wool generally, and follows the wool and lamb markets with keen interest. His experience working at the silo gives him an insight into the requirements and standards of the barley trade.

Peter excels in three sports in particular — golf, tennis and table tennis. He won C grade Championship in his first year with the Ardrossan Golf Club, and brought his handicap down to 11 in three

years. He plays in tournaments throughout Yorke Peninsula and has represented Ardrossan at matches in Adelaide and elsewhere.

He is the No. 1 player for the Petersville Tennis Club and has held the position of Captain. He is at present the President of the Club. He plays night tennis in the Ardrossan Association, and he has had considerable success playing Table Tennis for the Petersville team. He also plays social bowls.

While he was a student at Westminster School he joined a party of students on a three-week camping trip to Darwin and the Northern Territory. In 1982 he spent a month holidaying in New Zealand. In 1985 he went on an extended four month overseas trip, travelling overland for eleven weeks across Asia, the Middle East and Europe with "Top Deck" on a double-decker bus, camping all the way. He spent a short time in London and then went to Europe for six weeks, returning to Australia with a two day stop-over in Singapore.

Peter is a member of the Ardrossan Apex Club and during 1987/88 held the position of Treasurer of the Club. He helps with the many community projects undertaken in the district. He is also a member of the Petersville Agricultural Bureau.

Together with his brother, Christopher, he has assumed the management of the family farm, being the fifth generation of the family to live and work on the property. In 1989 he purchased his own house at Tiddy Widdy Beach, near Ardrossan, and is at present setting up his own household.

Christopher Lawrie Lodge was born on 25th March, 1962. He attended Ardrossan Area School until the end of Year 10 and then completed his education at Minlaton High School where he took part in a special agricultural course and gained a Farm Technology Certificate. The course involved theoretical as well as practical work and included one week working on a local farm and one week on a sheep station in the Far North of South

Skirting the fleece — Peter, Colin and Christopher Lodge, 1986.

### PAGE 345
Christopher Lodge, 1972.

Australia. He has also studied Wool Classing and First Aid.

On leaving school he completed an apprenticeship as a Motor Mechanic with Ardrossan Motors, and after working for that firm for another two years, he relinquished his position and took up share farming for a neighbour. His parting gift from Ardrossan Motors included a "Little Golden Book" entitled "Animals on the Farm" to help him, no doubt, tell a sheep from a cow!

Christopher is now sharing the management of the Lodge family farm with his brother, Peter, supplementing his income by working in shearing sheds as a wool classer, and by occasional contracts as a motor mechanic.

He is most interested in restoring old cars and uses his mechanical knowledge to good effect. His current project is the complete restoration of an FJ Holden car.

Chris has always been most interested in the land, and has always wanted to be a farmer. When he was in Grade 2 at the age of six years, he announced that he

Farm Technology Certificate — Christopher Lodge, 1978.

thought he would stay home and help Dad. After all, he could read and he could write and what more did anyone need?!

As a foundation member of the Ardrossan Apex Club, he has played an important part in organising and working on the many community projects in Ardrossan. He joined a party of Apexians who travelled to the South East of the State to assist farmers in the clean-up operations after the disastrous bushfires of Ash Wednesday in 1983. During the year 1987/88 he was President of the Club.

One of his main recreational interests is fishing, and he likes to catch them with a light and spear. Boat fishing and crabbing, and dabbing for garfish are all equally enjoyable. He also enjoys eating the results of his catch!

### PAGE 346
Christopher Lodge, President of Ardrossan Apex Club, 1987.

Chris played football for the Ardrossan Junior Colts Team and he plays tennis and table tennis for the Petersville Clubs.

In 1980 he went on a camping trip to Darwin and the Northern Territory; in 1982 he went to New Zealand for a month; and in 1984 he joined a cruise on the S.S. "Fairstar" to the Pacific Islands. While he was on the cruise there was a bomb scare, and all passengers were hastily disembarked. The scare turned out to be a hoax, but it was a frightening and unpleasant experience. Chris came home wearing a T-shirt bearing the words, "I survived the Fairstar Bomb Scare".

While he was share-farming, he entered a lamb in the district fat lamb competition, organised by the Ardrossan Football Club, and won first prize.

During his year as President of the Apex Club of Ardrossan he took a leading role in organising the Bi-Centennial Event of the year — "Ardrossan Alive" — an afternoon and evening of non-stop entertainment for people of all ages.

Christopher Lodge with the prize-winning lamb, 1985.

Melanoma Research and local charities benefited as a result of the entertainment. Christopher announced his engagement to Leanne Bulpitt, of Ardrossan, in December, 1988. They plan to marry in 1990 when Leanne completes her training as a Registered Nurse.

Malcolm Roderick Lawrie (known as Rod) was born at the Rose Park Private Hospital on 24th February, 1931. He was the youngest grandchild of Adam and Margaret Telfer.

He started school at Murray Bridge and then attended various schools in Melbourne. After his mother, Hazel, died, he lived with his father in Melbourne and attended Technical school. In 1946 his father, Cuth Lawrie, was severely injured in a road accident and spent many months in hospital. When he recovered Rod came back to Adelaide, but he did not enjoy city life. He worked as a jackeroo on Commonwealth Hill Station, via Tarcoola — the largest sheep station in the world, being 120,000 square miles in area.

In 1955 he married Amelia Clark and they had one son, Roderick James Lawrie, known as Jim.

The family went to New South Wales where Rod was appointed Overseer for the Brewarrana Pastoral Company at Narrandera, and after that he worked on another station near Moree.

### PAGE 347
Rod and Amelia now live at Kippa Ring, a few miles north of Brisbane.

Amelia is an artist and likes to paint in oils and water colours. Rod is a very keen fisherman, and the family often go to Fraser Island where they enjoy camping holidays and the very good fishing.

Rod works at Sure Screens during the week, but he has a well equipped workshop at home and is building up a weekend business as well.

Roderick James Lawrie (known as Jim) was born on 11th November, 1956. He works for Con-Force as a steel fixer. When the construction of the new Commonwealth Bank Building in Brisbane is completed, he intends to go to the Gulf country to work.

Jim shares his father's love of fishing. They recently went to Swains Reef, 120 miles north east of Gladstone, a trip of some 14 hours. The fishing was a huge success in spite of rather unfavourable weather. The wind blew at 40 knots for four out of the five days, but they fished from a 12 ft. dinghy and thoroughly enjoyed the experience.

Roderick Lawrie, 1946.

### PAGE 354
## THE TELFER FAMILY REUNION

The idea of holding a Telfer Family Reunion was first suggested by Hope Simmons, a granddaughter of John and Caroline Telfer, who settled at Hawker. Hope had been researching the Telfer Family History for some years, and after contacting many Telfer relatives, several meetings were held to plan and organise the event.

A committee consisting of -
Bob Telfer (Chairman)
Hope Simmons (Secretary)
George Telfer (Treasurer)
Eric (Rick) Telfer
Claude Rankin
Vida Templer

Barbara Lodge
Colin Telfer
Garth Beckwith
Reg Telfer

was formed to make the necessary arrangements and they met each month for more than a year. Several locations were considered for the Reunion the first favourite being Kersbrook because of the association of the family with that district. However facilities were inadequate, and the location finally chosen was Marden High School in Adelaide. Extensive advertising and correspondence were undertaken to ensure that the largest possible number of Telfer descendants were aware of the function.

Sunday, 13th April, 1986, was a perfect autumn day, and the success of the function was assured from the arrival of the first guest to the departure of the last.

An impressive display of family photographs and other memorabilia kept visitors interested until a short thanksgiving service conducted by the Rev. Peter Whittington, with Mr. Eddie Telfer as Reader, formally opened proceedings.

Part of the display of historical photographs at the Reunion — Adam and Margaret Telfer and family.

Now thank we all our God
With hearts, and hands, and voices;

With ever-joyful hearts

## Benediction

## ADDRESS GIVEN BY THE REV. PETER WHITTINGTON

We are here today to celebrate the history of the Telfer family. You might think that you know the history of the Telfers, but I know the inside story. As one who has only married into the family, I can reveal the truth! But before I do that, let us remind ourselves of the heritage we are celebrating today.

Francis Telfer, a shepherd, arrived at Holdfast Bay on the "Prince George". The "Prince George" arrived on 26th December, 1838. Francis Telfer was accompanied by his wife, a son and two daughters. A baby boy had not survived

the voyage. In later years, Francis Telfer was followed to South Australia by a niece and four nephews. A stream of life whose character had been forged by the struggle of generation after generation to maintain a normal standard of living in an environment which could be gentle, but also harsh and very cold — that stream of life was now taking root in an equally harsh but very hot and quite different environment. The character of this family would be tested very greatly, for the struggle was now not just to survive, but to adapt, and to establish a

### PAGE 357
way of life which would meet the challenges of a new land without denying the values and traditions of the past.

Having travelled through the Border country of Scotland on a number of occasions, I have wondered why Francis Telfer and others left. The rolling hills of Roxburgh can be very beautiful, but I know that economic pressures and the long winters played their part. But, to uproot so completely!

I talked recently to a woman who had migrated from England, and she told me that it took her ten years to get used to gum trees. They were so alien and untidy! Imagine the contrast that Francis Telfer and his family experienced — new sounds, new smells, the often unbearable heat, the bizarre, to their eyes, animals and the brilliant bright light.

Today we are celebrating the character of the Telfer family, which did adapt and flourish, and we are giving thanks for this demonstration of the human spirit.

Now for the inside story! Am I going to name the black sheep of the family? Well, there must have been some surely!

In history, alongside Thomas Telford, or Telfer, the Engineer, I would put Jamie Telfer in the well-known Border ballad. The English, of course, would consider him a cattle rustler, but he was really a freedom fighter! Come to think of it, the village of Whittington, my family name, is only a few miles across the Border. There may even be a few old scores to settle!

No — the inside story is not the black sheep of the family. The inside story, the truth I have to reveal, is in fact what the writer to the Hebrews describes. The inside story of the Telfers, which accompanies the external story of emigration and settlement, is one of faith, and faith, said the writer, is the assurance of things hoped for, and the conviction of things not seen. The writer is saying that, at the heart of the Christian message, is not human achievement, but a readiness to trust God. A Christian is not a self-made man, but a person whose strength of character derives from God. To support

The Rev. Peter Whittington, guest preacher, giving the address at the thanksgiving service.

his argument, the writer reminds his readers of those great Old Testament heroes, and gives examples of trusting faith. For example, by faith, Abraham obeyed when God told him to set forth to a new country, and he went out not knowing where he was going.

On reading through the Telfer history, I was struck by the references to Church-going. Various denominations are mentioned, not, I think, because Telfers were always changing their allegiance, but rather because worship of God was first priority, and they joined whatever Church was available.

Today we can celebrate the human spirit as exemplified by the character and success of the Telfer family, and we can celebrate the inside story.

Now to the present generation of Telfers! Many different surnames are linked to this family. We have considerably greater comforts than our ancestors, but the world is in many ways a much more dangerous place. Has the pioneering spirit died out, or can we look

### PAGE 358
to the same source as our forebears for the strength of character to meet the challenges that confront us?

"Wherefore," said the writer to the Hebrews, "God is not ashamed to be called their God, for He has prepared for them a city."

Amen.

Following the service, Mrs. Hope Simmons, as Secretary of the Organising Committee, outlined the events which had led to the Reunion, and then the Committee Chairman, Mr. Bob Telfer, officially welcomed all the Telfer descendants. Greetings were read from Scotland and Canada.

"Hullo Folks!"

A word of cheer to all the Telfer kin gathered together to celebrate their long association with South Australia. May you continue to multiply and prosper for many years to come. 'Lang may your lum reek!'

From another Telfer living in the Scottish Borderland.

All good wishes — Cathie H. Telfer And from Canada -

"It is a great honour to say hello from Canada on this very special day! My father, George Telfer, was born here. Some of my relatives include Thomas G. Telfer, born here in 1863, George Telfer, born in Roxburghshire in 1814, and Thomas Telfer, born 1777, in Roxburghshire, Scotland. I was born here in 1936. We have two sons, Thomas George William, born in 1963, and David John, born in 1966. Thomas is in Law School and David is in an Arts Course at the University of Western Ontario. My wife, Hazel, and I are both teachers. We look forward to coming your way in the near future. Good luck, good health and best wishes to all. Please try to visit us when you can. All the best —Thomas and Hazel

Telfer

After luncheon, time was set aside for the taking of official family photographs,

Bob Telfer, Chairman of the Telfer Reunion Committee, welcoming the many descendants at the Reunion, while Hope Simmons, Secretary of the Committee, looks on.

Miss Cathie Telfer in her garden at Newcastleton.

### PAGE 359
but the main enjoyment of the day was the opportunity for the renewal of old acquaintances and the meeting of new and hitherto unknown relatives.

Representatives of all branches of the family were present, as well as members of other branches of the Telfer family

previously unknown to the South Australian group.

It was a most successful and happy day and the large numbers of people who attended ensured that the Telfer pioneers were honoured in South Australia's Jubilee year.
