### PAGE 362
## ROBERT TELFER

ROBERT TELFER (1803-1876) m. ELIZABETH OLIVER (1807-1846)

(Robert Telfer was a brother of Francis Telfer, and an uncle of James, Elizabeth, Robert John and Adam Telfer, all of whom came to South Australia.)

Early in 1987, a letter was received from Miss Christine Henry, who had seen a copy of the booklet "A Brief Account of the First Members of the Telfer Family to Settle in South Australia" which had been sent to Miss Cathie Telfer in Newcastleton. Parts of the letter are reproduced here:-

"I learned from Miss Cathie Telfer of Newcastleton that you are interested in research on the Telfer Family tree. As

part of my family tree I have investigated the Telfer branch of our family and from the information in your booklet it seems that our branches are related. My branch comes from Robert Telfer (1803-1876), the brother of Francis Telfer (1809-1895) who emigrated to Australia. It seems that our branch of the family has not been as productive as yours and there are no Telfers left — only a few Nichols and their descendants.

I enclose a copy of our branch of the family tree and I hope it may be of some interest to you."

View of the Village of Newcastleton taken from the hillside on the property of Mrs. Margaret Henry, a descendant of Robert Telfer, brother of James and Francis.

### PAGE 363
Most of the daughters of Robert Nichol still live in or near Newcastle. I was brought up in Newcastle, but have since moved away. My parents still live in Newcastle, in a house which was owned by Robert Nichol, and we think was probably lived in by Elizabeth Telfer before him."

Christine wrote a further letter, explaining the steps she had taken to discover the information about her ancestors.

"The following is a summary of how I got back to James Telfer and Elizabeth

Hutton; — My grandfather was Robert Nichol. He died in 1954, aged 85 years, at Newcastle, Roxburghshire. On his death certificate he is said to be Robert Nichol (formerly Robert Telfer) and his mother is listed as Elizabeth Telfer (domestic servant) — there is no father listed. I went from this to his birth certificate which, as expected, showed him to be illegitimate. This explained why he was called Robert Telfer, but not why he changed his name to Nichol. Family folklore says that his father was a man called John Nichol who offered to

### PAGE 364
Elizabeth Telfer, daughter of Robert and Elizabeth Telfer (nee Oliver), grandmother of Margaret Henry.

marry his mother but was turned down, but Robert Nichol took his name anyway.

I then found Elizabeth Telfer's death certificate which gives her as 59 years old in 1893 and her mother and father are listed as Robert Telfer (clogger) and Elizabeth m.s. Oliver. As the profession of clogger is fairly unusual it was easy to track Robert Telfer. For instance, on his death certificate in 1878 he is listed as 74 years old and his father and mother are James Telfer (woollen weaver) and Elizabeth m.s. Hutton.

I have fleshed these bones out a bit from census records for Newcastleton and other sources but have not been able to follow it further back than James Telfer and Elizabeth Hutton.

I went to Castleton graveyard to look at "our" gravestone, i.e. the Robert Nichol (Telfer) one, which also has a lot of his

"Stonegarthsido Hall", the family home of Cathie Telfer, just over the border in Cumbria. The area was the scene of many skirmishes between the Scots and the English. Cathie now lives in retirement in Newcastleton, Roxburghshire.

family including Elizabeth Telfer and the original Robert Telfer and Elizabeth Oliver. Just next to it was a gravestone with a James Telfer and Elizabeth Hutton of about the right ages for ours. Also near these two was the one with Francis, Jenet and Adam Telfer. I admit I jumped to the conclusion that this James Telfer and Elizabeth Hutton were "ours" and that probably the Francis, Jenet and Adam Telfer were connected, as from their dates and ages I supposed them to be James Telfer's brothers and sister. If you could see how the headstones are arranged together in a large graveyard, you would jump to conclusions too!

Your information has been most interesting as I had no idea that so many Telfers had emigrated to Australia."

It has been interesting to note that the 1841 Census lists James Telfer (aged 75) and Betty Telfer (Hutton) (aged 65) as living in the home of their son, Robert (36) and Betty (Oliver) aged 34 and their children, Elizabeth (7), James (5), Jean (3) and John (2).

Later another letter was received from Christine's mother, Mrs. Margaret Henry, who lives in Newcastleton. Her husband, Walter Henry, worked at "Stonegarthsido Hall", the farm owned by Cathie Telfer's family, before their marriage in 1952. They assumed that

### PAGE 365
there was some relationship because of striking likenesses in family photographs.

Christine Henry, who has been researching the family history, now lives in London. She has been studying for a PhD degree while working for the Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries. Christine's chief interest is in the diseases of crop wheat and sugar beet.

Margaret and Walter have a small holding of 55 acres near the village of Newcastleton. Walter also works for the Forestry Department. Their cows, calves and young stock go to the Holm Hill grazing during the summer months, so they still have a link with the old ways of the crofters.

## MARGARET TELFER (b. 1832)

(Margaret Telfer was the twin sister of Elizabeth Beattie Telfer who married John Charles Dillon. She was a sister of James, Robert, John and Adam Telfer, who also emigrated to South Australia.)

Margaret Telfer was the daughter of James and Elizabeth (nee Beattie) Telfer She was the twin sister of Elizabeth Beattie Telfer who emigrated to South Australia and married John Charles Dillon.

Margaret was born in 1832. On 16th September, 1864, the birth was recorded of Robert Telfer, at Falside, Southdean. The mother — Margaret Telfer; Father...

The next information is contained in the 1891 Jedburgh Census. Robert Telfer aged 26, Grocer, born Southdean, was head of a household. The other members of the household were Elizabeth Telfer (grandmother) widow, aged 87, living on private means, born Newcastleton; and Margaret Telfer, his mother, Widow, aged 59, Housekeeper, born Newcastleton.

Robert's grandmother, Elizabeth was Elizabeth (nee Beattie), and it would therefore appear that she spent her last years living with her daughter, Margaret, and her grandson, Robert. Elizabeth died in 1891.

## "OTHER" TELFERS

Since beginning this project, contact has been made with many other Telfer families. While their connection with "our" family is not known, it can safely be assumed that in many cases some sort of relationship is likely. Many of the families originated in the same area of Roxburghshire; the same Christian names are used over and over again; and many of them began life as shepherds.

We are pleased to include the information given in the hope that at some future time the actual family connections will be traced.

Beverley Wilson, of Castle Hill, New South Wales, is a descendant of William Telfer, who arrived in Australia in 1825, possibly the first Telfer to come to the new land. He was accompanied by his brother, Andrew, and together they played an important part in the sheep industry and pioneering in the New England area of New South Wales. The story of the two brothers and their association with the Australian Agricultural Company is reproduced here. The information was sent by Beverley Wilson.

### PAGE 366
## In Australia (1825-1850)

## And his Association with the Australian Agricultural Company

William Telfer was born 7th October, 1802, the fourth child of William Telfer, a shepherd, and his wife Janet (nee Nichol) who were living in the Parish of Oxnam, Roxburgh, Scotland. His brother Andrew had been born four years earlier on 28th April, 1798.

Both became very proficient in caring for sheep. From an early age they had been shepherds and had herded sheep in the area of the Cheviot Hills — the range of hills on the border of Scotland and England. At times they experienced hard winters and bad seasons and work was difficult to obtain.

Their association with the Australian Agricultural Co. began early in 1825.

The Australian Agricultural Co. was formed in London in 1824 to develop the fine wool industry. Initial capital was to be £1,000,000 ($2m) made up of 10,000 shares of £100 ($200) each; a grant of 1,000,000 acres to be made to the Co.; no rival concern with similar objects was to be established in N.S.W. for 20 years and ultimately 1,400 convicts were to be employed.

An Australian Advisory committee was appointed when the Co. was formed — Hannibal Macarthur, James Macarthur, John Bowman, Archdeacon T.H. Scott (who refused to act) and Captain P.P. King.

Robert Dawson was appointed as agent and manager and was sent to Europe to purchase sheep, while assistants were selected during his absence.

William and Andrew Telfer were signed on as Indented Servants — “Free People” — to the Australian Agricultural Co. and under contract to the Co. for a term of 7 years with an annual salary of £30 ($60) each. William’s agreement was dated 10th February, 1825 and he was “engaged with high recommendation as to

character", Andrew's agreement was dated 16th February, 1825 and endorsed "from Scotland — where he had been accustomed to the care of sheep".

The Co. chartered two ships — the "York" of 429 tons built in 1819 had previously transported convicts to Australia and the "Brothers", a ship of 425 tons built 1815 which had also been used for convict transportation.

On 13th November, 1825 the Co.'s first ship, the "York", sailed into Sydney Harbour. Two days later, the "Brothers" arrived. It had been a long voyage; both ships had left Cowes on 26th June, 1825 and called at Rio de Janeiro in September where they had remained for 14 days, but experienced bad weather and adverse winds which had delayed their arrival in Sydney. The news of the arrival was mentioned in the Sydney Gazette, 24th November, 1825.

William Telfer was aboard the "York" while his brother Andrew was on the "Brothers".

The passengers remained on board for several days while arrangements were made for them to be taken to "Retreat" 30 miles west of Sydney at Bringelly, a total of 25 men, 14 women and 40 children. "Retreat" was a 2,000 acre property built by Thomas Laycock, a former lieutenant in the N.S.W. Corps in 1820 and leased by the Australian Agricultural Co. as a temporary resting place.

The passengers landed on the 27th November. Twenty carts had been hired for their conveyance but these were insufficient and their start was delayed while another twenty were hired for women and children. The long journey to "Retreat" took well into the night and with the development of a heavy thunderstorm, the first night on land in

### PAGE 367
the Colony was spent in miserable conditions.

The Governor, Sir Thomas Brisbane, allowed the livestock — in all, 12 cattle, 15 thoroughbred horses and mares and 720 rams and ewes from France and Saxony to graze in the Government Domain until they were sent to “Retreat” a fortnight later. Only 18 sheep had died during the voyage, the remainder reportedly landed in better condition than when put aboard at Cowes.

In February, 1826 Robert Dawson (manager), the staff, stock and equipment moved to Pt. Stephens, the headquarters of the Australian Agricultural Co. The staff and equipment travelled on a 200 ton brig which was chartered for the thirty hour voyage to Pt. Stephens while the stock travelled overland and unfortunately arrived in fairly poor condition.

Their first accommodation was in tents and bark huts built by aboriginals who gave valued and unexpected assistance. There were about 100 aboriginals in the area at the time. Dawson also bought sheep and cattle locally to build up stock. John Macarthur sold 800 ewes at £4 ($8) a head and 100 cows at £12 ($24) a head; Hannibal Macarthur — 200 ewes at £4 ($8) a head and 70 heifers at £12 ($24) a head; John Bowman — 200 ewes at £4 ($8) and 32 cows at 10 guineas ($21); Captain King sold 80 cows. All were members of the advisory committee for the Australian Agricultural Company.

The first area established by the Australian Agricultural Co. was at Tahlee in 1826. Stroud (Mill Creek) was also established in 1826 and in the following years convicts erected many buildings, including the Hospital — 1830's School "Quambi", 1831; St. John's church, 1833 which Sir Edward Parry, Commissioner, had erected at his own expense; Stroud House 1827-32; Rectory, 1836-39; 1841 convicts built underground silos for grain for the Co. These were circular completely bricked with locally made bricks, about 20' diameter at the base.

From 4th October, 1827 William Telfer was Shepherd in Charge at Booral, the first in this position when it opened, supervising the care of up to 1877 sheep. Booral was formed on an extensive flat bordering the Karuah River. The first sheep dip was at Washpool, 16km from Booral. Booral House was convict built in Lowe's Lane in 1831.

Eventually there were also areas at Carrington, Karuah and Gloucester.

In 1827 Robert Dawson was dismissed from Pt. Stevens. He had been criticised for purchasing stock from committee members and a complaint was made that the amount paid had increased the price of stock locally. James Ebsworth was left in charge.

From the 3rd February, 1828 till May, 1828 William Telfer was Shepherd in Charge at No. 7 Station at Pt. Stevens. In 1828 he was supervising shepherds at three stations extending 7 miles and supplied them with fresh meat three times a week. This was done on horseback. By this time he had earned the title of Master Shepherd. His brother Andrew was designated as a shepherd. As well as the £30 ($60) p.a. they had allowances of 11 lbs. of flour and 7 lbs. of fresh meat a week.

1829 the directors of the Australian Agricultural Co. cancelled the appointment of the committee and Sir Edward Parry, the Arctic explorer, was appointed sole manager and Commissioner.

William Telfer's agreement expired in February, 1832 but he was induced to stay on and was granted an increased allowance of £5 ($10) as "Overseer of Shepherds" from 10th February, 1832.

On 5th March, 1832 William Telfer accompanied Sir Edward Parry, Chas. Hall, Henry Dangar (surveyor) and six other white employees and two aboriginals to examine the country on the Liverpool Plains and on the Peel River to assess the suitability of country tentatively selected for the Co. by Dangar. Twentythree squatters were already occupying part of the land and as

### PAGE 368
squatters, they had no rights whatever to the land. Joseph Brown of Wallamore Station shifted his stock to Attunga Station; Henry Dangar of Goonoo Goonoo went to Nemingha and Moonby Station and Mr. McCann on the Nundle portion of the estate moved to the other side of the river. The property of 600,000 acres taken up was divided into two parts — “Warrah” (from Aboriginal phrase meaning ‘it’s raining’) which was near Quirindi and “Goonoo Goonoo” on the Peel River near Tamworth.

Extract from Parry's Records "Journey to Liverpool Plains", "23rd March, 1832 ... The men at Warrah told me that the pine is sound and strong and splits well. Their hut is built of it. In the case of the Company's obtaining this patch of land Warrah would be the first part occupied. Mr. Hall and myself pointed out to William Telfer a situation best adapted for a store and huts on a gentle rising of forest land, just above the present stock station, which was deeply flooded the other day ..."

A despatch from Parry, 12th October, 1832 -

"Mr. Charles Hall, accompanied by William Telfer ... set out for Liverpool Plains last week to commence the building of a small store, and Shepherd's huts for three Sheep Stations on the land granted to the Co. as a temporary occupation, near Warrah, at Liverpool Plains.

It is intended, after the present shearing (which commences on the 25th inst) to send out to these new Stations about six thousand young and healthy sheep.

I believe I have before informed you of my intention to give William Telfer the entire charge of our new Sheep Stations in that direction, adding perhaps £15 ($30) to £20 ($40) to his salary ... Economy is my sole object in making this arrangement which I have every reason to believe will answer every useful purpose. William Telfer is an intelligent, sober, industrious man, and as careful of the Co.'s property as if it were his own;

and we shall cautiously select such men as are known to be steady and quiet, for sending out to those Stations."

William Telfer was selected instead of Mr. Dangar, the Co's original choice for the position.

A further despatch from Parry, 25th January, 1833 —

"Of William Telfer, whom I have selected to take charge of our new sheep stations, I have the highest possible opinion for sobriety, industry, knowledge of sheep and strict honesty in the case of stores committed to his charge. He is, in all respects, a valuable and faithful servant of the Co., and will, I think, answer all our purpose at least till the second year. I enclose for your perusal William Telfer's first report from Warrah (4th November, 1832) merely to show that what he writes, as well as what he does, is all to the purpose."

With his brother Andrew and two others, William showed his skill by moving the first sheep from Pt. Stevens to Warrah for the Co. 6,000 lambing ewes were moved into the area with the loss of only one sheep.

William Telfer was the first Manager, staying 2 years.

By 10th September, 1835 both the Telfers were being paid £37/10/- ($75), not agreeing to stay for less than this amount. It was then decided to add a further £2/10/- ($5) p.a. to their wages.

In October, 1835 with Parry and two others he went to take up the Peel River estate. He was put in charge of Goonoo Goonoo sheep station. Commissioner Parry always travelled by compass and on the trip from Pt. Stephens William Telfer had learnt to travel the same way, never keeping to the road but travelling the shortest distance with the aid of a compass and with no danger of getting lost. At that time transportation of wool to the coast for shipment from the Peel River was extremely difficult. The road to Maitland was often impassable, and a bullock dray could take nearly six months to complete the journey.

### PAGE 369
Commissioner Dumaresq, 19th December, 1835 in a despatch referred to the need for a less difficult route, "... for carting provisions to the Peel". It was desirable that a route could be discovered avoiding rugged country around the Manning and Barnard Rivers. Following shearing it was intended that William Telfer "who professes a singular aptness for this line of duty" would undertake the exploration.

William Telfer with two white men and six aborigines took eleven weeks to complete "an excellent line of road from Glouster to Tamworth".

The following describes the venture and its impact:

Extract from explanatory notes for Thesis by J.F. Atchison, 21st August, 1973, the information collected by Mr. L.L. Green of Tamworth.

"... Telfer followed up the Barnard River to the high narrow valley around Giro explored by Nisbet, Dangar and Hall during 1831. Telfer then mounted the Dividing Range by the long, steep spur of the Hungry Hill to the tableland around Nowendoc. This tableland, separating the waters of the Manning from those of the Hastings and the Macleay, provided him with a means of access to the Peel. Telfer explored along the south bank of the Nowendoc River across Mukki and Peel Creeks, to the base of Whites Sugarloaf after which he followed down the ridge of the Hell-Hole to Nowendoc Junction. He then rode to the top of the present Port Stephens Cutting. Instead of travelling down the present Pt. Stephens Cutting-Topdale route Telfer came down the next ridge east into the valley of Dungowan or Ogunbil Creek. This was north of Ralfé's entry point (separated by 'aradise and Shearing creeks) from The 'alf Spur and The Pinnacles. Telfer's route then linked with the established track down the Ogunbil to the Peel and thence to Tamworth and Goonoo Goonoo.

Despatch from Dumaresq, 26th April, 1836:

"... I consider William Telfer's discovery of the utmost importance to the

Interests of the Company. It obviated the necessity of a long difficult carriage through the settled Districts of the Hunter, and will enable us to Ship all the Company Produce from this Port. Your Estates are now in fact more compact than those of any Settler I know, with extensive Flocks."

Extract from "Origin and History of Tamworth and District" 2nd September, 1918:

"... The road from Gloucester to the Peel was reduced to about 80 miles by the discovery in 1834 of a new road. This road as is well known ran from Tamworth up and across Dungowan (Orgunbil) Creek and on to the tableland, where the Pt. Stephens cutting now is. The cutting almost follows the old mule track, and an old A.A. Co. button has lately been found near it. It was no uncommon sight to see 30 or 40 pack mules driven by 14 or 15 Chilean muleteers or Spaniards and half a dozen pack bullocks driven by Davey Peebles carrying wool down to the Port, and supplies back, along the marked line. It was only a blazed track and William Telfer is claimed to be the man who marked it out. The mules and asses were imported by Captain King from Valparaiso and two Maltese asses for mule breeding were also purchased ..."

The "line of road" was through some of the wildest parts of Australia and where the aboriginals in the area were very savage and reportedly cannibalistic.

From that time all sheep, except breeding ewes, were taken down to Pt. Stephens to be shorn and returning after shearing to Peel River. The mules from Valparaiso, were in the care of twelve muleteers and used to transport the Co's provisions, each carrying 2 cwt, thus the 30 mules could carry 3 tons of flour at one time taking about five weeks from Pt. Stephens. They also had 6 pack bullocks and though they could carry a little more than a mule, they were much slower and did not travel as well in the mountains. The aboriginals who frequented the marked trail would follow the Co's sheep

### PAGE 370
tracks looking out for any strays, killing and eating them.

The aboriginals in the Peel River area were not hostile and were humanely treated by Australian Agricultural Company managers.

The earliest written record of the name Tamworth was on a letter dated 27th September, 1836 from Colonel Dumaresq (Australian Agricultural Co. Superintendent at the Peel). Tamworth was named after the Staffordshire town represented in the British Parliament by Sir Robert Peel, then also Prime Minister of England, after whom the Peel River was named.

Before long the Australian Agricultural Co. became well known throughout N.S.W. for the quality of their sheep. The original sheep had coarse wool which had been crossed with merinos and other breeds until fineness and length of wool was perfected, and squatters for hundreds of miles were purchasing Australian Agricultural Co. sheep to improve their flocks. By 1838 horses bred by the Co. were also in demand; imported thoroughbred animals had improved the stock.

For the first time in 10 years William Telfer wrote to his family in Scotland. His mother had despaired of ever hearing of him, or Andrew again. One of his sisters in reply offered to come out to N.S.W. to care for the brothers but this did not eventuate.

By the end of 1837 William Telfer had left the Co.'s service, having rejected an offer of £80 ($160) p.a. to continue, for what he considered more eligible employment. However, he was with the Company as an overseer at Goonoo Goonoo 1838, early 1839, although there was some dispute over salary with the Co. at this time.

1837, 1838, 1839 were years of drought; Goonoo Goonoo became a dust bowl. A devastating flood in 1840 broke the drought; several people lost their lives; many sheep, horses and cattle were swept away. Drays with loads of wool were chained to trees to prevent them from

drifting with the flood. Now fences were destroyed. Thick fog penetrated the area and men on the marked trail from Pt. Stephens were lost for a fortnight having to kill their horses for survival. However, the newly ploughed area of the race course which had been planted with wheat to relieve the burden of carrying flour from Pt. Stephens over rough mountain terrain was irrigated by the flood and an exceptional crop was harvested.

At this time Assigned Servants (convicts) of the Australian Agricultural Co. were stationed at West Tamworth - the west side of the Peel River. The Co. had built a Courthouse and a lock-up. Most of the police were formerly soldiers.

In 1839 William Telfer had a small store in West Tamworth.

On 27th September, 1840 he married Margaret Brown of Peel River at St. Andrews (Presbyterian) Church, Singleton, the ceremony performed by the Rev. Irving Hetherington. They lived in West Tamworth until 1844. During this time three of their four children were born:

William born 26th July, 1841 at Calala (the native name for the Peel) then the Head Station of the Company)

Elizabeth

Andrew born 24th June, 1844

Apparently William was persuaded to rejoin the Co. His eldest son, William, had been born at their head station and in 1842 a "Statement of the Present and Proposed plans of Superintendance at the Peel" disclosed that he headed the list of 'present' overseers and was the highest paid at £120 ($240) p.a. — the other overseers earned between £30 ($60) and £60 ($120) p.a.

A report from Commissioner King on 6th June, 1842 reveals:

"I have accordingly appointed William Telfer to the charge of the Peel Establishment with a Salary of £250 ($500) a year ... William Telfer has borne the reputation of being a faithful servant, and it is a pleasing part of my duty to be

### PAGE 371
able to do that justice to his merits which his long tried zeal so much deserves."

A further reference to William Telfer by King on 11th April, 1844 reports that he had "resigned his situation at the Peel."

In Tamworth 1844 Roderick Mitchell, son of Sir Thomas Mitchell, Surveyor-General N.S.W. was Crown Lands Commissioner; his assistant Mr. Frank Allman. About this time many of the Assigned Servants (convicts) received tickets of leave. If they left the area they had been sent to, they were liable to have any property confiscated by the Crown. Commissioner Mitchell was the first person to organise native police, enlisting seven Tamworth aboriginals as mounted police. There were two magistrates, George Jenkin, "Glengarrin" and Charles Hall, "Goonoo Goonoo". At the lower end of Peel Street and on the Manilla Road, Mr. C.T. Williams, a retired lieutenant of the Peninsular War had the only hotel.

William Telfer, with his family, left Tamworth on 4th October, 1844 for Quirindi on the Liverpool Plains, where he had purchased the leasehold of a Mr. Roach's business which consisted of a store and accommodation house where squatters and travelling public stopped overnight. Quirindi was then quite small, two or three slab and bark huts and Loders Station owned by squatter, George Loder who had come from Windsor.

One traveller who stayed with the Telfers was Sir Thomas Mitchell returning to Sydney from one of his inland explorations. He arrived on Christmas eve 1845 and spent Christmas day with them before resuming his journey.

William Telfer built the first public house in Quirindi in 1846, known as "The Squatters Home" on 200 acres he had leased from the N.S.W. Government. This was "on the flat over the bridge" close to the Jacob and Joseph Creeks where at the time a number of aboriginals from the Kamilaroi tribe were camped. It was here in 1848 that W.C. Wentworth stayed en

route to the Burburgate Station which he owned. Also spending the same night at Telfer's inn was the Rev. John Dunmore Lang who was returning from Moreton Bay. Both were members of the newly formed Legislative Council of Sydney. An income of £500 ($1000) a year was one of the qualifications for a seat in the Chamber Dr. Lang "a powerful debater, outspoken and true patriot" was having financial difficulties at the time and William Telfer with others subscribed the amount to keep him in the House.

1850 and the Telfers were back in Tamworth. Another son, Edward, had been born and the two eldest children, William and Elizabeth were attending school; financed jointly by the Church of England and the Australian Agricultural Co. — a bark hut, the sides of bark instead of slab, with a bark roof. This was used for about six months until a brick building was erected which also served as a church. Mr. Porter was the school master.

Tamworth in 1850 was a collection of mainly bark and slab huts on the east side of the river. There were two stores, one conducted by Mr. L.W. Levy on the east side, while Mr. Wm. Cohen operated one on the west side which incorporated the post office. There were two public houses in West Tamworth, "Gannons" and Barnes".

The need for vegetables and the very warm summer climate took its toll of many of the early employees of the Australian Agricultural Co., particularly Assigned Servants (convicts). A diet of salt meat and damper led to blood and liver complaints and many suffered from heart disease. At times two were buried in the one coffin and as many as four in the one grave as there was little room left in the old cemetery.

William Telfer died in Tamworth on 16th September, 1850 and was buried in the old cemetery.

Little is recorded about what happened to the family or how they coped after William's death. The only reference to his wife, Margaret, appeared in the N.S.W. Government Gazette on 29th August,

### PAGE 372
1851 when intention to apply for administration of William Telfer's estate was published, as he had died without leaving a will. She was still living in Tamworth at that time.

His son, William, 'the oldest native of West Tamworth', born 26 July, 1841, appears to have had little schooling following his father's death as in 1851 he was working for Mr. George Jenkin (Magistrate) "Glengarrin", 4 miles from Tamworth. Later he was droving and shepherding in the Liverpool Plains District and in 1859 made an epic droving trip to Maranoa in Queensland. He married Anne Plunkett and lived in Quirindi. They had 4 children. 1900 he was at Macdonalds, Wallabadah; 1913/14 at Currabubula near Tamworth; 1925 at Gosford with daughter, Mary, where he died.

Nothing is known of the other children until they were adults and had families of their own. Elizabeth became Mrs. Mansfield and lived in Tenterfield. Andrew married Martha Bowden and had 4 sons and 4 daughters. He headed the family grazing properties of Telfers Pty. Ltd. with holdings in the Emmaville and Tenterfield districts. They lived at Tent Hill. Edward married and managed Torryburn Station at Muswellbrook for a Dr. McKellar. He went to Western Australia to inspect land for the Doctor but disappeared without a trace despite intensive searching. He left a family of 4 daughters and 2 sons.

William Telfer's brother, Andrew, died in Tamworth on 11th March, 1858. He had married Mary Hayes and they had four children —

William born 1847

Mary born 1849 (Mrs. A.L. Bowden)

Andrew born 1850

Elizabeth born 1854 (Mrs. A. Bolton)
(Betsy)

Andrew had purchased some of the first land available for sale in W. Tamworth on 22nd August, 1851 (part of Goonoo Goonoo Section J — Lots 3 and 12). This land was released for sale by the Australian Agricultural Company.

## COPY OF REPORT

4 November, 1832 by William Telfer at Warrah to Superintendent Charles Hall.

Sir,

I beg leave to inform you that we are all in good health and getting on as fast as we can. Cope and Devitt don't split so much as I expected as the Timber is very bad. They have got near enough of slabs for this place so that I think by the 10th they will be able to begin on the stuff for hurdles. We have got plenty of Bark for both places — John Kendrick has got this Building slabbed nearly all round but he has not got the roof up tho the stuff is all ready. The rats have been very busy about the flour Bags since you went. I found the Big Steelyards don't agree with the small ones by 5lbs to the hundred. I tried some of the Bags of flour and found they weighed only 95lbs by the large Steelyards. By saying that the men dont split so much as might be expected I dont mean that they are idle for they fell a great number of Trees they cannot split they have split mostly pine the Ironbark is as bad as any of it — Thos Hinton looks after his Bullocks very well they dont stray much about now. I have been making enquiries about the way to Seganhoe Stockstation at the head of the Hunter and by what I can learn from Blacks it is a very hilly country between here and there. Neverthelefs I will try to come that way. I think to be able to load the dray about the middle of Dec.r and then I can start Myself immediately after. The beef has been very Muggotty and we had to Salt it over again.

I am Sir your Most Obedt Servant (Sgd.) William Telfer (Enclosed D to Despatch No. 95)

Australian National University,
CANBERRA
Ref. 78/1/13 p. 412

### PAGE 373
## SOME TELFER CONNECTIONS

Thomas Telford (1757-1834) was the famous Scottish Engineer who is supposed to have changed his name from Telfer to Telford, later regretting this action. He was born in Westerkirk, Dumfries, Scotland, the son of a shepherd.

He married Ellen Davidson and they had two children — Robert and Janet Davidson Telford.

Robert Telford, who married Elizabeth Hill, came to Victoria on the ship "Poictiers" in 1854. He was a shepherd from Northumberland, engaged by Splatt & Pysant, C/- Mr. Williams, in January, 1854, at a wage of £120 for twelve months.

Robert and Elizabeth had eight children — Thomas, George, Elizabeth, Ellen, Margaret, Adam, James and Mary Jane, who married William Doddrell. George, aged 13; Ellen aged 11, Margaret aged 9 and Adam aged 5, accompanied their parents to Australia.

Janet Davidson Telford married Mr. Cooper and their daughter, Janet Davidson Cooper married Joseph Harris.

Janet and Joseph had two sons who were both connected with the Methodist Church. Abraham Kemp Harris, who married his cousin, Bessie Hill Doddrell, was Methodist Home Missioner at Tarcoola, South Australia, and his brother, Daniel Harris, was a Methodist Minister in South Australia.

Mr. C.R.A. Lloyd, of Casuarina, Northern Territory, has sent information concerning Mary Jane Telford. As the names Telfer and Telford are often interchangeable, it is possible that there is some connection between the families.

Mary Jane Telford was the daughter of Thomas Telford. She married Joseph Taylor, the son of James and Elizabeth Taylor. Joseph was baptised in the Church of St. Mary and St. Michael, Doddington, Northumberland, on 18th October, 1863.

He was a police sergeant at South Shields for many years.

He died on 22nd June, 1938, and is buried at Harton Cemetery, South Shields.

The marriage of Mary Jane Telford and Joseph Taylor took place at St. Mary's Church, Tyne Dock, on 7th April, 1886. Joseph and Mary had two sons and eight daughters. Mary died on 29th April, 1942.

When Joseph Taylor died, an entry appeared in the South Shields Gazette on Saturday, 25th June, 1938. Among other things it stated that Mary Jane Telford had been companion to the late Lady Runciman and nursemaid to Lord Runciman.

Anne Marshall Taylor, one of the daughters of Joseph and Mary Taylor, married John McVay, and their daughter, Audrey, married Mr. C.R.A. Lloyd.

Mr. and Mrs. Aubrey Telfer, of Belcatta, Western Australia, have told us something of the descendants of another Francis Telfer who was born in 1839. He arrived in Victoria on the ship "Star of India" in July, 1863, at the age of 29. He had five children — William, Isabel, Mary, John and Bertie David.

William Telfer was born in 1875, at Neereman, Victoria. He married Beatrice Tippett on 13th January, 1896, at

### PAGE 374
Bendigo, Victoria. They were living in Western Australia when their son, Francis William Telfer, was born on 24th December, 1903. Their place of residence was given as "Woolgar" near Coolgardie, and William Telfer was a miner. They already had a daughter, Iris, who married Frank Luff. They later had another child, Innis Telfer.

Iris Telfer and Frank Luff had two sons, Les and John. Francis William Telfer married Hilda May Harris on 24th November, 1928, in Perth. He was a Clerk, residing at Mount Lawley, Western Australia. They had a son, Douglas Francis Edwin Telfer, born on 29th July, 1933.

Isabel Telfer married Aubrey Warren, who owned Bangawan Station, north of Lenora, Western Australia. They had five daughters and one son, Warren, who died young. One of their daughters married Max Jones, and their son, Warren Jones is well known for his participation in the America's Cup Challenge.

Walter Telfer O'Bryan.

Mary Telfer married Mr. Boucher and they lived in Victoria.

John Telfer had three sons.

Bertie David Telfer was born in Bendigo on 4th December, 1886. He went to Western Australia before the First World War. He joined the 10th Light Horse Regiment and was wounded in France. He had five children — Gladys, Aubrey, Robert, Joyce and Neville. Robert had one daughter and four sons.

Mrs. Hilda O'Bryan of "Forest View", Trundle, New South Wales, wrote that her husband was descended from Margaret Telfer, the daughter of James Telfer and Katherine Armstrong, of County Roxburghshire, Scotland. Margaret was born about 1850, and came to Australia in 1869 at the age of 19, in company with her Aunt, also called Margaret Telfer, who was engaged to John Rutherford, of the Young area of New South Wales.

Margaret was one of seven children. Her brothers and sister were Lawrence, John, Martin, William, Andrew and Lucy.

Margaret married Martin O'Bryan at Wangaratta in 1870 when she was 20 years of age. Their children were Henry, Eliza, Katherine, Julia, Walter and Martin (twins), Margaret (whose twin sister, Dolly, died), Christina and Georgina.

Margaret and Martin O'Bryan came to Trundle about 1898 from Oaklands near Urana. They farmed in the area for some years before retiring in Parkes. Margaret died on 27th August, 1915, and Martin died on 10th October, 1921. Both are buried in the Parkes Cemetery.

Their son, Walter Telfer O'Bryan, believed that his mother, Margaret, had been born in Gretna Green. Walter was born on 25th February, 1885, in a tiny one-roomed shack made from 4-gallon drums. This shack was on a property known as "Clear Hills", a farm near Urana, New South Wales. He died at Trundle on 6th November, 1979, aged almost 95 years. He married Cathreen Laydon, and their children were Telfer

### PAGE 375
(who married Jelma Turner), Jean (Mrs. Phil Hunt), Jim (who married Joan Johnson), Jack, Martin, Meg (Mrs. Maxwell), Bonnie (Mrs. Hunt), Edward (who married Thelma Crowley), Alan and Kevin.

Jim O'Bryan was born at Peak Hill, New South Wales. He married Joan Johnson who was born at Griffith, New South Wales. They had thirteen children — Margaret (Green), Dorothy (Glasson), Roma Joan (Wackit — McCowan), John Lawrence m. Margaret, Barry m. Elaine Henry; Gordon James m. Maree, Keith, who died aged 6 months, Lorraine Ellen who married Charles Spiteri, Judith Anne m. Pat Griffin, Kathleen Mary m. Colin Wiles, Raymond David m. Arleen Strudwick, Carmel June m. Kim Stradhorn, and Colin Lesley, who was killed in a car accident at the age of 17 years.

Lorraine O'Bryan married Charles Spiteri and they have eight children — Sharleen Gaye, Cheryle Joan, Louise Pauline, Mark Charles, Andrew Arthur, Brendon John, Darrin Patrick and Suzanne Lorraine.

Mr. Jack Telfer, of Otorohanga, New Zealand states that his grandfather was Alexander Telfer, born in 1842, at Laminton, Lanarkshire, Scotland. He came to Australia in 1863, at the age of 20, on the ship "Champion of the Seas" as an assisted passenger. Also on the same ship was George Telfer who was thought to have been Alexander's uncle. A brother and sister also came to Australia.

Alexander Telfer settled in the Gippsland area of Victoria where he met and married Margaret Whitbourn, the daughter of John and Helen Whitbourn (nee Scott). The Whitbourn family owned a lot of land on the Lindenew Flats near Bairnsdale in Victoria. The marriage took place on 14th February, 1878 when Alexander was 36 and Margaret 19 years of age. It has been recorded that at the 90th birthday celebrations of John Whitbourn, a speech was made by George Telfer.

Alexander and Margaret farmed on the river flats near Bairnsdale for 17 years. Their chief crop was maize, but they were dogged by bad seasons and a succession of caterpillar plagues and floods. They had only two good seasons during their 17 years at Bairnsdale. They lost their home in a flood and left the district. The series of disasters was a major cause of the break-up of their marriage.

Alexander and Margaret had seven children — Ellen (b. and d. 1878), Alexander (1879-1943), Ellen Janet (1882-1969), George (1884-1896), Mary (1888-1958), James (1891-1931) and Henry (1896-1985).

Alexander Jnr. left Australia and went to New Zealand about the year 1900. He returned to Australia for a short time but decided that there were better opportunities in New Zealand so he returned there to live permanently.

After Alexander Snr. had separated from his wife, he too went to New Zealand. Four of his children — Janet,

Marriage Certificate of Mary Anne Telfer and Frederick William Banks.

### PAGE 376
Mary, Jim and Henry all went to New Zealand at different times.

Alexander Jnr. married Alice Loft. They had eight sons — Frank (1901-1919), who was killed in the First World War, George Alexander (b. 1905), James (b. 1906), Archibald (b. 1907), Douglas (b. 1908), Colin (b. and d. 1910), Jack (b. 1916) and Hedley (b. 1924).

Jack Telfer married Claire Ethel Williams and they have four daughters — Yvonne (b. 1940), Valerie (b. 1942), Joy (b. 1946) and Robyn (b. 1950). Jack and Ethel live at Otorohanga, which is known as the “Kiwi Town of New Zealand”. It is not far from the Waitoma Caves.

Henry Telfer was the youngest son of Alexander Snr. and Margaret Telfer. He settled on a farm at Ratapiko in New Zealand, having won a ballot for a Soldier Settlers Block after World War I. Henry's son, Allan, and his wife Moira, live at

Taranak, New Zealand. Alexander's daughter, Janet Telfer m. Owen Meglade, and her sister, Mary, married Tom Kemsley.

Jack Telfer is compiling the story of his early years and experiences. He is interested in the family history and in the course of his research, he contacted another Jack Telfer, who is descended from the brother of Alexander Snr. There were many coincidences and not a little humour between the two families as both are "Jack and Pat Telfer". Each time that Jack (son of Alexander) put a birth notice in the paper on the arrival of a daughter, the other Jack announced the birth of a son! This happened on four occasions, and then the "other" Jack and Pat announced the birth of a daughter at a place nearby and the two families became acquainted for the first time. They have remained in contact ever since.

I. Allan Telfer m. Moura

Jean Telfer wife of Herbert Victor Telfer, of Dalkeith, Western Australia, sent the following information —

James Gibson Telfer, born 21st October, 1861, in Jedburgh, Scotland, emigrated to Victoria, and married Sarah Ann Armstrong. While living in Victoria they had two sons.

The family moved to Collie in Western Australia, where another son and daughter were born. During the First World War they moved to West Leederville, a suburb of Perth.

Kay Burgess, of Essendon, Victoria, is also researching her Telfer ancestors. She is a descendant of James Telfer who married a Miss Wilson. James was born in Scotland.

Their son, Alexander, was born in Scotland in 1855. He came to South Australia about 1869 where he married Sarah Ann Boothey at Wesley Church, Mount Gambier, on 4th June, 1879. Sarah Ann Boothey was born on 8th May, 1860, at Morphtettville, South Australia, the daughter of John and Hannah Boothey (nee Field).

Alexander and Sarah Ann Telfer had four children — Ethel Telfer, James Alexander Telfer b. 1880, Henrietta Janet Telfer b. 1882, and Agnes May Telfer b. 1884 at Dimboola, Victoria.

Agnes May Telfer married George Joseph William Irwin on 23rd October, 1911. She died on 12th November, 1952. Alexander Telfer died at the age of 31 at Dimboola, Victoria. He had been in Australia for 18 years. His wife, Sarah Ann, also died at Dimboola on 22nd September, 1942.

Mrs Heather Telfer, of Whyalla Norrie, South Australia, states that Frederick Thomas Telfer and his family lived at Fife, Scotland. He came to Australia and settled at Weston, near Maitland, New South Wales.

He married a Miss Hogg and they had two sons, Mark Hogg Telfer, and Frederick Telfer.

Heather married Mark Hogg Telfer in Newcastle, New South Wales, and they had one son, Mark James Telfer.

Frederick Telfer was killed at Port Kembla several years ago. He had two sons, one of whom was killed in a motor cycle accident at the age of 18. The other son is Rodney Telfer.

Mark James Telfer and his wife, Mary, have three children — Belinda, Paul and Thomas.

Thomas Telfer (1859-1931) was one of seven brothers, born in Kent, England. His family originated in the Border areas of Scotland. He married Agnes Kay.

## THOMAS TELFER (1859-1931) m. AGNES KAY

Grant Telfer is the proprietor of Grant Engineering at Monash, South Australia. As well as producing farm sheds and equipment, Grant Telfer was responsible for the design and building of the adult playground at Monash, one of the most important tourist attractions in South Australia's Riverland. Although he has no formal engineering qualifications he has an extremely inventive mind, and he has designed many useful gadgets. One of the most important of his recent projects has been the "Gopher" a special aid for the handicapped.

From Mrs. Jean Smith of Coolbellup, Western Australia —

Her great grandparents were George and Jeanette Telfer. They lived at 30 Dudley Road, Clacton, England.

George was the son of James Telfer who died in 1843. George and Jeanette had eleven children, and some of the family, including Mary Ann Telfer and her husband, Frederick William Banks, migrated to South Australia in 1911. Some brothers and sisters went to Queensland and others to the United States of America.

The family of George and Jeanette Telfer were —

Alexander (migrated to the U.S.A.)

George (migrated to the U.S.A.)

William

Richard and Alice (twins)

Elizabeth (migrated to Australia)

Jeanette (migrated to Australia)

Agnes (migrated to Australia)

Mary Ann (migrated to Australia)

Caroline (migrated to Australia)

Robert (migrated to Australia)

Mrs. Alice Parkinson, of Port Lincoln, South Australia, and Mrs. Jean Smith, of Coolbellup, Western Australia, are both descendants of George and Jeanette Telfer.

George Telfer was a wood cutting machine operator, and Jeanette was a tailoress. Their daughter, Mary Ann (1878-1923) married Frederick William Banks who was a painter by trade. The marriage took place at St. Cuthberts Church in the Parish of Hampstead, County Middlesex, on 23rd May, 1896.

Robert Adam Telfer, one of nine children, was born in Glasgow, Scotland. He came to South Australia and married Janet Campbell in Adelaide. They had two daughters, Jean and Barbara. Robert Adam Telfer was the Manager of the Grosvenor Hotel in Adelaide for many years.

His nephew, Donald Telfer, married Doreen Cotton, and they had three children — Susan, Jane and John McMartin Telfer who married Judy Lehman. John and Judy have a son, James Stephen Telfer.

John Bowden took up Section 6085 in 1838 and named it Kersbrook, after the farm in Cornwall where he was born. 6180

Transferred from Francis Telfer to John and George in 8-5-1877. Sold again to Jabez Harvey Bowden 5-12-1882.
